   A Mathematical, Patristic, Scriptural, and Logical Defense of the Right and Christian Doctrine of
Election as Opposed to the Deceptive Doctrine of the Devil

By: Isaiz James Tomey
Manistee, MI




    [0] Dearest Reader, I would like to begin this work, this substantive defense of that most ancient and
true doctrine, which has been part and parcel of the Christian faith from the time of Adam to Noah to
Abraham to Moses to David to the Prophets to our Lord and his Apostles to the Early Church—and, in
spite of the machinations of those who, like the most vile of sorcerors, cloak themselves in the most evil
of fabric, in this case being fabric of late Arminianism, an egg of the old Pelagianism, which we had well
hoped had been long since been devoured by that most ancient serpent from which it spawned, but is sit
upon and brooded by the wanton wits of their degenerate and apostate spirits, continues to this day. That
belief is, as I am sure you inferred from the title of this work, Election. I would like you to imagine, dear
reader, the universe as a wheel, the Wheel of Creation, which burns with the Glory of the Lord, who is the
fount of all glories. In the center of the wheel, in the Hub, you have God, and the spokes which connect
the wheel to the center, are the elect. The rest of the wheel, the outer wheel, which is only connected to
the Hub by the spokes, are the reprobate and wicked, who will be cast out into that place of fire and
gnashing of teeth, when the day of judgement comes. The spokes are joined directly to the hub. Their
relationship to the center is not incidental; it is constitutive of their place in the wheel. The elect belong to
God, and their connection to Him is what establishes their position in the whole. By contrast, the outerim,
is not connected to God, their place is outside of His direct connection, yet, they are still a part of the
wheel. Here in lies the question, which has long been irking my mind. Is it rational of us, to presume that
the rim of the wheel can somehow become the spokes? That is nonsense to presume, yet, many do so.
They, being sons of the devil, not of God, seek to destroy the glory of God’s free and severing grace, and
the salvation of the elect through the redemption that is in Jesus Christ (which is external, or none at all),
which means it therefore follows that the defense of this true and ancient doctrine, must be foremost of
the unfeigned desires and utmost aims of all that are truly Christian. To that end, I do pray that, this work
suffices a defense.
    [1] The end of Christ's death comprises both the purpose which the Father and the Son intended
through it and the effect which the appointed work actually accomplished. These belong to one
undertaking, though intention and fulfillment answer distinct questions. The Holy Ghost's expressions
concerning each therefore determine how their connection must be understood. A purpose appoints an
end for particular persons, and its accomplishment requires an operation sufficient to bring those persons
into the condition intended. Every inference which follows depends upon preserving this relation between
the intended recipients, the means appointed, and the effect produced.
     [2] A saving purpose concerns determinate persons brought into an intended good. Its
accomplishment consists in the actual production of that good through the operations appointed to procure
it. The language of salvation can therefore conceal a profound difference when opportunity is attributed to
the work by one account and effective deliverance by another. The revealed undertaking supplies the
grounds upon which its several relations can be distinguished and their connections established. Its
recipients retain their concrete history throughout — each person has a standing, a will, a relation to
Christ, and a course of conduct, and salvation involves actual transformations within these relations. The
Wheel of Creation in paragraph 0 gives the inquiry its figure. The spokes have their place by their relation
to the Hub, and we may now follow the work by which God brings his own into the good he intends for
them.

                              [3] H={x:xis human},      R(x),   F(x,t),   S(x). (1)
     [4] H designates the domain of human persons. Within that domain, R(x) states that Christ undertakes
the representative saving oblation for x. F(x,t) states that x exercises saving faith at time t. S(x) states that
x finally enjoys salvation. The initial content of R identifies the undertaking together with its intended
recipient. The consequences belonging to that undertaking require their own establishment in the
argument.
    [5] The inference from R(x) to S(x) depends upon the premises concerning Christ's offering, his
intercession, and the production of faith. The definition of R supplies no concealed conclusion concerning
their efficacy. Nor does the fact that every person is not finally saved, considered by itself, explain the
cross. That fact acquires its force for the present inquiry through its conjunction with what has been
established concerning the efficacy of the cross.
     [6] The intention governing Christ's entrance into the world is declared by Christ himself, who knows
his own undertaking and the secrets of his Father's bosom — the “Son of man came to save that which
was lost”. Recovery and salvation of lost sinners constitute the purpose of his coming. His apostles
receive and declare the same purpose, as Paul writes, “This is a faithful saying, and worthy of all
acceptation, that Christ Jesus came into the world to save sinners.” The persons embraced by this gracious
intention are likewise specified in the undertaking. Christ came to “give his life a ransom for many.”
Those elsewhere called us, and identified as believers distinguished from the world, are the recipients for
whose sins he “gave himself for our sins, that he might deliver us from this present evil world, according
to the will of God and our Father.” The Father's will orders the gift of the Son to their salvation and their
separation from the present evil world. The same persons are identified as his church, “He loved the
church, and gave himself for it; that he might sanctify and cleanse it with the washing of water by the
word, that he might present it to himself a glorious church, not having spot, or wrinkle, or any such thing;
but that it should be holy and without blemish.” The final clauses explain the end for which he gives
himself, since the persons for whom he acts are to become fit for God and to be brought into his presence.
This purpose is repeated in the declaration, “He gave himself for us, that he might redeem us from all
iniquity, and purify unto himself a peculiar people, zealous of good works.” Christ and his Father thus
intend a determinate work for determinate recipients — their salvation, deliverance from the evil world,
cleansing from pollution, holiness, zeal, and fruitfulness in good works, through which they are rendered
acceptable and brought to God. Through Christ “we have access into the grace wherein we stand”. The
several expressions disclose what the sending of the Son is appointed to produce. Salvation, cleansing,
and access to God are actual changes in persons whose former condition required deliverance. Their
causal content therefore carries the inquiry through the undertaking to the provision by which those
changes occur. The recipients remain the subjects of the intended transformation from its appointment to
its accomplishment. (Matthew 18:11; 20:28; Luke 19:10; 1 Timothy 1:15; Galatians 1:4; Ephesians 5:25–
27; Titus 2:14; Romans 5:2)
    [7] The actual effect of Christ's death, the shedding of his blood, and his oblation is declared with the
same fullness as its intention, and often with still greater distinction. First, reconciliation removes the
enmity between God and us, “when we were enemies we were reconciled to God by the death of his
Son.” The apostle describes God as “God was in him reconciling the world unto himself, not imputing
their trespasses unto them” and affirms that he has “reconciled us to himself by Jesus Christ.” The
operation producing this peace is itself specified — Christ “he abolished in his flesh the enmity, the law
of commandments consisting in ordinances; for to make in himself of twain one new man, so making
peace; and that he might reconcile both unto God in one body by the cross, having slain the enmity
thereby”, so that “he is our peace.” Secondly, justification removes the guilt of sin through the remission
and pardon procured for us, redeeming us from its power and from the curse and wrath to which it made
us liable. Thus “by his own blood he entered into the holy place, having obtained eternal redemption for
us”. “He redeemed us from the curse, being made a curse for us”. And “his own self bearing our sins in
his own body on the tree.” Although we have “all sinned, and come short of the glory of God,” we are

“justified freely by his grace through the redemption that is in Christ Jesus, whom God has set forth to be
a propitiation through faith in his blood, to declare his righteousness for the remission of sins”. For “in
him we have redemption through his blood, even the forgiveness of sins.” Thirdly, sanctification purges
the uncleanness and pollution of sin, renews God's image in us, and supplies the graces belonging to the
Spirit of holiness. Accordingly, “the blood of Christ, who through the eternal Spirit offered himself to
God, purges our consciences from dead works that we may serve the living God”, and “the blood of Jesus
Christ cleanses us from all sin.” “By himself he purged our sins.” To “sanctify the people with his own
blood, he suffered without the gate.” His relation to the church is expressed in the same causal order, “He
gave himself for the church to sanctify and cleanse it, that it should be holy and without blemish.” Among
these graces of the Spirit, faith has its own express place, “it is given to us,” ὑπὲρ Χριστοῦ, “for Christ’s
sake, to believe on him”, God “blessing us in him with all spiritual blessings in heavenly places.”
Fourthly, adoption confers the evangelical liberty and glorious privileges proper to God's children, “God
sent forth his Son, made of a woman, made under the law, to redeem them that were under the law, that
we might receive the adoption of sons.” Fifthly, the effects of this death extend to the recipients'
establishment in heaven, in glory and immortality forever. Their inheritance is a “purchased possession”,
and the connection of the purchase with its enjoyment is declared, “And for this cause he is the mediator
of the new testament, that by means of death, for the redemption of the transgressions that were under the
first testament, they which are called might receive the promise of eternal inheritance.” The death and
bloodshedding of Jesus Christ therefore effectually procure eternal redemption for all who are concerned
in them, embracing grace in the present and glory hereafter. These benefits remain distinguishable within
their common procurement. Guilt concerns liability. Reconciliation concerns the overcoming of enmity.
Sanctification concerns the disposition and exercise of the person's powers. Adoption concerns filial
standing and its privileges. One redemption supplies the ordered provision through which each becomes
actual, and their conjunction proceeds from that provision without dissolving the differences between
their respective operations. (Romans 5:10; 3:23–25; 2 Corinthians 5:18–19; Ephesians 2:14–16; 5:25–27;
1:3, 14; Hebrews 9:12, 14–15; 1:3; 13:12; Galatians 3:13; 4:4–5; 1 Peter 2:24; Colossians 1:14; 1 John
1:7; Philippians 1:29)
    [8] The recipient of these benefits must be considered through the different relations which
redemption changes. Liability, disposition, union, and outward conduct belong to the same sinner, yet
each identifies a distinct condition requiring its own account. Altering judicial liability and renewing the
will are distinguishable operations. A renewed will, in turn, is distinguishable from each particular act
proceeding from it. The saving work consists in their ordered conjunction, through which the person
receives the several benefits already enumerated.
    [9] The need for another's righteousness and our inability to attain life stand together in the Epistle to
Diognetus because the sinner's condition includes both guilt and powerlessness. Their connection leaves
their respective necessities intact. Justification requires a sufficient ground upon which liability is
removed, while life requires an operation sufficient to restore the person who lacks it. An account which
absorbs both into an undifferentiated benefit conceals part of the difficulty which redemption resolves.
Removal of liability and actual restoration must therefore be followed together through the salvation of
the person who formerly stood under condemnation. (Epistle to Diognetus 9)
                                  [10] Xx(t)=⟨Lx(t),Wx(t),Ux(t),Cx(t)⟩. (2)
     [11] X designates the person's creaturely condition within the present inquiry. L names judicial
liability. W the disposition of the will. U the saving relation of union. C the course of conduct. Each term
identifies a condition or relation together with its respective kinds of possible states. The notation assigns
no measured quantity to guilt or grace and does not make their different relations commensurable.
    [12] The change asserted by a scriptural passage takes its meaning from the coordinate which that
passage concerns. A complete account of salvation establishes, in addition, how the coordinates are
connected. Justification, sanctification, and adoption consequently retain their actual differences while

being procured through one redemption. The categories give precision to the assertion of these relations,
and their content remains the scriptural content already assigned to each.
    [13] The scriptural declarations concerning the ends and effects of Christ's death possess a clarity and
fullness proportionate to their foundational place in Christian religion. Yet scarcely any matter has
become more disputed. The spreading doctrine of a general ransom extends Christ's redeeming death to
every descendant of Adam, including every person beyond the many, the church, and God's elect. Its
advocates understand the difficulty arising when this extension is joined to the effects already established
from Scripture. If redemption, cleansing, sanctification, and glory are the immediate fruits of the death
appointed to procure them, either God and Christ fail to attain their intended end, or every descendant of
Adam receives those fruits. The first conclusion makes Christ's death an insufficiently proportioned
means of accomplishing the Father's purpose, although no independent cause of the alleged failure can be
assigned. It blasphemously injures God's wisdom, power, and perfection, while diminishing the worth of
the death which he appointed. The second conclusion requires the salvation, purification, sanctification,
and glorification of all mankind. The advocates themselves will scarcely maintain it, and both Scripture
and the miserable experience of millions exclude it. Their doctrine therefore obtains its apparent
plausibility by changing what the Father intended and what the Son immediately procured. The absolute
purpose and purchase of the benefits recounted above are denied. In their place stands a benefit common
to every soul, including those who remain cursed in unbelief and finally damned. Until faith distinguishes
some persons from others, the death itself is said to effect nothing for one which it does not equally effect
for all. Faith must then stand outside Christ's procurement, since a faith equally procured for everyone
would require an account of its unequal possession. This construction weakens the virtue, value, fruits,
and effects of Christ's satisfaction, and provides the foundation for a dangerous, comfortless, and
erroneous persuasion. The scriptural claim and the proofs upon which it rests must therefore be declared
together, with dependence upon the Lord's assistance and prayer that his Spirit lead us into truth, give
understanding, and reveal what remains hidden to those who judge otherwise. The number of proposed
recipients cannot be enlarged while leaving the proposition concerning efficacy untouched. When one
complete saving operation is attributed to every person, its established consequences belong to every
person. Both the extent of those represented and the content of Christ's operation consequently govern the
controversy.
   [14] An end is the good which an agent intends to accomplish through the operation proper to his
nature and chosen in his present condition. The means comprise what he undertakes to attain that good.
Together they constitute the intelligible order of action in free agents acting through choice or election.
Noah's construction of the ark had the preservation of himself, his family, and others from the flood as its
appointed end. His obedience gave that intention its executed form, “According to all that God
commanded him, so did he.” The ark was thus a means through which the intended preservation was
obtained. Absalom's contrary undertaking exhibits the same relation within a wicked purpose. Intending
revolt against his father and possession of the crown and kingdom, “he prepared him horses and chariots,
and fifty men to run before him”. Through flattering words and accommodating appearances “he stole the
hearts of the men of Israel”. And under the pretense of sacrifice at Hebron he assembled a powerful
conspiracy. These operations formed the means of his intended usurpation. Their connection to the end
explains their place within one undertaking. A means is therefore identified through what it effects, the
conditions under which it effects it, and the contribution of that effect to the accomplishment sought. The
end gives significance to the several operations because it orders their distinct effects toward the good or
advantage which the agent proposes to obtain. (Genesis 6:22; 2 Samuel 15:1, 6, 12)
     [15] The completion of rational life, as Athenagoras explains it, consists in enduring contemplation of
God and includes the reconstitution of the embodied person. The final cause consequently embraces the
creature's entrance into the good for which he was made, extending beyond relief from a present distress.
This completed condition determines the adequacy of an account of the saving means — their relation to
its attainment must be established. An explanation ending with the availability of an opportunity for life

would leave unexplained the actual possession of the appointed good and, with it, the completion of the
undertaking. (Athenagoras, On the Resurrection of the Dead 25)
     [16] End and means explain one another through different kinds of causality. The end moves and
governs the undertaking as that for whose sake the whole is performed. Without its determination of an
effect and a way of acting, an agent would have no reason to undertake one operation rather than another.
The builders in the old world, seeking continued habitation together and perhaps security against another
destruction, declared their purpose in appointing its means, “Go to, let us build us a city, and a tower
whose top may reach unto heaven; and let us make us a name, lest we be scattered abroad upon the face
of the whole earth.” Their intention of unity, common residence, and a name preceded the choice of the
city and tower through which they expected to secure it. A wise agent's method likewise receives its order
from the end proposed in counsel. The end is first in intention and contrivance, while the means procure
its realization. Food preserves life, a ship permits the intended sea passage, and laws maintain the quiet
continuance of society. Each exists within the undertaking for the end's sake, and each brings about the
end in the manner proper to its operation. That procurement admits two distinct considerations. First,
where action and result are judged under a moral rule prescribed to the agent, the means may deserve or
merit the end. Had Adam continued innocent in obedience to the law given him, eternal blessedness
would have followed. Sinful action now has death and the law's curse as its deserved issue. Secondly, the
natural relation of the means to the resulting event concerns instrumental efficiency. Joab, intending
Abner's death, “he smote him with his spear under the fifth rib, that he died.” The wounds which Benaiah
inflicted upon Shimei at Solomon's command likewise efficiently caused his death. Murder of an innocent
and execution of an offender possess the same relation between wound and death under this natural
consideration. Moral judgment, however, introduces χάσμα μέγα between them because their conformity
to the governing rule differs. The end which follows an action morally thus depends upon its desert, while
the natural event follows the operation's efficient power. Appointment and execution retain their different
explanatory orders throughout. The intended end accounts for the choice and ordering of the means, while
their actual operation accounts for its historical realization. This reciprocal explanation attributes no
efficient power to a later event over its own earlier occurrence. It distinguishes the intention which orders
the undertaking from the operations through which the undertaking becomes complete. (Genesis 11:4; 2
Samuel 3:27; 1 Kings 2:46)
    [17] An undertaking receives determinate order from the end governing its appointment and from the
transitions accomplishing it. Within temporal history, a condition supplies the occasion and material for a
subsequent operation. A transition rule states this dependence by describing the actual changes which
specified operations produce. Their theological character belongs to the rule's content and remains part of
the explanation of the transformation. The foundations of cybernetics include this precise treatment of
transformations and their dependence upon the operations under which they occur. (Ashby, An
Introduction to Cybernetics, chs. 2–4)
                              [18] Xx(t+1)=Tt(Xx(t),ux(t);d),       G⊆X. (3)
    [19] X designates the creaturely state. U comprises the temporal means and occasions relevant to its
change. D denotes the governing appointment. This appointment does not locate God within the
creaturely sequence as a changing state. T states the transition occurring under the appointed provision,
while G designates the class of states in which the specified end is fulfilled.
    [20] The intention to bring a person into G carries a claim concerning the operations securing that
outcome. A possible transition into G supplies a different and lesser claim than the actual direction of the
appointed operations to the person's arrival there. The difference bears directly upon any account which
substitutes possible salvation for the accomplished deliverance described in Scripture. The provision of
access to an outcome must retain its distinction from the effective procurement of that outcome.
   [21] The distinction between moral desert and natural production discloses a further distinction
between the end of the agent and the end of his act. Where an agent's defect or perversity leads him to

choose means unsuited to his proposed end under the rule governing his action, the work produces a
moral consequence which he did not intend. Adam, enticed by the desire to become like God, makes that
likeness his aim and eats the forbidden fruit as its supposed means. The act instead contracts guilt which
his intention never embraced. Where an agent acts rightly, choosing an end appropriate to his perfection
and condition and employing means fitted to obtain it, the work and the worker possess the same end.
Abel's faithful sacrifice, intended as worship of the Lord, was acceptable worship. The person who seeks
salvation through Christ accordingly applies himself to obtaining an interest in Christ. The divergence
possible in the former case arises from the condition of secondary agents. A law appointed by another
governs their actions and determines consequences which attend their working whether they intend them
or not. God's will and good pleasure, by contrast, constitute the sole rule of his outward works. No
deviation can introduce an end attending his action which he has not precisely intended. Any alleged
failure consequently requires a determinate cause — an inadequate operation, an absent necessary
condition, or an intervention which defeats execution. The denial of failure in the saving undertaking
includes the provision of its necessary conditions and the government of the circumstances through which
the appointed operations proceed.
    [22] Failure explains nothing until the condition producing it has been identified. A missing capacity,
a condition left unprovided, an obstruction outside the undertaking's government, or a defective choice of
means must account for the result. God's securing of the saving end therefore concerns the entire ordained
course, including the creaturely operations through which the purpose is fulfilled. The affirmation
receives its truth from the revealed purpose and faithfulness already declared. The formal distinction
identifies precisely the kinds of defect which that affirmation excludes from the undertaking.
                          [23] ∃h∈Ax:End(h)∈G ⇏ ∀h∈Ax:End(h)∈G. (4)
    [24] A(x) designates the set of histories admitted under an explicitly specified account of means and
conditions. End(h) designates the terminal issue under consideration. The expression on the left affirms
the availability of a successful history. The expression on the right affirms success throughout the
histories permitted by that account. Their different scope determines the different content of the claims.
    [25] A single successful course satisfies an assertion of mere possibility even when the same account
permits final failure. Secured salvation requires the further establishment of a causal provision which
excludes that failure. The stronger property consequently belongs to the provision only when its
operations have been examined and their adequacy established. Permitted histories cannot simply be
defined by the conclusion sought, since such a definition would contribute nothing to its demonstration.
    [26] A free agent's end may name the effect produced or the good for whose sake he produces it. The
builder who constructs a house for rent effects the house while gain moves him to undertake its
construction. A physician effects the patient's cure while the promised reward may supply his motive.
Judas likewise went to the priests, bargained with them, conducted the soldiers to the garden, and kissed
Christ in order to betray his Master. The thirty pieces of silver supplied the further end for whose sake he
undertook the betrayal, “What will you give me, and I will do it?”. In Christ's death God effected the
satisfaction of his justice, while the good for whose sake he effected it embraces both his own glory as the
supreme end and our glory with him as a subordinate end. An accomplished effect thus has a place within
the more comprehensive purpose which orders its accomplishment. Satisfaction and the manifestation of
glory retain their proper connection because the former can serve as an intermediate result within the
latter, without either term losing the office it occupies in the causal account.
   [27] The goodness belonging to a means admits two considerations. First, an operation may possess
genuine goodness in itself while also serving a further end. Study, the soul's most noble employment,
possesses such goodness. Considered specifically as a means to wisdom or knowledge, however, its
goodness concerns its conduciveness to that end, apart from which it becomes “a weariness of the flesh,”
Eccl. xii. 12. A means precisely as means receives its character from the further good to which it is
ordered. Intrinsic desirability belongs to a different consideration of the same operation. Secondly, some

means possess no independent desirability and receive their entire relative goodness from the end they are
fitted to attain. Amputation preserves life through the loss of a limb. A bitter medicine restores health.
Corn and cargo are thrown into the sea to avert shipwreck. The death of Christ belongs to this latter
consideration, as its subsequent treatment will establish. Adequacy is therefore relational throughout —
the means must be fitted to the specified end, the persons concerned, and the circumstances of execution.
Suffering abstracted from this order leaves unexplained the obedience and procurement accomplished
through it. Its place within the ordered undertaking gives that suffering its determinate mediatorial office.
    [28] Redemption presents these general relations in their determinate theological content — the agent
who works, the means through which he works, and the end which his work effects constitute
distinguishable terms within one ordered undertaking. Their several offices give an adequate
apprehension of the whole. The first receives its treatment, σὺν Θεῷ, in paragraphs 33–72. Agent,
operation, recipient, condition, and result must therefore remain distinguishable throughout the formal
account, while their connections establish the unity of the work. Once these relations are determined, an
objection can be examined according to the particular relation it alters and the consistency of that
alteration with the undertaking as a whole.
    [29] Each cause has its own office within the execution of the common purpose. The operation
receives its source from the agent and acts upon a determinate subject. The resulting effect supplies a
condition for the continuation of the work. An earlier benefit may therefore condition a later benefit
because the purpose appoints their connection as well as both benefits themselves. One historical cause
may likewise produce several consequences, whose common origin preserves their unity without making
the consequences identical.
                           [30] AB, BC,       Γ⊢(A⇒B)∧(B⇒C)⇒(A⇒C). (5)
    [31] The labelled arrows designate causal operations, with the kind of operation in each case specified
by the prose. The implication signs state consequences established from the premise set Gamma. A true
material implication, by itself, leaves the producer of its consequent unidentified. A causal arrow carries
the additional content concerning the operation through which that consequent is produced.
     [32] Conjunction and production must each receive their own proof. Two propositions may always be
true together while the causal connection asserted between them remains disputed. Conversely, naming a
cause leaves the required conditions of its operation to be established. The inquiry therefore concerns both
the truth of the consequence and the actual operation realizing it. Their distinction prevents an apparent
concession concerning one relation from substituting for the explanation required by the other.
    [33] The whole blessed Trinity is the chief author and agent of redemption, since the outward works
of the Deity are undivided and belong equally to each person, with the distinct manner and order of their
subsistence preserved. Other agents acted instrumentally within Christ's passion, but their activity
supplies no ground for assigning the redeeming work to them. In relation to the Father, their endeavors
issued in a result contrary to their intentions, accomplishing only what the “hand and counsel of God had
before determined should be done”. In relation to Christ, their own powers were insufficient to attain even
the death they intended — he laid down his life himself, and none could take it from him. These
instruments consequently fall outside the present consideration of the work's author. Scripture assigns
distinct acts and operations to the several persons who jointly author the whole. Our weak apprehension
receives their distinction by considering these operations severally, beginning with those attributed to the
Father. The undivided agency excludes competing independent saving intentions, while the distinction of
personal operations remains real within the common work. The persons given, those redeemed, and those
effectually brought into the intended good must accordingly be followed through one undertaking whose
unity grounds the connection of its several operations. (Acts 4:28; John 10:17–18)
   [34] The Ephesian church is addressed by Ignatius as appointed before the ages to enduring glory. His
subsequent account of its ascent joins the Father's preparation, Christ's cross, the Spirit's operation, and

the recipients' faith. Several distinct relations thus belong to a single saving history. Faith has its actual
place within that history and depends upon prior relations which its exercise leaves intact. The distinction
of the divine operations consequently preserves the common undertaking in which each has its office and
through which the persons concerned are brought toward the appointed issue. (Ignatius, Epistle to the
Ephesians, greeting; 9)
    [35] Two acts within redemption by Jesus' blood are properly assigned to the Father — he sends his
Son into the world to undertake the work, and he lays upon him the punishment due to our sin. The
sending constitutes the mediatorial commission, while the imposition of punishment concerns the
commission's execution on behalf of the represented persons. Their difference establishes the order within
one purpose and one authorship, allowing each relation to be understood through the office it actually
fulfills.
    [36] The Father's love for the world is expressed in his sending of the Son to die — he “sent his Son
into the world that the world through him might be saved,” “sending his Son in the likeness of sinful
flesh, and for sin, condemned sin in the flesh, that the righteousness of the law might be fulfilled in us,”
and “set him forth to be a propitiation through faith in his blood.” The saving purpose governs the mission
throughout, for “when the fullness of the time was come, God sent forth his Son, made of a woman, made
under the law, to redeem them that were under the law, that we might receive the adoption of sons.” More
than twenty references in John's Gospel identify this sending, and the descriptions by which Christ names
himself and his Father preserve its personal order — he is “Him whom the Father has sent”, and the
Father is “He who sent me.” The act belongs appropriately to the Father according to the promise that he
would “send us a Savior, a great one, to deliver us” and Christ's own profession, “I have not spoken in
secret from the beginning; from the time that it was, there am I: and now the Lord God, and his Spirit, has
sent me.” The Father is accordingly called our Savior, “According to the commandment Θεοῦ σωτῆρος
ἡμῶν,”—“of God our Savior.” Some copies read Θεοῦ καὶ σωτῆρος ἡμῶν, “of God and our Savior.” The
insertion of καὶ arose, doubtless, from the mistaken supposition that Savior names Christ alone. The
parallel construction, Κατ’ ἐπιταγὴν τοῦ σωτῆρος ἡμῶν Θεοῦ, “According to the commandment of God
our Savior,” supplies the same attribution where that conjunction cannot be inserted. Other passages
similarly assign the title to the Father, “My spirit has rejoiced in God my Savior.” and “We trust in the
living God, who is the Savior of all men, specially of them that believe.” The latter concerns his
providential preservation of all, while most of the passages concern his sending of Christ for redemption.
The sending itself contains three distinguishable acts, whose order discloses the content of the
commission. It includes the Son's assumption of the condition in which obedience and sacrifice are
accomplished, and thus connects the intended deliverance to an appointed historical operation. The
efficacy attributed to the mission belongs to that concrete operation together with the application ordained
with it. The personal relation of sender and sent consequently bears the entire saving content of the
undertaking. (John 3:16–17; 10:36; 5:37; Romans 8:3–4; 3:25; Galatians 4:4–5; Isaiah 19:20; 48:16; 12:2;
40:10; 45:15; 1 Timothy 1:1; 4:10; Titus 1:3; 2:10; 3:4; Luke 1:47; Deuteronomy 32:15; 1 Samuel 10:19;
Psalms 24:5; 25:5; Jeremiah 14:8; Micah 7:7; Habakkuk 3:18)
    [37] The mediatorial office is first authoritatively imposed by the Father and voluntarily received by
Christ. Within this dispensation the Father exercises an ordered superiority to which the Son willingly
humbles himself, although “in the form of God.” Reception of the office and voluntary assumption of its
burden are two relations within the same commission, which itself admits two further considerations.
Willing agency belongs to the causal efficacy of the obedience — a purpose accomplished through a
voluntary act retains its accomplishment precisely through the appointed agent's actual willingness to
perform it. (Philippians 2:6–8)
   [38] The first consideration concerns the eternal counsel by which the Father sets apart his incarnate
Son for the mediatorial office, “You are my Son; this day have I begotten you. Ask of me, and I shall give
you the nations for your inheritance, and the uttermost parts of the earth for your possession.” and “Sit
you at my right hand until I make your enemies your footstool,” for “the Lord swore, and will not repent,

You are a priest for ever after the order of Melchizedek.” He appoints him “heir of all things”, having
“ordained him to be Judge of quick and dead”. For this office he was “ordained before the foundation of
the world.” and “determined, ὁρισθείς, to be the Son of God with power”, “that he might be the first-born
among many brethren.” The act is eternally established in God's mind and will, and its causal priority
differs from the temporal ordering of the subsequent acts which begin in the fullness of time. It is their
spring and source, “Known unto God are all his works from the beginning of the world.” Ordinary speech
comprehends the purpose within the expression describing its accomplishment, and in that sense the
eternal counsel belongs within the account of sending. Its place does not turn eternity into an earlier
moment of the creature's succession. Rather, the historical exercise of the office depends upon the
appointment which determines its order and issue. The temporal undertaking receives its constitution
from that purpose, while the purpose itself belongs to God's eternal counsel. This relation allows
appointment and accomplishment to be considered together without confusing their different manners of
priority. (Psalms 2:7–8; 110:1, 4; Hebrews 1:2; Acts 10:42; 15:18; 1 Peter 1:20; Romans 1:4; 8:29)
    [39] The second consideration concerns Christ's actual inauguration and solemn admission into the
appointed office, “committing all judgment unto the Son”. “making him to be both Lord and Christ”.
“appointing him over his whole house”. This is the “anointing of the most Holy”, God “anointing him
with the oil of gladness above his fellows”. The setting apart is called an unction because the holy things
which prefigured him, including the ark and altar, were consecrated through anointing. The heavenly
testimony to his nativity likewise belongs to the public declaration of the office. One angel announced to
the shepherds, “Behold,” “I bring you good tidings of great joy, which shall be unto all people; for unto
you is born this day in the city of David a Savior, which is Christ the Lord”, and the host of heaven
completed the message with its triumphant praise, “Glory be to God on high, on earth peace, towards men
good-will”. The voice later heard from the excellent glory renewed the Father's testimony, “This is my
beloved Son, in whom I am well-pleased.” Three acts give this inauguration its historical order. First, the
glorious proclamation of the nativity occurred when the Father “prepared him a body” and brought his
First-begotten into the world, declaring “Let all the angels of God worship him” and sending angels to
proclaim the message already recounted. Secondly, at Christ's baptism the Spirit descended visibly upon
him in the form of a dove, furnishing him in fullness for the work and office appointed to him. The voice
from heaven accompanied the descent and acknowledged him as the Father's only-beloved. Thirdly, the
“crowning of him with glory and honor,” appeared in his resurrection, ascension, and session “on the
right hand of the Majesty on high”, with “him as his king upon his holy hill of Zion”, when “all power
was given unto him in heaven and in earth”, “all things being put under his feet”, and Christ himself was
highly exalted and “a name given him above every name, that at.” Witnesses of every appropriate kind
were appointed to these acts — angels from heaven, the dead coming from their graves, the apostles
among and before the living, and the more than five hundred brethren who saw him at once. The
inauguration thus possessed several degrees and public attestations under the Father's declaration, “It is a
light thing that you should be my servant to raise up the tribes of Jacob, and to restore the preserved of
Israel: I will also give you for a light to the Gentiles that you may be my salvation unto the end of the
earth.” Each concerns the historical exercise of the office already appointed. The difference between
appointment and inauguration preserves the difference between the settled purpose and the temporal
possession of the effects which that purpose ordains to be accomplished. (John 5:22; Acts 2:32, 36; 1:10;
Hebrews 3:1–6; 10:5; 1:3, 6; 2:7–8; Daniel 9:24; Psalms 45:7; 2:6; Exodus 30:25–28; Luke 2:10–11, 14;
24:4; Matthew 3:16–17; 17:5; 28:18; 27:52; 2 Peter 1:17; Philippians 2:9–11; 1 Corinthians 15:6; Isaiah
49:6)
     [40] Between eternal appointment and actual inauguration stands a twofold promise. God promises a
Savior and Mediator to his people according to his former purpose, “The seed of the woman shall break
the serpent’s head;” and “The scepter shall not depart from Judah, nor a lawgiver from between his feet,
till Shiloh come; and unto him shall the gathering of the people be.” Sacrifices, types, and prophetic
predictions give prior testimony to this promised coming, “Of which salvation the prophets have inquired
and searched diligently, who prophesied of the grace that should come unto you; searching what or what

manner of time the Spirit of Christ which was in them did signify, when it testified beforehand the
sufferings of Christ, and the glory that should follow. Unto whom it was revealed, that not unto
themselves, but unto us they did minister the things which are now reported unto you by them that have
preached the gospel unto you with the Holy Ghost sent down from heaven; which things the angels desire
to look into.” He also promises the application of the appointed Savior's purchased benefits to those who
believe in him, who will be given in the fullness of time according to the earlier promises. Abraham is
told that “in his seed all the families of the earth should be blessed,” and is himself justified through the
same faith. These matters belong chiefly to application, which possessed equal saving force before and
after the actual mission. Promise introduces into temporal history an intelligible declaration upon which
faith can act before the sacrifice occurs. The efficacy of the promised accomplishment and its subsequent
execution therefore retain their distinction from the chronology in which particular recipients hear the
promise, believe, and receive its fruits. (Genesis 3:15; 49:10; 12:3; 15:6; 1 Peter 1:10–12)
    [41] The Father’s second act in sending the Son is to furnish every gift and grace required by the
office to which he sends him. The Son’s charge over the house of God, the work he must undergo, and the
end for which he undertakes it determine the capacities that belong to his commission. An appointment
would be deficient if powers indispensable to its execution had to be obtained from some source outside
the provision made for it. In Christ, however, the perfection that belongs to him essentially and the
fullness communicated to him for the execution of this particular work are two distinct things.
    [42] The perfection of the Son in his divine nature belongs to him as one with the Father. His glory
was “the glory of the only-begotten of the Father.” He was “in the form of God, and thought it not
robbery to be equal with God” and thus the “fellow of the Lord of hosts.” It is in the presence of this glory
that the seraphim cry to one another, “Holy, holy, holy, is the Lord of hosts: the whole earth is full of his
glory. And the posts of the door moved at the voice of him that cried, and the house was filled with
smoke.” Isaiah in turn says, “Mine eyes have seen the King, the Lord of hosts.” The apostle identifies the
object of that vision when he says, “Isaiah saw him, and spoke of his glory.” The Son ἐκένωσε, emptied
himself for a season, when he was “found in the form” or condition “of a servant, humbling himself unto
death” and laid aside the outward manifestation of his glory so that he appeared to have “neither form, nor
beauty, nor comeliness, that he should be desired.” This abasement does not give the divine perfection a
history of acquisition or loss. It belongs essentially to the person eternally begotten of the Father. The
temporal changes therefore concern the humanity assumed and the persons saved through it. The
knowledge and purpose governing those changes are already complete in God. (John 1:14; 12:41;
Philippians 2:6–8; Zechariah 13:7; Isaiah 6:3–5; 53:2)
    [43] The fullness communicated by the Father belongs to the Son in the execution of his office as
“Mediator between God and men, the man Christ Jesus”. He receives it as “Emmanuel, God with us” and
as the “son given to us, called Wonderful, Counselor, The mighty God, The everlasting Father, The
Prince of Peace, upon whose shoulder the government was to be.” Its communication is distinct from
what belongs to him as “Lord of hosts,” for the grace in question is infused into the humanity personally
united to the divine nature and remains habitually in that humanity. It does not possess the absolute
infinity of essential deity. Nevertheless, it extends to every kind of grace, and every grace in it is present
in the highest degree. The Father gives this fullness for the accomplishment of the appointed work, and
what belongs to Christ in virtue of that communication knows neither limit nor exhaustion. It is in him as
light in the sun’s beams and as water in a living fountain whose supply cannot fail. Christ is the
“candlestick” from which the “golden pipes do empty the golden oil out of themselves” into all who
belong to him. He is “the beginning, the first-born from the dead, in all things having the pre-eminence;
for it pleased the Father that in him should all fullness dwell.” The Father so ordered it that there were
“hid all the treasures of wisdom and knowledge” in him. Moreover, “in him dwelt all the fullness of the
Godhead σωματικῶς,” substantially or personally, so that “of his fullness we might all receive grace for
grace” through continual supply. The Son accordingly identifies this provision as he enters upon the work
of redemption. “The Spirit of the Lord God,” he says, “is upon me; because the Lord has anointed me to

preach good tidings unto the meek; he has sent me to bind up the brokenhearted, to proclaim liberty to the
captives, and the opening of the prison to them that are bound; to proclaim the acceptable year of the
Lord, and the day of vengeance of our God; to comfort all that mourn.” He received the “anointing with
the oil of gladness” “above his fellows”. Of the oil it is said that “it was upon his head, and ran down to
his beard, yea, down to the skirts of his garments” so that each one covered in the garment of his
righteousness should partake of it. “The Spirit of the Lord did rest upon him, the spirit of wisdom and
understanding, the spirit of counsel and might, the spirit of knowledge and of the fear of the Lord”. This
gift differs from our reception of sanctifying grace in portions proportioned to our measure, for “he
received not the Spirit by measure” when he reached the fullness of his stature. The gift and its
manifestation had their degrees before that maturity, as Scripture indicates when it says he “increased in
wisdom and stature, and in favor with God and man.” To this the Father added “all power in heaven and
earth, which was given unto him” and “power over all flesh, to give eternal life to as many as he would.”
What the Mediator receives for the execution of his office, what he does with it, and what his people
receive through its exercise therefore belong to an order of dependence. The powers or response of the
recipients cannot constitute the fullness from which they receive. (1 Timothy 2:5; Matthew 1:23; 28:18;
Isaiah 9:6; 61:1–2; 11:2; Zechariah 4:12; Colossians 1:18–19; 2:3, 9; John 1:16; 3:34; 17:2; Psalms 45:7;
133:2; Ephesians 4:13; Luke 2:52)
     [44] The completeness of the provision for an office is determined by the work to which it is
appointed. Every power indispensable to that work must be furnished. An abundance of some other power
makes no difference to the sufficiency of the provision if a necessary capacity is absent, and a capacity
with no bearing on the work adds nothing to its adequacy. It is in this definite relation to the commission
that the Mediator’s fullness is affirmed.
                                       [45] Kreq(o)⊆Kgiven(o). (6)
     [46] K required designates the capacities indispensable to the appointed office o, and K given
designates the capacities furnished for its exercise. The inclusion states the adequacy of the provision to
that work, each capacity contributing in accordance with its own nature.
     [47] A commission embracing the whole work cannot be retained while a power indispensable to it is
treated as a supplement which the persons to be saved happen to furnish. The people’s reception is an
effect of the Mediator’s undertaking. His sufficiency to undertake it already belongs to the fullness with
which he is sent.
    [48] The Father’s third act in sending the Son is to covenant with him concerning the work he
undertakes and the issue to which it is directed. The compact orders the accomplishment and its fruit
together, for it gives the Son assurance both of the assistance by which the work will be performed and of
the result promised upon its performance. The subsequent acts take their place within the obligations and
assurances of these two provisions.
    [49] The Father first engages to preserve and assist his Son through the whole execution of his
commission. This assurance comprehends the aid required in his trials, the strength necessary against
those who oppose him, the encouragement by which he meets temptation, and the consolation that carries
him through the terrors appointed to him. The burden to which he consents in reliance on this promise is
as heavy as the assistance is comprehensive. He must “become a Savior, and be afflicted in all the
affliction of his people” and though he is “the fellow of the Lord of hosts,” bear the “sword” directed
against him as the “shepherd” of the sheep. He is represented as “treading the winepress alone, until he
became red in his apparel” and as “stricken, smitten of God, and afflicted; wounded for our
transgressions, and bruised for our iniquities; to be bruised and put to grief; to make his soul an offering
for sin, and to bear the iniquity of many” even to the loss of consolation expressed in his cry, “My God,
my God, why have you forsaken me?” It is under these conditions that the Father promises to make “his
mouth like a sharp sword, to hide him in the shadow of his hand, to make him a polished shaft, and to
hide him in his quiver, to make him his servant in whom he would be glorified” and gives him the further

assurance that though “the kings of the earth should set themselves, and the rulers take counsel together,
against him, yet he would laugh them to scorn, and set him as king upon his holy hill of Zion”. The same
promise means that although the “builders did reject him,” he will “become the head of the corner,” to the
astonishment of the world. The Father will “lay him for a foundation, a stone, a tried stone, a precious
corner-stone, a sure foundation” so that “whosoever should fall upon him should be broken, but upon
whomsoever he should fall he should grind him to powder.” In this engagement the Savior has the ground
of his confidence that the Father will never forsake him in the execution of the work for which he has sent
him. “I gave,” he says, “my back to the smiters, and my cheeks to them that plucked off the hair: I hid not
my face from shame and spitting.” The confidence with which he offers himself to shame and sorrow is
expressed in these words, “The Lord God will help me; therefore shall I not be confounded: therefore
have I set my face like a flint, and I know; that I shall not be ashamed. He is near that justifies me; who
will contend with me? let us stand together: who is mine adversary? let him come near to me. Behold, the
Lord God will help me; who is he that condemn me? Lo! they shall all wax old as a garment; the moth
shall eat them up.” He is thus brought as a “lamb to the slaughter, and as a sheep before her shearers is
dumb, so he opened not his mouth” with the disposition described in the testimony that “when he was
reviled, he reviled not again; when he suffered, he threatened not; but committed himself to him that
judges righteously.” The Father’s covenant of protection gives him assurance in his most extreme
sufferings and a ground for the exercise of the graces with which he has been furnished. The perils and
afflictions he undergoes are real, and the assistance that carries him through them belongs to the same
appointment. The occurrence of the danger gives no ground for placing its outcome outside the
government of that undertaking. (Isaiah 63:2–3, 8–9; 53; 49:2–3; 28:16; 50:6–9; 53:7; Zechariah 13:7;
Psalms 22:1; 2:2, 4, 6; 118:22–23; Matthew 21:42, 44; Mark 12:10; Luke 20:17; Acts 4:1, 11–12; Peter
2:4; 1 Peter 2:23)
    [50] The Father’s further promise concerns the issue in which the sufferings of the Son accomplish
their purpose. The preceding provisions clarify this part of the compact because the one who undertakes
the work with the powers and protection supplied for it seeks the very end the Father has engaged to give
him. The promised result is consequently what the Son claims upon fulfilling the work committed to him.
“You shall be my servant,” the Lord says, “to raise up the tribes of Jacob, and to restore the preserved of
Israel: I will also give you for a light to the Gentiles, that you may be my salvation to the end of the earth.
Kings shall see and arise, princes also shall worship, because of the Lord that is faithful.” The certainty of
the accomplishment is expressed again when he says, “I will preserve you, and give you for a covenant of
the people, to establish the earth, to cause to inherit the desolate heritages; that you may say to the
prisoners, Go forth; to them that are in darkness, Show yourselves. They shall feed in the ways, and their
pastures shall be in all high places. They shall not hunger nor thirst; neither shall the heat nor sun smite
them: for he that has mercy on them shall lead them, even by the springs of water shall he guide them.
And I will make all my mountains a way, and my highways shall be exalted. Behold, these shall come
from far: and, lo, these from the north and from the west; and these from the land of Sinim.” The people
thus promised are a glorious church of believers gathered to Christ from Jews and Gentiles throughout the
world, conducted to abundant pasture and refreshed by the living waters that flow from God in Christ to
their everlasting salvation. The gathering of the children of God, their approach to him, and their entrance
into eternal salvation are the end for which the Son accepted the work on the strength of this promise. The
substance of that end, as the subsequent argument will show, overthrows the general ransom or universal
redemption. In the fifty-third chapter of the same prophecy, the Father specifies the issue still more
directly. It says that when the Son “made his soul an offering for sin, he should see his seed, and prolong
his days, and the pleasure of the Lord should prosper in his hand; that he should see of the travail of his
soul, and be satisfied; by his knowledge he should justify many; that, he should divide a portion with the
great, and the spoil with the strong.” The seed which the Son sees by covenant is therefore a faithful
people raised to God and preserved through all generations. Some advocates of universal redemption have
expressly maintained “that the death of Christ might have had its full and utmost effect and yet none be
saved,” and the others concede the same thing tacitly when they assign the proper ends and effects of his

death. Their account cannot be reconciled with a covenant that promises him a seed. “The pleasure of the
Lord,” is to “prosper in his hand;” and what prospers in his hand is the “bringing of many sons unto
glory;” for “God sent his only-begotten Son into the world that we live through him”, as the argument
will establish more fully below. The agreement of the Father’s promise and the Son’s own purpose is
nowhere clearer than in what the Son asks on completing the work. He asks neither more nor less than
what the Father had undertaken to grant. “I have,” he says, “glorified you on earth, I have finished the
work which you gave me to do.” Having asked for the manifestation of the eternal glory of which he had
emptied himself for a season, he asks that the love of God and all its fruits in faith, sanctification, and
glory reach those given to him. He sanctifies himself as a sacrifice for their sake and asks that they be
sanctified and preserved in peace, in communion with one another and union with God. In the words “I
pray not for these alone” he first identifies the apostles. The same prayer reaches beyond them, “but for
them also which shall believe on me through their word; that they all may be one; as you, Father, are in
me, and I in you, that they also may be one in us.” He then prays for their glory, saying, “Father, I will
that they also, whom you have given me, be with me where I am; that they may behold my glory, which
you have given me.” Each petition has its ground in the Father’s promise. Their express object is the
people given to the Son, and their scope excludes all and every one. The appointment, provision, and
covenant are thus the three particulars of the Father’s first act. The last of them specifies a reward realized
in a people brought to glory, and its specification therefore determines what the accomplished obedience
secures. (Isaiah 49; 49:6–12; 53:10–12; Hebrews 2:10; 1 John 4:9; John 17:4–5, 9, 17–21, 24)
    [51] A promise whose issue is a people brought to glory specifies the relation between the Son’s
undertaking and its completed fruit. It reaches further than the occurrence of a preliminary event
concerning a population. The appointment, the assistance under which the work proceeds, its
performance, and the issue promised upon that performance thus belong to one purpose. Differences in
the histories of its recipients do not partition the common undertaking into separate ventures for their
salvation.
                    [52] Undertake(o,P)∧Fulfilled(o)∧Promise(o,GP)⇒GP. (7)
    [53] P designates the people specified in the covenant, o the appointed obedience, and G(P) the
promised issue for that people. The truth of the promise and the fidelity of him who gives it are among the
theological premises on which the implication depends.
    [54] The conclusion derives its content from what is promised. A promise of opportunity would
secure opportunity, but the issue affirmed here is a people brought to glory, and the effect promised is
accordingly realized in those people. The formula states the relation under that premise. Neither its form
nor the mere designation of an undertaking as a promise establishes any effect beyond the content
revealed.
     [55] The Father’s second act is to lay on the Son the punishment due to sins. The prophet gives this
act as a command, “Awake; O sword, against my shepherd, against the man that is my fellow, says the
Lord of hosts: smite the shepherd, and the sheep shall be scattered.” and the Gospel declares as
accomplished what the command enjoins. “I will smite the shepherd, and the sheep of the flock shall be
scattered abroad.” It is accordingly said that “He was stricken, smitten of God, and afflicted;” that “the
Lord laid upon him the iniquity of us all;” and that “it pleased the Lord to bruise him, and to put him to
grief.” The apostle supplies the judicial relation in which these expressions have their meaning. “He made
him to be sin for us, who knew no sin; that we might be made the righteousness of God in him.” In the
opposition between Christ made sin and our being made righteousness, each adjunct stands for its subject.
“Him who knew no sin,” and therefore deserved no punishment, “him has he made to be sin,” in that God
laid on him the punishment that sin deserved. The latter expression may also denote an offering for the
expiation of sin, since ἁμαρτία corresponds here to the Old Testament ‫ַח ָּט את‬, which signifies both sin and
its sacrifice. The act is the Lord’s own. When Herod and Pontius Pilate came together with the Gentiles
and the people of Israel, they did only “what his hand and counsel had determined before to be done.”

The greatest anguish of Christ accordingly appears in his immediate encounter with the Father’s wrath
and the burden the Father himself imposed. While the Savior was still with his three chosen apostles in
the garden, before the traitor or his company appeared and when no outward hand subjected him to any
torment, he “began to be sorrowful, even unto death” and was “sore amazed, and very heavy.” This was
the hour described as “in the days of his flesh, when he offered up prayers and supplications with strong
crying and tears unto him that was able to save him from death” and the evangelist relates the manner of
his supplication. “There appeared an angel unto him from heaven, strengthening him. But being in an
agony he prayed more earnestly: and his sweat was as it were great drops of blood falling down to the
ground.” The immediate trial from the Father is evident from the contrast with Christ’s meek and willing
submission, without corresponding disturbance of spirit, to the violence and bodily cruelty of men until
the conflict is renewed and he cries, “My God, my God, why have you forsaken me?” The identity of the
one with whom Christ chiefly has to do tells us what he undergoes for sinners and helps determine on
whose behalf he undergoes it. His sufferings are not simply bodily afflictions and the impressions they
produce in the soul. He frees us from the curse of God’s law “by being made a curse” and that curse
comprises all the punishment that divine justice inflicts for sin and that the law exacts for disobedience.
Temporal death indeed exhausts the curse considered in its function as an instrument of the Jewish polity.
To make that political measure the limit of the curse of the law as the universal rule of obedience and the
bond of the covenant between God and man is a foolish dream. Christ intends our good by dying directly
in our stead. The punishment due to our sin and the chastisement that obtains our peace fall on him. In
nature, weight, and pressure these are the pains of hell, though their tendency and duration differ because
death cannot detain him. Denial of that identity would impugn the justice of God, which inevitably
inflicts those pains eternally upon sinners. God has indeed permitted a substitution in the person who
suffers, as under the former law, where in the carnal part of the ordinances the life of a beast was accepted
for the life of a man. This relaxation is revealed and believed. There is no intimation of any corresponding
alteration in the nature of the punishment. Hence the prophet says, “All we like sheep have gone astray;
we have turned every one to his own way; and the Lord has laid on him the iniquity of us all.” It is strange
to suppose that Christ endures the pains of hell in the stead of those who were already enduring them and
will endure them eternally, for “their worm dies not, neither is their fire quenched.” The universalist must
therefore choose among three accounts of the wrath God imposes and Christ endures. Either it is due to
all the sins of all men, to all the sins of some men, or to some sins of all men. On the third account every
person has sins for which he must answer, so that no one can be saved. Were God to enter into judgment
with all humanity for even one sin, no flesh would be justified in his sight. “If the Lord should mark
iniquities, who should stand?” We might all cast all we have “to the moles and to the bats, to go into the
clefts of the rocks, and into the tops of the ragged rocks, for fear of the Lord, and for the glory of his
majesty.” On the second account Christ suffers in the place of the elect throughout the world for all their
sins. This is what we affirm. On the first account there is no ground on which all men are not freed from
the punishment of all their sins. The answer given is “Because of their unbelief; they will not believe.”
But if unbelief is not a sin, punishment for it has no ground. If it is a sin and Christ bore its punishment, it
can no more bar the unbeliever from the fruit of his death than any other sin whose punishment he bore. If
Christ did not bear its punishment, then he did not die for all their sins. The choice is unavoidable because
the imposition is representative and judicial. The bare fact of suffering does not establish satisfaction. The
relation that does so includes the persons whose sins are borne, the divine appointment of their
representative, and the effects that follow when his bearing of them is accepted. (Zechariah 13:7;
Matthew 26:31, 37–38; Isaiah 53:4, 6, 10; 2:20–21; 2 Corinthians 5:21; Acts 4:27–28; Mark 14:33;
Hebrews 5:7; Luke 22:43–44; Galatians 3:13; Psalms 130:3)
    [56] The satisfaction the Son undertakes receives its scope from the end the Father has covenanted to
give him. As established in paragraphs 49–55, the promise is of a people actually delivered, and the work
the Son accepts is the work by which those people are brought to glory. We may designate these
determinate persons, the elect, E(c). An account which provides them an opportunity and still permits
them to remain finally unsaved has described a different issue from the one by which this undertaking

succeeds. The question is whether the appointed operations attain the promised end in each person for
whom they are undertaken. (John 6:37–40; 17:2, 24; Romans 8:29–30)
                      [57] ∀h∈Ad,      Ec⊆G∞(h),Ld(h)={x∈Ec:x∉G∞(h)}=⌀. (8)
     [58] E(c) designates the people elected under the saving purpose d, and A(d) the nonempty class of
histories consistent with the provision appointed, taking account of its actual efficacy. G(infinity)(h) is the
set of persons finally glorified in history h, and L(d)(h) identifies an elected person missing from that
issue. The covenantal and scriptural argument determines what these relations among persons, purposes,
operations, and outcomes comprise. The histories must be delimited by the means provided and what
those means effect, for otherwise excluding a history would amount to stipulating the very result the
argument is meant to establish.
     [59] The indefectibility of the purpose consists in there being no history consistent with its actual
provision in which an elected person is finally lost. There may be unbelief, suffering, correction, and
delay in the temporal course through which a person is brought to glory, since none of these conditions by
itself constitutes final exclusion. The displayed condition concerns the attainment of the end. How it is
attained requires a further account of the giving of faith and the continuance of intercession. The inclusion
likewise determines what becomes of each person elected. The further conclusion that the final people
and the elected people are identical requires the scriptural premises that establish their extent. Keep the
spokes of paragraph 0 in view as the promise is followed into its performance. The people given to the
Son are the people whose attainment of the end is here in question.
     [60] The three alternatives in paragraph 55 determine the consequences of assigning different scopes
to the satisfaction made under this undertaking. The person represented, the sins borne for him, and the
judgment consequent upon their accepted bearing belong to a single judicial relation. If the satisfaction is
accepted as answering a liability in full, that identical liability cannot remain to be exacted in the final
judgment from the person on whose behalf it has already been answered. A demand for its satisfaction
would deny the satisfaction asserted. The constraint has its ground in justice and the acceptance of the
surety, and the law of noncontradiction forbids affirming that the same liability under the same relation is
at once answered and outstanding.
                       [61] C(x) ⇔ ∀s∈Σx P(x,s),C(x)⇒¬J∞(x),           S(x)⇒C(x). (9)
     [62] Sigma(x) is the nonempty collection of sins considered in the final judgment of a person x. P(x,s)
states that Christ’s appointed and accepted satisfaction answers for the sin s on behalf of x under the
saving undertaking, and C(x) states that the satisfaction for x is complete. J(infinity)(x) designates final
condemnation for the liability in question, and S(x) final salvation. The first implication gives the judicial
effect of complete and accepted satisfaction. The second expresses the impossibility of salvation where a
sin remains finally unanswered. Both premises derive from Owen’s account of substitution and justice. P
attributes accepted satisfaction and neither measures the pain undergone nor treats it as transferable
energy.
    [63] The consequence attaches to accepted satisfaction as defined. If P is made to signify that an
offering renders satisfaction available upon a condition, the first implication holds only with a further
account of the fulfillment of that condition. The efficacy attaching to the former relation cannot be
assigned to the latter by using the same letter for both. Likewise, the exclusion of final condemnation
gives only the judicial part of the account. The positive procurement and application of saving benefits
belong to the same undertaking and are necessary to explain the attainment of glory. As established in
paragraphs 51–54, the covenant promises that issue itself, which exceeds the bare absence of a
contradictory demand.
   [64] If the satisfaction answers every sin of every person, C holds of every person and the judicial
premise excludes final condemnation altogether. A Universalist who retains the alleged extent must

therefore accept that effect or dispute the efficacy here attributed to satisfaction. He cannot retain both
extent and efficacy while introducing unbelief as an outstanding liability without first determining
whether it belongs to the sins already answered. Under these premises the final condemnation of anyone
represented contradicts a satisfaction at once universal and effectual. This is the force of Owen’s first
alternative, and the conclusion attaches to the premises an advocate actually maintains.
     [65] If the satisfaction answers some sins of every person and leaves at least one of each person’s sins
outstanding in the final judgment, C holds of no one. Since salvation requires that every condemning
liability be answered, no one can be saved under that provision. The number or gravity of the sins already
covered cannot remove the remaining liability. Some further ground of satisfaction would have to be
supplied if the account were to stand with the admitted fact that God saves his people. The defect of the
third alternative follows from the incompleteness it ascribes to the satisfaction for each person.
     [66] Complete satisfaction for a determinate people gives a consistent account of the salvation of
some and the final condemnation of others. The preceding premises require that C hold of some people
and that it fail of some, while the covenantal argument identifies the persons of the saving undertaking as
the elect. The three alternatives establish what follows from the proposed scopes of satisfaction. The
identity of the persons and the procurement of the promised goods are established by the evidence proper
to those claims. The particularity lies in the appointed representation, for the worth of the sacrifice derives
from the person of Christ, while the appointment and the undertaking determine whose sins he actually
answers for. (compare paragraphs 303–308.) (John 10:11, 15, 26–29; Ephesians 5:25–27)
     [67] An appeal to unbelief therefore has to preserve both the judicial relation and the causal order in
which a person comes to believe. If unbelief is a sin, a satisfaction that answers for every sin of the person
represented answers for that sin as well. The removal of its guilt and the production of faith are distinct
operations. Owen’s further claim is that the saving undertaking procures the very condition through which
its benefits are enjoyed. An adversary who points out that a man must believe has named a necessary
condition of reception. He has yet to show that its bestowal lies outside the purchase, and repetition of the
word conditional does nothing to establish the omission.
                 [68] ux∈Σx ∧ C(x) ⇒ P(x,ux),R(x)⇒∃t F(x,t),            F(x,t)⇒A(x,t). (10)
     [69] u(x) designates unbelief insofar as it is a sin of the person in question, and Sigma, C, and P have
their preceding meanings. R(x) designates the saving representation of x, F(x,t) his actual saving faith at
the appointed time, and A(x,t) his reception through faith of the benefit in question. The first implication
follows from completeness of satisfaction. The next expresses the procurement of faith, and the last its
instrumental place in reception. The theological grounds for those latter claims are developed in
paragraphs 252–273 and paragraphs 371–405. Answering the guilt, bringing the person to faith, and
receiving through faith have each their own place in the order represented.
      [70] The reception of a benefit can be conditional while its attainment is secured because the
undertaking itself provides the faith by which it is received. A conflict arises only when the condition
indispensable to the promised issue is left outside the provision. The argument therefore turns upon what
brings the person to believe. If Christ has purchased a people who will believe and be saved, then the
coming of those people to faith belongs to the success of his purchase. An arrangement in which salvation
merely follows on a faith for which no provision is made can have no such assurance concerning its final
people. The offering, the renewal effected by the Spirit, and the intercession of the Son determine how
faith enters the accomplishment for those represented and explain why the promised issue cannot finally
fail.
    [71] The trilemma thus leads back to the success promised in the covenant. The appointment
determines whose liabilities are borne, and their complete satisfaction answers the judicial demand
against those persons. Through the faith procured and bestowed they enter the enjoyment of the purchased
good, and through preservation they continue to its consummation. These are distinct relations ordered

toward the same people. An account of general provision consistent with the final loss of an appointed
recipient must show which of them it denies or weakens. Extending the stated scope cannot supply a
deficient efficacy, and asserting efficacy does not establish its extent. The argument concerning
satisfaction and perseverance returns to these relations in paragraphs 362–405 and paragraphs 982–1017.
     [72] Clement’s words “his flesh for our flesh,” and the exchange celebrated in Diognetus express an
undertaking in which the one who suffers and the persons for whom he suffers have distinct places. The
extent of the representation therefore has to be determined together with what that representation effects.
To accept the exchange with the force the preceding premises give it is to accept the consequence when
its recipients are identified. An adversary who refuses the consequence must dispute a premise, the effect
attributed to the exchange, or the connection between them. (Clement of Rome, First Epistle to the
Corinthians 49; Epistle to Diognetus 9)
     [73] The Son acts in this undertaking by willingly receiving the office imposed upon him. When the
Lord says, “Sacrifice and offering he would not: in burnt-offerings and sacrifices for sin he had no
pleasure,” the Son replies, “Lo, I come, (in the volume of the book it is written of me,) to do your will, O
God.” The Son undertakes the work when all other ways are rejected as insufficient. He is the one “in
whom alone the Father was well pleased.” Christ accordingly says that “he came not to do his own will,
but the will of him that sent him” and accounts the accomplishment of his Father’s will and the
completion of his work his meat and drink. His first recorded words already express this relation, “Knew
you not that I must be about my Father’s business?” and at the close of his work he says, “I have glorified
you on the earth; I have finished the work which you gave me to do” What he calls the work of his Father
is the work appointed by the Father and personally performed by the Son. His action has three parts. The
first supplies a common foundation for the two that follow, since what they accomplish depends on it as a
means, yet it is also an act whose goodness is proper to it in relation to the end common to all three. The
willingness of the Son thus enters as an operative cause of the fulfillment. The certainty of the
appointment and the voluntariness of his obedience concur because the willing obedience is part of what
accomplishes it. (Hebrews 10:6–7; Matthew 3:17; John 6:38; 4:34; 17:4; Luke 2:49)
    [74] The Son first takes flesh and pitches his tent among us. His “being made of a woman,” is
ordinarily called his ἐνσάρκωσις or incarnation, since it is “the mystery of godliness, that God should be
manifested in the flesh” and consists in assuming our human nature into personal union with himself
rather than assuming a particular human person. Scripture gives the reason in the condition of the
children, “forasmuch as the children are partakers of flesh and blood, he also himself likewise took part of
the same; that through death he might destroy him that had the power of death, that is, the devil.” These
are the “children whom the Lord gave him.” He partakes of flesh and blood because they partake of it and
sanctifies himself for their sake. The posterity of Adam as a whole is not the object specified in that
