reason. The emptying, humiliation, and dwelling among us are the act of the second person, or of the
divine nature in that person. The Father and the Spirit concur in eternal counsel and approbation. In the
historical order of the commission, the incarnation supplies the human nature in which the Mediator
actually renders the obedience and undergoes the suffering appointed for those children. (John 1:14;
Galatians 4:4; 1 Timothy 3:16; Hebrews 2:13–14)
    [75] The Son’s second act is his oblation, his “offering himself up to God for us without spot, to purge
our consciences from dead works”. The testimony that “for he loved us, and washed us from our sins in
his own blood.” specifies what he does for his people, and the words “He loved the church, and gave
himself for it, that he might sanctify and cleanse it” give the end for which he gives himself. He takes the
cup of wrath due to us from his Father’s hand and drinks it “but not for himself” since “for our sakes he
sanctified himself” as an offering for sin, and “when we were yet without strength, in due time Christ died
for the ungodly”. The institutions, ordinances, and sacrifices of the former dispensation prefigure this
oblation. As they reach their end, Christ says, “Lo, I come to do your will.” Scripture treats the
completion of the offering principally under the aspect of what Christ suffered, since it is the means by
which the three divine persons attain the further end. Yet suffering is his action as an oblation because he

willingly gives himself in it. Without the concurrence of his will it could never have purged our sins. He
was the “Lamb of God, which takes away the sin of the world” whom God himself provided for the
sacrifice, and the manner of his consent is expressed in the prophecy, “He was brought as a lamb to the
slaughter, and as a sheep before her shearers is dumb, so he opened not his mouth.” He says of himself, “I
lay down my life. No man takes it from me, but I lay it down of myself. I have power to lay it down, and I
have power to take it again.” God might have imposed the suffering, but it is the Son’s consent that
constitutes his death a gift and offering. “But he loved me,” says the apostle, “and gave himself for me.”
The freedom belonging to a gift is present where “he loved us, and gave himself for us an offering and a
sacrifice to God for a sweet-smelling savor.” His readiness appears in the words “Lo, I come to do your
will, O God” and in that “his own self bore our sins in his own body on the tree.” The oblation thus
comprehends the whole dispensation in which God is manifested in the flesh and dwells among us. Its
scope includes the prayers and supplications he offered with strong crying and tears in the days of his
flesh and the entire course of his ministry until he gave his soul as the price of redemption for many, “by
himself purged our sins, and sat down on the right hand of the Majesty on high” and “expecting till his
enemies be made his footstool”. His entrance into the holy of holies sprinkled with his own blood and his
appearing for us before the majesty of God, which some treat as a continuation of his oblation, belongs
under the further act to be considered. The representative relation of the offering follows from the
appointment, the intention of the one who gives himself, and the acceptance of the satisfaction he renders.
To determine what the outward event procures, it must be understood under the meritorious office in
which it is performed. (Hebrews 9:14; 10:9, 13; 1:3; Revelation 1:5; Ephesians 5:2, 25–26; Daniel 9:26;
John 17:19; 1:29; 10:17–18; Romans 5:6; Isaiah 53:7; Galatians 2:20; 1 Peter 2:24; Matthew 26:28)
    [76] The Son’s third act is to intercede for each and every person for whom he offered himself. The
greater act of suffering for them is followed by the exercise of the priestly office by which its fruits reach
them. To omit the latter would be to treat the price of redemption as something that could be expended
upon perishing souls without regard to their fate. The intercession is itself appointed with a promise. “Ask
of me,” says the Lord, “and I will give you the nations for your inheritance, and the uttermost parts of the
earth for your possession.” The Son therefore tells his disciples of a work he is to perform for them in
heaven. “I go,” he says, “to prepare a place for you, that I may come again and receive you unto myself.”
The former priestly office establishes the relation between the offering and the entrance into the
sanctuary. As “the high priest went into the second [tabernacle] alone once every year, not without blood,
which he offered for himself and the errors of the people” so “Christ being come an high priest of good
things to come, by his own blood entered once into the holy place, having obtained eternal redemption for
us.” The sanctuary entered in virtue of this blood and the end of that entrance are specified together, for
“he is not entered into the holy places made with hands, which are the figures of the true; but into heaven
itself, now to appear in the presence of God for us.” He appears before God as the advocate who pleads
for the application of the good things he procured for the persons for whom he was an offering. Hence the
apostle says, “If any man sin, we have an advocate with the Father, Jesus Christ the righteous.” and
establishes the ground of the advocacy when he adds, “He is the propitiation for our sins.” Christ
intercedes because he is ἱλασμός, the propitiatory sacrifice for their sins. Both acts therefore have the
same persons for their object. The exclusion of the world from his prayer follows the same order. “I pray
for them,” he says, and specifies its object in the words “I pray not for the world, but for them you have
given me.” There is no ground for such intercession for the world because he is not its ἱλασμός or
propitiation. “I knew,” he says to the Father, “that you hear me always,” and the Father hears him by
granting his request under the engagement already established. If the Son interceded for all, therefore, all
would certainly be saved, for “he is able to save them to the uttermost that come unto God by him, seeing
he ever lives to make intercession for them.” This is the ground of the apostle’s confidence, “Who shall
lay any thing to the charge of God’s elect? It is God that justifies. Who is he that condemns? It is Christ
that died, yea rather, that is risen again, who is even at the right hand of God, who also makes intercession
for us.” Those for whom Christ died can accordingly conclude that he intercedes for them and that no
charge will stand against them. That consequence breaks the general ransom, which has him die for

millions who have no part in his intercession and upon whom the charge of their sins remains until they
perish under it. The nature of the intercession confirms the consequence. It is the authoritative appearance
of the one seated at the right hand of the Majesty on high, who presents himself before the Father
sprinkled with his own blood, and claims for his people the spiritual goods procured by his offering. It is
no dejected supplication beneath the dignity of that state. He says, “Father, I will that those whom you
have given me be with me where I am” and appears with his satisfaction and merit on behalf of those for
whom he suffered. The Father’s promise specifies what he claims when his work is accomplished. He is
to be the captain of salvation to all who believe on him and effectually bring many sons to glory. With
such a high priest over the house of God, we can draw near in the full assurance of faith, since by one
offering he has perfected forever those who are sanctified. The fuller account below will follow how the
efficacy of that accomplished act continues through the intercession in the temporal reception of its fruits
by those same persons. (Psalms 2:8; John 14:2–3; 17:9, 24; 11:42; Hebrews 9:7, 11–12, 24; 7:25; 10:14; 1
John 2:1–2; Romans 8:33–34)
     [77] The historical accomplishment of the offering and the continuing enjoyment of its fruits belong
to the same procurement. In his intercession the priest presents what he has accomplished so that its goods
are bestowed upon the persons for whom he purchased them. The succession of times at which they
receive those goods calls for the exercise of that office. The purchase through which they come to enjoy
them has already been made.
                               [78] O(x)⇒I(x),       I(x)⇒Bestow(Bx). (11)
     [79] O(x) designates the offering for x, I(x) the intercession for x, and B(x) the purchased benefits.
The first implication expresses the conjunction of the two priestly acts. The second depends upon the
efficacy of the intercession and the times appointed for the bestowal of those benefits.
    [80] The saving extent of the offering depends upon its conjunction with intercession for those it
represents. Once this conjunction is established, the consequences of both acts can be traced in the same
persons. The fuller treatment in paragraphs 91–113 and the consolation to be drawn from it have their
ground in this undivided provision.
    [81] The Holy Spirit, third in the order of the blessed Trinity, concurs by his own operation in each of
the principal parts of the work, which can be distinguished under three heads. Through his efficient action
the appointed good becomes actual in the creature who is to receive it. His personal operation therefore
belongs to the undivided undertaking established in paragraphs 33–72 and remains part of its causal
account when procurement is distinguished from application.
     [82] The Spirit acts first in the incarnation and in the fullness of the assistance given to the Son
throughout his life among us. His mother was found ἐν γαστρὶ ἔχουσα, with child, and “to have conceived
in her womb of the Holy Ghost.” The angel gives Mary the cause as far as the mystery is disclosed to us.
“The Holy Ghost shall come upon you, and the power of the Highest shall overshadow you: therefore also
that holy thing which shall be born of you shall be called the Son of God.” The overshadowing names the
operation by which the Spirit alone effects the conception. Its expression recalls birds covering their eggs
until their warmth brings forth the young, and the Spirit may thus be said to “incubare fœtui,” as at the
beginning of the world. Conceived by that power, the child was filled with the Spirit and “waxed strong”
in it, until he possessed the gifts and graces in fullness and without the restriction of a measure. The
Spirit’s action thus concerns both the historical formation of Christ’s humanity and the capacities with
which it is furnished for the work. Those capacities are really exercised in the obedience for which they
are given. (Matthew 1:18; Luke 1:35, 80)
    [83] The Spirit’s second operation concerns the one act considered as passion in relation to what
Christ suffered and as oblation in relation to what he did through and under those sufferings. Scripture
says that “by the Eternal Spirit he offered himself without spot to God.” Whether this refers to the bloody
sacrifice upon the cross or to his continued presentation of himself before the Father, the offering is

through the Eternal Spirit. His willing self-offering through that Spirit is the eternal fire under the
sacrifice that makes it acceptable to God. The identification of the Eternal Spirit with the Savior’s own
deity has little ground. Some Greek and Latin copies read Πνεύματος ἁγίου where the ordinary reading is
Πνεύματος αἰωνίου, in which case the question disappears. The Son may offer himself through the Holy
Spirit just as he is “declared to be the Son of God, according to the Spirit of holiness, by the resurrection
from the dead” and “quickened by the Spirit.” The Spirit’s operation is requisite in his dying and offering
as in his quickening and resurrection. Since the suffering and the obedient giving of himself concur in
constituting a sacrifice, the Spirit’s action belongs to the constitution and exercise of the very offering
whose merit reaches those represented. (Hebrews 9:14; Romans 1:4; 1 Peter 3:18)
    [84] The Spirit’s third operation concerns the resurrection. The apostle joins the accomplished raising
of Christ to the promised quickening of believers through the indwelling of the same Spirit. “But if the
Spirit of him that raised up Jesus from the dead dwell in you, he that raised up Christ from the dead shall
also quicken your mortal bodies by his Spirit that dwells in you.” The saving accomplishment
consequently comprises the resurrection itself and the relation appointed for its fruit to reach the mortal
bodies of its recipients. (Romans 8:11)
     [85] The personal operations of the three agents are distinguishable in the order in which they
accomplish the one undertaking. Yet each act is ascribed to the whole nature in which each person
partakes “in solidum” and the same concurrence by which they begin it continues in the application until
its final issue. We are therefore to “give thanks to the Father, which has made us meet” through his Spirit,
“to be partakers of the inheritance of the saints in light: who has delivered us from the power of darkness,
and has translated us into the kingdom of his dear Son: in whom we have redemption through his blood,
even the forgiveness of sins.” The effects continue toward one end, while the distinction of persons allows
the operations of accomplishment and application to be described in their proper order. It provides no
interval in which an indispensable cause of the common end must come from outside the appointed
provision. (Colossians 1:12–13)
     [86] The unity of the work consists in the order in which distinct operations attain its end. The good
procured is applied by an efficient action in its recipient. The person renewed in that application himself
believes, and the believer is preserved by the further means ordained for him. His act is real because the
renewal actually brings him to act, and its dependence upon the divine undertaking is precisely what gives
it a place in the causal description.
                          [87] RenewSpirit(Wx)→Believex→Receivex(B). (12)
     [88] The first arrow gives the efficient production of the renewed disposition, and the second the act
of the person renewed. The last relation gives the instrumental place of that act in reception. Throughout
the order, the accomplished work of Christ remains the meritorious ground of the gifts received.
    [89] The Spirit renews the believer, and the believer himself believes. The subjects of the two
operations are distinct, and the act proper to the creature occurs through the exercise of the will which
grace has renewed. Efficacious grace consequently has to be explained through the actual production and
exercise of the recipient’s powers, in accordance with the distinctions of causation developed below.
    [90] Barnabas describes the heart as a corrupt habitation before God renews it and admits its
occupants to his temple by giving repentance. The one who has been changed is the one who actually
repents, so that the giving of repentance and the repentant act occupy distinct places in the same order. To
say that repentance is ours determines who performs the act. It leaves to the account of renewal the
question of how one formerly enslaved to death comes to perform it. (Epistle of Barnabas 16)
    [91] In the order of execution, the actions already distinguished are the means ordained to attain the
intended end. The operations of the Father and Spirit are exercised toward Christ and terminate in him as
God and man. It is therefore Christ and his performances that constitute the means considered here, with
the concurrence of the other persons necessarily preceding or accompanying them. The historical relation

of these means to their end is visible in their execution. What comes first in purpose need not come first
in the experience of the recipient, and the distinction established in paragraphs 14–32 consequently
governs the relation of a benefit’s procurement to its later enjoyment.
    [92] The means ordained by the divine agents is the whole dispensation in virtue of which Jesus Christ
is called Mediator, carried through to its appointed end. It comprises his oblation and his intercession. Its
efficacy belongs to the entire provision and has to be judged accordingly. A conclusion about what the
undertaking secures cannot be drawn from an act considered apart from the intercession and application
appointed to carry its effects to completion.
    [93] Christ’s oblation is consummated upon the cross, where as the spotless Lamb he offers himself to
the Father and bears our sins in his own body on the tree. The act consummated there includes his whole
humiliation and self-emptying, for under the law he voluntarily obeys it so as to become its end to those

who believe, and he bears its curse in the miseries and sufferings of his life as well as in his submission to
death upon the cross. Every act done as Mediator concurs in the complete means. His intercession
likewise comprises his heavenly appearance in the most holy place for the application of what his offering
has purchased and every act of his exaltation ordered to that application, from his resurrection to his
“sitting down at the right hand of the Majesty on high, angels, and principalities, and powers, being made
subject unto him.” The resurrection is the foundation of the acts that follow, “for if he is not risen, then is
our faith in vain” so that we would be “yet in our sins” and “of all men most miserable,” if he had not
risen. Scripture often ascribes a great part of the effect to this foundation, as when it says that “he was
delivered for our offenses, and was raised again for our justification.” The resurrection here includes the
dispensation that follows it and the perpetual intercession of Christ for us in heaven, for “God raised up
his son Jesus to bless us, in turning every one of us from our iniquities.” The earlier obedience likewise
enters historically into the identity of the sacrifice completed at the cross. Its acts accumulate in one
fulfillment of the Mediator’s office and require no repetition of the satisfaction. (Romans 10:4; 4:25; 1
Corinthians 15:13–14, 17, 19; Acts 3:26)
    [94] The whole dispensation, with especial regard to Christ’s death and the shedding of his blood, is a
means whose desirability derives from the end to which it is ordered. In Christ’s sufferings, distinguished
from his obedience, the good consists in their contribution to the manifestation of God’s glorious grace.
The cruelty of Herod and Pontius Pilate, of the Gentiles and the people of Israel assembled against God’s
holy and anointed child, gives the event no good in itself. Nor does any beauty attach to the Son of God
being made sin and a curse, being bruised and afflicted, and enduring a wrath at which the frame of nature
itself trembles. The darkness of the means is overcome by the glory of its end. It is for that end that the
blessed agents intend the act, for it is God by “whose determinate counsel and foreknowledge he was
delivered and slain”. His enemies did to him “whatsoever his hand and counsel had determined”. The end
itself will be specified below. Already the same event can be understood through the wicked intention of
those who inflict it and through the saving purpose that orders it. The two intentions have distinct causal
and moral relations to what happens, and providence does not turn the perpetrators’ malice into the
obedience of Christ. (Acts 4:27–28; 2:23)
    [95] The oblation and intercession of Jesus Christ are distinct acts. Scripture often assigns to each an
immediate product or issue of its own, and the particular effects will be considered in their place. For the
present the distinction gives us a way to identify what each act does and how one depends upon the other.
It gives no warrant for separating them in regard to any person or thing, since in its own manner each
concerns whatever the other concerns in its manner. The proposed separation would require a saving
operation with an intended recipient or a promised fruit outside that conjunction. Whoever makes that
further claim must show where such an operation has been appointed and how its effect comes about. In
the wheel of paragraph 0 the same company is in view when we follow the offering and when we follow
the intercession. The several acts have their several offices in the one undertaking for those people. The
union itself has several grounds.

    [96] Both acts are intended to attain the same complete end, the effectual bringing of many sons to
glory to the praise of God’s grace. That end governs the understanding of the intermediate effects. To
bring the sons to glory is to provide what actually brings them there. Every indispensable intermediate
effect has its place within the accomplishment and cannot be assigned to an independent source by
leaving its cause unstated.
    [97] The offering procures good things for precisely the persons for whom the intercession applies
them, for “he was delivered for our offenses, and was raised again for our justification.” Neither act
therefore has an object wider than the other. Christ intercedes for all and only those for whom he offers
himself, as he says, “For their sake I sanctify myself” in offering himself, “that they also might be
sanctified through the truth.” The equal extent follows once it is established that each act reaches the
persons reached by the other. It is the priestly and scriptural relations of the acts that establish those
inclusions, and the distinction of their names leaves the question of their extent open until those relations
are shown. (Romans 4:25; John 17:19)
    [98] The two inclusions follow the actual operation of the priestly office. Those represented in the
offering are secured the intercession, and those for whom he intercedes receive the fruits of the offering.
The equality of extent is the consequence of both relations together. To write that equality without its
grounds would merely put the proposition in dispute into another form.
                                     [99] O⊆I,    I⊆O ⇒ O=I. (13)
    [100] O designates those for whom Christ offers under his saving representative office, and I those for
whom he intercedes under the same office. The two inclusions express conclusions of the respective
scriptural and priestly arguments. Their grounds do not arise from the notation.
    [101] Once the two inclusions are established, the extent of one operation can be enlarged beyond the
other only by contesting a ground on which the equality rests. Retaining both inclusions retains their
consequence. The remaining argument determines which premise is rejected in each proposed separation
and what that rejection does to the completeness of the priestly office.
    [102] The oblation is the foundation of the intercession because it procures everything bestowed by
virtue of that intercession. The end for which Christ procures anything by his death is its application to
those for whom he procured it. The two acts consequently constitute one entire means to one effect. Were
the things bestowed through intercession to fail of application, the offering itself would fail of the end for
which it was made. Its procurement cannot therefore extend to a person or good beyond the extent of the
intercession by which the good is conferred. The Son intercedes for all the good he has purchased, and he
prevails in all his intercessions because the Father always hears him. Every person for whom he died
accordingly receives all the good things purchased on his behalf. This conclusion destroys the adverse
account, and its principal proof lies in the proper end intended and effected by the death of Christ, to be
established below. There are also reasons that stand independently of that proof. Their ground is already
visible in the way the accomplished act continues to bear fruit through the office founded upon it.
Procurement and bestowal take place at different times, but through the intercession the later enjoyment is
an effect of the earlier accomplishment.
    [103] Scripture first supplies the conjunction by repeatedly joining the offering and the intercession in
their effects. “By his knowledge shall my righteous servant justify many, for he shall bear their
iniquities.” The actual justification of sinners, the immediate fruit of the intercession, follows certainly
upon his bearing their iniquities. In the next verse God joins the acts themselves. “He bore the sin of
many” gives the oblation, “and made intercession for the transgressors;” the intercession for the many
whose sin he bears. The same chapter ascribes the full application of the good for which he intercedes to
the passion itself. “With his stripes we are healed.” The whole healing is the fruit he procures by his
stripes, in which the oblation is consummated. The apostle gives the same conjunction, “He was delivered
for our offenses, and was raised again for our justification.” It is for the justification of those whose

offenses he bore that he rises. If he died for all, then all must be justified, unless the Lord failed of his
purpose in both the death and resurrection of his Son. Some have had the boldness to affirm that
blasphemous conclusion. The apostle instead grounds our assurance of eternal glory and freedom from
every accusation in the death of Christ and the intercession which inseparably follows it. “Who,” he asks,
“shall lay any thing to the charge of God’s elect?” and answers, “It is God that justifies. Who is he that
condemns? It is Christ that died,” “yea rather, who is risen again, who is even at the right hand of God,
who also makes intercession for us.” The elect are the persons concerning whom the whole argument
proceeds. If none can be condemned for whom Christ dies, the general ransom fails. Death for all and
intercession for some cannot be made to agree with this testimony, especially because the whole
assurance rests on the love by which God delivered his Son to death for us all. The apostle infers that,
having given him, God will certainly give us all good things in him. That inference cannot stand with the
claim that the Son was given for millions to whom God will give neither grace nor glory. The same
apostle says, “When we were yet without strength, in due time. Christ died for the ungodly;” so that
“being now justified by his blood, we shall be saved from wrath through him.” These and many other
passages join the two acts with their fruits in the same persons. To change the population referred to
between the clauses would change the proposition on which their consequence depends. (Isaiah 53:5, 11;
Romans 4:25; 8:32–34; 5:6, 9)
     [104] Offering and interceding are both acts of the same priestly office, and each belongs to its
faithful discharge. One who fails to sacrifice for those for whom he is priest, or fails to intercede for the
effect of his sacrifice on their behalf, leaves the office incomplete. Christ is both the advocate and the
propitiation, as the apostle says. “If any man sin, we have an advocate with the Father, Jesus Christ the
righteous: and he is the propitiation for our sins.” To be the merciful high priest over the house of God
through whom the children approach God with confidence, he must exercise both functions. The Epistle
to the Hebrews describes their execution in the offering of himself by the shedding of his blood and in the
intercession which saves to the uttermost. Upon the performance of both it exhorts us to draw near to the
throne of grace, for he has “come an high priest of good things to come, not by the blood of goats and
calves, but by his own blood he entered into the holy place, having obtained eternal redemption for us.”
The bloody oblation gives him entrance into the holy place not made with hands, where he accomplishes
the remaining part of his office. The apostle compares his entrance into heaven for us to the high priest’s
entrance into the sanctuary with the blood of bulls and goats, there to pray on behalf of those for whom he
had offered. Christ thus presents himself before the Father so that his offering has its efficacy. His
priesthood is ἀπαράβατον ἱερωσύνην because he continues forever, and he is “able to save to the
uttermost them that come unto God by him” so that we have “boldness to enter into the holiest by the
blood of Jesus.” He exercises neither act for a person for whom he is not priest, and in faithfulness to the
office he exercises both for those for whom he is priest. Their objects must therefore be equal in extent.
The adversaries must answer whether he intercedes for all. If he does not, their account makes him but
half a priest for those for whom they claim he offered. If he does, they must maintain either the error that
all shall be saved or the blasphemy that the Father does not hear the Son and the Son cannot prevail in an
intercession which even the saints on earth are assured will prevail when they pray according to God’s
will. The Father always hears him. If that was true in the days of his flesh while the work was unfinished,
it is all the more evident now that he has completed the Father’s will, sits at the right hand of the Majesty
on high, and asks for the fulfillment of the promises made when he undertook the work. The priesthood is
complete in its exercise for those represented because the operation through which the offering’s fruits are
secured belongs to the very office through which they were purchased. (1 John 2:1–2; 5:14; Hebrews 9:7,
11–13; 7:24–25; 10:19–22; Romans 8:27; John 11:42)
    [105] Polycarp calls Christ our everlasting High Priest in a prayer that its recipients be built up in faith
and receive their portion among the saints. The title belongs with the continuance and completion of the
saving good sought for those persons. What the priest has provided for them and what he communicates
to them therefore both enter the account of the office already established. If the offering left its appointed

fruits unsecured, there would still be an account to give of the priestly work by which those fruits are to
reach them. (Polycarp, Epistle to the Philippians 12)
    [106] The nature of intercession supplies a third ground of its inseparability from the offering. In
heaven it is the real presentation of Christ himself before the throne of grace, sprinkled with the blood of
the covenant on our behalf. The perfection of this act has no need of vocal entreaty, tears, or the
humiliation of his former supplication. “For Christ,” says the apostle, “is not entered into the holy places
made with hands, but into heaven itself, now to appear in the presence of God for us.” He appears for us
in the presence of God, presenting the sacred body in which he suffered for us. In the ninth chapter of
Hebrews the apostle compares this entrance to the high priest’s entrance into the holy place with the
blood of bulls and goats. The Savior comes with his own blood and presents himself so that the oblation
retains its efficacy until the many sons given to him are brought to glory. Under this description his
intercession is the offering continued. He was a “Lamb slain from the foundation of the world.” Before
the oblation was actually made in the fullness of time, his intercession was the presentation of his
engagement to accomplish it in due time. What he presents afterward is the fulfillment of that same
engagement. Its remembrance and declaration are the means by which he claims the things it purchased.
There can be no larger extent to one act than to the other when the intercession consists in presenting the
offering for those for whom he suffered, to obtain the goods purchased for them. This continuing
presentation is the operative relation of the accepted offering to its bestowal, and it requires no repeated
report to supply a deficiency in divine knowledge. (Hebrews 9:12–13, 24; Revelation 13:8)
    [107] A fourth ground is that the oblation and death of Christ procures the actual bestowal of every
good conferred through his intercession. The acts therefore have the same aim and are means tending to
the same end. The particulars of the compact have already been set out in paragraphs 33–72. What
matters here is the relation in which the promise stands to the work the Son freely accepted. On his
voluntary engagement to redeem, the Father proposed to him an end to be reached through his suffering,
and everything for which the Son afterwards intercedes belongs to that end. It is the reward promised for
his labor and the fruit of what he merits in the performance. Considered as an entreaty, whether virtual or
formal and whether its exercise is understood as real or oral, the intercession for any good must rest upon
a promise. Nothing is bestowed in answer to it beyond what that promise gives him a right to claim. The
Father’s word is “Ask of me, and I will give you the heathen for your inheritance,” and that inheritance is
promised on account of the sufferings to which the psalm refers when it says that “the kings set
themselves, and the rulers took counsel together against him”. The apostles understand this of Herod,
Pontius Pilate, and the people of the Jews, who persecute him to death and do to him “whatsoever the
hand and counsel of God had before determined to be done.” The promise founding the intercession is
thus the Father’s engagement actually to confer upon the persons for whom the Son suffered everything
the Son merited and purchased by dying for them. It follows both that he intercedes for all for whom he
dies and that the death procures every good that is bestowed in response. Before those goods are
bestowed, the accomplished act has yet to bear its full fruit in those persons. Whether the claim that Christ
by dying procures what is never granted can stand with Scripture or common sense will be seen below.
The relation established by the promise already explains what mere succession would leave unexplained.
The Father appoints the work with its end, the Son performs it, and the bestowal promised upon that
performance follows through his intercession. The benefit conferred later therefore belongs to the efficacy
of the obedience already accomplished. (Psalms 2:2, 8; Acts 4:27–28)
    [108] Christ himself joins the oblation and the intercession by offering and interceding at the same
time. As regards his own will and intention, he then offers himself as perfectly as on the cross and
intercedes as perfectly as he does now in heaven. The difference in historical manner allows the
operations to be distinguished. Their union in his own undertaking gives no ground to separate them.
Scripture indeed makes the efficacy of each inseparable from the other, since complete remission and
redemption cannot be obtained for us without the high priest’s entrance into the most holy place. The

office retains both acts for each intended recipient because each is indispensable to their common issue.
(John 17; 17:4; 1 Corinthians 15:17; Hebrews 9:12)
    [109] A sixth ground appears in the consolation lost when Christ’s death and intercession are assigned
different objects. The general ransom cuts the sinews of the strong consolation God is willing that we
should receive, as the fuller argument below will show. The particular loss here concerns the assurance of
the soul that knows Christ died for it. The confidence attainable in this life amounts to “joy unspeakable,
and full of glory” because what the oblation procures the intercession obtains in actual bestowal. Christ
follows the charge of our sins into every court until they are fully pardoned and clearly expiated. He does
not leave those who come to God through him until he has saved them to the uttermost. Without his
resurrection, his death would have profited us nothing and our faith would have been vain. Sever the
death from the resurrection and ensuing intercession, whether in his intention or in what the acts
separately procure, and the ground of consolation is lost. “What good will it do me to be persuaded that
Christ died for my sins, if, notwithstanding that, my sins may appear against me for my condemnation,
where and when Christ will not appear for my justification?” The apostle asks, “Who is he that
condemns?” and answers, “It is Christ that died,” To this it might be said, “Why, God by his law may
condemn me, notwithstanding Christ died for me!” But the apostle continues, “He is risen again, and sits
at the right hand of God, making intercession for us.” Christ does not rest in having died for them, for he
certainly intercedes for those for whom he died, and no accuser or sin can appear against them where he
appears for them. In this conjunction the soul has a sure foundation. Objections against this text will be
taken up below, but the identity of the object of the acts argued in paragraphs 103–108 is already
confirmed in what they secure together. Knowledge of the accomplished act gives this consolation
through knowledge of its promised consequences. Procurement alone supplies no assurance of the final
issue once the relation to actual bestowal is removed. (Hebrews 9:26; 7:25; 1 Corinthians 15:17; Romans
8:34)
    [110] Confidence in the final enjoyment of an accomplished benefit follows from a known saving
implication and a warranted premise concerning the believer himself. His recognition occurs later in
experience than the provision whose effect he recognizes and contributes nothing to bringing that
provision about. This return in knowledge from the recognized effect to its cause will become central to
the account of consolation.
    [111] R(x)⇒S(x),    KxR(x)     ⇒ KxS(x) under warranted recognition of the implication. (14)
     [112] K(x) designates the person’s warranted knowledge under the evidential conditions stated in the
argument. The inference proceeds from premises recognized by that person and requires those premises to
be true. Confidence of itself does not establish that condition.
    [113] The strength of an assurance is bounded by what the saving premise entails. A ransom
compatible with the final perdition of a person cannot give him knowledge of his final salvation merely
by becoming known to him. Firm consolation depends on the actual content of the saving relation and on
the warrant for recognizing it, which will be examined in paragraphs 982–1017.
    [114] The argument in paragraphs 103–113 establishes that Christ continually intercedes for the same
persons for whom he once offered himself. In their equal extent the acts constitute a joint means to a
definite end, whose nature is next to be determined. Before that determination, the objections to their
conjunction have to be removed “as a man removes dung until it be all gone.” Each proposed division has
to be tested against the relations established in paragraphs 91–113, since a change in the office, the
recipient, the benefit, or the accomplishment may remove the very premise from which the effect
followed.
    [115] Sacrifice and intercession belong to the same person in the exercise of the high priesthood. The
name is answered only in the performance of both acts. Christ is the only true high priest, in whom the
perfections weakly represented by the former types are actual, and he accordingly sacrifices and

intercedes for the persons for whose sake he holds the office. An opponent who treats the office as
complete for a person while withholding one of these acts has to explain how its faithful exercise can lack
an operation that constitutes it.
    [116] The answerers undertake to answer a related argument, which they state in these words, “The
ransom and mediation of Christ is no larger than his office of priest, prophet, and king; but these offices
pertain to his church and chosen: therefore his ransom pertains to them only.” The priestly, prophetic, and
kingly offices belong to the one Mediator, but the immediate object of action differs among them. Any
inference from the extent of an office therefore depends upon the particular relation of that office to the
saving goods in dispute.
    [117] The point of the argument as intended is that Christ offers nothing for one for whom he is no
priest, and that he is priest only for those for whom he also intercedes. Its strength rests on the
conjunction of representation and intercession in the exercise of his office. The opponents have an interest
in stating it as lightly as possible so as to appear to have dismissed it fairly. A fuller statement would
bring out the weight of its premises, for the inference depends upon their determination of the persons for
whom he acts, and the bare repetition of the title does nothing to establish that extent. The evasion can be
tested as it stands.
     [118] “This,” the answerer says, “is a sober objection.” Owen initially supposed it received that
friendly description because it appeared easy to satisfy. The answer proved to be an occasion for venting
new and false conceptions, without the appearance of meeting what had been urged. This “objection,”
received milder treatment than the other arguments, which are as painful to the answerer’s party as they
are impossible either to bear or evade, and which accordingly receive only “This is horrid, that
blasphemy, that detestable, abominable, and false.” The opening of the pretended answer supplies the
actual reason for the distinction. It says that “this objection does not deny the death of Christ for all men,
but only his ransom and mediation for all men.” In that form the supposed argument scarcely deserves the
name “sober objection,” nor would a man of sound judgment propose it. Christ says he came to “give his
life a ransom for many,” and to have him die for all without being a ransom for all contradicts what his
death is in its most general account. The answerer and his party themselves make the ransom as extensive
as anything in, about, or following his death. They have already divided the ends of the death by saying,
“For some he not only paid a ransom, but also intercedes for them; which be does not for all for whom he
paid a ransom.” Are they now to add an earlier division, by which Christ dies for all and pays a ransom
only for some of those for whom he dies? Those for whom he merely dies would have to be sought
beyond all and every man, since by their account all and every man are ransomed. The ease with which
they can pronounce another distinction supplies neither an operation producing the alleged result nor a
determinate premise that defeats the inference. Their remaining answer can stand on its own merits.
(Matthew 20:28)
    [119] “The answer to this objection,” he says, “is easy and plain in the Scripture, for the mediation of
Christ is both more general and more special; — more general, as he is the ‘one mediator between God
and men’; and more special, as he is ‘the mediator of the new testament, that they which are called might
receive the promise of eternal inheritance.’ According to that it is said, ‘He is the Savior of all men,
specially of those that believe.’ So in all the offices of Christ, the priest, the prophet, the king, there is that
which is more general, and that which is more special and peculiar.” The proposed generality has to be
determined through what it actually does. Common preservation, the publication of a doctrine, and
effectual intercession are distinct operations. An argument which changes between them while retaining
the same word conceals the relation on which the question turns. (1 Timothy 2:5; 4:10; Hebrews 9:15)
    [120] The answerer gives this as a plain answer from Scripture and leaves its application to
conjecture. What he can mean is that Christ ransoms only those for whom he is mediator and priest, but
that he is a general mediator and priest for all, a special one for some, and that he pays the ransom under
the general office. The expression is barbarous, and the substance is an unchristian madness so remote

from common sense that it better merits contempt than reply. Owen does not expect much order of
thought or expression from men who pass directly from manual trades into preaching and writing, but the
reception given to this wildness in rags while sober truth is shut out is lamentable. What sense is there in
the question, “Christ is either a general mediator between God and man, or a special mediator of the new
testament?” Mediation is between parties, and their agreement supplies the covenant of which the
mediator is mediator. Scripture knows Christ as mediator of the new testament. If a general mediation
beyond it is alleged, the covenant to which that other mediation belongs must be named. The covenant of
works would make him mediator of a canceled covenant, derogate from his honor, and overthrow the
gospel. Scripture calls him a “surety” and asks whether it is not “of a better testament?” Such bold
assertions show a greater need of catechesis than a fitness to preach. The author has taken up something
heard from an Arminian doctor and made poor work of expressing it. The occasions for those accounts
and the Scriptures with which they are colored make it possible to remove the evasion. A general relation
can answer a conclusion concerning the particular mediation only by showing how it contains the
operation in question, and that requires keeping the office and the persons for whom it is exercised fixed
throughout. (Hebrews 7:22)
    [121] A distinction between the more general and more peculiar fruits of Christ’s mediation can be
tolerated in a certain sense. To distribute his offices and his own relation to them between general and
peculiar is a gross and unformed fancy. There is no general mediation or function of office in Christ
extending beyond his church or chosen. It is his “church” he “redeemed with his own blood”. It is also his
“church” whose sanctification the apostle explains in the words “he loved and gave himself for it, that he
might sanctify and cleanse it with the washing of water by the word, that he might present it to himself a
glorious church.” The ones he “laid down his life for” were his “sheep”. He likewise “appears in heaven
for us.” Scripture assigns the mediation to no others. His incarnation is “because the children were
partakers of flesh and blood” and the reason concerns the children rather than the world as a whole. At his
oblation he says, “For their sakes,” meaning “those whom you have given me,” “do I sanctify myself” and
the sanctifying of himself is his offering, the work then in hand. Of his resurrection Scripture says, “He
was delivered for our offenses, and was raised again for our justification.” Of his ascension he says, “I
go,” “to my Father and your Father, and that to prepare a place for you.” His perpetual intercession is
exercised to “save to the uttermost them that come unto God by him?” None of these acts is a general
mediation for all, and the Son excludes it expressly in his words, “I pray not,” “for the world, but for them
which then have given me.” Benefits distinct from the complete saving undertaking may have different
recipients. Their extent therefore has to be established through the benefit actually asserted, its
procurement, and the result its operation entails. (Acts 20:28; Ephesians 5:25–27; John 10:15; 17:9, 19;
14:2; Hebrews 9:24; 2:14; 7:25; Romans 4:25)
    [122] The first text brought to establish this distinction says, “For there is one God, and one mediator
between God and men, the man Christ Jesus.” The argument passes from the fact that Christ mediates
between God and men to the assertion that he mediates for every man. The elect are men, the children
partake of flesh and blood, and the church consists of men. Each of these descriptions is true without
extending the company described to the whole of mankind. The apostle’s words would remain true had
Christ mediated only for his apostles. “Apage nugas!” The existence of one Mediator leaves the recipients
of the several benefits of his office to be established by what the passage says about the exercise of that
office. Where the proposition names men indefinitely, a universal conclusion requires a premise which
fixes the relation of the office to every person it is alleged to reach. (1 Timothy 2:5)
    [123] The other proof adduces the words “Who is the Savior of all men, specially of those that
believe.” The argument might have had more appearance of force had Scripture said, “Who is the
Mediator of all men, specially of them that believe,” but its actual subject is the “living God” in whom we
trust, as the preceding words of the verse show. The passage says nothing about Christ as mediator. God
the Father is often called Savior and is the person intended here, as sound interpreters agree, because the
subject under discussion is his protecting providence, general toward all and peculiar toward his church.

He is said to “save man and beast” in the words Ἀνθρώπους καὶ κτήνη σώσεις κύριε, where the Greek
renders the Hebrew ַ‫ ּתֹוִׁש יע‬by σώσεις, “You shall save or preserve.” God is thus “Savior of all,” in his
deliverance and protection of those in danger, exercising that providence especially for believers. To use
his preservation of temporal life to prove the extent of eternal redemption would require a premise
connecting distinct effects, which the citation does nothing to supply. (1 Timothy 4:10; Psalms 36:6)
    [124] The context determines the effect from the encouragement the apostle gives. Despite the injuries
and reproaches continually directed against the people of God, they are to run the appointed race with joy
because God preserves all, for “in him we live, and move, and have our being” and does not allow injury
to remain unavenged. Believers are as the apple of his eye and receive his special protection. If he permits
them to be pressed for a season, they have reason to keep their confidence, continue in well-doing, and
trust him. It would give them no encouragement to be told that God would save those who do not and
never will believe. And it would be strange for Christ to save those who are never saved, who receive no
grace from him to believe, and for whom he refuses to intercede, since intercession is a substantial part of
the mediation by which he saves sinners. The assertion “He is the Savior of all men,” consequently has
neither the subject nor the predicate those who invoke it for universal redemption assign to it. “He,” refers
to God the Father, and the saving in question is his providential preservation. He governs and protects all
and keeps especial watch over his own, so that they are not always exposed to the cruelty, slander, and
other pressures under which they labor. The apostle describes his own practice of trust in the words “But
we had the sentence of death in ourselves, that we should not trust in ourselves, but in God which raises
the dead: who delivered us from so great a death, and does deliver us: in whom we trust that he will yet
deliver us;” for “he is the Savior of all men, specially of those that believe.” Some may take the words
“Because we hope in the living God, who is,” as a statement of the principal doctrine for which Paul
endures his labors and afflictions rather than as an account of his confidence in enduring them. This
reading also asserts the true God and dependence on him against the idols of the Gentiles and the vain
devices by which they put themselves on the throne of the Most High. Neither reading makes Christ
Savior of persons never saved from their sins as his people are, or of persons who never hear of salvation
or a Savior. Neither yields a twofold redemptive office, one for all and one for believers, in which belief
makes him Savior in a special manner though he neither procures nor purchases that belief. As for
accepting that sense of the passage, “credat Judæus Apella.” Whoever has Christ for Savior in the work of
redemption is saved to the uttermost from infidelity and disobedience, receives grace here, and is brought
to glory hereafter. The temporal preservation established by this context cannot be enlarged into a
universal redemption simply by retaining the name Savior. (Acts 17:28; Psalms 145:14–16; Genesis 9:5;
Zechariah 2:8; Deuteronomy 32:10; John 17:9; 2 Corinthians 1:9–10; Matthew 1:21)
    [125] The further attempts to sustain this evasion also turn on the relation between the alleged act of
intercession and its result. Whether that act is general can be determined only by specifying what the Son
requests, the persons for whom he requests it, and what that request is said to obtain.
    [126] “Christ,” they say, “in some sort intercedes and puts in for transgressors, even the sons of men,
yet in and of the world, that the Spirit may so still unite and bless those that believe on him, and so go
forth in their confessions and conversations, and in the ministration of the gospel by his servants, that
those among whom they dwell and converse might be convinced and brought to believe the report of the
gospel; as once; as himself left a pattern to us; that so the men of the world might be convinced, and the
convinced allured to Christ and to God in him; yea, so as that he does in some measure enlighten every
man that comes into the world. But in a more special manner does he intercede.” Believers are first
united, then bear witness in word and conduct, and the hearer is then said to receive the report and
eventually believe. These are several operations. A consequence for the whole world cannot be drawn
from the sequence unless each transition has a cause adequate to what it is supposed to effect. (Isaiah
53:12; Luke 23:34; John 21:21–23; 1:9; Matthew 5:14–16; Thomas More, The Universality of God’s Free
Grace)

    [127] The twofold intercession attributed to Christ as Mediator amounts to a petition for all sinners to
believe and a further petition that believers be saved. The first claim is concealed under the cloudy
expressions and is the one we deny. The two petitions have different immediate objects. Their relation has
to account both for the change from unbelief to faith and for the believer’s subsequent preservation, so
that the source of each indispensable condition is established.
    [128] Faith is a necessary transition in the account. If the proposed intercession obtains faith, the
transition from unbelief must occur as a real change in the person on whose behalf the intercession is
made. If it does not obtain faith, then the sufficient cause of that indispensable transition has been left
outside the saving provision described. Calling it a condition establishes no operation by which it occurs.
                      [129] Unbeliefx(t)Fx(t1),      t1≥t,    Nx∈Px or Nx∉Px. (15)
    [130] Unbelief designates the unbelieving condition of the person, F his believing condition, and N
the operation that actually effects the change. P comprises the operations procured in the proposed saving
provision. The alternatives concern the inclusion of that indispensable operation in the provision the
account treats as secured.
     [131] The necessity of faith gives rise to a question about its production. An account that requires it
must identify the operation by which it comes to exist in the person. The remaining objections return to
that dependence even when they distinguish the kinds of intercession under which they propose to explain
it.
    [132] The author says, “It is an interceding in some sort.” Its manner remains to be stated. Does Christ
intercede directly for those persons or indirectly, in virtue of his blood shed for them or on some other
ground? Does he intend that they obtain the good for which he intercedes or purpose that they go without
it? Does he intercede for every man, or only for persons within the outward bounds of the church? Is the
object faith, or another good, and is the request absolute or conditional? The asserted efficacy is
unintelligible until the request, its ground, the persons intended, and the relation to its fulfillment have
been made definite.
    [133] The first question is whether Christ intercedes directly or whether the alleged good comes only
by consequence. In the prayer cited, believers are the direct object because the request is that God bless
them so that those among whom they live might believe the gospel report. What comes to the others is
represented as a result of a blessing obtained for the believers. That good may be treated as an accident
consequent upon their flourishing, κατὰ συμβεβηκός, or as a further end which the blessing is intended to
attain. In the former case their good is no more intended than their evil. In the latter, the failure of the
result becomes a failure of the Savior’s intention. Was the wisdom to choose means proportioned to the
end wanting, or the power to effect the end intended? An indirect effect is established only through the
intervening operations that actually produce it. The addition of those operations increases the relations to
be proved.
    [134] The second question concerns the ground of the intercession. If it is the blood shed for those
persons, Christ intercedes that they receive what the oblation procured for them, for this is what it means
to ground his intercession in his death. Since the alleged prayer is that all believe, the merit of his death
must on this account have procured faith for all. The advocates of universal redemption do not maintain
this. Among the ends they assign to the death of Christ they do not include the effectual and infallible
bestowal of faith on all for whom he died. Nor can they explain why it is not bestowed if it was purchased
by his death and asked for in his intercession. The concurrence of these two acts is sufficient to obtain that
spiritual good. If the intercession has another ground, they must describe what Christ does in it that differs
from his appearing for us in heaven sprinkled with his own blood. The procurement and the request
founded on it concern the same persons, and a procurement for one group gives no account of a saving
request for another until the alleged connection is shown.

     [135] The third question is whether Christ intends that those for whom he asks faith should believe. If
he does not, the intercession is a mock entreaty for what he would not have the Father grant. If he does,
the asserted prayer leaves it unexplained why all do not believe. Indeed, if he died for all and prayed for
all that they might believe, why are all not saved, since the Father always hears him? The intention and
the efficacy of the appointed means must be accounted for together. A saving petition left unanswered
would require a demonstration of where their conjunction fails. (John 11:42)
     [136] The fourth question determines the population for which this intercession is made. If it reaches
only those within the outward bounds of the church, it has been extended beyond believers but remains
short of a general intercession. The hypothesis requires every person without exception. If it is made for
all, its extent must be reconciled with the alleged petition, “that the Spirit would so lead, guide, and bless
believers, and so go forth in the ministration of the gospel by his servants, that others (that is, all and
every one in the world) may be convinced and brought to believe?” Millions never see a believer and
never hear the report of the gospel. A petition whose operation proceeds through those encounters could
establish intercession for them only through a further premise extending its operation beyond the persons
who actually have them.
     [137] A fifth question concerns whether Christ intercedes for faith absolutely or upon a condition, and
if conditionally whether the condition is on the part of God or man. An absolute petition obtains its
object, so that all must believe if it is true that the Father always hears him. A condition on God’s part can
only mean that the Father will give faith if he pleases. If this marks ignorance in the Son concerning his
Father’s will, it is incompatible with the unity of his person in glory and with the assurance that his Father
will hear whatever he asks. If it expresses his submission to the Father’s will as the prime cause of the
good bestowed, it is consistent with the absolute petition, in which case all must again believe. A
condition on the part of the persons for whom he intercedes has to be named and shown in Scripture.
Where is it said that Christ asks faith for them if they first do some other thing? What such condition can
be rationally assigned to his request? “Some often intimate that it is, if they suffer the Spirit to have his
work upon their hearts, and obey the grace of God.” Obedience to the grace of God is believing, so that
the petition would be that they believe if they believe. Others assign the good use of the means of grace
they enjoy as the condition of the benefit. But the good use of those means is submission to them, which
is itself believing, and some do not have the means to use well or ill. Further, either Christ prays that they
make good use of them or he does not. If he does not, he does not pray for the believing that consists in
yielding obedience to them. If he does, his prayer is once more either absolute or conditional, and the
original question returns. There is ample ground here to expose the madness of the assertion before
considering its proofs. The condition of the petition requires a source, and a petition that presupposes the
very faith it is said to procure leaves the first production of faith unexplained. (John 11:42; Psalms 2:8)
     [138] In feedback an output already produced enters a subsequent operation. Its temporal order
therefore presupposes an initiating state and causes by which the earlier output is brought into being. A
circle requiring its own completed effect before the first operation can begin has no such order. To require
faith before that very faith is first bestowed supplies neither the initiating state nor an operation adequate
to produce it.
                        [139] yt→ut+1,       F0⇒Grant(F0),      Grant(F0)⇒F0. (16)
     [140] The first relation gives the entry of an output into a later operation. The final two expressions
give a mutually presupposed condition where no prior grant, independent capacity, or initiating operation
is supplied. Both expressions can hold without the occurrence of either event.
     [141] Mutual implication alone leaves the actuality of both terms undetermined. Explaining the
condition requires the cause by which it comes to exist. A condition on the reception of faith therefore
concerns a necessary part of the causal account, for the issue is whether the saving undertaking supplies
the first act of believing whose necessity it admits.

    [142] The first proof is taken from Isaiah’s words, “He made intercession for the transgressors.” The
transgressors may be all those for whom Christ suffered, as their description in the passage most naturally
indicates. Or, as some suppose, the expression may refer only to the transgressors by whose actions he
suffered. On the first reading he intercedes for all whose sins he bears, which is our contention. On the
second, the act to which the opponents next appeal is the fulfillment of the prophecy, so what he did in
that act determines the scope of what Isaiah foretells. On either reading the description must be kept with
the relation in which the passage puts those transgressors to his suffering. The fact that their sin has a
name common to others gives no ground for replacing the persons specified with every possible bearer of
that name. (Isaiah 53:6, 12)
    [143] “Then said Jesus, Father, forgive them; for they know not what they do.” From that prayer the
conclusion drawn is “Therefore there is a general intercession for all, that they may believe.” Its weakness
may be left to the reader’s judgment, but the ablest of that party have relied so much upon this place for a
general intercession that fails of its effect that the dependence of their inference on the Gospel words
should be examined. The prayer supplies evidence concerning its actual recipients and the benefit
requested. An inference extending it to everyone and assigning it a saving effect must establish both
enlargements. (Luke 23:34)
    [144] The prayer concerns the handful of Jews by whom Christ was crucified. To infer from it a
prayer for every human being who ever has lived or ever will live is a wild deduction. A petition may be
as earnest as possible and still establish nothing concerning persons outside the historical group it names.
    [145] Even among those who crucified him, the stated reason for the prayer identifies those who acted
in ignorance. “For they know not what they do.” That some of the rulers acted ignorantly is certain from
the passage that says they did so, and some of them were afterward converted. Neither proposition
warrants the assertion that all acted ignorantly. The petition for pardon for the sin of those who crucified
him without knowing what they did cannot give ground for an intercession that all should believe, even
those who never heard of the crucifixion. The ignorance specifies its recipients, and the bestowal of the
benefit must be followed with that condition intact. (Acts 3:17)
    [146] The object of the prayer is that the particular sin of crucifying him be forgiven and not laid to
their charge. The words do not even request that those men believe. It is a strange inference that would
conclude from the forgiveness sought for those who committed that offense that Christ intercedes for all
men to believe. Forgiveness of the specified offense and procurement of the complete saving condition
can be connected only by establishing their relation, for the description of either by itself leaves the other
unproved.
    [147] The prayer is further limited to the persons present at his death. Many present no doubt came
from the curiosity usual on such occasions rather than from malice or spite. The continuing unbelief of the
chief priests is accordingly beside the point, since it cannot be proved that they were present at the
crucifixion. The historical presence of those for whom the prayer is made specifies its immediate object,
and extension beyond it would require positive grounds in the intention of the petition.
   [148] It cannot with probability be maintained that the Savior prayed for every one of them on the
supposition that some were finally impenitent. He knew “what was in man” and “knew from the
beginning who they were that believed not.” We ourselves have the rule “There is a sin unto death,”
which forbids prayer for those known to be finally impenitent and to sin unto death. The Mediator’s
knowledge of the persons and their ends therefore belongs to the account of what he intends in prayer.
Their final condition is no contingency hidden from him. (John 2:25; 6:64; 1 John 5:16)
   [149] The supplication is best understood as effectual. The Father heard the Son in this request also
and granted faith and forgiveness to the persons for whom he prayed. Its historical fulfillment therefore
gives no ground for a general and unsuccessful intercession. Of the people whom Peter charges with
having “denied the Holy One, and desired a murderer” “and killed the Prince of Life” about five thousand

believed. “Many of them which heard the word believed, and the number of them was about five
thousand.” If there were others for whom the Savior prayed, they may have been converted afterward.
The rulers were not beyond the reach of its fruit, for “a great company of the priests were obedient to the
faith.” The subsequent grant establishes how the prayer took effect in history. The interval between the
petition and the enjoyment of its good does not break the causal relation between them. (Acts 3; 3:14–15;
4:4; 6:7)
    [150] There is a further distinction between the prayers Christ offers by virtue of his mediatorial office
and those he makes in fulfillment of his own duty under the law. The one who was made subject to the
law was indeed the Mediator. Yet the acts by which he obeys the law as a private person are acts of the
person who mediates without being acts of mediation. As one subject to the law he was bound to forgive
wrongs done to him and to pray for his enemies, as he taught us to do and here did himself. “I say unto
you, Love your enemies, bless them that curse you, do good to them that hate you, and pray for them
which despitefully use you, and persecute you.” That instruction follows from the law, “You shall not
avenge, nor bear any grudge against the children of your people, but you shall love your neighbor as
yourself,” and directly opposes the wicked gloss of the Pharisees. Under the prohibition of revenge and
the duties of pardon and prayer he could thus pray for his enemies and the persons who crucified him.
Such an act cannot establish anything concerning a general intercession under the office in which the
Father always hears him. The consequence proper to an office belongs to acts performed in its exercise,
even where the same person also acts under another obligation. (Matthew 5:44; Leviticus 19:18)
    [151] The prayer that the world might believe and know the Father sent the Son is also urged for a
general intercession. Christ asks that the unity, concord, and flourishing of his servants have this effect.
The citation has a show of evidence only so long as the reception of the disciples’ visible unity is left
undistinguished from the saving efficacy attributed to it. The unity gives the observer a fact he can
acknowledge, and that acknowledgment does not by itself constitute the faith whose procurement is in
dispute. (John 17:21–23)
    [152] An intention that all men should believe would have included a request for means more
effectual than the sight of the blessed condition of Christ’s people, a sight granted only to a small part of
the world. It would at least have included the preaching of the word to all, as the only ordinary way for
them to come to the knowledge of him. There is no record that he prayed for this or that God granted it.
On the contrary, Christ blessed the Father because it had seemed good in his sight that it should not be so.
The intended effect of saving faith requires a provision adequate to produce it, and the visibility of the
disciples’ condition does not account for the inward change. (Matthew 11:25–26)
    [153] The prayer has to be read in the same discourse as the express words “I pray not for the world;”
True, holy, saving faith is as great a privilege as any Christ procures or seeks for his own. To make it the
good he asks for the world would put the interpretation in direct contradiction to his limitation of the
prayer. An expansion of one clause at the expense of another changes the premises supplied by the
discourse. (John 17:9)
    [154] Some understand the world in this place as the world of the elect who are to be saved, the
people of God throughout the world. The term is certainly used figuratively, pro mundo contento, for the
men contained in the world, rather than properly, pro mundo continente, for the world that contains them.
It need not mean every man in the world, a sense in which Scripture seldom employs it, as the argument
below will show. It can refer indefinitely to people in the world, few or many, as the elect are distributed
through their generations. This exposition has the support of considerable authorities, yet it cannot be
adopted without reserve here. Throughout the chapter the world is used either of reprobates as opposed to
those the Father gives Christ, or of unbelievers as opposed to those Christ commits to the Father. These
are the same people under different descriptions. The possible meanings of the word show why the
relations in the passage itself must decide which it bears.

    [155] Believing and knowing in this passage signify the conviction and acknowledgment that Christ
really came from God and can protect and do good to his own. The observers cease to regard him as the
seducer and false prophet they had supposed him to be. Scripture often calls this kind of conviction
believing, and the evidence that it does so here has secured the agreement of expositors of all sorts.
Believing in the strict saving sense would mean apprehending and receiving Christ so as to become sons
of God. That has never been and never will be accomplished in everyone in the world, nor did Christ ever
pray for it. The acknowledgment in this passage is ordered to the vindication of his people and the
exaltation of his own glory rather than to a saving good for the world. It therefore gives no proof of what
is asserted. The use of the word “world” will be examined below. The present inference turns on retaining
the meaning of believing wherever the alleged saving effect depends upon it. (John 17:21, 23)
     [156] In the passage from Matthew, the Savior instructs his apostles to put the light and knowledge
they have received and will yet receive to use in preaching the word and living holy lives, so that they
may be a means of drawing men to glorify God. Its place in the argument is to make up the number of
citations, like the many other places adduced without regard to what they prove or why they are used. All
Mr More’s strength and skill cannot bring this one within many leagues of his conclusion. Conduct and
speech both communicate testimony, but the thing testified, the understanding of the person who receives
it, and the inward disposition from which he answers remain distinct in the encounter. The instruction
concerns the witness the apostles are to give and can be laid aside as proof of the alleged general
intercession. (Matthew 5:15–16)
    [157] The other passage in John is urged no more aptly, and Mr More wretchedly glosses it as “In
some measure enlightening every one that comes into the world.” Scripture says that “Christ is the true
Light, that lights every man that comes into the world;” “In some measure,” says Mr More. If he
introduces a measure, what is the degree to which Christ illuminates, and who makes up the rest by what
means independently of him? Who supplies the alleged defect in Christ? The object is to bring in
illumination by the light of nature and whatever common helps can be imagined for persons utterly
deprived of the gospel means of grace, so as to give them knowledge both of God as Creator and of God
in Christ as Redeemer. These calves of Mr More’s own setting up should not receive the sacrifice of the
meaning of Scripture and the worth of Christ’s grace. Christ enlightens every one because he is the only
true light, and whoever is enlightened receives his light from him, the sum and fountain of it. An
illumination must be understood through what it actually enables or effects. The general name of light
cannot give creaturely understanding the effects of regeneration. The general defense of an ineffectual
intercession thus fails, though a further reply is made concerning Christ’s priesthood in particular. (John
1:9)
    [158] When asked to show God, Theophilus directs attention to the condition of the soul’s sight. An
object can be present while the person to whom it is presented lacks the capacity to apprehend it. The
communication of more about the object does not of itself restore the recipient. If a general illumination is
to be credited with the work of regeneration, the operation by which that incapacity is overcome has to be
shown. The recurrence of the word light establishes nothing concerning that operation. (Theophilus of
Antioch, To Autolycus I.2)
     [159] The process of communication includes what is disclosed, what the hearer apprehends, and how
he acts on what he has apprehended. An outward manifestation can give true content while the disposition
of the person who receives it remains hostile. The distinction depends on the actual relation of the
communicated object to that person, and the universality of an outward light supplies no cause of the
inward saving change.
                    [160] mx=Receivex(c),        ax=Gx(Wx,mx),          =Nx(Wx). (17)
    [161] c designates the communicated content, m the content actually apprehended, W the recipient’s
disposition, G the relation that produces his response, and N the efficient operation of renewal. The last
expression gives renewal where it occurs. Its occurrence does not follow from the existence of c.

    [162] A man can receive a true report of the Savior and refuse the one reported. The outward message
and the inward operation thus have distinct effects. Where grace produces saving faith, it renews the
person so that he willingly relies upon the Savior disclosed to him. This distinction will govern the
subsequent treatment of proclamation and human judgment.
     [163] The third reply concerns the priesthood in respect of several alleged ends. “As a priest in respect
of one end, he offered sacrifice, — that is, propitiation for all men; — in respect of all the ends,
propitiation, and sealing the new testament, and testification to the truth; — and of the uttermost end in
all, for his called and chosen ones.” The material that follows in the reply repeats a passage already
answered. Each additional end has to be understood through the operation that provides for it. A general
benefit and the complete saving undertaking cannot be credited with one another’s effects while their
ends remain different. (Hebrews 2:9; 9:14–15, 26; John 1:29; 1 John 2:2; Matthew 26:28)
    [164] The words in this reply hardly yield a tolerable sense, much less the clear answer claimed for
them. Scriptural expressions are wrested into the service of error and made to bear a part in propositions
whose relations cannot be determined. What is meant by the question, “He offered sacrifice in respect of
one end, then of all ends, then of the uttermost end in all?” Begin with the last expression, “uttermost end
in all?” Does “in all,” mean among all the ends proposed and accomplished, among all the persons for
whom he offered, or the last end intended by God and Christ in his offering? The last end in that sense is
the glory of God, and none of the passages quoted intimates it. Nor do the passages establish the
uttermost end subordinate to God’s glory. One speaks of obtaining redemption and the other of shedding
his blood for remission of sins. The reply has already assigned this to the first end of Christ’s death under
the word “propitiation,” which means an atonement for remission of sins. Remission and redemption are
substantially the same and are both immediate fruits and the first end of his death. The reply therefore
confounds first and last and demolishes its own construction, whose foundation is the claim that the death
of Christ has different ends toward different persons, some of which belong to all while all belong only to
some. This is the πρῶτον ψεῦδος of the whole book. The passage in which Christ offers himself to put
away sin is put forward for the first end, and the passage in which he sheds his blood for remission of sins
for the last. The author must give the difference between those two before asking anyone to assign them
to opposite ends of the scheme. Finally he says, “He offered sacrifice in respect of one end, — that is,
propitiation for all men.” If he understands sacrifice and propitiation, that can scarcely appear to him on a
second view to make sense. The office, the end, and the persons concerned must remain determinate
