throughout the argument. Otherwise words that appear to agree are made to express different
propositions. (Hebrews 9:14–15, 26; Matthew 26:8, 28; Ephesians 1:7; Colossians 1:14)
    [165] The proposed distinction distributes Christ’s priesthood according to different ends — with
respect to one end of his sacrifice, he undertakes for every person. With respect to the others, he
undertakes only for the chosen and called. That distribution is precisely the τὸ κρινόμενον, the matter
requiring proof. An answer which merely restates it leaves the argument untouched, for any opponent
could dispose of a demonstration by setting his own opinion beside its conclusion and calling the
disagreement a reply. The goods procured through Christ’s death cannot simply be divided among
different classes of recipients at pleasure. Their ordered relation belongs to the priestly act by which they
are procured, and the argument from Christ’s priesthood was introduced to establish why their objects
cannot be separated in the manner alleged. Repeating the separation supplies no account of its possibility,
no reason for rejecting that argument, and no explanation of the operation through which a single priestly
undertaking could acquire the proposed diversity of objects. The distinction consequently remains a claim
concerning the partition of benefits, whose adequacy depends upon its agreement with the priestly
undertaking and the ordered provision established in paragraphs 91–113. The proposed division would
assign one end of the priestly undertaking to the whole wheel and its other ends to the spokes. The
conjunction established in paragraphs 91–113 is what the division has to answer.
   [166] The cited Scriptures must establish the relation which the distinction asserts. Their presence
supplies no missing premise, and the number of references cannot compensate for the absence of the

required assertion within them. An indefinite statement that Christ bore or took away sin does not entail
that he acts as priest for every individual with respect to propitiation. The allusion to the paschal lamb
makes the difficulty more apparent — the typical cleansing and ceremonial purification belonged to
Israel, the people who represented God’s elect, and did not extend indiscriminately to every person,
including unbelievers and reprobates. Two other cited passages deserve separate examination because
they appear to carry weight in the general dispute. Neither, however, contains language which establishes
this division of the priestly office. The argument concerning the equal objective extent of Christ’s
oblation and intercession therefore retains its force. The means through which the blessed Trinity
accomplishes the appointed end have been identified, and the end itself can now receive direct
examination. Scriptural testimony provides evidence through what it actually asserts. An inference
receives no causal foundation from the mere accumulation of texts which leave its governing relation
unstated. (Hebrews 9:26; 2:1, 9; John 1:9, 29; 2:2)
    [167] The proper end of Christ’s death governs the whole controversy concerning its extent. Upon this
determination the competing conclusions depend — grant the end commonly assigned by our adversaries,
and the assertion that Christ died for every individual follows without the present objection. Establish the
end maintained here, and its extension beyond the elect and believing ceases to be possible. The intended
accomplishment must therefore be stated and demonstrated with sufficient precision to govern everything
subsequently attributed to the sacrifice. Its general expression has already been given — our
approximation to God, or the recovery of sinners from alienation, misery, and wrath into grace, peace,
and eternal communion with him. Within this recovery the end of the agent and the end accomplished by
the work coincide. Their separation in human undertakings arises from defects in the worker — he may
misunderstand his end, choose means unsuited to it, lack the knowledge required for their use, or possess
too little power to carry suitable means into effect. None of these failures belongs to the blessed Three in
One. The appointed means are Christ’s oblation and intercession, whose unity of object has already been
established. And neither insufficiency in those means nor imperfection in their divine appointment and
execution can be admitted without blasphemously denying the wisdom, power, and perfection of God.
What the Trinity intended through them was therefore accomplished, and what Scripture attributes to
them as their accomplished fruit was intended by the Trinity through them. The intended end and the
effected end require no separate accounts, though either may furnish the premise through which the other
becomes known. When a benefit appears as the fruit of Christ’s death, its divine intention follows. When
that intention is established, the efficacy of the appointed means follows with it. The complete purpose
accordingly contains both the result intended and the provision by which it is secured. Suitable means and
effective government of their execution belong together in the assertion that the work accomplishes the
worker’s intention.
    [168] The ends of Christ’s death possess an order — one is supreme and ultimate, while others are
intermediate and serve its accomplishment. Subordination does not render those intermediate goods
dispensable, since their actual attainment constitutes the appointed way through which the ultimate end is
reached.
    [169] 1. The supreme end is God’s glory, the manifestation to us of his glorious attributes, and
especially of justice and of mercy exercised together with justice. God necessarily intends himself first
because he is the highest good, possessing goodness absolutely and essentially rather than receiving it
through participation in another. Every divine work consequently proceeds toward the manifestation of
his glory, and this work, greatest among them, accomplishes that manifestation in every respect and
degree intended. He “makes all things for himself”. All must finally “redound to the glory of God”. And
Christ himself is called “God’s” with respect to the administration committed to him and directed toward
that glory. Election from eternity, redemption through Christ, and every spiritual blessing bestowed in
him are ordered to “the praise of the glory of his grace;” and, “That we should be to the praise of his
glory.” The benefits flowing from Christ’s death share that same end, “we are filled with the fruits of
righteousness, which are by Jesus Christ, unto the glory and praise of God”. And the universal confession

is appointed “That every tongue should confess that Jesus Christ is Lord, to the glory of God the Father.”
Romans 9 establishes the independence and supreme dominion underlying this order. Nothing in the sons
of men supplies the originating cause or occasion of God’s purposes. He acts freely for his own sake and
directs his works toward his own glory. Their consummation appears when every creature declares,
“Blessing, and honor, and glory, and power, be unto him that sits upon the throne, and unto the Lamb for
ever and ever.” This is ἀναμφισβήτητον. Glory is manifested through the actual exercise of justice and
mercy, whose communication to determinate recipients occurs through determinate works. The effects of
those works therefore remain essential to the account of their ultimate end. (Proverbs 16:4; 2 Corinthians
4:15; 1 Corinthians 3:23; Ephesians 1:6, 12; Philippians 1:11; 2:11; Revelation 5:13)
    [170] 2. The intermediate end concerns the effects of Christ’s death in us — the bringing of sinners to
God. Relative to his oblation and intercession, this constitutes one complete end. Within that end,
however, the benefits stand in an ordered relation, so that one serves as the means through which another
is attained. God has appointed their connection. The goods purchased by Christ comprise both the
condition and the inheritance promised upon its fulfillment, both the way and the terminus toward which
that way conducts the recipient. Each belongs equally to the purchase, for the omission of either would
render the other fruitless, as the subsequent argument will establish. Both communicate God and his
goodness to us and make us participants in him, whether the distinction is expressed as grace and glory,
holiness and blessedness, or faith and salvation. In the last pair, faith names the appointed means and
condition, while salvation names the promised inheritance. Faith here includes all saving grace
accompanying it. Salvation includes the whole “glory to be revealed,” the glorious liberty of God’s
children and their eternal enjoyment of the blessed God. The procurement of faith includes its effectual
external and internal means, the word and the almighty sanctifying Spirit. It includes the changes of
standing which accompany faith—justification, reconciliation, and adoption into God’s family—and the
fruits which proceed from it in sanctification and universal holiness, together with every present privilege
of believers arising from the redemption and reconciliation purchased through Christ’s oblation. The end
intended and accomplished through his bloodshed, together with the inseparably conjoined acts of his
mediation, is the real, effectual, and infallible application of all these benefits to every person for whom
he died. Means and end, condition and conditioned gift, faith and grace, salvation and glory, belong to the
one saving provision. His death purchases for each such person a right to them, and their enjoyment
follows certainly in the appointed time. Christ obtains grace and glory from the Father so that they shall
actually be bestowed upon those for whom he offers himself. Some benefits are bestowed upon the
condition of believing. Faith itself is bestowed absolutely, upon no prior condition. The removal of false
accounts of the end will permit the fuller demonstration of this relation. Faith is at once an effect procured
and an operative means through which further goods are received. Its causal source consequently
determines whether the saving provision secures its own accomplishment. (Romans 8:18, 21)
    [171] Faith’s necessity to salvation concerns its office in reception. That necessity leaves intact its
dependence upon procurement. A saving provision can produce in its recipient the very disposition
through which further benefits are subsequently enjoyed. The recipient who receives that disposition also
acts through it, and the actuality of his action neither removes the gift nor places its source outside the
undertaking. The condition thus retains its operation without becoming an unprovided power upon which
the saving purpose must depend.
                          [172] R(x)⇒∃t F(x,t),       F(x,t)⇒Receive(x,B,t). (18)
    [173] Terms. The first relation expresses the procurement of actual faith. Its theological grounds
receive fuller demonstration below. The second expresses the instrumental reception of the relevant
benefits through the faith so procured. B takes its meaning from the particular benefit under
consideration, since the fruits of the undertaking differ in the temporal manner of their possession.
    [174] Consequence. The necessity of the condition and the certainty of its fulfillment belong within
one saving purpose. Conditional enjoyment therefore supplies no inference to a conditional divine

intention. The intention may itself provide the operation through which its required condition becomes
actual. Whether it does so is the governing question pursued in paragraphs 252–273 and paragraphs 371–
405.
    [175] Polycarp’s account of the faith given to the Philippians places the disputed condition within the
saving provision itself. Faith remains necessary to salvation’s enjoyment while arising through the
provision which makes that enjoyment actual. Its necessity establishes no independence from its giver.
The contrary conclusion requires a further premise, namely that the decisive act lies beyond what the
saving undertaking provides. Our adversaries cannot derive this premise from the fact that the recipient
believes, since the person’s believing and the gift by which he believes concern compatible relations
within the same action. The recipient acts through what he receives. (Polycarp, Epistle to the Philippians
3)
    [176] The preceding argument has established that Christ’s death, oblation, and bloodshed receive
their character as means from their appointment to an end. The suffering is not desirable in itself, for the
reason for undergoing it lies entirely in the good it accomplishes. What cannot be regarded as good cannot
occupy the place of an end at all, since bonum et finis convertuntur. The good in view must therefore
concern the Father, Christ himself, or us. To determine which of these the offering intends is to explain
why this particular costly means is appointed, what accomplishment it is fitted to secure, and in whom its
purchased fruits become actual.
    [177] I. Christ’s own good cannot constitute the immediate end in question. In his divine nature he
eternally possesses the whole glory proper to deity, whose manifestation admits degrees relative to us
while its essential perfection remains eternally complete. His concluding petition accordingly concerns
the glory already his with the Father “before the world was.” His human nature, likewise, was eternally
appointed to personal union with the second person of the Trinity from conception, without a foreseen
work or suffering furnishing the cause of that appointment. And his death during the course of his earthly
obedience procured no meritorious benefit which he needed for himself. Perfect righteousness left him no
need to suffer on his own behalf. The glory placed before him in “enduring the cross, and despising the
shame,” concerned chiefly the bringing of many children to glory, according to the promise already
examined, rather than an increase in his own possession through the exaltation of his nature. His
exaltation, dominion over all flesh, and appointment as judge of living and dead followed his humiliation
and suffering. That succession, however, does not establish that these were meritoriously purchased by
his satisfaction for sin or constituted its intended end. Universal dominion supplies no proof of universal
redemption. He is appointed “heir of them, and upholding them all by the word of his power.” “He is set
over the works of God’s hands, and all things are put in subjection under him.” The psalm from which the
apostle draws this testimony identifies what is included among those “all things,” and for the things so
identified no death of redemption can be alleged. Angels also fall under his authority. Principalities and
powers are subject to him, and he will judge angels at the last day, with the saints attesting his righteous
judgment. Yet angels possess no share in the redemptive dispensation of God manifested in flesh — some
have no sin requiring redemption, and the others are eternally excluded from it. “He took not on him the
nature of angels; but he took on him the seed of Abraham,” and God’s establishment of him as “king
upon his holy hill of Zion,” with authority to subdue his enemies and govern them “with a rod of iron,”
does not immediately proceed from a death offered for those enemies. The Father’s immediate love for
the Son grounds the placing of all things in his hand, including universal sovereignty and judgment.
Essential glory, communicated mediatorial glory, and the bringing of sons to glory thus require distinct
predicates. Their predication of one person does not make a consequence established concerning one
transferable to the others. (John 17:5; 3:35; Hebrews 1:2–3; 2:7–8, 16; Psalms 8:5–8; 2:6, 9; 1 Corinthians
6:2–3; Matthew 11:27)
    [178] Even granting the unproved premise that Christ purchased his judicial power through death,
universal redemption would not follow. Judicial authority includes condemnation as well as salvation, for
the authority committed to him is “all judgment”. “He has authority given unto him to execute judgment,

because he is the Son of man;” and its exercise reaches the hour “when all that are in their graves shall
hear his voice and come forth; they that have done good, unto the resurrection of life; and they that have
done evil, to the resurrection of condemnation.” A death which redeems persons cannot have their
condemnation as its saving intention. If redemption of those persons was intended, the acquisition of
power to condemn them was not the end of that redemptive act. If the latter alone was intended, the
former has not been established. The jurisdiction extends to opposed outcomes, and its universal scope
therefore cannot determine the extent of the saving benefit exercised within it. (John 5:22, 27–29; 2
Corinthians 5:10)
    [179] II. The Father’s good likewise cannot constitute the proximate product of Christ’s death. The
distinction concerns the immediate accomplishment, since the ultimate end of the offering and all its
purchased benefits remains “the praise of his glorious grace.” The offering obtains good things from God
for us rather than supplying a good which God previously lacked. Arminius, his followers, and the other
Universalists instead place the principal end in the acquisition of a divine liberty to save — satisfaction
removes the obstacle presented by justice, after which God may pardon upon any condition he chooses.
On this account the accomplished satisfaction leaves it “integrum Deo fuit”, wholly within God’s disposal
whether anyone shall be saved, and whether faith or works shall constitute the condition. “God,” they
maintain, “had a good mind and will to do good to human kind, but could not by reason of sin, his justice
lying in the way; whereupon he sent Christ to remove that obstacle, that so he might, upon the prescribing
of what condition he pleased, and its being by them fulfilled, have mercy on them.” This proposed
permission to act is made the chief, if not the sole, end of the oblation. Its falsity appears through the
reasons which follow. The ultimate manifestation of divine glory and the immediate saving effects in
recipients have distinct relations. Their confusion conceals the operation through which the intended
salvation is accomplished.
     [180] First, the assertion rests upon an unproved limitation of divine mercy — that God could not
have mercy upon mankind without satisfaction from his Son. Given God’s decree, purpose, and
constitution to manifest his glory through vindicative justice, the appointed way necessarily stands, since
with him there is “no variableness, neither shadow of turning”. The necessity follows from his
appointment. An absolute inability antecedent to that appointment is another proposition altogether, for
which Scripture supplies neither express assertion nor sound consequence. To affirm it as settled doctrine
is to introduce an unwritten tradition. Should its denial require further defence, the case can be examined
with the Lord’s assistance. Meanwhile Augustine’s judgment suffices, “Though other ways of saving us
were not wanting to his infinite wisdom, yet certainly the way which he did proceed in was the most
convenient, because we find he proceeded therein.” The necessity internal to an ordained undertaking
must therefore retain its distinction from an independently imposed limitation of God. What governs the
present argument is the means he actually appoints and his faithfulness in bringing them to their end.
(James 1:17; 1 Samuel 15:29; Owen, On Divine Justice, referenced in the editor’s note to The Death of
Death II.2)
   [181] Secondly, this account makes the sending of the Son proceed from a common inclination to
benefit mankind, a wish that mercy might become possible for all, rather than from the complete divine
purpose of knowing, redeeming, and saving the elect. The subsequent argument disproves that
substitution. A purpose embracing its means constitutes an actual undertaking. A possibility whose
decisive conditions remain undetermined contains no corresponding provision for its realization, and
cannot receive the consequences belonging to the complete intention.
    [182] Thirdly, the proposed acquisition changes the immediate recipient of redemption. If Christ dies
to obtain for the Father a right to save notwithstanding justice, the liberty purchased belongs first to God
— he is released from an incapacity to accomplish the good toward which his nature and desire incline
him. Our release from the evil condition in which we must otherwise perish becomes secondary. The
scriptural declaration that Christ redeems his people from their sins would then require replacement by an
assertion that he purchases liberty for his Father. Scripture nowhere supplies that assertion. The sending

of the Son expresses God’s love toward us, and the good immediately attained through his offering
belongs to human recipients. The answer offered is that this newly acquired divine right ultimately serves
our good, since God may now bestow mercy upon those who fulfill whatever condition he chooses. But
this answer dissolves the merit of Christ’s death toward us. Merit establishes that the thing obtained ought
to be bestowed, rather than merely that its bestowal has become permissible. Whether the relation arises
absolutely or through agreement, genuine merit produces a real right to its procured good in the person
who merits or the person for whom the merit operates. A labourer who has completed his day’s work has
acquired more than his master’s freedom to pay. The proper assertion is not “Now his wages may be
paid,” but “Now they ought to be paid”. The labourer possesses a right to his wage. A supposed merit
which obtains only the possibility of bestowal lacks that constitutive relation to its object. Christ’s
meritorious offering therefore cannot purchase merely the Father’s permission to apply its fullness to
some or all, leaving the application itself unpurchased. “To him that works,” the apostle declares, “is the
reward not reckoned of grace, but of debt.” The fruits of Christ’s death are as truly procured for us
through his death as benefits obtained through our own labour would be procured by that labour. Their
reception is free grace relative to us, while their bestowal is a debt relative to his purchase. Permission
newly acquired by God and deliverance actually acquired for persons are different results. The scriptural
descriptions determine which constitutes the offering’s fruit. (Romans 4:4)
    [183] Fourthly, a complete end which could be fully attained without the salvation of a single sinner
cannot constitute the end Scripture assigns to Christ’s death. Scripture attributes remission, grace, and
glory to that death, whereas the procurement of a divine permission leaves all of them unrealized. Even
the full attainment of the alleged right would permit a history in which no one receives eternal life. The
Father might decline to exercise the right, since this account makes no engagement bind him to its
exercise. Possession of a right alone leaves its holder free to use or withhold it. He might instead prescribe
works as a condition which fallen persons cannot fulfill. In either case the supposed end of the death
would be complete, while salvation through it would remain impossible for the sinful persons concerned.
Such an accomplishment cannot answer the declaration that Christ came to save sinners and to “save that
which was lost?”, nor sustain his prayer, “Father, I will that those whom you have given me be with me
where I am; that they may behold my glory?”. Other reasons against this account will appear in the
positive determination of the end — it substitutes possible remission for remission procured, salvability
for salvation, and an opened way toward peace for reconciliation and peace themselves. The governing
test already exposes the deficiency. An effect fully compatible with the salvation of no recipient does not
of itself entail salvation. The causal operation which makes persons saved remains outside the account
and still requires provision. (John 17:24)
     [184] The histories admitted by an account disclose the content of its proposed end. Where the cross
can completely accomplish its sole intended end while every person finally perishes, that end contains
less than the salvation of the persons concerned. No premise of particular redemption is needed to
establish the difference. The test identifies what each account actually includes within the
accomplishment it attributes to the cross.
              [185] Γposs∪{∀x∈H,¬S(x)} may be consistent;Γsave⊢R(a)⇒S(a). (19)
    [186] Terms. Gamma possible names the account in which a saving possibility alone is asserted.
Gamma save names the effectual saving undertaking established by the passages being examined. The
particular person a provides the case through which the consequences admitted by each account become
determinate.
    [187] Consequence. Calling both outcomes salvation does not make their contents identical.
Scriptural purpose clauses and descriptions of accomplished effects must determine which content is
warranted. With that determination made, the formal test discloses the histories compatible with the
asserted accomplishment and those which its actuality excludes.

    [188] The new covenant supplies the proposed exercise of the Father’s acquired liberty. Once Christ’s
death has satisfied the justice said to obstruct divine goodwill, God can enter a covenant of grace whose
benefits are applied upon fulfillment of its condition. Yet Christ himself, together with his death and
passion, is the chief promise of that covenant, which cannot in this sense be treated as something first
procured by his death. The covenant’s own constitution also excludes an account which leaves its good
things wholly dependent upon an obedience independently supplied by its recipients. The obedience and
the whole fulfillment of the condition are themselves promised within the covenant confirmed and sealed
by Christ’s blood. God does receive the manifestation of his glory through the death of his Son, and it is
in this respect that he calls him “his servant, in whom he will be glorified.” The bringing of the entrusted
sons to glory manifests and praises glorious grace, disclosing divine love toward the elect as Christ bears
God’s salvation to the earth’s furthest parts. That full manifestation through mercy tempered with justice
—for “he set forth Christ to be a propitiation through faith in his blood, that he might be just, and the
justifier of him that believes in Jesus,”—is what accrues to God. No previously unavailable liberty is
purchased to release him from a justice that had made his desire impossible. Relative to us, the oblation
secures the bestowal of its goods. By virtue of the compact and covenant grounding Christ’s merit, God
will confer the things which Christ intended to purchase in offering himself to God for us. His offering
secures more than a permission which the Father may decline to exercise. The positive content of those
procured goods follows. A power to prescribe a condition leaves its actual performance unexplained. The
causal provision becomes complete when it includes the supplying of that indispensable condition in
those whose salvation is intended. (Genesis 3:15; Jeremiah 31:33; Isaiah 49:3; Romans 3:25–26)
    [189] The scriptural thesis stated at the opening can now be applied in its developed sense, “Jesus
Christ, according to the counsel and will of his Father, did offer himself upon the cross, to the
procurement of those things before recounted; and makes continual intercession with this intent and
purpose, that all the good things so procured by his death might be actually and infallibly bestowed on
and applied to all and every one for whom he died, according to the will and counsel of God.” The many
relevant passages fall into three coordinated groups. First come those declaring God’s intention and
counsel, together with the mind of the Saviour whose will in this undertaking is one with his Father’s.
Second come descriptions of the actual accomplishment of the oblation, identifying what it procured and
produced. Third come the passages specifying the persons whom God appointed as the objects of
redemption in its intended end. Each group contributes a distinct relation among purpose, operation,
recipients, and effect. Their conjunction supplies the scriptural account corresponding to the definitions
established in paragraphs 1–32.
    [190] I. The first group declares the counsel, purpose, mind, intention, and will of God and the
Saviour in this work, “The Son of man is come to save that which was lost;” Christ repeats this
declaration on another occasion. In one place it introduces the parable of the lost sheep. In the other it
concludes the recovery of Zaccheus. Both identify the Father’s will accomplished in Christ’s coming as
the recovery of lost sinners. Zaccheus is converted, brought into the free covenant, and made a son of
Abraham. The sheep is found, taken upon the shepherd’s shoulder, and carried home. Seeking reaches its
end in finding, and saving reaches its end in recovery. Were Christ to leave unrecovered the persons he
came to save, his stated purpose would fail. The saving end thus contains actual changes in the lost
themselves, whose occurrence requires the provision through which those changes are produced.
(Matthew 18:11; Luke 19:10)
    [191] Secondly, the angel’s declaration identifies the same end in Christ’s coming in flesh and
consequently in his sufferings — he will “save his people from their sins.” His coming intends everything
required for the complete salvation of his peculiar people from their sins. A merely partial effect, leaving
the decisive saving operation unaccomplished, contradicts the fullness announced and offends Christian
confession. The declaration concerns deliverance itself, whose actuality changes the people’s condition.
The availability of deliverance alone does not supply the relation between the Saviour’s work and the
salvation which its purpose names. (Matthew 1:21)

     [192] Thirdly, Paul declares the end appointed by the Father’s will and counsel — Christ came to
“save sinners.” The declaration reaches beyond the opening of a passable door which persons may enter if
they possess the will or power, and beyond the purchase of a reconciliation and pardon which may never
become theirs. Its object is their actual salvation from sin’s guilt and power and from God’s wrath against
it. If that salvation remains unaccomplished, the stated end remains unfulfilled. And since failure cannot
be attributed to Christ’s coming, its intended recipients are those in whom this saving effect is procured.
The Father’s compact and promise concerning “seeing his seed, and carrying along the pleasure of the
Lord prosperously” have already been examined. They join the decree of Christ’s “making his soul an
offering for sin,” inseparably to the actual gift of the believing generation whom he calls “The children
that God gave him.” That gift belongs to the end of his undertaking. A possibility permits salvation never
to occur. The accomplishment asserted in these declarations excludes that outcome for those whose
salvation is its object. (1 Timothy 1:15; Isaiah 53:10–12; Hebrews 2:13)
    [193] Fourthly, the apostle states the purpose with equal fullness, “Forasmuch as the children are
partakers of flesh and blood, he also himself likewise took part of the same; that through death he might
destroy him that had the power of death, that is, the devil; and deliver them who through fear of death.”
The incarnation and offering deliver the children given to Christ from death, hell, and the devil, and
thereby bring them near to God. The declaration contains no purchase of a merely possible release for
every individual. Its persons are the children entrusted to him, and its effect is their deliverance from
death and from the one who possessed death’s power. Since neither description belongs indiscriminately
to all persons, the taking of flesh and blood for this purpose cannot be assigned indiscriminately to all.
The defeat of the enemy’s power is ordered to the release of these children. That relation between
defeated power and released persons constitutes the inference. (Hebrews 2:14–15)
    [194] Fifthly, the same intention governs “Christ loved the church, and gave himself for it; that he
might sanctify and cleanse it with the washing of water by the word, that he might present it to himself a
glorious church, not having spot, or wrinkle, or any such thing; but that it should be holy and without
blemish”, “He gave himself for us, that he might redeem us from all iniquity, and purify unto himself a
peculiar people, zealous of good works.” The Holy Spirit gives in these passages a fullness of expression
which no human restatement can improve. Both describe the same act, “He gave himself,” and identify its
recipients through “For his church,” and “For us,” expressions equal in extent and force. The end in
Ephesians is “To sanctify and cleanse it, to present it to himself a glorious church, not having spot or
wrinkle.” The end in Titus is “To redeem us from all iniquity, and to purify unto himself a peculiar
people, zealous of good works.” Every person cannot be included within this church or within the

company to which Paul and Titus belong, since every person is not cleansed, purified, sanctified, made
glorious, and brought near to Christ. The alternative would make Christ’s purpose fail toward most of its
intended objects, which cannot be conceded. Love issues in self-giving, self-giving in cleansing, and
cleansing in the final presentation of the church. The intermediate purification and the eventual
presentation concern the same people for whom he gives himself. (Ephesians 5:25–27; Titus 2:14)
    [195] Barnabas joins redemption from darkness to the preparation of a holy people, while elsewhere
describing repentance as a gift. Holiness enters the saving account as an effect produced within its
recipients. The proposed covenant must therefore disclose whether its provision embraces the change
through which those recipients fulfill its requirements. Exclude that change, and an indispensable saving
operation has been assigned to a source for which the undertaking makes no provision. The condition
remains necessary. The account has removed the means sufficient to secure it. (Epistle of Barnabas 14;
16)
    [196] Sixthly, Christ himself determines the object, design, and end of his death, “For their sakes I
sanctify myself, that they also might be sanctified through the truth.” “For their sakes.” The persons in
view are “The men whom you have given me out of the world,” the persons for whom he prays, as
distinguished from the world excluded from that prayer. His declaration “I sanctify myself.” concerns his
appointment “To the work I am now going about, even to be an oblation,” and its end is expressed as Ἵνα

καὶ αὐτοὶ ὦσιν ἡγιασμένοι ἐν ἀληθείᾳ·—“That they also may be truly sanctified.” The ἵνα, “that they,”
identifies intention and purpose — their sanctification is the end toward which he acts. Its
accomplishment constitutes our hope, the hope of the gospel (“for the Deliverer that comes out of Sion
turns away ungodliness from Jacob,”). The Father’s will concurs with this purpose, for its fulfillment is
the Father’s will which Christ came to do. The referent of their sakes remains continuous throughout —
those represented in his offering are those whose personal sanctification that offering intends to secure.
(John 17:6, 9, 19; Romans 11:26)
    [197] Seventhly, the counsel appears in the declaration that the Lord Jesus “gave himself for our sins,
that he might deliver us from this present evil world, according to the will of God and our Father,” further
unfolded by the apostle, “God sent forth his Son, made of a woman, made under the law, to redeem them
that were under the law, that we might receive the adoption of sons.” Sonship includes deliverance from
the law and thus from sin’s guilt. Adoption, the reception of the Spirit, and approach to God all belong to
the Father’s purpose in giving his only Son for us. These remain distinguishable benefits, each with its
appointed execution in time, while the purpose which orders their whole provision embraces each of
them. (Galatians 1:4; 4:4–6)
    [198] Eighthly, one further passage suffices from the many available, “He has made him to be sin for
us, who knew no sin, that we might be made the righteousness of God in him.” God’s making his Son sin
intends that those represented in that act become righteousness in him. The Father sends and Christ
willingly becomes sin toward the same end. To suppose that God purposed what he knew would never
occur, or that he intended its occurrence while providing no operation through which it would occur,
introduces atheistical assertions concerning his counsel. Christ was therefore made sin for those who
actually become righteousness in him. These passages establish the Father’s counsel and the Son’s
corresponding intention to fulfill it through oblation and bloodshed. The representative exchange
concerns a changed standing in the represented persons, and its reasoning requires that both their identity
and the representative’s own innocence remain determinate throughout. (2 Corinthians 5:21)
   [199] The argument follows from the conjunction of intention and efficacy. Whatever the Father and
Son intend Christ’s death to accomplish in every person for whom he dies is certainly accomplished in
those persons. The divine efficacy asserted in this premise admits full defence with the Lord’s assistance.
The scriptural passages have established the intended content — redemption, purging, sanctification,
purification, release from death, Satan, and the law’s curse, freedom from all sin, righteousness in Christ,
and nearness to God. Christ consequently dies for all and only those in whom these goods become actual.
Whether every individual receives them is a question anyone acquainted with these things can judge. The
conclusion depends jointly upon the efficacy of the divine purpose and the determinate effects which
constitute its content. Enlarging its class of recipients necessarily enlarges the class in whom those effects
must be accomplished.
     [200] The inference from intention stands upon two premises, each of which does work the other
cannot do. The efficacy of the intention is a theological proposition grounded in divine fidelity, while the
content of the intention is an exegetical proposition established by the preceding passages. Those
passages identify a people whom Christ saves, cleanses, and brings to God. It is this particular end, joined
to the fidelity with which God executes it, that carries the inference.
        [201] ∀x [R(x)⇒Intend(d,S(x))]∀x [Intend(d,S(x))⇒S(x)]⇒ ∀x [R(x)⇒S(x)]. (20)
     [202] Let d denote the divine saving purpose under consideration. The first premise specifies what
God intends for those whom Christ represents, namely their salvation. The second asserts the efficacy of
that intention on the theological grounds already given. The final expression gives the consequence of
taking those premises together.
    [203] The extent of the undertaking follows once both premises have been defended. Since some
persons are never saved, R cannot extend to every person in the sense established here. This conclusion

arises from what the undertaking intends to secure and from its efficacy in securing it. The definition of
the persons represented supplies neither premise by itself.
    [204] The second class of passages identifies what the offering actually accomplishes in those for
whom it is made. In “By his own blood he entered in once into the holy place, having obtained eternal
redemption for us. … The blood of Christ, who through the eternal Spirit offered himself without spot to
God, purge your consciences from dead works to serve the living God.” the blood of Christ produces an
effect with respect to God, “It obtains eternal redemption;” and an effect with respect to us, “It purges our
consciences from dead works.” The offering which Christ presents to God through the eternal Spirit, and
by whose blood he enters the holy place, thus procures eternal redemption from the guilt of our sins and
the wrath they deserve. From the same offering proceeds sanctification within us, here called a “purging
our sins.” These effects belong to the blood itself, the one as justification before God and the other as a
purification in the persons justified. Scripture assigns the meritorious purging of sin to the offering Christ
made before his ascension. “When he had by himself purged our sins, he sat down on the right hand of the
Majesty on high.” It speaks more expressly still in “He has appeared to put away sin by the sacrifice of
himself.” The putting away of sin by sacrifice therefore involves the actual sanctification of those for
whom the sacrifice is made, even as “the blood of bulls and goats, and the ashes of an heifer sprinkling
the unclean, sanctifies to the purifying of the flesh.” The legal ordinances make the force of this relation
clear. Under the carnal ordinances “which had a shadow of good things to come,” the polluted or guilty
person for whom an expiation was authorized obtained two actual benefits. The sacrifice cleansed him
legally to the purification of the flesh, and it released him from the punishment which a breach of the law,
as the appointed rule of life for God’s people, would otherwise have incurred. The sacrifice was carnal,
but within that order it accomplished both benefits for the person admitted to it. Since those ordinances
were “shadows of good things to come,” their spiritual fulfillment in Christ must accomplish for every
person represented what they could only prefigure. Those for whom Christ offers himself receive spiritual
cleansing in sanctification and release from the guilt of sin. The question of extent therefore comes down
to whether these effects are accomplished in all persons and for them all. Obtaining and purging designate
operations whose benefits are secured by the offering. An announcement can describe the same benefits
while leaving their actual production unprovided for, and would therefore assert something different from
what these passages say the blood does. (Hebrews 9:12–14, 26; 1:3)
    [205] Melito’s declaration that “the death of the sheep became the life of the people,” is borne out by
his later account of the changes Christ effects in delivering his people. Deliverance names an operation
whose recipients come to stand in a different relation to the bondage from which they are released. The
possibility of their release would leave that relation open and would therefore fail to express the effect
Melito attributes to Christ. The point in dispute is whether the offering actually secures the saving good
assigned to it and who stands within the undertaking that secures it. (Melito of Sardis, On Pascha 31; 68)
     [206] Christ’s death is the act in which he is said to “bear our sins”, “His own self bore our sins.” The
passage identifies the act in “Bore our sins” (ἀνήνεγκε, he carried them up with him upon the cross),
gives its intended end in “That we being dead unto sins, should live unto righteousness.” and then names
its effect in “By his stripes we are healed.” This last expression comes from the passage of the prophet in
which the Savior is said to “bear our iniquities, and to have them laid upon him” and explains what Christ
accomplished by “bearing our sins;” a form of speech Scripture uses more than once for the same
operation. To bear our iniquities in this sense is to procure our release by undergoing in our place the
stripes and afflictions which belonged to them. What he suffered in offering himself for us is the ground
on which we go free, since those for whom the suffering was borne are thereby delivered from the
liability to undergo it themselves. Scripture repeatedly puts Christ and his people in this relation of
exchange with respect to suffering. “He delivered us from the curse of the law, being made a curse for
us.” Further instances will appear in the subsequent argument. The judicial character of the office makes
the consequences of the suffering intelligible. The one who undergoes it does so as the representative of

others, whose release is the fruit of his bearing the liability that was theirs. (1 Peter 2:24; Isaiah 53:5–6,
10–12; Galatians 3:13)
    [207] Peace and reconciliation with God are effects of the same offering. Peace in this relation
consists in the removal of the enmity on both sides and of the causes which sustain it. Scripture attributes
this actual peace to the oblation in “And you, that were sometime alienated and enemies in your mind by
wicked works, yet now has he reconciled in the body of his flesh through death, to present you holy and
unblamable and unreprovable in his sight”, “You who sometimes were far off are made nigh by the blood
of Christ: for he is our peace; having abolished in his flesh the enmity, even the law of commandments,
that he might reconcile both unto God in one body by the cross, having slain the enmity thereby.” The
passages which trace full deliverance from anger, wrath, death, and the one who holds the power of death
to the same act give further particulars of what the offering produces. Every impediment that held the
church at enmity and kept it from the enjoyment of God’s love and favor is included in the redemption
Christ effects for it “with his own blood.” For that reason every person for whom he died is entitled to say
“Who shall lay any thing to our charge? It is God that justifies. Who is he that condemns? It is Christ that
died, yea rather, that is risen again, who is even at the right hand of God, who also makes intercession for
us.” The rejoicing expressed there is the assurance of persons actually reconciled, and no evidence is
given that every child of Adam has been placed in a condition that would warrant it. That all for whom
Christ died receive these effects, by contrast, follows from the causal connection Scripture gives between
his being slain and what takes place in them “he redeemed them to God by his blood, out of every
kindred, and tongue, and people, and nation; and made them unto our God kings and priests” and from its
explanation “he made an end of their sins, he made reconciliation for their iniquity, and brought in
everlasting righteousness.” An account in which the same grounds of enmity remain in force and the
persons involved are only offered peace cannot express the reconciliation these passages attribute to his
death. It is the removal of those grounds through the sacrifice appointed to remove them which changes
the relation itself. (Colossians 1:21–22; Ephesians 2:13–16; Romans 5:8–10; 8:33–34; Acts 20:28;
Revelation 5:9–10; Daniel 9:24)
     [208] The same connection holds in the passages that attribute our life to Christ’s death. He “came
down from heaven to give life to the world.” The world for which he gives his life consequently receives
life from him. It is the world of “his sheep, for which he lays down his life” for whom he acts so that he
might “give unto them eternal life, that they might never perish.” and so he appeared “to abolish death,
and to bring life and immortality to light.” Life belongs here to the persons in whom the appointed
communication takes effect. Whatever account is given of that communication must therefore contain the
operation through which life becomes actual in them, since that is the very good the passages attribute to
his death. (John 6:33; 10:15, 28; 2 Timothy 1:10; Romans 5:6–10)
    [209] Each of these passages can independently be urged against a general ransom or a universal
extent of Christ’s merit. Their common argument can be given without taking up each separately. If the
death of Christ as a sacrifice to his Father sanctifies those for whom he offers himself, purges their sins,
redeems them from wrath, curse, and guilt, makes peace and reconciliation for them, and procures life and
immortality by bearing their iniquities and healing their diseases, then those for whom he dies are those
who in the event are sanctified, purged, redeemed, justified, released from wrath and death, given life, and
saved. The passages already considered establish the efficacy asserted in the premise, and Scripture and
experience make plain that these effects are absent in some persons. The persons who lack them cannot
therefore be the proper objects of the death to which these effects belong. Any one of the saving effects
would suffice to test the universal claim. Once an effect is secured for everyone within a proposed
domain, anyone in whom it fails to obtain stands outside that domain.
   [210] The third class of passages describes the persons whom God appoints to be the particular
objects of Christ’s redemption. They are called many in “The blood of the new testament is shed for
many, for the remission of sins.” “By his knowledge shall my righteous servant justify many, for he shall
bear their iniquities.” “The Son of man came not to be ministered unto, but to minister, and give his life a

ransom for many.” and Christ was to “bring many sons unto glory;” and accordingly to be the “captain of
their salvation, through sufferings.” The word many can on occasion include all and would therefore be
insufficient by itself to determine the extent of the death. The accompanying descriptions identify the
many by relations which do not obtain in everyone. They are the “sheep” of Christ and the “children of
God that were scattered abroad” and are the persons whom the Savior calls “brethren.” They are “the
children that God gave him,” which were “partakers of flesh and blood” and are repeatedly called “those
who were given unto him of his Father”, whose preservation is certain. They are the “sheep” of which he
was the “Shepherd, through the blood of the everlasting covenant”, his “elect” and his “people” which
Scripture further explains to be his “visited and redeemed people.” They are the people he “foreknew” in
the same sense in which he already had a people at Corinth before their conversion, namely as his people
by election, the people he “suffered for without the gate, that he might sanctify them.” They are his
“church, which he redeemed by his own blood”, which “he loved and gave himself for” and the “many”
whose sins he took away and with whom he made a covenant. These descriptions disclose the same
persons under several determinate relations. They belong to the Savior as his people, stand under his
keeping as his sheep, and receive the benefits of his offering as the church for which he offers it. Since the
relations belong to the elect and are absent in those who are not elected, the many so described are all
those and only those whom God chooses to receive eternal life through the offering and bloodshed of
Jesus Christ. It is the conjunction of the descriptions, in the relations under which Scripture employs
them, that determines the extent. The objections made with such confidence against this conclusion do not
undo that conjunction and can be met on their own terms. (Matthew 26:28; 20:28; 1:21; Isaiah 53:11;
Mark 10:45; Hebrews 2:10–11, 13–14; 13:12, 20; 9:28; Romans 5:19; 8:33; 11:2; John 10:15; 11:52;
17:2, 6, 9, 11; Luke 1:68; Acts 18:10; 20:28; Ephesians 5:25; Daniel 9:27)
    [211] The argument from Christ’s death for “many,” his “sheep,” his “elect,” and the other
descriptions requires an answer to what these descriptions establish in combination. The author calls it a
“reason,” and describes it as “weak and of no force, equivocal, subtile, fraudulent, false, ungodly,
deceitful, and erroneous;” multiplying epithets for the purpose (“Universality of Free Grace,” page xvi.).
The resulting copia verborum displays the abundance of his unlearned eloquence while doing little for the
cause which requires so formidable a name for an argument he cannot readily answer. When the Pharisees
could not resist the spirit by which the Savior spoke, they called him “devil and Samaritan.” The waters
that make the greatest noise are often shallow, and the Scythian proverb has it that the “dogs which bark
most bite least.” The force of the reply is best judged by hearing “quid dignum tanto feret hic responsor
hiatu,” in his own words. An answer to a term taken in isolation cannot meet an inference that depends
upon the description attached to it and upon the efficacy asserted of the death.
    [212] “First, This reason is weak and of no force: for the word many is oft so used, that it both
signifies all and every man, and also amplifies or sets forth the greatness of that number; as in the cited
passage, and in other places, where many cannot, nor is by any Christian understood for less than all
men.” To establish that many sometimes includes all is to establish a possibility of usage. The persons
under discussion and the predicates by which the passage identifies them decide whether that possibility
is realized in the passage at issue. (Daniel 12:2; Romans 5:19)
     [213] The exception would have some force if the argument proceeded from the word many alone. It
proceeds from the description of those many together with the distinction among persons already
established by God’s purpose, for which the arguments that follow furnish further grounds. Suppose a
man divides the inhabitants of London into rich and poor, those who have abundance and those who are
in want, and then declares that he will distribute his bounty to many in London, namely to the poor and
those in want. The description fixes the recipients even though the word many, considered by itself,
would not do so. Neither passage adduced in objection directly establishes that the word must there be
taken for all. In the first, the word is distributed to the two parts of the affirmation. The dead rise, many to
life and many to shame, as the sense would naturally be expressed in another language. Such Hebraisms
are ordinary. Many may also be said to rise to life because, as the apostle says, “All shall not die.” The

corresponding point applies to the other passage. Even if the many there appear to include all, the
expression was not chosen for the purpose of designating all, “with an amplification” which in any case is
unlikely to bear a relation of many to all. The comparison concerns what Adam’s disobedience and
Christ’s righteousness respectively effect and how death and life are communicated from each. The
numbers of those who die through the first and live through the second are not set against one another,
since the modes of participation and their effects are the terms of the comparison. The author promises
further instances, although the passages he has already produced are the ones ordinarily urged by the
Arminians in this dispute. Even if additional examples could be produced, they would not affect the
argument. It is the attached account of the persons, including their relation to the Shepherd, that yields the
restriction. A further instance of broad cardinal usage would still leave that account intact. (Daniel 12:2;
Romans 5:19)
    [214] “Secondly, This reason,” he adds, “is equivocal, subtile, and fraudulent; seeing where all men
and every man is affirmed of, the death of Christ, as the ransom and propitiation, and the fruits thereof,
only is assumed for them; but where the word many is in any place used in this business, there are more
ends of the death of Christ than this one affirmed of.” The proposed reply assumes the very extent of the
quantifier that is under examination. Consequences belonging to a universal reading cannot displace the
restricted account until the universal reading itself has been established.
    [215] The reply begins by treating it as conceded that Scripture declares the death of Christ to be for
“all men” or for “every man;” There is no such concession, and the proposition cannot enter as a premise
while the passages cited for it remain in dispute. The further claim that Christ intends an end of his death
apart from the fruit of his ransom and propitiation is false. Something may follow the death incidentally,
but a directly intended end of paying the ransom and making the atonement is the fruit, effect, or product
of that act. The distinction that assigns the ransom and propitiation with their fruits to all, and confines the
other ends of the death to the many, is therefore an assertion of remarkable equivocation, subtlety, and
fraud, even on the most intelligible construction available for words which in themselves bear no tolerable
sense. The observation on which it rests, that the passages concerning many give several ends whereas
those concerning all name only ransom, fails in three respects. First, it concedes the argument by granting
that many cannot mean all where ends are assigned to the many which do not belong to all. The other
answers which try to make many denote all consequently run against the author's own concession.
Second, the distinction is idle unless some end of Christ’s death can be shown to lie outside the fruit of
his ransom, which the author does not establish. Third, the alleged distinction is false on the face of the
wording. Where Christ’s death is spoken of as for many, he is said to “give his life a ransom” for them,
the same words used where he is said to die for all. The choice of expression cannot ground an
observation which requires a difference between the expressions. The tenth chapter of the same work
proceeds in much the same way, asserting throughout that those for whom redemption is procured by the
oblation are invariably spoken of in the third person, by “all the world,” or similar expressions. The
author's own book elsewhere adduces, in support of general redemption, passages which speak of those
for whom Christ suffers in the first or second person. The universal scope claimed for the texts needs
positive interpretive grounds. It cannot be secured by assuming it at the outset or by making distinctions
the language of the texts does not observe. (Thomas More, The Universality of God’s Free Grace 1;
Matthew 20:28; 1 Timothy 2:6; 1 Peter 2:24; 3:18; Isaiah 53:5–6; 1 Corinthians 15:3; Galatians 3:13)
   [216] The third objection is “This reason is false and ungodly; for it is nowhere in Scripture said that
Christ died or gave himself a ransom but for many, or only for many, or only for his sheep; and it is
ungodliness to add to or diminish from the word of God in Scripture.” Whether a conclusion is exclusive
depends on what the premises together establish. Their conjunction may exclude the contrary case even
though no sentence taken alone contains the word only.
   [217] Christ declares that he gives his life for “many,” for his “sheep,” and is said to die for his
“church.” Scripture also establishes in innumerable places that there are persons who are neither his sheep
nor his church. Taken together, these propositions support the consequence that he did not die for persons

who stand outside those relations. If bringing out a consequence contained in the word of God amounts to
adding to it, no one can speak from it without incurring the same charge. The point is still clearer in the
very passage where the Savior says that he “gave his life for his sheep,” and immediately adds that some
are not of his sheep. He distinguishes the sheep for whom he gives his life from others in the course of the
same declaration, which is equivalent for the present purpose to saying his sheep only. A reciprocal
accusation would be easy, but the restriction stands on the combined account of the persons represented
and the effects secured for them. These are the premises against which its consequence is to be judged.
(John 10:26)
    [218] A proved implication can exclude a case without any premise expressly containing the word
only. Where membership in one class entails an effect, and persons in another class lack that effect, those
persons cannot belong to the class to which the implication applies. The exclusion follows from the
implication together with the actual absence of its consequent.
                               [219] R⊆S,     N∩S=⌀       ⇒ R∩N=⌀. (21)
    [220] R denotes the class represented in the saving undertaking, S those finally saved, and N those
finally not saved. The inclusion follows from the efficacy already defended. The disjointness expresses
the opposition between the final outcomes and requires no assertion that observers can now identify every
person to whom either outcome belongs.
    [221] Anyone who keeps the premises and denies the restriction must produce a person who is saved
and finally not saved in the same respect. To avoid that consequence he must challenge the theological
grounds of the inclusion or alter the saving predicate. Either course locates the dispute in a premise which
can be examined in its own right.
    [222] The fourth objection is given in these words. “But,” the author continues, “the reason is
deceitful and erroneous, for the Scripture does nowhere say, — 2. a 2Those many he died for are his
sheep (much less his elect, as the reason intends it). As for the place, usually instanced to this end, it is
therein much abused: for our Savior, did not set forth the difference between such as he died for and such
as he died not for, bor such as he died for so and so, and not so and so; cbut the difference between those
that believe on him and those who believe not on him. One hear his voice and follow him, the other not.
dNor did our Savior here set forth the privileges of all he died for, or for whom he died so and so, but of
those that believe on him through the ministration of the gospel, and so do know him, and approach to
God, and enter the kingdom by him. eNor was our Savior here setting forth the excellency of those for
whom he died, or died for so only, wherein they are preferred before others; but the excellency of his own
love, with the fruits thereof to those not only that he died for, but also that are brought in by his
ministration to believe on him. fNor was our Savior here treating so much of his ransom-giving and
propitiationmaking as of his ministration of the gospel, and so of his love and faithfulness therein;
wherein he laid down his life for those ministered to, and therein gave us example, not to make
propitiation for sin, but to testify love in suffering.” The objection treats the identity of the persons in
question as if it were settled by the time at which they receive the benefits of the relation. A person can be
designated under an appointment before he possesses every effect which that appointment secures for
him. (John 10:3–5, 9, 11, 14–15, 26–27; 10)
    [223] Owen judged that a reader acquainted with the conditions of his time would understand why it
was necessary to spend time examining and reproducing language of this kind. The answer has to be
judged as it stands, since its author affords no better. Its expressions do not fit one another, and their
arrangement obscures the relations on which the question turns. This obscurity does the work an argument
ought to do. It hides the issue from readers already liable to be seduced by it, occupying their attention
with the words while keeping the matter they profess to explain out of view. The argument the author
undertakes to answer asserts that Christ dies for “many,” and that the many for whom he dies are
described and designated as his “sheep.” The expressions just reproduced yield no proposition answering
either assertion or the consequence which follows from them together. The difference between the force

of this one argument and the confusion of the reply would justify passing over the whole evasion once
that difference had been brought to the reader’s attention. To remove whatever appearance of substance
the reply may retain, its points can nevertheless be examined under the marks already assigned to them,
leaving the complete vindication of the passage to the ensuing positive argument. A distinction
contributes a premise only if it identifies a difference the text actually makes and specifies the relation in
which the difference obtains. (John 10)
    [224] The first point, marked a, concerns the many for whom Christ dies, who have already been
shown to be his sheep. The language of the passage is used in its proper sense when the Savior
distinguishes those for whom he would die as his “sheep”, the persons to whom he would “give eternal
life” and those “given him by his Father.” These descriptions distinguish them from persons who bear no
such relation to him. Whether the immediate occasion of his speech gives priority to this distinction is
immaterial. The argument proceeds from the truth expressed in the words by which he accomplishes the
end he does have in view, the consolation of believers. It is a distinction attached to his act as Shepherd
and to the relation in which his sheep stand to him, which a difference in their outward appearance at a
given time could not supply. (John 10; 10:15, 28; 17:9)
    [225] The second point, marked b, concerns the alleged difference between those he “died for so and
so,” and those he “died for so and so,” We agree that Christ makes no such difference, since the
qualification “so and so” specifies nothing corresponding to a divine purpose or to the Savior’s intention
in this act. He dies in the same manner and toward the same end for those for whom he dies. Anyone
proposing a further saving relation must say in what manner or benefit the death differs under it.
    [226] The third point, marked c, concerns the ground of the distinction. The primary division the
Savior makes is between elect and non-elect, his sheep and those who are not his sheep. It becomes
manifest in the believing of the first, here called “hearing of his voice and knowing him,” and the unbelief
of the others. That difference in their acts has its foundation in their different standing with respect to
God’s purpose and Christ’s love, as the opposition in the cited passage shows, “You believe not, because
you are not of my sheep,” and “My sheep hear my voice.” Hearing here involves the obedience of faith,
and the passage traces the difference in hearing to the prior distinction in condition. Some hear because
they are the sheep whom Christ loves and for whom he gives his life. The rest fail to hear because they
are not his sheep. Election, vocation, and actual believing thus have distinct places in the explanation, and
the order in which their effects become observable cannot determine the order of their dependence. (John
10:26–27)
    [227] Ignatius warns against those who are not “the planting of the Father.” The relation expressed by
planting concerns the source of a condition which only afterward becomes visible in fruit. In the Savior’s
words now under examination, the same question of dependence governs what it means that the sheep
hear. Their hearing reveals a relation whose stated foundation is that they are his sheep. To make the
hearing constitute that relation would reverse the explanation, and such a reversal cannot follow from the
fact that hearing is what we observe. It would need to be established from the passage itself. The spokes
have their place by their relation to the Hub, as in paragraph 0. So here the sheep hear because they are
his, and their hearing makes known whose sheep they are. (Ignatius, Epistle to the Philadelphians 3; John
10:26–27)
    [228] The fourth point, marked d, raises privileges which the Savior does indeed express but which do
not answer the present question, namely for whom he gives his life. The repeated and undefined so and so
merely perplexes the reader who is least able to supply what the terms leave unspecified. Christ dies only
for those whom the ministration of the gospel will certainly bring to him. There is therefore no “Not only
those whom he died for, but also those that are brought in unto him;” since those for whom he dies are his
sheep and his sheep hear his voice. One can describe the same persons by the death undertaken for them
or by their subsequent entrance into his fold. Their privileges concern the exercise of the relation for

which he undertakes the act of representation, and the saving provision already established connects those
descriptions without dividing their subjects.
    [229] The fifth point, marked e, concerns the difference between the occasion on which the Savior
mentions his death and the persons for whom he offers it. He gives the latter question an express answer
when he calls them his “sheep,” a description which does not extend to all. He intends to declare the
giving of his life for a ransom, in accordance with the “commandment received of his Father.” What
brings him to speak of that giving and whom it concerns are separate subjects, and an answer about the
occasion cannot by itself determine the persons represented. (John 10:18)
    [230] The sixth point, marked f, concerns “The love and faithfulness of Jesus Christ in the
ministration of the gospel,” which is his performance of the office of mediator of the new covenant. Its
character is nowhere more plainly seen than in his giving his life for a ransom. In laying down his life he
also gave us an “example,” but nothing in this passage employs the act for that purpose. The long
discussion therefore mistakes both the text and the question put to it, a difficulty the author may have
recognized when he added the further evasions that follow. The Shepherd’s faithfulness consists in
exercising his office effectually for his sheep. What that exercise produces in them shows how the
intention belonging to his office is fulfilled. (John 15:13)
    [231] “Besides,” the author says, “the opposition appears here to be not so much between elect and
not elect, as between Jews called and Gentiles uncalled.” The proposed division makes ethnic
membership determine what the passage predicates of sheep. The test is whether it can account for the
persons who already bear that relation while remaining outside the gathered community.
    [232] The opposition between sheep and those who are not sheep concerns election. If the “not sheep”
are taken to be Gentiles on the ground that they had not yet been called, the passage defeats the reading
by naming them sheep in designation before their call. If the Jews are identified as the sheep on the
ground that they had already been called outwardly, the reading again fails, since many Jews were not
sheep. This is a foul corruption of the word of God employed to evade the force of the distinction, and no
small provocation to his glory. The standing of the sheep under the appointment precedes the historical
call, so absence from the fold at the time of speaking does not prove permanent exclusion. (John 10:16,
26)
    [233] The author adds “Besides, there is in Scripture great difference between sheep, and sheep of his
flock and pasture, of which he here speaks.” To distinguish sheep from gathered sheep, one must explain
what gathering does to those whom it concerns. A further act can change their condition in time while its
recipients remain the persons to whom the original description applied. (John 10:4–5, 11, 15–16)
    [234] If anyone can make this unrighteous distinction intelligible, its supposed contribution may then
be examined. The only distinction the passage can sustain is that the “sheep” simply so called belong to
Christ by the Father’s donation, while the “sheep of his pasture,” have already been brought home to
Christ by the effectual working of the Spirit. The chapter mentions both under these respective conditions.
Together they constitute the sheep for whom he gave his life and to whom he gives life. A legitimate
distinction preserves the passage from designation to gathering in the same persons. It cannot displace the
people concerned by introducing another population to which the gathering has no such relation. (John
10:16, 27)
    [235] The next reply is “Besides, sheep, the cited passage are not mentioned as all those for whom he
died, but as those who by his ministration are brought in to believe and enjoy the benefit of his death, and
to whom he ministers and communicates spirit.” This interpretation derives the whole meaning of the
prior description from what the ministration subsequently effects. It leaves unexplained why the passage
gives that description to persons whom the ministration has yet to gather. (John 10:4–5, 11, 15)
    [236] The substance of this exception and those like it is that sheep means believers. The passage
already calls some sheep whom Christ has yet to gather into his fold, so their present believing cannot

supply the meaning of the description. The further claim that he does not mention his sheep as the persons
for whom he dies contradicts the terms of “I lay down my life for my sheep.” The relation between those
for whom he died and those whom he brings in by his Spirit is the same as the relation between Peter,
James, and John and the three apostles who accompanied the Savior on the mount of transfiguration. The
descriptions differ while the persons are the same. To insert the disputed opinion in place of an answer
and then assume the distinction on which that opinion depends is childish sophistry. Being brought in to
believe and to enjoy the benefit of his death is itself a special fruit of the death. Every person for whom
Christ dies will certainly receive it, since apart from it his death would do that person no good. The
appointment and its efficacy have priority, and the entrance of the believer is the temporal consequence of
their ordered execution. (John 10:15–16)
   [237] The appointment of a person to a saving relation and his enjoyment of its effects require distinct
temporal indices. Gathering in the future concerns the very person who is absent from the fold now,
whose present absence leaves in force the appointment that secures his entrance. An observation about his
condition at one time cannot by itself give a complete account of the purpose ordering the successive
conditions of his history.
                                [238] E(x)∧¬F(x,t0)∧∃t1>t0 F(x,t1). (22)
    [239] E(x) records the person’s election in the scriptural sense. The temporal predicates record that he
is now in unbelief and later believes. Election gives the purpose governing his history, while the other
predicates name conditions that succeed one another within it. There is accordingly a possible history of
one elect person in which all three predicates hold.
    [240] Particular appointment can therefore obtain throughout a period in which its saving fruit has yet
to be received. Contradiction would require that the same condition be affirmed and denied at the same
time and in the same respect. The subsequent account of wrath, justification, and assurance depends upon
keeping these temporal and relational differences in view.
    [241] “Besides, here are more ends of his death mentioned than ransom or propitiation only, and yet it
is not said, ‘Only for his sheep;’ and when the ransom or propitiation only is mentioned, it is said, ‘For all
men.’ So that this reason appears weak, fraudulent, ungodly, and erroneous.” A sacrifice can have several
effects in the same persons. The proposed enlargement of its extent therefore requires proof that its
benefits have different recipients, since the difference among the benefits leaves that question open.
    [242] Everything said here about Christ’s death concerns what he accomplishes as a propitiation and
ransom for us, including the fruits that follow certainly and infallibly from it. If the passage does give
several ends and some of them do not belong to all, the author thereby provides his own reason to
understand Christ as speaking of his sheep only. The truth is nearer than the objection allows. There is no
identified passage saying “Of all men,” whereas Christ is expressly said to “give his life a ransom,” and
this is mentioned only where it is not said to be for all. Ransom, propitiation, and the keeping of the sheep
belong to a connected saving office. The good secured by the one undertaking is ascertained by following
how these operations belong together. (Matthew 20:28; Mark 10:45)
   [243] The reader can judge from the grounds now given whether the argument or the exceptions
deserve to be called “weak, fraudulent, ungodly, and erroneous.” A sufficient reply would have to specify
which causal connection fails or which identification of the recipients the evidence does not support. The
exceptions leave both in force.
    [244] The discourse immediately following these exceptions calls for notice, even at the risk of taxing
the reader’s patience, because the author proceeds to exhibit his skill against a figure of his own
construction. He invents another objection and attributes it to the opponents of universal redemption, as
though they had urged it against his interpretation of the general expressions in Scripture. The objection
he puts into their mouths is “That those words were fitted for the time of Christ and his apostles, having
another meaning in them than they seem to import.” None of his opponents contributed a penful of ink to

the construction of this figure. Once it has been set up and suitably dressed, he brings against it charges of
error, blasphemy, and falsehood, and follows these with exclamations and vehement outcries until it falls.
The achievement is more intelligible when one notices that he was able to make the objection no stronger
than he could readily demolish. It is a poor show of judgment in those who admire his prowess when the
antagonist with whom he fences is his own shadow. Much of Mr More’s book consists of exercises of this
kind, proving what his opponents do not deny and answering what they do not object. The actual
controversy cannot be carried on by making one party change the terms of the other party’s position.
When a new description is proposed, its effect can be judged only by retaining the operations and
recipients already established and asking whether the new terms alter either of them on grounds the
argument can support.
    [245] Further reasons confirming the preceding account belong to the positive arguments against the
general ransom. The immediate difficulty is the answer commonly given to the passages already
produced, the φάρμακον πάνσοφον by which our adversaries suppose they can bear the whole weight of
the evidence. Its distinction is to be judged within the complete saving undertaking. The fact that two
descriptions can be conceived separately does not establish that the corresponding benefits are finally
separated in the persons whom they concern.
    [246] The proposed distinction concerns the goods Christ procures by his offering. Impetration is his
obtaining them, and application is their actual bestowal upon particular persons. “The first,” the
opponents maintain, “is general, in respect to all. Christ obtained and procured all good things by his
death of his Father, — reconciliation, redemption, forgiveness of sins, — for all and every man in the
world, if they will believe and lay hold upon him: but in respect of application, they are actually bestowed
and conferred but on a few; because but a few believe, which is the condition on which they are
