bestowed. And in this latter sense are the texts of Scripture which we have argued, all of them, to be
understood. So that they do no whit impeach the universality of merit, which they assert; but only the
universality of application, which they also deny.” They vary the terms in which they give the answer
according to the other opinions each wishes to support, but the causal question survives these changes in
expression. They assign procurement to everyone and enjoyment to some. The difference in the latter
therefore depends upon a condition whose occurrence the account must explain. Until it determines what
produces that condition, the fact that the benefits are actually enjoyed by some and never by others has
merely been restated under the distinction.
    [247] The Arminians hold that by his death and passion Christ, in accordance with God’s intention,
absolutely purchases for each person for whom he dies forgiveness and reconciliation with God, or
restoration to a state of grace and favor. Each person actually receives the advantage of this purchase
provided that he believes. The purchase is thus said to be universal, with enjoyment restricted by the
condition of faith. The account is complete only if it explains whether Christ’s purchase also supplies the
condition on which its benefits become actual in those who receive them.
    [248] Camero and the French divines who follow the new method he devised also say that Christ dies
for all. With respect to some he does so conditionally, if they believe or will to believe, though he knows
they have no power to do it of themselves. For his own he dies absolutely, intending to bestow upon them
the faith and grace through which they actually possess the purchased benefits. If there are two intentions,
each must be specified by the good it provides and by the persons for whom it provides it. Consequences
which follow from the absolute undertaking cannot be transferred to the general one on the strength of the
shared description that Christ dies for persons. (Owen, The Death of Death II.4, note naming Cameron,
Testardus, and Amyraut)
    [249] Another account distinguishes two forms of reconciliation and redemption. The first is wrought
by Christ with God on behalf of every person. The second is wrought by Christ in persons, so that they
actually enter into peace with God. These are distinguishable relations, one with respect to God and the
other with respect to the person changed. The issue is the appointed connection by which both effects are

accomplished for those whom the undertaking concerns. (Thomas More, The Universality of God’s Free
Grace, as cited by Owen)
    [250] Other formulations vary the terms while resting their weight upon the same distinction. In
impetration Christ is said to obtain redemption and reconciliation for all, while in application these goods
are bestowed upon those who believe and continue in faith. The common account therefore couples
universality in procurement with restriction in application. An explanation of why the application is
restricted must specify the source of the condition that intervenes between the two and show how that
condition is brought to effect.
    [251] The grounds alleged for the general ransom and universal reconciliation are a separate question
and will be examined afterward. The present question is the meaning of the distinction and whether the
use made of it follows from what it means. The relation of the terms needs to be settled before either term
is used to establish how many persons fall under it.
    [252] The distinction has a sound use, whether it is expressed as impetration and application or as
procuring reconciliation with God and working reconciliation in us. Impetration is the meritorious
purchase by which Christ obtains every good for us with and from his Father. Application is our actual
enjoyment of those goods upon believing. When a man pays a price for the release of captives, the
payment corresponds to impetration and the setting of the captives free corresponds to application. The
price can be paid before the captive enjoys his release, so the operations have distinct places in his
history. Their unity lies in the secured relation between the good procured and the person to whom it
actually comes through the appointed application.
    [253] The distinction obtains in the things procured and their reception, while both operations are
united in Christ’s intention. His full end in acting is our deliverance from evil and the actual bestowal of
every good which he procures. The goods can accordingly be considered as purchased by him or as
bestowed on us. Their eventual bestowal belongs to his purpose as the end for which they are purchased,
and the difference in time and operation supplies no ground for assigning him an independent intention to
procure them without applying them.
    [254] God’s will in this undertaking is absolute. He does not give Christ to obtain peace,
reconciliation, and forgiveness on the supposition that we may believe. He wills that the goods be
procured and bestowed, while the order of their bestowal includes conditions among the things
themselves. The absolute purpose can therefore provide that a condition required in execution will
certainly occur, so conditional enjoyment and certain provision of its condition are compatible.
    [255] The testimonies about given faith and given repentance make the distinction established in
paragraphs 171–195 especially plain. The recipient may be required to act in the execution of a purpose
whose provision extends to the occurrence of that very act. Describing his enjoyment as conditional
therefore leaves open whether the condition itself is provided. Our adversaries must show that God’s
undertaking excludes the condition from the gifts he provides. Otherwise their distinction describes a
necessary relation among goods whose communication in that order remains certain. Return to the spokes.
The faith by which they receive the benefit belongs to the provision by which the Hub brings them into its
enjoyment. (Polycarp, Epistle to the Philippians 3; Epistle of Barnabas 16)
    [256] Some of the things Christ obtains are bestowed absolutely, and where a benefit is bestowed
upon a condition, the condition itself is obtained without a further condition and is bestowed by virtue of
the purchase. Forgiveness of sins and eternal life are purchased to be enjoyed upon believing. The faith
upon which their enjoyment depends is also procured, but absolutely. If a further condition were assigned
to the bestowal of faith, it would either prove vain or lead back into the circle to be examined afterward.
The decisive question is therefore the origin of the faith by which a person receives salvation. Salvation
requires believing, and the same provision procures that the recipient shall believe. Faith has its office as
a condition within the accomplishment whose further fruits the believer thereby receives.

    [257] A remedy which requires a renewed will has yet to explain how the recipient enters into its
benefit if the renewal of the will is left entirely unsecured. Here faith itself belongs to the gifts obtained.
The procurement grounds an efficient operation by which the person is brought to believe. He then
exercises the faith he receives in receiving the further benefits promised upon believing.
                        [258] R(x)⇒◇Fx,          F(x,t)⇒A(x,t),       R(x)⇒◇Ax. (23)
    [259] The diamond marks eventual occurrence in the history appointed for the person, A denotes the
application in question, and F denotes actual saving faith. Procurement gives the first implication its
meritorious ground, while the intermediate act that brings about faith is efficient renewal. The last
implication follows by composition when the premise linking faith with application concerns the same
history and benefit.
    [260] The intervening acts are necessary to the consequence. The final issue is certain because the
condition is actually produced and exercised, and its certainty therefore leaves the requirement of the
condition in force. Conditional enjoyment can belong to an absolute provision for a person precisely
because that provision secures the act on which his enjoyment depends.
    [261] Impetration and application concern the same individual persons. Whatever good Christ obtains
by his death for a person will certainly be applied and actually bestowed upon him, so that the person for
whom the good is obtained will enjoy it in due time. In those for whom he works reconciliation with God,
he works reconciliation unto God. The reach of one operation is therefore not wider than the reach of the
other. This identity of persons is sufficient to dissolve the proposed use of the distinction, and the reasons
for it follow. For each recipient the claim is that he will actually enjoy the good, and it makes no assertion
that every recipient enjoys it at the same time. Their histories can differ while the persons embraced by
the two operations remain the same.
     [262] The unity of the personal object leaves the dates of enjoyment diverse. One beneficiary may
believe before another is born, because a promise can secure the reception of a benefit at its appointed
time without making it present beforehand. The effects of one saving undertaking can consequently be
distributed over many historical moments without any indeterminacy in the persons whom it concerns.
                         [263] ∀x [R(x)⇒∃t A(x,t)] ⇏ ∃t ∀x [R(x)⇒A(x,t)]. (24)
     [264] The first statement assigns an appointed time of application to each represented person. The
second assigns the same time to all. The exchange of quantifiers changes what is asserted. Chronology by
itself proves neither proposition, and the first depends upon the efficacy and promise already established.
    [265] The objection from delay mistakes the claim that each recipient has an appointed time for a
claim that all receive at one time. The certainty of procurement is consistent with application occurring in
time. The elect person before conversion is therefore to be described according to the benefits he then
possesses, even though the same undertaking secures benefits which will become his only afterward.
    [266] If Christ procures saving goods for the sake of applying them, the goods must be applied to
everyone for whom he procures them, since otherwise his undertaking would fail of its end. There are two
reasons why application belongs to that end. If Christ aims only to obtain redemption and salvation, his
death could have its full intended effect even though neither benefit were ever applied to a single soul. All
might perish notwithstanding what he had done, a consequence which cannot stand with the dignity and
sufficiency of his oblation, the purpose of his Father, and the intention of him who “came into the world
to save sinners, — that which was lost,” and to “bring many sons unto glory.” The second reason
concerns the Father’s act in sending his Son, laying the weight of iniquity upon him, and delivering him
to an accursed death. If God intends that we be saved through these acts, he intends their application to us.
If he does not, the action is undertaken without a determinate saving issue for those whom it concerns. To
say that God was uncertain what end the death of his Son would have is blasphemous and contrary to
Scripture and right reason. It makes him appoint a Savior without settling whom he will save and appoint

a Redeemer without determining who will be redeemed. The means would then be resolved upon while
their end remained unresolved, an assertion contrary to all the glorious properties of God. A procurement
that can be complete while every recipient remains without the good is therefore distinct from what the
offering actually obtains. The end of this procurement includes the secured passage into enjoyment.
     [267] What a purchase obtains for a person belongs to that person by right through the very act that
obtains it. Given the appointed bestowal already established, what thus belongs to him in right must in
due time become his in fact. The same relation is implied whether the act is described as merit,
impetration, purchase, acquisition, or obtaining. The act is directed to the good of certain persons, and the
good so obtained is theirs. A thing obtained through prayer or entreaty belongs to the petitioner for whom
it is obtained, since the one from whom it is obtained has granted it to him. The objection is “It is
obtained upon condition; and until the condition be fulfilled no right does accrue.” If the condition is
procured along with the things bestowed upon it, the condition leaves intact the inference that everything
procured will be applied. If its fulfillment is uncertain, the answer makes God uncertain what end the
death of his Son will have. It also denies what the argument has established without answering the
grounds upon which it was established. The right and its exercise remain distinct. The right identifies
whose the benefit is by purchase, and the certainty of bestowal supplies the connection to actual
enjoyment at the appointed time.
    [268] Scripture joins impetration and application in such a way that they cannot be distributed to
different persons. In “By his knowledge shall my righteous servant justify many,” we have the application
of the good things and in “for he shall bear their iniquities,” their impetration. Those whose iniquities he
bears are those he justifies. “But he was wounded for our transgressions, he was bruised for our iniquities:
the chastisement of our peace was upon him; and by his stripes we are healed.” His wounding and our
healing belong together as procurement and application, just as his chastisement and our peace do. “He
was delivered for our offenses, and was raised again for our justification,” “By the righteousness of one”
gives the impetration and “the free gift came upon all men unto justification of life,” gives the application.
The persons called “All men,” are accordingly clear. “He spared not his own Son, but delivered him up
for us all, how shall he not with him also freely give us all things? Who shall lay any thing to the charge
of God’s elect? It is God that justifies. Who is he that condemns? It is Christ that died, yea rather, that is
risen again, who is even at the right hand of God, who also makes intercession for us.” This passage gives
three distinct grounds for the same conclusion. First, God freely gives all things in his Son to those for
whom he gives the Son. The things obtained by his death are therefore bestowed upon those for whom he
dies. Second, those for whom Christ dies are God’s elect, justified and beyond condemnation, so that
nothing can be laid to their charge. They stand in that condition by virtue of the goods Christ purchased,
which must therefore be applied to them. Third, he intercedes for those for whom he dies. It is conceded
that his intercession seeks the application of the purchased things, and the Father always hears him. Those
whom the death concerns are consequently the persons for whom the application is secured in
intercession. Christ comes so that “his might have life, and have it abundantly,” and “By the which will
we are sanctified,” expresses application while “through the offering of the body of Jesus Christ,” gives
the means of procurement. “for by one offering he has perfected for ever them that are sanctified.” Every
passage already adduced to establish the proper end of Christ’s death also supplies evidence for this
relation. The connection among the operations is part of the efficacy attributed to the undertaking, and the
persons to whom the effects are applied are the same persons for whom the good things are obtained. The
formal account merely expresses the conjunction found in Scripture, which carries the same recipients
through the benefits bestowed upon them in their order. (Isaiah 53:5, 11; Romans 4:25; 5:18; 8:32–34;
John 10:10; 1 John 4:9; Hebrews 10:10, 14)
    [269] In Diognetus the exchange proceeds into the justification of the very persons whose wickedness
required covering. The continuity of the persons matters to what the passage asserts. To enlarge the
recipients of the earlier benefit while restricting the recipients of the consequence attributed to it requires
an explanation of where the change occurs. The same discipline applies to the relation of procurement and

application. A general noun or recurring pronoun supplies no grounds for replacing one population with
another in the course of the inference. (Epistle to Diognetus 9)
    [270] Procurement and actual application define classes whose membership can be identical while the
operations remain distinct. The forward inclusion rests upon the purpose and efficacy already argued. The
reverse rests on the proposition that no one enjoys the saving fruits apart from what Christ procures. The
theological grounds for each inclusion belong to the passages cited and remain necessary to the inference.
                           [271] P⊆A,      A⊆P ⇒ P=AA={x:∃t A(x,t)}. (25)
    [272] P denotes the persons for whom the saving goods are procured and A the persons in whom they
are applied at the appointed times. Equality of the classes concerns membership and is consistent with
diversity in their histories and dates of application.
    [273] A distinction between the classes requires the opponent to reject one of the inclusions. Keeping
both premises entails their equality. That equality, in turn, draws its force from the scriptural grounds
already given in the purpose, purchase, intercession, and effectual bestowal.
    [274] The use to which the advocates of universal redemption put the distinction is now plain from the
account they give of what Christ secures. “Christ,” they continue, “died for all men, and by his death
purchased reconciliation with God for them and forgiveness of sins: which to some is applied, and they
become actually reconciled to God, and have their sins forgiven them; but to others not, who, therefore,
perish in the state of irreconciliation and enmity, under the guilt of their sins. This application,” and “is
not procured nor purchased by Christ, — for then, he dying for all, all must be actually reconciled and
have their sins forgiven them and be saved, — but it attends the fulfilling of the condition which God is
pleased to prescribe unto them, that is, believing:” Some effectively say that the person can fulfill this
condition by his own strength, even though they do not state it in those terms, while others hold that God
must give the ability to fulfill it. In either case they take the scriptural statements that Christ reconciles us
to God, redeems us, saves us by his blood, and satisfies for us by undergoing the punishment of our sins
to mean that he does something from which these effects will follow if we meet a required condition.
They attribute glorious goods to his death and then take back what they have assigned to it by making
their enjoyment depend upon a condition for us to fulfill which his death does not procure. In express
terms they make the full and proper end of Christ’s death the removal of an impediment in divine justice,
after which God could save sinners if he would and on whatever condition pleased him. This opens a door
of grace to whoever will come in, while actual justification, forgiveness, life, and immortality remain
possibilities. The death is then said to obtain that they may be, without procuring that any of them will
become actual in the persons for whom it is undertaken. The assertions that follow put the whole account
in view so that the venom concealed in its use of the distinction can be judged from its own premises. The
inward operation which produces the decisive condition lies outside the procurement as they explain it,
and the universal reach of the words they use cannot supply the omitted operation.
    [275] Their first assertion is “God,” they continue, “considering all mankind as fallen from that grace
and favor in Adam wherein they were created, and excluded utterly from the attainment of salvation by
virtue of the covenant of works which was at the first made with him, yet by his infinite goodness was
inclined to desire the happiness of them, all and every one, that they might be delivered from misery, and
be brought unto himself.” They call this inclination God’s universal love and antecedent will, in virtue of
which he desires every person to be saved and sends Christ out of that desire. Yet the fallen condition
gives the initial incapacity from which the persons under discussion need deliverance. A provision
adequate to their salvation must address that condition itself as well as the benefits they are to enjoy when
it has been changed.
   [276] God is under no natural necessity, whether arising from his goodness or any other property, to
do good to us or to any creature. Everything he wills with respect to us proceeds from his free will and

good pleasure. The particular object and the ordered means of the saving undertaking therefore belong to
the purpose by which he appoints it, and no antecedent claim in the recipient compels that operation.
    [277] An antecedent conditional will in God whose fulfillment depends on a contingent act or work of
ours is injurious to his wisdom, power, and sovereignty and can scarcely be excused from blasphemy. It
also contradicts “Who has resisted his will?” The decisive question is whether the act on which the issue
depends has been secured by the intention that sets the undertaking in motion. A purpose can provide for
a condition, while a will whose outcome depends upon an act it leaves unsecured lacks that provision.
The conditional form of the expression alone cannot distinguish the two. (Romans 9:19)
    [278] The freedom, fullness, and dimensions of the love Scripture gives as the cause of God’s sending
his Son cannot be expressed by a common inclination to do good to every person. The passages in
question give the character of that love in “God so loved the world, that he gave his only-begotten Son,”
“Having made known unto us the mystery of his will, according to his good pleasure which he has
purposed in himself,” “It pleased the Father that in him should all fullness dwell,” “God commends his
love toward us, in that, while we were yet sinners, Christ died for us.” The two observations will receive
their full defense in the second part of the controversy, with the Lord’s assistance as he gives life and
strength and his people give encouragement. The intensity and efficacy of the love are known through the
good it actually wills and brings about. Its numerical extent alone cannot say what saving operation
belongs to it. (John 3:16; Ephesians 1:9; Colossians 1:19; Romans 5:8)
    [279] The love which moved God to send his Son to die does not have all mankind for its object.
Scripture describes its particularity through what God has done “made some for the day of evil”, “hated
them before they were born”, “before of old ordained them to condemnation”, being “fitted to
destruction”, “made to be taken and destroyed”, “appointed to wrath”, to “go to their own place.” The
scope of the consequences depends upon the object of this saving purpose as established by these acts.
The goodness of God to creatures in general cannot determine the extent of a love specified by an
undertaking of this kind. (Proverbs 16:4; Romans 9:11, 13, 22; Jude 1:4; 2 Peter 2:12; 1 Thessalonians
5:9; Acts 1:25)
   [280] Their second assertion is “The justice of God being injured by sin, unless something might be
done for the satisfaction thereof, that love of God whereby he wills good to all sinners could no way be
brought forth into act, but must have its eternal residence in the bosom of God without any effect
produced.” Since the objection concerns a juridical obstacle, the act alleged to remove it must be
described in terms which preserve its juridical character. An account which begins with one obstruction
and ends by explaining the removal of another has left the first in place.
    [281] Neither Scripture nor right reason proves that God absolutely lacks the power to save sinners by
his own will without satisfaction to justice. Given his purpose to save in the way he appoints, it cannot
come about otherwise. Apart from that appointment he could have effected it, and the supposition implies
no violation of his holy nature. The necessity of the means therefore arises from the order God establishes
and from the fidelity of its execution. It is not an independent restriction upon his power.
   [282] To attribute to God an actual and necessary velleity for an end he cannot accomplish apart from
a work to be performed outside him is contrary to his eternal blessedness and all-sufficiency. A desire
dependent upon an outside condition which it can never secure lacks what belongs to an effectual
purpose. The account must answer for that deficiency if it assigns such a desire to God.
     [283] Their third assertion is “God, therefore, to fulfill that general love and good-will of his towards
all, and that it might put forth itself in such a way as should seem good to him, to satisfy his justice, which
stood in the way, and was the only hindrance, he sent his Son into the world to die.” The sending belongs
to the purpose already specified and is explained through the means actually provided for that purpose. To
name a universal goodwill is still to leave open whether the conditions necessary to salvation are actually
produced throughout the proposed extent.

     [284] The defect in this assertion becomes clear in the fuller account of the love from which the
sending of Christ properly proceeds. The object and effects specified for that love govern how the sending
is to be understood, since an undefined general affection cannot supply the character of its particular
effect.
    [285] The fourth assertion is given by Arminius as “Wherefore, the proper and immediate end and aim
of the purpose of God in sending his Son to die for all men was, that he might, what way it pleased him,
save sinners, his justice which hindered being satisfied,” and by Corvinus as “That he might will to save
sinners,” “And the intention of Christ was, to make such satisfaction to the justice of God as that be might
obtain to himself a power of saving, upon what conditions it seemed good to his Father to prescribe.” The
descriptions of actual deliverance already given supply a test of this proposed end. A course of action
newly open to God and a people actually brought to glory are different effects, and the scriptural
descriptions require an account adequate to the latter.
    [286] Whether this is the Father’s intention in sending his Son can be judged from the passages
already examined. They determine whether he means to procure liberty for himself to save if he chooses
or to obtain the certain salvation of his elect. The argument in paragraphs 167–203 gives the premises for
deciding between these accounts. The question is whether the intended end includes actual salvation in
the person to whom the undertaking is directed.
    [287] Melito describes deliverance as a passage from bondage into freedom accomplished in the
persons delivered. An account of the purpose now in question must specify its effect with the same
precision. If it includes deliverance, its explanation must reach the event that changes their condition. If it
includes only an arrangement under which deliverance may afterward occur, its object is different. The
difference has to be defended in the substance of the two propositions and cannot be removed by giving
them the same name. (Melito of Sardis, On Pascha 68)
    [288] Tender consciences will scarcely accept a possibility of salvation, or at most a willing of it upon
an uncertain condition left for us to fulfill, as the full, proper, and only immediate end of Christ’s death. A
possibility requires some admissible course that ends in salvation, whereas a secured accomplishment
excludes the relevant courses ending in failure. The two claims therefore have different force even before
their grounds are examined.
    [289] The same conditional promise can belong to an effectual purpose or to an account which
provides only a possibility. Its truth is consistent with the condition never being fulfilled and thus supplies
no instance of believing by itself. The account of salvation must give the operation that produces the
condition in the persons for whom the promised benefit is intended.
              [290] ∀x [Fx⇒Sx] ⇏ ∃x Fx;∀x [Rx⇒◇Fx]supplies a further premise. (26)
     [291] The first expression gives the universal conditional connection. The existential expression
affirms an actual instance of faith. The final expression adds the procurement premise defended in the
work. A true conditional may hold even if its antecedent has no instance, and it is the added premise
which gives grounds for asserting that faith actually occurs.
     [292] However emphatically the promise is made to all who believe, its universality does not explain
its reception. Everyone who believes is saved, as both sides agree. The point at issue is who produces the
believing, whether its production is secured, and how that operation belongs to the saving undertaking
already accomplished.
    [293] The supposed procurement of an ability for Christ to save upon a condition he can prescribe has
no ground in Scripture. It also falls short of the determinate end Scripture gives for laying down his life,
which was to “save his sheep,” and to “bring many sons to glory.” The power to prescribe terms leaves
their fulfillment unexplained. A complete account must specify the provision through which the required
terms actually take effect in the recipients.

   [294] The fifth assertion is “Christ, therefore, obtained for all and every one reconciliation with God,
remission of sins, life and salvation; not that they should actually be partakers of these things, but that
God (his justice now not hindering) might and would prescribe a condition to be by them fulfilled,
whereupon he would actually apply it, and make them partake of all those good things purchased by
Christ.” Here the distinction between impetration and application enters, and those who use it differ
remarkably in explaining what they mean. The assertion combines universal procurement with a further
conditional relation. Its competing explanations therefore turn upon the source of the condition, since its
production is what determines whether the procured good comes to be enjoyed.
    [295] Boræus and Corvinus extend the claim to the reception of all persons into a new covenant, with
Adam standing as a common person in their redemption as he did in their fall from the old covenant. All
are consequently restored in him, and the only persons condemned are those who actually sin against the
condition into which they are born and fall from the standing into which Christ’s death brought mankind.
A more recent writer goes so far as to say that all are reconciled, redeemed, saved, and justified in Christ,
although he cannot say how. The French defenders of the efficacy of grace are more careful. They hold
that we are by nature children of wrath and that the wrath of God remains on each until he comes to
Christ, so it has not actually been removed from any before then. A new common standing and actual
saving union carry different consequences. Calling both covenant cannot establish the transition from one
to the other. (Thomas More, The Universality of God’s Free Grace, 10)
    [296] On another account Christ’s satisfaction removes original sin in all persons and therefore
removes original sin only. The calamity, guilt, and alienation arising from the first fall having been
removed, God may save everyone upon a new condition. On that view all infants dying before the use of
reason must certainly be saved, even those of Turks and Pagans outside the covenant. Others observe
more carefully that the blood of Christ is said to “cleanse from all sin” and hold that he dies alike for all
sinners, conditionally for all and absolutely for none. Corvinus further says that after Christ’s satisfaction,
or after its consideration in divine prescience, the prescribed condition was absolutely undetermined. God
could have returned everyone to the law and covenant of works. More instead includes among the fruits
of Christ’s death the procurement of a new way of salvation through faith. A liability removed in one of
these accounts must retain its distinction from the complete condition of the persons whose salvation is at
issue. Removal of one impediment cannot establish every benefit that belongs to their redemption.
(Thomas More, The Universality of God’s Free Grace, 35; 1 John 1:7; 1 Peter 1:18–19; Isaiah 53:6)
    [297] Some assign fulfillment of the prescribed condition to our own strength assisted by such means
as God is ready to furnish to everyone in every time and place. Others, including Amyraldus and Camero,
maintain that believing requires effectual grace flowing particularly from election. The first establish the
idol of free will to preserve their account, while the second undo their account in order to establish grace.
The assistance proposed must be judged by its adequacy to the required act. If its efficacy does not extend
to the decisive performance, another ground is needed to explain why one person performs the act and
another does not.
    [298] Some make the love with which God sends Christ equal to all. Others hold that God loves some
more than others even though he sends his Son to die for all, and even though no love can be greater than
the love by which God gives his Son to die for us. They therefore assign a greater good to Christ’s
purchase for some and a lesser good to his purchase for others. The innumerable and ill-formed
distinctions they employ to explain this amount, as one has called them, to extinctions of the sense and
reason of Scripture. Testardus and Amyraldus furnish examples, and any reader of English can find them
in T. M[ore.] Hence the several ends assigned to the death, some of which are fruits of ransom and
satisfaction while others are left without an intelligible description. Christ dies for one person in one
unspecified way and for another in another. It becomes harder to ascertain the view hidden in these
expressions than to answer its reasons once it is found. A difference in love explains a difference of
saving effect only if it specifies a corresponding difference of purpose and operation. Variation in words
cannot do that work. (Romans 8:32)

   [299] They agree in denying that Christ’s death procures or merits faith for us. On this point they are
consistent with their own principles, since the whole structure of universal redemption would fall if faith
belonged to the goods procured. They divide again when they assign its cause. Once an indispensable
condition has been excluded from the purchase, its absence has consequences throughout the account of
how the goods purchased become actual in their recipients.
    [300] Some maintain that God sends Christ to die for all upon the condition that they believe or will to
believe. On this account he dies for those who believe and does not die for those who fail to believe, as if
the act of belief were the cause of its own object. Others say he dies absolutely for all to procure every
good, while the goods remain unenjoyed until the condition prescribed for them is fulfilled. Both make
him a public person in the place of mankind as a whole, so that in sustaining their persons in death he has
no more regard to the elect than to others. A belief exercised later cannot efficiently produce the death
which takes place earlier. What believing evidences about redemption and what it does instrumentally in
application belong to distinct relations and must be stated accordingly.
    [301] The accounts divide still further over the immediate effect of Christ’s death. Some locate it in a
power acquired by God, some in his will, some in opening a door of grace, and some in purchasing for
himself a right to save whomever he pleased. Others give the death no end with respect to us, so that
everyone might perish after Christ has done all. Others assign to this one act distinct ends almost beyond
reckoning according to the differences among the persons for whom he dies, although they acknowledge
those persons were already distinguished by an earlier decree. It remains unintelligible what purpose God
would have in sending his Son to die for persons whom he has determined not to save, or at least to pass
by and leave to ruin for their sins without remedy. The twofold destination invented to explain the act
leaves that difficulty where it found it. The force of the truth is evident in the dispersal of its opponents
among these several positions. If they will not yield to it, they are left in the darkness and error to which
their own devices drive them. The account defended here requires none of their involved distinctions or
shifts, for its fundamental claims do not contradict one another. To judge whether any effect is universally
accomplished, the effect itself must first be specified. On this matter the competing formulations differ
about the very thing they claim to establish for everyone, while the positive account can give its issue
directly.
    [302] “God, out of his infinite love to his elect, sent his dear Son in the fullness of time, whom he had
promised in the beginning of the world, and made effectual by that promise, to die, pay a ransom of
infinite value and dignity, for the purchasing of eternal redemption, and bringing unto himself all and
every one of those whom he had before ordained to eternal life, for the praise of his own glory.” The
death of Christ is the meritorious cause of all the benefits by which we pass from death to life and from
hell and wrath to heaven and glory. Its proper effects include release from every evil from which we are
delivered and the enjoyment of every good bestowed upon us. The several propositions that follow give
the parts of this account. The purpose determines the persons, the representative payment secures their
goods, and the production of faith belongs to the same provision that grants pardon, sanctifies, and brings
them to glory. The operations have their proper order, and the completeness of the undertaking consists in
their conjunction for the same persons.
    [303] God sends Christ out of his eternal love for the elect alone. The full grounds of this proposition
belong to the second general head of the controversy. That love is the source from which the sending
proceeds, and a response made by the recipient in time cannot constitute its cause. The causal priority
determines the direction in which the undertaking is to be explained.
    [304] The ransom Christ gives in giving himself has a worth and dignity that cannot be measured. It is
sufficient to accomplish any appointed end and procure any good for every person for whom it is
intended, even if there were millions more persons than have ever been created. The grounds of this
sufficiency will be considered further afterward. “God purchased his church with his own blood,”
“Redeemed not with silver and gold, but with the precious blood of Christ.” This is in accordance with

the mind and intention of Almighty God, “As the Father gave me commandment, even so I do;” who
willed a price adequate to ground the economy of love and grace he intended and the manner in which he
would dispense it. “Through this man is preached unto you the forgiveness of sins; and by him all that
believe are justified from all things, from which you could not be justified by the law of Moses,” “We are
ambassadors for Christ, as though God did beseech you by us: we pray you in Christ’s stead, be you
reconciled to God. For he has made him to be sin for us, who knew no sin; that we might be made the
righteousness of God in him.” The worth of the price concerns the dignity of the one who pays it and its
adequacy to what is procured. Its distribution concerns the appointment by which the saving fruits belong
to certain persons. Greatness in the first relation supplies no enumeration of persons in the second. (Acts
20:28; 13:38–39; 1 Peter 1:18–19; John 14:31; 2 Corinthians 5:20–21)
     [305] The worth of the sacrifice and the extent of its appointed fruits each retain their full force when
their relations are distinguished. The first concerns the dignity and adequacy of the offering. The second
concerns the persons for whom the benefits are intended and in whom they are effectually bestowed.
Increasing the proposed number of beneficiaries cannot add intrinsic strength to the act, and appointing its
fruits to particular persons cannot diminish its intrinsic worth.
                         [306] Worth(o),     Adequate(o,B),    Appoint(o,x,B) (27)
    [307] The first predicate states the worth of the offering, the second its adequacy to obtain the
specified benefit, and the third its appointment for a person. These are qualitative predicates. A finite
count of the persons concerned cannot measure the dignity of the divine person who offers himself.
    [308] The extent is established through the relation in which the benefits are appointed to their
recipients. It cannot be inferred by measuring the offering’s value. The same distinction governs universal
proclamation. The Savior is sufficient for every person who comes to him, and this sufficiency is
consistent with the provision by which the actual coming of those saved is procured.
    [309] The Father intends by this work to bring many sons to glory. These are the elect whom he freely
chooses from persons of all sorts, nations, and conditions, taking them into a new covenant of grace with
himself. The former covenant, as to them in its final issue, is null and abolished. Jesus Christ is the first
and chief promise of the new covenant because he procures for them every other good promised in it, as
the subsequent proof will show. Persons appointed to glory must receive the means necessary to reach it.
Their transformation in time is therefore explained in relation to the end to which the Father appoints
them.
   [310] Ignatius joins the appointment of the Ephesian church before the ages to its enduring glory. That
end determines the inquiry into the means by which those persons are brought to it. As established in
paragraph 14, what gives a means its place in an undertaking is its relation to the intended
accomplishment. The faith, cleansing, continuance, and final restoration of the persons appointed to glory
belong to that order. An account terminating in an earlier benefit leaves the accomplishment named by
Ignatius unexplained. (Ignatius, Epistle to the Ephesians, greeting)
     [311] The proper effects of Christ’s death and ransom are forgiveness of sin, release from wrath and
the curse of the law, justification, sanctification, reconciliation with God, and eternal life. The persons for
whom these goods are purchased will certainly possess and enjoy them in due time. The will of the Father
who sends Christ to procure them, Christ’s intention in laying down his life, and the reality of the
purchase ground his intercession, which he begins on earth and continues in heaven. The Father always
hears him as he desires and demands the actual bestowal of the goods upon every person for whom he
procured them. The several acts therefore explain the issue without the obscurity and needless severance
of operations to which the opposing account resorts. The argument that follows confirms this account,
overturns the other, and answers the objections made against it. Since the distinction already examined
carries the whole weight of the opposing position under the several forms in which its advocates present
it, the relation it asserts calls for one further examination before those arguments are given. The benefits

are received in due time through the operations appointed for their bestowal. The certainty of that
reception leaves intact the distinction between what Christ has already purchased and what a recipient has
yet to enjoy.
    [312] The distinction has already been stated in the forms in which its proponents use it. Its sound
meaning has been given, along with reasons against the use made of it under any of those forms. In the
sense our opponents give it, it is the πρῶτον ψεῦδος of their position and warrants a further blow at the
relation on which it depends. Distinguishing the operations is legitimate where the distinction preserves
the secured passage from one to the other.
    [313] The operations can be distinguished, but their recipients cannot be separated. The good obtained
by Christ is to be applied to the person for whom he obtains it, and the person for whom he works
reconciliation with God must actually be reconciled to God. The virtue of his blood cannot be understood
as medicine stored in a box for whoever comes to take it, applied to one person now and another later
without respect to any person for whom it was intended. Such an account leaves it indifferent and
uncertain whether the good procured will ever become ours. The Arminians accordingly assign to the
death such goods as forgiveness and reconciliation and say Christ procures them for all, while holding
that the very persons for whom he procures them may be damned, as the greater part are and certainly
shall be. The distance introduced by this account is between a procurement called saving and the actual
possession of the good by the person for whom it is made. Its intended application determines in what
sense the procurement is saving, so the account must include both the accomplished act and the operation
by which its fruit becomes the actual good of its recipient.
     [314] Ordinary speech already distinguishes a good obtained for a person from a good whose
acquisition remains uncertain. When a man obtains an office, or someone else obtains it for him, it is his
by right even before it is his in possession. What is impetrated by petition belongs to the person for whom
it is obtained. To say that a thing has been obtained for someone and that it may or may not be his does
violence to common sense, since the first assertion already gives him a right to it. The same relation holds
in the purchase made by Jesus Christ for those for whom he dies. Entitlement follows from what has been
obtained for them, and enjoyment follows from the further promise by which its bestowal is secured. Each
premise does its own work, so the consequence need not be concealed in the word obtaining.
   [315] God’s intention that Christ die for a person is an intention that the person have a share in that
death. A death cannot reasonably be said to be applied to him in that intention if no share in its merits
ever belongs to him. Application in this respect means that the death is appointed to be his by the will of
God. On the account we oppose it is applied in that sense to all, yet the fruits of the death are never even
made known to far the greatest part of them. A saving appointment concerns the good of the person for
whom the death is undertaken. If he can never possess the good to which the operation is directed, the
application attributed to it falls short of the deliverance it was said to intend.
    [316] A ransom paid under a compact for the deliverance of captives entails their release according to
the compact. Christ’s death is a ransom of this kind. The Father’s promise, which binds him to Christ in
his undertaking to be a Savior and in his assumption of the office appointed to him, is that the captives
will be delivered upon his performance, as already established. It is therefore contrary to reason to
suppose that he performs the office, pays the price, and leaves the greatest number of the captives for
whom he pays without release. The compact makes actual liberty the issue of the ransom. Its causal
account accordingly includes the authority and the operations by which those persons are set at liberty.
(Matthew 20:28)
   [317] Scripture also contradicts the separation, as already shown at length. See also paragraphs 515–
523. The evidence establishes a conjunction, and retaining the name of procurement cannot answer that
evidence while the application which gives the procurement its saving end is removed.

    [318] Our adversaries believe that a slight distinction is sufficient to remove the entire argument. “It is
true,” they say, “all things that are absolutely procured and obtained for any do presently become theirs in
right for whom they are obtained; but things that are obtained upon condition become not theirs until the
condition be fulfilled. Now, Christ has purchased, by his death for all, all good things, not absolutely, but
upon condition; and until that condition come to be fulfilled, unless they perform what is required, they
have neither part nor portion, right unto nor possession of them.” They name the condition in several
ways. It may be an absence of resistance to the redemption offered, a yielding to the invitation of the
gospel, or in plain terms faith. Let it be granted for the argument that Christ purchases all things for us to
be bestowed on condition that we believe. The objection still owes a causal account of the condition it
proposes. To say that enjoyment depends upon believing is to specify what must occur in those who
receive the good, while the further question is how they come to believe.
     [319] If the purchase is intended in earnest for every person for whom it is made, its condition ought

to be revealed to every one of them. They must have means to know that Christ’s death will benefit them
if they believe, particularly since the one who intends their good is the only one with power to give them
those means. Suppose a man asks a physician who can cure a disease to treat everyone who comes, but
leaves many of the sick ignorant of the arrangement even though he alone could tell them of it so that they
might go to the physician and be cured. He cannot be said to intend their healing. The same dependence
holds in the case at issue. Where knowledge of the remedy is necessary for its application, the
communication of that knowledge belongs to the provision. A complete intention to save includes the
means by which knowledge actually reaches those whom it is meant to benefit.
    [320] The required condition is either within the power of the persons required to fulfill it or it is
beyond their power. The first supposition gives all persons the power to believe, which is false. On the
second God either grants the grace by which they can fulfill it or withholds that grace. If he grants it
effectually to all, all should believe and be saved. If he withholds it, the proposed impetration of salvation
and redemption for all amounts to this. God intends Christ to die for all to procure forgiveness,
reconciliation, eternal redemption, and glory, while no one can receive the least benefit without
performing what God knows he has no power to perform. Only God can give the power, and with respect
to far the greater part he determines not to give it. Such a provision serves to expose them to shame and
misery. It no more intends their good than a promise of a thousand pounds to a blind man upon condition
that he see. Ability, assistance, and the performance of the act are distinct predicates, and anyone who
would explain the difference between saved and unsaved persons by the condition must give the passage
from one to the next.
    [321] Passing from one prerequisite to another does not locate the source of faith. If faith depends
upon a prior condition, the account must also explain the production of that condition. The sequence of
requirements may extend over time and each may have a determinate place in the causal order. The
existence of the sequence by itself gives no initiating operation capable of producing the first
indispensable change.
              [322] Cn⇒Cn−1⇒⋯⇒C1⇒Fx;Actual(Cn)requires its sufficient ground. (28)
    [323] Each C denotes a prerequisite proposed by the account under examination. The arrows express
implications asserted to be sufficient, while the operations giving them causal content have to be specified
separately. The list alone supplies no actual instance of its first member.
    [324] The necessary transition requires either an adequate prior capacity or an efficient gift which
supplies it. Otherwise it remains unexplained. The account defended here identifies the efficient gift and
the act which merits it. To answer that account, an opponent must address this relation rather than supply
another name for the required condition.
   [325] Faith is either procured by Christ’s death or it is not. If it is not, the chief grace without which
redemption can be of no benefit does not depend upon the grace of Christ as the meritorious cause that

procures it. This is exceedingly injurious to the Savior and diminishes the honor and love due to him. It is
also contrary to Scripture. “He became sin for us, that we might be made the righteousness of God in
him.” It is hard to see how we become the righteousness of God apart from believing. The apostle is
express in “It is given to us for Christ’s sake, on the behalf of Christ, to believe in him” and “God
blessing us with all spiritual blessing in him”, and faith surely belongs among the foremost of these gifts.
If faith is a fruit of Christ’s death, the assertion that he dies for all entails the question why all do not
receive it, especially when the rest of the procurement can do them no good without it. An added
condition for its bestowal will be examined afterward. For the present, suppose the condition proposed is
that men do not refuse or resist the means of grace. If the fruit of Christ’s death is not applied to all who
meet that condition, then it does not explain their reception. If it is applied to all who meet it, then all who
have not resisted must be saved, including pagans, infidels, and the infants to whom the gospel was never
preached. The alternatives therefore remain. Either faith is procured and its actual production belongs to
the efficacy of the procurement, or faith is excluded and the decisive condition has to be explained from
another source. (Titus 3:5–6; 2 Corinthians 5:21; Philippians 1:29; Ephesians 1:3)
   [326] The proposed separation makes Christ half a mediator, procuring the end while omitting the
means by which it is accomplished. The exception leaves intact the grounds already given for the
conjunction of impetration and application. The end requires the means through which it comes to pass.
To remove provision of the condition while retaining the promise of the accomplished end is to divide the
undertaking that has to be explained.
    [327] Christ dies for all God’s elect so that they will believe and through believing have eternal life.
He does not die for anyone upon a condition that he afterward believes. Faith belongs among the principal
fruits of the death, as the subsequent argument establishes. Neither Scripture nor reason says that if we
believe, Christ died for us, as though the act of belief could produce its own object and bring about what
otherwise had not been. He dies for us so that we may believe. Salvation is bestowed upon a condition,
and the faith which is the condition is procured absolutely. With the point of difference thus established,
the arguments, demonstrations, and testimonies that follow give the grounds in which the position is
contained and upon which it stands. The general foundations already laid remain integral to these proofs,
none of which can be thoroughly answered while those foundations remain unshaken. The saving
provision produces believing, and believing receives the good promised upon it. When the believer
afterward recognizes that relation, the order of his inquiry can move back from the received good to its
cause without reversing the order in which it came to be his.
    [328] Polycarp’s given faith and Barnabas’s given repentance can now be read in the order of
dependence they express. A condition performs a real office in the recipient’s enjoyment, although his
exercise of it can proceed from a gift received before he acts. Conditional enjoyment therefore decides
neither whether the gift is uncertain nor whether its production belongs to redemption. That provision is
the issue. To answer that people must believe is to repeat a proposition both sides affirm while leaving the
disputed ground of believing untouched. (Polycarp, Epistle to the Philippians 3; Epistle of Barnabas 16)
    [329] The historical offering and its appointed application bring about saving fruits, which in turn can
furnish evidence by which the believer recognizes his interest in the offering. The order of knowing
returns from the effects to the cause. That return is real, while the act by which the cause is subsequently
known leaves its causal priority intact.
                   [330] Rx→Fx→Recognizex(Rx)Recognizex(Rx)↛Produce(Rx). (29)
    [331] The first arrow gives the mediated causal dependence established above, including efficient
renewal. The second gives the relation by which the saving effects warrant recognition. The final denial
excludes the proposition that the believer’s later recognition produces the earlier work of representation.

    [332] The act of believing receives the Savior disclosed to it and recognizes the fruits of an
antecedent provision. It does not create its object. The same distinction will later show why a demand for
prior assurance cannot be allowed to obstruct the sinner’s obedience to the command to come to Christ.
    [333] The first argument proceeds from the covenant of grace, which Christ establishes, ratifies, and
confirms by his death. It is the testament of which he is testator, and because his death ratifies it, his blood
is called “The blood of the new testament.” The effects of that death cannot extend beyond the covenant it
ratifies. The covenant is made with particular persons rather than with all, and its benefits therefore
determine the persons intended in the death. The way to identify those persons is to follow what the
covenant promises to accomplish in them, including the inward transformation necessary to their actual
participation. (Matthew 26:28)
    [334] The particular extent of the covenant follows from its nature, as set out in “I will make a new
covenant with the house of Israel, and with the house of Judah: not according to the covenant that I made
with their fathers in the day that I took them by the hand to bring them out of the land of Egypt; which my
covenant they brake, though I was an husband to them, says the Lord;” and “Not according to the
covenant that I made with their fathers in the day when I took them by the hand to lead them out of the
land of Egypt; because they continued not in my covenant, and I regarded them not, says the Lord. For
this is the covenant that I will make with the house of Israel after those days, says the Lord; I will put my
laws in their mind, and write them in their hearts: and I will be to them a God, and they shall be to me a
people: and they shall not teach every man his neighbor, and every man his brother, saying, Know the
Lord: for all shall know me, from the least to the greatest.” Its condition is absolutely promised in “I will
put my fear in their hearts.” The difference between the old covenant of works and the new covenant of
grace lies in what God does with respect to the condition. Under the old he prescribed it and required its
fulfillment. Under the new he promises that he himself will effect it in the persons with whom he makes
the covenant. Without that inward efficacy the new covenant would be as weak and unprofitable for the
end of bringing us to God and binding us to him as the old covenant proved to be. The defect for which
God in his mercy abolished the old was the incapacity sin had produced in us, which left us unable to
fulfill its condition, “Do this, and live?” Apart from that incapacity the connection remains true, “he that
does these things shall live.” The same incapacity cannot be answered merely by replacing the condition.
A man could fulfill the whole law by his own strength as readily as he could repent and believe the
promise of the gospel savingly. What makes the new covenant effectual is accordingly that the Lord
undertakes to produce the condition in all the federates, the persons to whom he extends the covenant. If
he merely exacted its obedience and left the required operation unperformed in us, the new covenant
would increase our misery under the appearance of communicating grace and mercy. The terms of the
testament themselves establish, and further evidence could abundantly confirm, that free grace certainly
effects the condition in every person taken into the covenant. Its extent therefore reaches no further than
the persons in whom the condition is accomplished. The writing of the law upon the heart names the
inward operation through which the person comes to obey. By promising to produce the disposition from
which the act proceeds, God gives the cause of the condition his covenant requires. (Jeremiah 31:31–32;
Hebrews 8:9–11)
    [335] The promise to write the law upon the heart supplies an operation which a command by itself
does not perform. It produces in the recipient the condition from which the commanded life proceeds. The
person understands the law and himself hears, remembers, wills, and acts. The efficiency of the promise
concerns the renewal by which he becomes the subject who actually performs those acts.
                                         [336] ,     ax=G(,cx). (30)
    [337] W minus and W plus designate the person’s dispositions before and after the renewing
operation N. The communicated command and promise are c, and a is the person’s act in relation to that
content. The superscripts distinguish his conditions without assigning any numerical measure to holiness.

    [338] The Spirit and the renewed person each act in the relation proper to them. The Spirit renews,
and the person so renewed believes and obeys. The voluntary response is therefore as real as the inward
transformation, since what the transformation produces is the disposition from which the response
proceeds. (Jeremiah 31:33; Ezekiel 36:26–27; Philippians 2:12–13)
    [339] The effects of the covenant are plainly absent in some. Scripture says “all men have not faith,”
and “of the elect of God:” so that the covenant cannot be extended beyond the remnant according to
election. Every blessing promised in the covenant will certainly be communicated to every person who
belongs to it. If the covenant is general, either faith is no blessing of it or all must have faith. The
objection that God promises to write his law upon the heart and put his fear within us only upon a
condition would answer the argument if a condition could be given. The only one that can be imagined is
believing, which is already the act of a person whose heart bears the law. On that account God promises
to write the law upon the heart of the person who already has it written there. The promised
transformation is thus made to depend upon its own prior occurrence, and its initiating cause remains
unexplained. This is the question of provision established in paragraphs 252–273. There is no reason to
say that God enters this covenant with every person, including those who never hear of the covenant, its
grace, or its condition and who receive no grace for fulfilling it, without which it can do them no good.
Adam is acquainted with the covenant God makes with him. Its renewal with Noah is made known to
him, and its establishment with Abraham comes with a full and rich declaration of the chief promises.
These operations are plainly not performed toward all, as will appear afterward. The earliest distinction
between the seed of the woman and the seed of the serpent is itself enough to overturn the pretended
universality. To say that the covenant of grace is made with everyone is to say that God makes it with the
seed of the serpent. (Genesis 3:15; 12)
    [340] The covenant of grace and every promise belonging to it express a distinguishing mercy toward
the persons God foreknows. Its extent is therefore ascertained in the persons in whom those promises are
fulfilled. Christ’s blood is the blood of that covenant, and because he is the surety of that covenant alone,
his offering is directed to obtaining the goods it promises. Its personal object cannot extend beyond the
persons embraced by the covenant itself. The formal consequence depends upon both the efficacy of the
promises and the identification of those whom they concern. (Hebrews 7:22)
    [341] The second argument proceeds from the communication required by the proposed universal
purchase. If God intends that Christ procure forgiveness and reconciliation for every person, and Christ
by his death obtains those goods for them on condition that they believe, then the divine goodwill and the
purchase made on their behalf ought to be made known to them through the word in order that they may
believe. “for faith comes by hearing, and hearing by the word of God” If the purchase and intention
remain undisclosed to any of the persons whom they concern, two consequences are possible. Either those
persons can be saved without faith in and knowledge of Christ, which they cannot have without his being
revealed to them and which has already been shown to be false, or the divine goodwill and Christ’s
purchase are vain with respect to them. In the latter case the transaction amounts to a mockery. It does
nothing to draw them out of their misery and cannot serve justice by making them inexcusable for failing
to embrace and use a benefit of which they never heard. Divine wisdom cannot be reconciled with the
supposition that God sends Christ to die so that people might be saved, makes hearing and believing
indispensable to their salvation, and never causes those people to hear. No wise man pays a ransom for
captives to enjoy by means of knowledge which he is sure they will never obtain. Nor does it agree with
God’s goodness to hold out in pretense to all his poor creatures the incomparable love displayed in
sending his Son, to leave them ignorant of it, and then to condemn them for not believing it. The
resolution attributed to Christ at his death would amount to “I will now, by the oblation of myself, obtain
for all and every one peace and reconciliation with God, redemption and everlasting salvation, eternal
glory in the high heavens, even for all those poor, miserable, wretched worms, condemned caitiffs, that
every hour ought to expect the sentence of condemnation; and all these shall truly and really be
communicated to them if they will believe. But yet, withal, I will so order things that innumerable souls

shall never hear one word of all this that I have done for them, never be persuaded to believe, nor have the
object of faith that is to be believed proposed to them, whereby they might indeed possibly partake of
these things?” Can that be the design of our merciful high priest? God forbid. Suppose a prince proclaims
that certain captives are in sore bondage, that he has ample treasure, and that he will redeem every one
who thanks him for his goodwill. Yet he takes no care to inform the captives, while knowing that unless
he himself gives them the information it will never reach them. His proclamation would be an
ostentatious flourish without an actual intention to do them good. Suppose instead that a physician has a
medicine adequate to every disease and announces that he means to cure all the sick, while making his
medicine and his intentions known to very few and knowing that without his own information the
knowledge can reach very few. Nothing in that course of action expresses an intention to restore every
one of them. In each case the connection between means and end supplies the test of the asserted purpose.
Where communication is necessary to the enjoyment of a benefit, a complete provision for that enjoyment
includes communication. The saving account must accordingly explain how the message reaches the
intended recipients and how its reception is connected with the faith they must exercise to receive the
purchased good. (Romans 10:17)
     [342] Scripture and the experience of successive ages establish that innumerable persons, and entire
nations for long periods, have been passed by in the declaration of this mystery under both the old and
new dispensations. The Lord does not provide for every person to know it in any measure. Many hear
neither a rumor nor a report of it. Under the Old Testament this distribution appears in “In Judah was God
known, and his name was great in Israel; in Salem was his tabernacle, and his dwelling-place in Zion.”
“He showed his word unto Jacob, and his statutes and his judgments unto Israel. He has not dealt so with
any nation: and as for his judgments, they have not known them.” and gives rise to the designations of the
heathen and the imprecations in “Pour out your fury upon the heathen that know you not, and upon the
families that call not upon your name,” with their condition fully described in “Without Christ, aliens
from the commonwealth of Israel, and strangers from the covenants of promise, having no hope, and
without God in the world.” Under the New Testament the church has “lengthened her cords, and
strengthened her stakes,” and “many nations are come up to the mountain of the Lord,” and the number is
great enough to be called “all people,” “all nations,” and even the “world,” and the “whole world,” in
comparison with the small bounds of the Jewish church. The enlargement is real, yet Scripture and
experience still attest millions who never hear of Christ or of reconciliation by him. The reason to give for
it is “Even so, Father, for so it seemed good in your sight.” Scripture records that the Holy Spirit
expressly forbade the apostles to carry the word to certain places and directed them elsewhere, in a
manner answering in some particulars to the former dispensation, in which “he suffered all nations to
walk in their own ways.” Owen points, for evidence from experience, to the testimony available from
those who had spent time in the Indies. The historical distribution of proclamation matters because
proclamation is the means the universal account itself requires. The observed absence of the
communication therefore has to be accounted for within any assertion that this necessary means is
universally provided. (Psalms 76:1–2; 147:19–20; Jeremiah 10:25; Ephesians 2:12; Matthew 11:26; Acts
16:6–7, 9–10; 14:16)
    [343] The exceptions are frivolous and can be answered in their place. For the present, the question is
how redemption is revealed to the thousands of infants born to unbelievers whom the Lord cuts off before
they can trouble the world, persecute the church, or disturb human society. The same question concerns
their parents. Paul affirms that the works of God could lead them to know his eternal power and Godhead,
but from those works it was impossible for them to know redemption or a Redeemer. Knowledge of a
creator from what is created and knowledge of a determinate saving work have different contents. One
cannot supply a missing declaration of the latter simply by giving the former its name.
    [344] A communication necessary to salvation must be specified by what it makes known. The
existence of a creator, the accusation of conscience, and the historical Savior are distinct objects of
knowledge. To explain believing, one must give the content actually received and the means through

which its saving object comes to be known. The history of proclamation bears directly on that question
because hearing is given an appointed office in the reception of the message.
                       [345] Hear(x,c)⇒Availablex(c),         Availablex(c)⇏Fx. (31)
     [346] Availability means that the content is present to the recipient’s understanding in the relevant
way. The first implication concerns the communication by which this occurs. The second denies that the
presence of the content by itself entails the saving act, whose efficiency requires the further operation
distinguished in paragraphs 81–90.
    [347] The preached word performs an actual causal office, although its outward presence alone
cannot explain the inward reception of every person who hears it. A theory that makes its communication
universally necessary must also account for the persons to whom it is never made known. A means
required in establishing the argument remains necessary when its actual provision is examined.
    [348] The third argument concerns what follows if Christ dies for all, meaning that in accordance with
God’s purpose he obtains for them all the goods Scripture describes as the effects and fruits of his death,
comprehended in “eternal redemption.” He obtains these goods either absolutely or upon a condition they
must fulfill. If the purchase is absolute, each person must infallibly participate in the redemption so
purchased. There is no impediment which can defeat the enjoyment of what God absolutely intends and
Christ absolutely obtains for him. If the purchase is conditional, the condition is either procured or left
unprocured. If it is procured to be bestowed upon them and wrought within them, that procurement is
itself either absolute or conditional. An absolute procurement of the condition, joined to the procurement
of a good bestowed on that condition, is equivalent in the event to procuring the good absolutely. That is
our own account. Christ procures salvation to be bestowed upon those who believe and procures without
condition the faith by which they believe. Their salvation is accordingly as certain in its issue as it would
be if it were bestowed absolutely, even though faith has its necessary place in the manner of its bestowal.
On the universal supposition, all would therefore be saved. If the condition is itself obtained upon a
condition, that further condition must be named, and the same question arises whether it was procured
absolutely or upon yet another condition. The inquiry continues until some provision is fixed or the
account runs into a circle. What drives the conclusion is the saving content already established for the
death. Its supposed universality would extend that content and all its consequences to every person.
    [349] Suppose instead that Christ does not procure the condition on which the actual enjoyment of all
the purchased goods depends. The condition must first be made known to all, as shown in the second
argument. Next, all persons either have power to fulfill it of themselves or they do not. Since all sides
grant that the condition is faith in the promises, the former alternative makes everyone able to believe by
the power of his own free will, contrary to Scripture, as will be shown with the Lord’s assistance. If they
lack that power and God must give faith and work it within them by free grace, then in giving his Son to
obtain their redemption upon condition of faith he either intends to work faith in all or has no such
intention. If he intends it, why does he not perform it, seeing “he is of one mind, and who can turn him?”
Why do not all believe, unless his purpose fails? If he does not intend to give faith to all, that is equivalent
to intending not to give it to all. God’s will is no pure negation, since what he does not will to be he wills
not to be. The alleged universal purpose therefore reduces to an assertion that God gives Christ to die for
all on condition of an act which they cannot perform without him and which he purposes not to work in
them. The indispensable office of the condition makes the question of its source more pressing. The
availability of a benefit to whoever meets a condition does not procure the condition in any particular
person.
    [350] To assign God a will to do what he knows and orders will never be done is extreme madness.
On this account he grants a good upon a condition that cannot be met without his assistance, while
purposing to withhold that very assistance. Such a giving of a Redeemer can only delude the creatures to
whom it is directed. No good can arise from a provision that requires of them an act they can no more
perform of themselves than Lazarus could come out of the grave. The wisdom of God cannot be

expressed in a purpose he knows will never be fulfilled, nor can his goodness be expressed in pretending
to intend the profit of a redemption to persons he knows can never meet the condition upon which alone it
can profit them. A man who promises a thousand pounds to a blind man upon condition that he open his
eyes and see, knowing he cannot do it, does not thereby show pity for his poverty. He mocks his misery.
A king who promises a ransom for captives at Algiers upon condition that they first conquer their tyrants
and come away, knowing they cannot, does not act as a king ought. A price paid for captives while
making no provision that their chains be taken away is of no use toward their leaving the prison. The offer
of great rewards to dead men upon condition that they bring themselves to life accomplishes as much.
Each of these has as much effect toward its professed end as the obtaining of salvation for people upon
condition of faith when the faith itself is left unprocured. The will this assigns to Christ is “I will obtain
eternal life to be bestowed on men, and become theirs, by the application of the benefits of my death; but
upon this condition, that they do believe. But as I will not reveal my mind and will in this business, nor
this condition itself, to innumerable of them, so concerning the rest I know they are no ways able of
themselves, — no more than Lazarus was to rise, or a blind man is to see, — to perform the condition that
I do require, and without which none of the good things intended for them can ever become theirs; neither
will I procure that condition ever to be fulfilled in them. That is, I do will that that shall be done which I
do not only know shall never be done, but that it cannot be done, because I will not do that without which
it can never be accomplished” Let the reader judge whether such a resolution belongs to the wisdom and
goodness of our Savior. When one means to do a good only upon a condition he knows to be wholly
beyond the recipient’s strength, and knows his own concurrence is indispensable but determines not to
give it, the supposed intention of good is a fruitless flourish. On the proposed account Christ obtains
eternal redemption from his Father for persons who never partake of it, because they lack power to
perform its condition and God and Christ determine not to give it. The purchase would be unworthy of
Christ and unprofitable to the persons for whom it was made, a hard saying about anything he obtains for
the sons of men. A further question then arises. If God purposes through Christ to save all upon believing
because Christ dies for all, if Christ does not purchase faith, and if people have no power to believe of
themselves, how does anyone come to be saved? The intended outcome must be examined together with
the means actually provided. The effectual purpose established above includes what brings about the good
it intends.
     [351] The answer offered is “God bestows faith on some, not on others.” The question then is whether
