the distinguishing grace given to some and withheld from others was purchased for those who receive it.
If it was, Christ does not die equally for all. He dies so that some will have faith, and relatively to them
cannot be said to die at all for those for whom he does not procure faith, since he knows that without it all
the rest is fruitless. If the distinguishing grace is not purchased, those who are saved owe Christ no more
thanks than those who are damned, a strange conclusion contrary to “Unto him that loved us, and washed
us from our sins in his own blood, and has made us kings and priests unto God and his Father,” and the
accompanying testimony. Christ obtains salvation so fully that those for whom he obtains it will certainly
receive it. He purchases salvation to be bestowed upon those who believe and also purchases the faith by
which they believe. This account does not answer the question of obligation by denying that God
commands men to believe in Christ even when they have no power of themselves to do so. A command
declares what our duty is, which can be made known to us whether or not we can perform it. It does not
declare what God intends shall be done. The promises accompanying the command do not declare an
intention that Christ die for us if we believe, which would absurdly make the act constitute its own object,
though the object is prior to the act and presupposed in the call to believe. Neither do they declare the
supposed purpose that his death should become profitable to us if we believe, which has already been
refuted. They declare faith to be God’s appointed way to salvation, so everyone who believes will
undoubtedly be saved, the two being joined inseparably, as the argument afterward establishes. The actual
distribution of the distinguishing gift therefore decides the question of universality through its relation to
what Christ purchases. The appeal to a distinguishing gift thus brings the argument back to the Hub of

paragraph 0. Having ascribed the difference to God, the objector must now say what part the death of
Christ has in procuring the gift by which God makes that difference. (Revelation 1:5–6)
    [352] The fourth argument follows from the distinction which God's eternal purpose establishes within
mankind. Where Scripture describes two opposed conditions, expressly assigns Christ's death to persons
belonging to one, and nowhere assigns it to persons belonging to the other, the death cannot have every
person as its object — it embraces every member of the former company and no member of the latter. The
distinction itself must, however, possess grounds independent of the conclusion concerning redemption.
Once those grounds are supplied, the saving predicates can be followed through their proper subjects,
without allowing a description belonging to one population to pass silently into the other.
    [353] Scripture first establishes this distinction through the relations in which persons stand to God's
eternal purpose — those whom he “loves” and those whom he “hates”. Those whom he “knows,” and
those whom he “knows not”. Thus “I know my sheep”, “The Lord knows them that are his”, “Whom he
did foreknow”, and “His people which he foreknew;” stand over against “I know you not”. The
distinction becomes explicit in “I speak not of you all; I know whom I have chosen.” Appointment to life
and glory differs from appointment and fitness for destruction — the “elect” are distinguished from the
“reprobate;” those “ordained to eternal life,” from those “before were of old ordained to condemnation”.
The former relation appears in “He has chosen us in him”, “Ordained to eternal life”, and “Whom he did
predestinate, them he also called: and whom he called, them he also justified: and whom he justified,
them he also glorified.” Its opposed terms appear where the saved confess “God has not appointed us to
wrath, but to obtain salvation”, and where divine sovereignty is defended in full, “He has mercy on whom
he will have mercy, and whom he will he hardens. You will say then unto me, Why does he yet find fault?
For who has resisted his will? Nay but, O man, who are you that reply against God? Shall the thing
formed say to him that formed it, Why have you made me thus? Has not the potter power over the clay, of
the same lump to make one vessel to honor, and another to dishonor?” Scripture also speaks of persons
“Ordained to this condemnation”, “Made to be taken and destroyed;” and of “Sheep and goats”. The
distinction recurs throughout its testimony. There are those upon whom God has “mercy,” and those
whom he “hardens.” His “peculiar people” are “the children of promise,” and “not of the world,” they
constitute his “church;” while those opposed to them are described as “the world,” “not prayed for,” and
“not his people”. These descriptions receive their ground in God's purpose, will, and good pleasure. Thus
“The Lord has made all things for himself, even the wicked for the day of evil.” Christ himself declares “I
thank you, O Father, because you have hid these things from the wise and prudent, and have revealed
them unto babes. Even so, Father; for so it seemed good in your sight.” The distinction precedes the
performance of either good or evil, “The children being not yet born, neither having done any good or
evil, that the purpose of God according to election might stand, not of works, but of him that calls; it was
said unto her, The elder shall serve the younger.” Nor can willing or running constitute its independent
source, “So then it is not of him that wills, nor of him that runs, but of God that shows mercy. For the
scripture says unto Pharaoh, Even for this same purpose have I raised you up, that I might show my
power in you, and that my name might be declared throughout all the earth.” The connection between
purpose and historical salvation is then unfolded without any exchange of subjects, “Who are the called
according to his purpose. For whom he did foreknow, he also did predestinate to be conformed to the
image of his Son, that he might be the first-born among many brethren. Moreover, whom he did
predestinate, them he also called: and whom he called, them he also justified: and whom he justified them
he also glorified.” Scripture therefore supplies the first premise. Its opposed descriptions concern both the
divine appointment and the effects through which that appointment enters history. Their respective
relations remain distinct, so that a present appearance does not become an exhaustive disclosure of the
purpose from which salvation proceeds. (Romans 9:11–13, 16–21; 8:28–30; 11:2; John 10:14; 13:18; 10;
15:19; 17:9; 11:52; 2 Timothy 2:19; Matthew 25:12, 32; 11:25–26; Ephesians 1:4; Acts 13:48; 1
Thessalonians 5:9; Jude 1:4; 2 Peter 2:12; Titus 2:14; Galatians 4:28; Colossians 1:24; Hebrews 2:10, 12–
13; Proverbs 16:4)

    [354] Preparation, calling, faith, and fruit enter this inquiry through the early witnesses themselves.
The Father's planting in Ignatius, the dependence of calling upon the divine will in Clement, and the
people prepared in the Beloved in Barnabas each direct attention to the relation between a gift and its
manifestation. That relation has an order which governs the argument. A believer becomes known to us
through effects whose ground we then discern. The believer's actual constitution proceeds from that
ground into those effects. An inference by which we discover salvation cannot therefore become the cause
by which salvation is produced. (Ignatius, Epistle to the Philadelphians 3; Clement of Rome, First Epistle
to the Corinthians 32; Epistle of Barnabas 3)
    [355] Christ's death is expressly assigned to persons on one side of this division — his “people”, his
“sheep”, his “church” distinguished from the world, his “elect”, and his “children”, as the earlier
exposition has established. The consequent restriction excludes those he “never knew,” those whom he
“hates,” and “hardens,” those upon whom he “will not show mercy,” and those who “were before of old
ordained to condemnation.” It excludes, in other words, the reprobate and the world for which he would
not pray. The objection that Scripture names his “sheep,” his “elect,” and his “chosen,” without expressly
adding an exclusive particle mistakes the ground of restriction. Once persons have been distinguished
under opposed conditions as elect and reprobate, sheep and goats, an assertion assigning this saving death
to the elect possesses a determinate subject. Ordinary speech requires no additional word to prevent the
opposed population from being included. Scripture likewise contains indefinite assertions whose
necessary limitation belongs to their sense. When Christ declares “I am the way, the truth, and the life”,
the absence of an express exclusive does not permit another to share that identity. When Scripture
declares “It pleased the Father that in him should all fullness dwell;” no explicit “only,” is needed to
exclude the blasphemous supposition that the same fullness dwells in another. Restriction can thus belong
to the asserted relation without being supplied by a separate grammatical term. The objection leaves the
argument standing. God's purpose of election and reprobation would establish the same conclusion more
fully — Christ's death was appointed as the means of saving the elect, and was not suffered for those
whom the eternal counsel determined should perish in their sins without participation in its benefits.
Owen reserves fuller treatment of that purpose for the controversy concerning the cause of Christ's
mission. Its present consequence is already sufficient. These descriptions identify the persons for whom
the saving operation is asserted, and derive their restrictive force from the work, its effects, and the
opposed conditions which Scripture has already established. (Matthew 1:21; John 10:11, 14; 11:51–52;
14:6; Acts 20:28; Ephesians 5:25; Romans 5:8–9; 8:32–34; Hebrews 2:12–13; Colossians 1:19)
    [356] The scriptural predicates determine how the represented persons enter the domain of this saving
operation. Appointment, calling in history, and actual faith belong to connected relations, and the persons
described under each must be followed through those connections. The notation expresses the inclusions
warranted by the preceding exposition. It possesses no independent power to make their extensions
identical merely by assigning them a common symbol.
                          [357] Rx⇒◇Fx◇Fx⇒Sx∃y∈H ¬Sy             ⇒ R≠H. (32)
    [358] Terms. Procurement of faith supplies the first premise. The second concerns the saving promise
and preservation of genuine faith, whose actuality exceeds outward profession. The third states that final
salvation does not embrace everyone. R denotes the set of persons represented in the sense already
defined.
    [359] Consequence. Universal representative redemption, in the saving sense here asserted, is
excluded by the conjunction of these premises. An adversary may challenge their meaning or truth.
Retaining them together requires accepting what follows from them. Their formal connection therefore
makes the scriptural defence of each underlying relation more necessary, since the notation cannot supply
grounds which exposition has failed to establish.
   [360] The fifth argument concerns the authority adequate to a universal theological assertion. What
Scripture nowhere warrants cannot be established as its doctrine. Yet Scripture nowhere says that Christ

died for all men, still less for every man individually, a distinction whose consequences require their own
examination. Christ does give his life “a ransom for all,” but the disputed universal cannot be supplied
merely by adding all men to those words. Elsewhere his death is expressly assigned to many, to his
church, to believers, to the children given him by God, and to us. Equivalent descriptions also embrace
persons of every sort. Before all can yield a universal affirmative, its context must therefore exclude these
determinate senses — all believers, all the elect, the whole church, all the given children, or some from
every kind. Patient examination of the particular texts would bring this controversy to its proper issue.
The assertion that every individual belongs to the saving object requires evidence extending to every
individual. Repetition of a word whose domain remains in dispute cannot enlarge the evidence beyond
what the text actually supplies. (Revelation 5:9–10)
    [361] The sixth argument arises from the representative character of Christ's death. He died as
sponsor, taking the place of those for whom he suffered, “For when we were yet without strength, in due
time Christ died for the ungodly. For scarcely for a righteous man will one die: yet peradventure for a
good man some would even dare to die. But God commends his love toward us, in that, while we were
yet sinners, Christ died for us.” The same relation appears in “He was made a curse for us.” and “He has
made him to be sin for us.” Here one person is accepted in the place of others. The act is a substitution,
and its consequences follow from the relation which constitutes it. First, those represented are freed from
the anger, wrath, and liability to death which their surety undergoes for them, so that in him they are
reconciled and delivered from the bondage belonging to death. His suffering in another's place has
precisely this end, and justice requires that the liability borne by the accepted substitute cease to require
the same payment from the represented person. Christ is therefore ἔγγυος, “a surety of a better testament”:
as priest he bears the “chastisement of our peace,” and our “iniquities.” He is “made sin for us, that we
might be made the righteousness of God in him.” Yet reconciliation does not actually belong to every
person. Justification consists in righteousness imputed and iniquity not imputed. Those who have not
come to Christ remain under wrath, for “the wrath of God abides on them”. The text describes an abiding
condition consequent upon unbelief. It does not describe wrath returning after universal release through
Christ's death from the condition which belongs to all by nature. Its word is μένει, “it remains,” or abides
— the wrath has not been removed. To such persons the gospel becomes a savour of death unto death,
adding the grave condemnation of a despised gospel to the guilt of death already borne. The assertion that
every individual has nevertheless been redeemed, restored, justified, and made righteous in Christ and
through his death directly opposes this testimony. Scripture supplies it no foundation, and repeating the
assertion does nothing to resolve its contradiction. Secondly, a death undertaken as surety makes
satisfaction for the sins of the persons represented. Satisfaction to God's justice, and thereby redemption
to himself, explains why Christ undergoes death in their place. No other account supplies the purpose of
that substitution. If every individual were represented in this sense, satisfaction would consequently have
been made for every individual's sins. That consequent is false, as the following reasons establish. The
surety's act concerns another's liability through an appointed and accepted representation. Its effect
therefore belongs to the persons whose liabilities the act was appointed to bear. (Thomas More, The
Universality of God’s Free Grace, 45; Romans 5:6–8; Galatians 3:13; 2 Corinthians 5:21; Hebrews 7:22;
Isaiah 53:5–6; John 3:36; Ephesians 2:3)
    [362] The first reason follows from satisfaction itself. Where Christ has satisfied divine justice for
specified sins, justice is satisfied concerning them. The alternative would make his satisfaction an
insufficient offering rejected by God, an assertion of the highest blasphemy. Yet justice has not received
satisfaction in Christ for every sin of every person. Those who eternally undergo punishment for their
own sins remain subject to that punishment in satisfaction of justice. Their liability was consequently not
discharged apart from their punishment through the punishment of Christ. They have not been healed by
his stripes. That innumerable souls will endure the punishment due to their own sins requires no
demonstration among Christians. The difficulty for universal satisfaction is therefore exact — how can
justice demand from them what it has already accepted from Christ on their behalf? Satisfaction
completed and satisfaction still required cannot be affirmed of one liability in the same respect. Scripture

nevertheless declares that some will be required to pay “the uttermost farthing”. An accepted satisfaction
changes the standing of the obligation satisfied, and that change must be followed through the same
representative relation and the same liability. The appointed time of application belongs to this account. It
does not make satisfaction both accomplished and wanting in the identical respect. (Matthew 5:26)
    [363] The question raised in Diognetus—what could cover sins except Christ's righteousness—directs
the inquiry to the ground upon which a guilty person can be justified. That ground, within the account of
satisfaction established in paragraphs 55–72, retains the consequence attributed to its acceptance. Where a
specified liability has received satisfaction, the adversary must explain the respect in which that same
liability could nevertheless remain unsatisfied. The fact that some perish supplies no explanation of this
difficulty. It states the consequence against which the proposed account of satisfaction must establish its
coherence. (Epistle to Diognetus 9)
    [364] The second reason concerns the intention which constitutes the offering. In dying as surety,
Christ satisfied for those sins for which he intended to make satisfaction. An operation of this magnitude
cannot arise accidentally outside his will and purpose. His willing self-consecration as an oblation
belongs to the very conditions by which his death is acceptable to God. He did not intend satisfaction for
every individual's sins, for innumerable souls already lay in hell under the burden and punishment of their
own sins when he offered himself, beyond any redemption from that condition. An offering for those
known to be incapable of receiving any fruit from it would assign to Christ an intention deprived of every
possible accomplishment. The covenant blood cannot be treated as expended upon persons for whom he
intended no good. Nor could he intend their deliverance against the eternal decree of his Father and of his
own Deity. Cain and Pharaoh had been condemned ages before the suffering of Christ — did the Father
send him, and did he come, to die for them? “Credat Apella?” The appeal to a conditional benefit—his
death would have availed had they believed and fulfilled its condition—changes nothing. First, most had
never heard of any such condition. Secondly, at his death Christ knew both that they had not fulfilled it
and that death had removed every possibility of doing so. A purpose to benefit them by that means would
therefore be vain and frustrated, which cannot be attributed to the Son of God. Thirdly, the proposed
redemption conditioned upon belief itself receives examination and rejection below. The intended object
of representation determines whose liability is borne in the satisfaction undertaken. It belongs to the
mediatorial identity of the offering, rather than arriving afterward as an accidental use of an otherwise
indeterminate act.
    [365] The objection that Christ might satisfy for those already damned, to whom his death could bring
no use, just as he satisfied for those already saved, to whom it could bring no need, confuses two radically
different relations to his offering. First, those previously saved had been saved because Christ would
certainly suffer for them at the appointed time. His suffering operated through purpose and promise
before its historical execution, since God accounted the promised work as accomplished under the
covenant with Christ, ratified—so far as human conception can express it—by mutual and unchangeable
promises. The performance remained necessary precisely because their salvation depended upon its
certainty. No corresponding ground, inducement, or expected effect connected the already damned to that
performance. Secondly, consider captives whose deliverance depends upon a promised ransom. A man
sends word to their prison that he will pay the price of their release. Their keeper accepts his engagement,
and they are summoned to come out upon the strength of it. At payment, some have already left according
to the arrangement, while others have remained obstinately confined. Some of those remaining heard the
promise and others did not, in accordance with the promisor's own appointment, and they have since died.
The promised payment is plainly made for those released through the engagement. It is not thereby
intended for those who died stubbornly in the dungeon. Christ's passion can likewise be the price of
persons delivered before payment by virtue of his undertaking, without being the price of persons who
died in the prison of corruption before that payment occurred. Thirdly, universal substitution requires an
answer concerning the sins satisfied. Did Christ satisfy for every sin of every person, or for only some?
Satisfaction for some leaves every person with an unpaid liability and thus explains no salvation.

Satisfaction for all raises the question why all are not saved. If unbelief supplies the answer, unbelief must
itself be examined. If it is no sin, it cannot cause damnation. If it is a sin, either Christ died for it or he did
not. If he did not, he did not die for every sin of every person. If he did, its continued obstruction of
salvation requires explanation. Returning to the statement that men do not believe merely repeats the
difficulty — Christ supposedly failed to remove unbelief because men would not remove it themselves, or
satisfied for their unbelief upon condition that they should not be unbelievers. Such assertions cannot
sustain a sober account of his work. Payment occurs at one historical date while its beneficiaries live at
different times. The saving relation therefore proceeds through promise, accomplishment, and application,
and cannot be measured by chronological proximity alone.
    [366] Accomplishment and enjoyment retain the order given them by the promise. Those who
received the fruits before the cross did not, through that reception, produce its later occurrence. Their
enjoyment depended upon the faithful provision of the undertaking whose historical consummation
remained appointed. The sequence within the beneficiary's life and knowledge therefore cannot exhaust
the dependence of that life upon its saving ground.
                [367] tx<to or tx≥to;        Ground(Ax)=ounder the appointed promise. (33)
     [368] Terms. The recipient's enjoyment occurs at t(x), while t(o) designates the historical offering's
date. Ground names a meritorious dependence, rather than a future temporal event acting efficiently
backward upon an earlier one. Divine promise and the efficacy proper to that promise remain constitutive
of the account.
     [369] Consequence. Comparison of calendar positions neither explains this relation nor refutes it.
Appointment receives historical fulfillment, and promise possesses its appointed efficacy. Together these
relations explain the salvation of saints before the cross, while preserving both the reality of the offering
accomplished there and the necessity of its accomplishment.
     [370] The seventh argument follows the unity of Christ's mediatorial office. His self-offering to God
through the shedding of his blood is a principal act of mediation. Those for whom he died are therefore
those for whom he mediates. Yet his mediation does not embrace every individual. As mediator he is
priest, and priesthood, as already established, unites sacrifice and intercession, the procurement of saving
goods and their application to the persons for whom they are procured. Christ manifestly does not
perform this whole undertaking for everyone. Experience confirms what innumerable scriptural
testimonies declare concerning the determinate subjects of his mediation. The proposed escape—that he
mediates for some persons in certain acts but excludes them from others—has no scriptural foundation. It
divides the office precisely where its unity supplies the argument, leaving Christ a partial mediator for
those whose procurement receives no corresponding application. Such an account cannot be sustained by
assigning separate populations to operations which belong to one undertaking. The preceding defence of
the argument already establishes this unity — the office joins the offering to the operations necessary for
its saving application, returning the inquiry to the complete undertaking established in paragraphs 91–
113. (Hebrews 9)
    [371] The eighth argument proceeds from sanctification as the fruit of Christ's death. If his blood
actually washes, purges, cleanses, and sanctifies the persons for whom it was shed and for whom he
became a sacrifice, those persons are necessarily the ones in whom these effects are eventually realized.
They cannot be every individual, since faith constitutes the first principle of purification in the heart, and
“all men have not faith”. Faith is “of the elect of God.” No distinction can remove the consequence while
retaining the premise that this particular benefit is effectually procured. That premise receives its warrant
in two connected ways — first, through the sacrifices which prefigure Christ's offering. Secondly, through
the express declarations concerning the offering itself. The restriction therefore follows from the asserted
efficacy of the sanctifying blood. Its procurement embraces the persons in whom the sanctification
secured by it comes to actuality, and the relevant texts determine whether the benefit is indeed secured in
that sense. (Acts 15:9; 2 Thessalonians 3:2; Titus 1:1)

    [372] The first proof concerns the expiatory sacrifice which the apostle expressly places in relation to
Christ's oblation. The sacrifice under the law sanctified those for whom it was offered, “For,” he writes,
“the blood of bulls and goats, and the ashes of an heifer sprinkling the unclean, sanctifies to the purifying
of the flesh.” What the type accomplished legally and carnally must receive its spiritual accomplishment
in the antitype, the sacrifice of Christ prefigured by that shedding of animal blood. The following verse
states this relation, “How much more,” he asks, “shall the blood of Christ, who through the eternal Spirit
offered himself without spot to God, purge your conscience from dead works to serve the living God?”
Arminius and others answer that the former sacrifice sanctified through sprinkling rather than through
offering, and that Christ's blood likewise sanctifies through application rather than oblation. The answer
supplies the division which the argument is examining, and thus assumes the very point it must establish.
Oblation and application can be distinguished. Their separation with respect to the persons represented
has not thereby been proved. Repeating that separation as an answer makes the disputed premise perform
the work of evidence. Sanctification does indeed occur through application of the goods procured by the
blood. The argument establishes, however, that this application reaches everyone for whom the blood was
an oblation — Scripture ascribes sanctifying and purging efficacy to it, and the fulfillment must answer
the type which sanctified to the purification of the flesh. The comparison therefore concerns an actual
purifying office, with determinate persons as its subjects. Both sides of the relation must retain what the
apostle actually predicates of them. Otherwise the proposed explanation empties the comparison of the
efficacy through which it proceeds. (Hebrews 9:13)
    [373] The second proof comes from the passages which expressly assign these effects to Christ's death
and bloodshed and identify them as its intended end. Many have already been examined. Enough are
recalled here to establish the present argument, while the earlier testimonies concerning the end of his
death retain their full force. Their saving verbs describe an operation producing effects in its recipients.
They cannot be reduced to the provision of an occasion upon which an independent human operation
might or might not occur. The extent of the conclusion must therefore be assessed with the causal force of
those predicates intact.
    [374] “For if we have been planted together in the likeness of his death, we shall be also in the
likeness of his resurrection: knowing this, that our old man is crucified with him, that the body of sin
might be destroyed, that henceforth we should not serve sin.” The latter declaration supplies the reason
for the former — participation in Christ's death carries conformity to his resurrection, both in spiritual life
and in eternal life, because “Because our old man is crucified with him, that the body of sin might be
destroyed.” The death and crucifixion of Christ effectually and meritoriously slay our sinful corruption
and the depravity of our nature, disabling the dominion by which they held us in service. This is the
passage's evident sense, since that accomplished relation supplies the foundation for advancing through
every degree of sanctification and freedom from sin's power. The exhortation follows an actual change in
those addressed. Death and resurrection belong together through union with Christ, and the conduct now
required of the recipient proceeds within that transformation. Its further development does not detach the
recipient's life from the death through which sin's former rule has been broken. (Romans 6:5–6)
    [375] The apostle further declares that “all the promises of God are in him yea, and in him Amen, unto
the glory of God by us.” Their “Yea, and Amen,” means that they are confirmed, ratified, immutably
established, and irrevocably conveyed to us. This occurs “in him,” through his death and bloodshed,
which confirm the testament whose promises convey its legacies. Confirmation requires the “death of
him, the testator”, and Christ is “the surety of this better testament”. The testament or “covenant he
confirmed with many,” receives that confirmation through his being “cut off” for its beneficiaries. The
content of these confirmed promises determines the consequence. Their scriptural summary, repeated by
the apostle to describe the covenant ratified in Jesus' blood, comprehends free grace in sanctification as
well as justification. Circumcision of the heart and the gift of new hearts and spirits belong among them.
Our whole sanctification and holiness, together with justification and reconciliation to God, are
consequently procured and immutably established through his death. Thus “the heavenly or spiritual

things” are purified by his sacrifice. “For we have redemption through his blood, even the forgiveness of
sins”. And “By death he destroyed him that had the power of death, that is, the devil,” in order to “deliver
them who, through fear of death, were all their lifetime subject to bondage.” Ratification binds the
promises to the sacrifice already accomplished, and the effects promised cannot be removed from the
persons for whom that ratification is affirmed. The covenant connects the shedding of blood with the
saving goods bestowed under its promises. (2 Corinthians 1:20; Hebrews 9:16, 23; 7:22; 8:10–12; 2:14–
15; Daniel 9:26–27; Jeremiah 31:33–34; Deuteronomy 30:6; Ezekiel 36:26; Colossians 1:14)
    [376] The two preceding testimonies expressly assign cleansing and sanctification to the intention of
Christ, the agent, and therefore to the certain effect of his death and oblation, the work, according to the
relation already proved. One further passage makes explicit what should scarcely require additional
defence, that Christ's blood purges us from sin, “Who of God is made unto us wisdom, and righteousness,
and sanctification, and redemption.” God made him these things for us by setting him forth as “a
propitiation through faith in his blood.” Sanctification, together with the other effects of free grace, is
therefore immediately procured through his death. The conclusion follows in its full extent — holiness
and sanctification are the certain fruit of that death in every person for whom he died. Every individual
does not receive this purging, cleansing, and production of holiness. Therefore Christ did not die for every
individual, “quod erat demonstrandum.” The self-giving has cleansing and presentation within both its
intention and its effect. Their later production may require further operations, but those operations
actualize a fruit already comprehended within the provision of the offering. (Titus 2:14; Ephesians 5:25–
26; 1 Corinthians 1:30; Romans 3:25)
    [377] The objection that Christ's death cannot be the sole cause of these benefits because their actual
production also requires the Spirit's operation and faith's apprehension confuses different kinds of
causality. First, several total causes of one kind cannot each constitute the same total causation, but causes
of different kinds can concur in one effect while each remains sole within its proper order. The Spirit
produces holiness as its immediate real efficient cause. Faith receives pardon instrumentally by
apprehending Christ's righteousness. Neither efficient renewal nor instrumental reception prevents the
blood from being the sole moral and meritorious cause of those benefits. Indeed, both presuppose that
meritorious ground — it founds the Spirit's operation and accounts for faith's own existence within this
saving work. Consider a captive whose ransom is paid to the enemy detaining him. Upon payment, the
captor grants a warrant directing the keepers to remove the shackles, exchange the prisoner's rags for new
clothing, and release him according to the agreement, “Deliver him, for I have found a ransom.” The
jailer's removal of the shackles and the warrant authorizing discharge do not diminish the ransom's place
as the sole cause of release in its own order. Without payment neither warrant nor release would have
followed. These intervening operations are themselves effects of the ransom, as truly as the liberty
accomplished through them. So the Spirit's operation and God's grace concur in deliverance from sin
under kinds of causation distinct from Christ's death, while remaining fruits of that death together with the
deliverance which they produce. The ransom supplies the meritorious ground of the whole. Secondly, the
objection is removed at its root when faith itself is shown to be an immediate fruit and procurement of
Christ's death in every person for whom he died. The next argument establishes this separately, because
the truth of that proposition overthrows a general ransom or universal redemption, whereas its denial
leaves free will established however the remaining controversy is decided. Owen accordingly treats it as
decisive for the whole dispute. Procurement, efficient renewal, and instrumental reception retain their
distinct offices — Christ merits, the Spirit renews, and faith receives. Their ordered concurrence produces
one salvation without making each an immediate efficient cause of the same change.
    [378] The proposed division of grace begins in a confusion of causal offices. Efficiency produces a
change in the subject. Instrumentality receives or applies according to an appointed relation. Merit
supplies the ground upon which a benefit is provided and rightfully bestowed. Since these descriptions
answer different questions, one salvation can depend upon the Spirit's efficient operation, the believer's

instrumental faith, and Christ's meritorious procurement. No multiplication of rival efficient causes
follows from that ordered dependence.
                                     [379] oNx,    NxFx,    FxAx. (34)
    [380] Terms. The accomplished offering is o. N denotes the renewing operation bestowed. F the
recipient's actual faith. A the relevant enjoyment. These designations specify kinds of dependence whose
composition expresses ordered concurrence. Procurement thereby remains meritorious rather than
physical, and faith receives without becoming an additional satisfaction.
    [381] Consequence. Grace's actual efficacy includes the recipient's own act. When the end is secured
through production of its necessary willing condition, the person truly wills. The account explains both
why he does so and the ground upon which renewal is bestowed. The dispute therefore reaches the cause
of voluntary believing as well as the condition fulfilled through that believing.
    [382] The ninth argument requires several premises concerning the causal distinctions already
established. Faith enters it as an actual disposition and act of a person, possessing a determinate source,
office, and consequence. These relations govern the dilemma which follows, and prevent the disputed
term from changing its meaning during the proof.
    [383] First, every good freely bestowed upon us in and through Christ belongs wholly to the merit and
procurement of his death. What reaches those who are his through him has been purchased by him, with
his own blood as its price. The covenant between Father and Son for communicating every spiritual
blessing to the persons given him rests expressly upon this condition, “That he should make his soul an
offering for sin.” The comprehensive premise therefore concerns saving goods actually bestowed through
Christ. Whether faith falls within that procurement is established through the scriptural and covenantal
relations developed in the ensuing argument, rather than by merely including its name in a general
formula. (1 Peter 1:18–19; Isaiah 53:10)
    [384] Secondly, all parties acknowledge faith's indispensable necessity to salvation in persons capable
of understanding. Under the new covenant no sacrifice supplies its absence — however much God's love
has accomplished in sending his Son, and however much Christ has performed or continues to perform
through oblation and intercession for some or for all, the event brings no saving profit to a person without
faith. Provision thus encountered serves instead to aggravate condemnation, since this declaration remains
certain whatever else has been accomplished, “He that believes not shall be damned.” If both the power of
believing and its exercise proceed from ourselves as our independent possession, we accordingly possess
the power to make God's love and Christ's death effectual toward us or to prevent their efficacy. Our
believing then supplies by our own act the decisive condition of their effect, a consequence which the
most discerning adversaries have expressly acknowledged in the material Owen discusses elsewhere.
Faith's absolute necessity therefore makes its cause principal within the account of salvation — it causes
that without which the whole would fail to be actual in the recipient and through which the whole
becomes effectual. The necessity of the condition concerns whether the entire provision issues in
salvation, so the source producing that condition determines an indispensable dependence within the
saving order. (Mark 16:16; Owen, A Display of Arminianism)
    [385] Thirdly, the adversary must answer a determinate question without concealing its alternatives
beneath obscure distinctions — did Christ, through his death and the intercession already shown to be
joined with it, merit and procure faith for us? Equivalently, does actual faith belong among the fruits and
effects of his death? The ensuing argument follows whichever answer is given. The disputed object is
faith actually exercised by persons. Publicly available doctrine, or a capacity whose exercise remains
unproduced, cannot be substituted for it without changing the question itself.
    [386] An affirmative answer has force only when the thing procured is faith as a grace in a particular
person. The doctrine believed and the way of salvation made public in the gospel are objective things, and
to substitute either for that grace is to equivocate upon the question. We deny, indeed, that the granting of

the new way in which life and immortality are brought to light in Christ was itself procured by his
oblation. He is the principal thing given in that way and is himself the way. To make him the purchase of
his own death is to make the gift the procuring cause of its own grant, a contradiction appropriate to the
reasoning that produced it. The entire passage of the elect to life and glory through that way is his
accomplishment, which we maintain against all objections. The opening of the way proceeds from the
same free and unprocured love that first gave him. With those relations distinguished, did Christ obtain
faith for all on whose behalf he died absolutely, or subject to the fulfillment of a condition by them? An
inheritance procured absolutely belongs to its recipient whatever means obtained it, and the same relation
holds here. On universal redemption every person would therefore believe, although the apostle declares
“All men have not faith” and “Faith is of the elect of God.” A conditional answer must specify bona fide
the act on which the bestowal depends. The matter concerns every soul, and neither obscure distinctions
nor qualified phrases tell us what anybody must do. Name a condition that bears the proposed weight, and
en herbam amici! The cause is yours. Some require that the person should not resist God's grace, but to
refrain from resisting it in the requisite sense is to obey it, and to obey it in this matter is to believe. On
that account Christ procured believing on the condition that the person believes. The alleged explanation
merely restates the event it undertakes to explain. If they can identify a condition distinct from faith, we
ask whether Christ procured that condition. If he did not, the cause of faith still resides in us, and Christ is
no longer its author and finisher. If he did, the original question returns concerning that prior act. Was it
procured absolutely, or does it depend upon another act on our part? Depinge ubi sistam. So long as actual
bestowal is included in the effect, the people for whom faith is procured must include the people who
receive it, and every proposed antecedent condition incurs the same demand for a sufficient cause.
(Thomas More, The Universality of God’s Free Grace, 35; Genesis 3:15; 2 Thessalonians 3:2; Titus 1:1;
Facciolati, as cited in the editor’s note to Owen, The Death of Death III.4)
    [387] Polycarp calls faith a gift, and thereby gives the relation its proper direction. If the gift is
bestowed only after an antecedent condition has been fulfilled, the reason that the condition is fulfilled
belongs to the explanation of the gift's reception. Moving the unexplained act backward leaves the
person's actual believing as unaccounted for as it was before. The saving result still hangs upon an event
for which the proposed provision has supplied no sufficient cause. (Polycarp, Epistle to the Philippians 3)
    [388] Suppose the death procures faith for everyone whom it represents. If what is procured is the
actual bestowal of faith, representation of all entails that all believe. If its bestowal instead awaits a
further act, the act requires its own cause. Adding a condition to the proposition changes no person's
inward state and supplies no operation by which the sequence can begin.
                              [389] [∀x∈R,◇Fx]∧[R=H]⇒∀x∈H,◇Fx. (35)
    [390] Terms. The first premise specifies the efficacy of the procurement of faith, and the second
assigns the universal extent proposed for it. Substitution carries the asserted effect across exactly the same
domain. Whoever denies the conclusion must give up or qualify one of those premises.
     [391] Consequence. Whoever ascribes the actual procurement of faith to a death undergone for every
person owes an account of that effect in every person. To substitute a benefit available but never received
at the point where the consequence follows is to change what the argument asserts. The universal
proposition has all the consequences of the effect attributed to it.
   [392] The negative answer, which follows more consistently from the adversaries' own principles,
denies that Christ procured faith by his death. It thereby assigns the indispensable act of believing to an
operation outside the procurement to which it attributes the efficacy of salvation.
    [393] 1. On this account the will produces faith by its own power, independently of any grace working
that act in it. For, as established above, the free gifts that reach us in and through Christ are the gifts
purchased in his death and oblation. The denial therefore conflicts (1.) with the many places in which
Scripture expressly ascribes believing to grace and (2.) with the constitution of the new covenant, whose

operation upon everyone included in it effectually produces the condition it requires. It also (3.)
diminishes the free grace of God by raising the will in corrupt nature to the place occupied by grace and
(4.) contradicts the received doctrine of our natural depravity and incapacity for spiritual good, so that in
its plain consequence it overthrows the fundamental article of original sin. Finally, (5.) it contradicts
reason, because the existence of a natural faculty establishes neither its competence for a purely spiritual
act nor the occurrence of that act. The faculty requires a spiritual elevation adequate to the act. Denying
its procurement gives no account of how the fallen will acquires the very capacity which the proposed
account requires it to exercise. (Jeremiah 31:33–34; Ezekiel 36:26; Hebrews 8:10–11; 1 Corinthians 2:14)
    [394] 2. The denial also makes ourselves nearly the sole ultimate cause of our salvation. It assigns to
us the power to bring all that God and Christ have done for that end to effect or to frustrate their utmost
endeavors. The Father's love in sending his Son to die for us and the Son's self-offering in our stead or on
our behalf yield, by the adversary's admission, no profitable issue unless we believe. Yet Christ, on this
supposition, neither effected nor procured our believing through his death. The Lord cannot work it in us
in such a way that the decision whether we believe ceases to be ours alone. Whatever precedes the
decision thus remains ineffectual until an independent act supplies what is lacking. Whoever is given that
final power is given the cause that completes the outcome, which is why this account makes us the
authors of our happiness and the chief builders of our glory. The dispute over the praise of grace follows
from the location of that decisive cause.
    [395] The death of Christ procures faith itself, which is the point that the negative answer denies. The
several grounds that follow establish actual believing as an effect of his death. Joined to the admitted fact
that “all men have not faith:” this establishes that he did not die for each and every person.
    [396] 1. The eighth argument established at length that the death of Jesus Christ purchased holiness
and sanctification. Faith, understood as a grace of the Spirit inhering in us, is formally a part of that
holiness. The consequence is that Christ purchased faith, since the premise concerning sanctification has
been proved and the premise concerning what belongs to it is admitted. An adversary may suggest that
Christ procures the sanctification of hope, love, meekness, and other graces, while withholding faith from
the purchase. This concerns the parts of holiness, as distinct from degrees or measure within a part, and
the proposed division requires two things of its defender. He must show what authorizes him to put some
of the Spirit's graces within Christ's purchase and derive others from our own resources. He must also
give some ground for supposing us naturally more inclined and able to believe than to love or hope. The
bare fact that the graces are distinguishable supplies no reason to distribute their causes in that fashion.
     [397] 2. Jesus Christ purchases all the fruits of election. The declaration that “we are chosen in him”
places him as the sole fountain of the good things to which the Lord chooses us, to the praise of his
glorious grace and with Christ preeminent in everything. By him and for him the Lord actually and
certainly confers on the elect all the effects intended in the love by which he chose them. Faith is among
its principal fruits. The apostle says “We are chosen in him before the foundation of the world, that we
should be holy”, and in that holiness the faith that purifies the heart has a principal share. “Moreover,
whom he did predestinate, them he also called” describes the calling according to his purpose, in which
the mighty operation of the Spirit effectually works faith “according to the exceeding greatness of his
power.” God himself makes the distinction in the enjoyment of the means, so that they “believe” “who are
ordained to eternal life.” Their appointment to eternal life is the fountain from which their faith flows, and
thus “the election has obtained, and the rest were blinded.” Election supplies the purpose ordering the
event, and procurement through Christ supplies the means by which its fruit is actually conferred in time.
(Ephesians 1:4, 19; Romans 8:30; 11:7; 1 Corinthians 4:7; Acts 13:48)
    [398] 3. The one to whom the new covenant's promises are made and in whom they are ratified
purchases every blessing they contain. Those promises are the form in which the covenant's good things
are held forth and, by the operation of the Spirit of God, conferred. The confirmation of the promises in
Christ and their extension to his people were established in paragraphs 333–340. Christ and all that is

bestowed for his sake are the substance of the covenant, and its benefits accordingly issue as the purchase
of his death. The description of the covenant itself places faith among those good things, since the
promise extends to the renewal of the heart and so to the actual production of the inward condition. Other
passages establish the same point, though no abundance of examples is required in causa facili. (Galatians
3:16; Jeremiah 31:33–34; Hebrews 8:10–12; Ezekiel 36:25–27)
    [399] 4. Whoever fully and effectually procures salvation must procure the indispensable means
without which nobody can attain it. Christ is the author of salvation by purchase. An account which gives
him the full accomplishment of the end but withholds from his purchase a condition upon which its very
possibility depends leaves that accomplishment in the power of another cause. Now faith is indispensable
to salvation, and Jesus Christ, in keeping with his name, saves perfectly. He procures “eternal
redemption” and is “able to save to the uttermost them that come unto God by him”. Faith therefore
belongs to his procurement as a necessary means of the salvation he secures. (Hebrews 11:6; 9:12; 7:25;
Mark 16:16; Matthew 1:21)
    [400] An end secured by an appointed provision has within its ordained course the actual production
of each condition indispensable to it. The recipient's believing need not be meritorious for its production
to be necessary to the undertaking, and nothing in the argument attributes merit to every event of a
person's life. It follows from the sufficiency Scripture ascribes to this particular provision that the
provision cannot leave the possibility of salvation suspended upon a necessity for which it makes no
provision.
                         [401] Secure(d,Sx)∧□(Sx⇒◇Fx)⇒Secure(d,◇Fx). (36)
    [402] Terms. Secure designates the efficacy of the appointed provision, and the box marks the
necessity of faith for salvation stipulated in this account. The inference places the securing of that faith
somewhere in the ordained history. Its force depends upon security consisting in accomplishment by a
provision sufficient in actuality.
     [403] Consequence. A doctrine which claims to secure salvation is tested where its end depends upon
an inward event. It must supply the faith in whose absence salvation is impossible. The ground for
attributing that provision to Christ lies in the covenant and in the particular gift already established by the
preceding argument.
     [404] 5. Scripture ascribes the gift of believing to Christ in terms that cannot be evaded. “It is given
unto us, ὑπὲρ Χριστοῦ, on the behalf of Christ, for Christ’s sake, to believe on him.” The thing given is
faith, and the person for whose sake it is given is its procurer. “God has blessed us with all spiritual
blessings in him in heavenly places.” If faith is a spiritual blessing, it reaches us “in him,” and therefore
for his sake. If it is no spiritual blessing, the present dispute over its place among those blessings has no
object. Whatever direction others may look, we look to Jesus as the “author and finisher of our faith.”
Further arguments and testimonies could be adduced, but the ones given suffice for the inference. If the
effect which Christ's death procures and produces absolutely, independently of any condition to be
supplied by man, is not common to all, his death was not for all. Faith is just such an effect. It was
procured by his death for absolute bestowal upon those on whose behalf he died, and it is not common to
all. Our Savior therefore did not die for all. The formal statement records the causal dependence expressly
affirmed by Scripture and receives its force from that evidence. (Philippians 1:29; Ephesians 1:3;
Hebrews 12:2)
   [405] Arg. X. The correspondence between the type and the thing signified confines the oblation of
Christ to the elect of God. The apostle treats the remarkable events of Israel's history as typical of God's
church, and its institutions represent the spiritual things of the gospel. Priests, altar, and sacrifices were
shadows of the good things to come in Jesus Christ, and Canaan, Jerusalem, and Sion were figures of
heaven. The people as a whole represented the elect church that God chooses and calls. The designation
“holy people, a royal priesthood;” is therefore applied in allusion to them to believers, whom Scripture

repeatedly calls God's “Israel,” A true Israelite is a true believer, and the Jew in the sense here in view is
such in the hidden man of the heart. The deliverance of the people from bondage, their preservation, their
being brought near to God, and their entrance into Canaan thus supply the ordered course by which the
spiritual church is represented. Those whom Israel represented in its bodily and typical redemption are the
persons actually and spiritually redeemed by Christ. There is no ground for saying that some who share a
given condition and participate in the same benefit are typified in it while others who are supposedly in
precisely the same relation are excluded. Yet Israel's escape from Egypt and entrance into Canaan,
together with its institutions and ordinances, signify the elect alone. The claim that the type extended to
the world is senseless on its face. Were the Israelites and their ordinances types of the seven nations they
destroyed and displaced in Canaan, or of the Egyptians, unbelievers, and haters of God and his Christ?
Scripture identifies both the people and the benefits that correspond, and the due proportion between
figure and fulfillment therefore confines redemption by Jesus Christ to God's chosen church. (1
Corinthians 10:11; Hebrews 4:3, 9; 12:22; 8:8; 9; 10; Galatians 4:26; 1 Peter 2:5, 9; John 1:47)
    [406] Arg. XI. Sound doctrine accords with the thing it signifies and with the scriptural language that
directly states or necessarily implies it. An account that contradicts both cannot possess the sincerity of
the milk of the word. Universal redemption does precisely that, since neither the fact signified by
redemption nor the expressions under which Scripture sets it forth can be made to agree with it. To say
that all are redeemed while many die in captivity asserts together a deliverance and the continued bondage
from which it supposedly delivers. The term signifies an actual effect, and anyone who removes that
effect owes an account of the passages in which Scripture ascribes it.
    [407] The scriptural descriptions of Christ's death put the doctrine to a more exact test, because each
identifies a relation whose effect must be preserved in any account of the whole. Scripture speaks of
redemption, reconciliation, satisfaction and merit, of his dying for us and bearing our sins, and of his
suretyship. It speaks also of his being God, a common person, and a Jesus, of his saving to the utmost,
and of his sacrifice as putting away sin. The original prepositions and other words help determine these
relations. They all concern the same death, but the immediate effect denoted in one expression cannot be
substituted indiscriminately for that denoted in another. The general ransom, or universal redemption, fits
none of them without being cut to the measure of the bed.
    [408] Redemption, called λύτρωσις in some passages and more often ἀπολύτρωσις, is the deliverance
of a person from captivity and misery through a price or ransom, λύτρου. The blood of Christ is the price
of our deliverance. He calls it λύτρον, and it is also called ἀντίλυτρον, the valuable consideration received
for our release. What the payment is directed to accomplish is the deliverance from the evil suffered by
the persons for whom it is made. Civil and bodily redemption have that structure in common with
spiritual redemption, subject to the differences belonging to the thing itself. Thus the Holy Spirit
compares the “blood of Christ” with “silver and gold,” and the other considerations accepted as ransom in
civil affairs. The evil under which we lay was the punishment we deserved, which is the satisfaction
exacted when the debt is sin. Payment releases us from it, for we are “justified freely by his grace,
through the redemption that is in Christ Jesus” and Christ is the one “in whom we have redemption
through his blood, the forgiveness of sins.” Free justification answers to guilt, and pardon releases us
from the punishment owed for sin. These are effects procured by the payment, as when a man lays out his
estate to pay to a captor the price set on his friend's liberty, and thereby frees him. The price, the person
whose release is in view, the authority accepting satisfaction, and the actual deliverance belong to the
structure of the operation. Its spiritual instance nevertheless possesses several features for which civil
ransom has no counterpart. (Matthew 20:28; 1 Timothy 2:6; 1 Peter 1:18–19; Galatians 3:13; Romans
3:24; Ephesians 1:7; Colossians 1:14)
    [409] First, the authority who receives the ransom also provides it. Christ is the propitiation by which
the Lord is appeased, and it is the Lord who sets him forth in that office. Scripture therefore repeatedly
says that the Lord himself redeems us. His love is the cause of the price as provided, and his justice
accepts it in respect of its merit, for Christ “came down from heaven to do the will of him that sent him.”

In ordinary ransom the party who receives the price has no share in providing it. Here the divine gift of
the ransom precedes the recipient's acquisition of its fruits, so that grace belongs to the very source and
appointment of the payment. (Romans 3:24–25; John 6:38; Hebrews 10:9–10)
    [410] Secondly, the captive is brought into the favor of the authority under whom he was detained. In
civil redemption, payment at once frees the captive from the authority and power of his captor. Upon
payment of our ransom in the blood of Jesus we are “brought nigh” to God, and continue under his power
as people restored to his favor. Our punishment included banishment as well as bondage. The same
operation that releases us from liability therefore changes our relation to the authority whose justice was
satisfied, and issues in the positive good of reconciliation. (Ephesians 2:13)
    [411] Thirdly, the satisfaction due to the judge was joined with the conquest of the jailer. God, who
judges, permitted him to fight for the dominion he had usurped, though the judgment under which he
acquired it was justly inflicted by the Lord and our bondage was deserved. Strong as he was, he lost his
power by grasping beyond the ground on which it stood. Sin was the foundation of his kingdom, and by
attacking Christ, who did no sin and in whom he had no part, he lost his power over the persons Christ
came to redeem. So the strong man was bound and his house spoiled. Satisfaction answers to the judge's
claim, and conquest deprives the detaining power of its dominion. Their distinct effects concur in the
completeness of the deliverance. (Hebrews 2:14; Colossians 2:15)
    [412] In these circumstances and a few others, spiritual redemption differs from civil redemption,
while the proper meaning of the term remains in force. Scripture expresses it in two ways. Our Savior dies
for our redemption and for the redemption of our transgressions, and the expressions signify the same
thing. In εἰς ἀπολύτρωσιν παραβάσεων· some find a metonymy in which transgressions stand for
transgressors. Others take it as a proper expression for the payment of a price by which we are delivered
from the evil incurred through our transgressions. In the other form, attested in several passages, λύτρον
and ἀπολύτρωσις occur together. These words, and αντίλυτρον in particular, have a settled meaning. They
denote payment of a price or equal compensation for something to be done or granted by the party who
receives it. That common structure makes the term intelligible, while the specific causal relations
determining the extent and consequences of spiritual release must be taken from the saving undertaking
itself. It is according to those relations that the universal claim is to be judged. (Hebrews 9:15; Ephesians
1:7; Matthew 20:28; Mark 10:45; 1 Timothy 2:6)
     [413] A ransom received for a prisoner's liberty gives him a just claim to enjoy the liberty purchased.
If I pay a thousand pounds to the man who detains another and has power to release him, and the man
accepts that price as sufficient, his refusal to deliver the captive wrongs both the prisoner and me. In the
same way the conception of a redemption accomplished without the persons being redeemed, or of a price
paid without the purchase being consummated, dissolves the very relation under discussion. Universal
redemption requires all these absurdities together. The price is paid for everyone and few are delivered.
The redemption of all is consummated and few are redeemed. The judge is satisfied and the jailer
conquered, yet the prisoner remains in bondage. The terms “universal” and “redemption,” where the
greater part of men perish agree as well as “Roman” and “Catholic.” If all have been redeemed, then all
have been delivered, virtually or actually, by the intervention of a ransom from the misery that held them.
Why then are all not saved? Redemption by Christ is the full deliverance of the redeemed from the misery
in which they lay, bought at the price of his blood. The efficacy of that price includes the appointed
passage to liberty for those whose release it secures. Consequently the Universalists cannot make their
account fit redemption unless all are saved.
    [414] Deliverance occurs in an ordered history. The person under bondage and the same person in
liberty occupy different states, and the passage from the one state to the other is the operation of release.
An accepted ransom secures that passage through the authority and the appointed means by which the
benefit is applied. The procurement and the release can belong to different historical moments without
any diminution of the actual change in state.

                              [415] Boundx(t0)∧Rx⇒∃t1>t0 Freex(t1). (37)
    [416] Terms. Bound and Free identify the states of bondage and liberty under the saving relation in
view. R is the appointed procurement, and the inequality places the application after the prior state
described.
    [417] Consequence. If a price is accepted as ransom and every appointed passage to liberty is
nevertheless excluded, the effect attributed to the ransom has been changed. To answer the argument
requires contesting the relation between accepted procurement and release. The fact that the captive was
formerly in bondage establishes the condition from which he is released.
    [418] Arg. XII. Reconciliation is an effect of Christ's death, and all sides allow that it extends to
everyone for whom he died. Scripture ascribes to the death and blood of Jesus Christ both the reconciling
of God to us and the reconciling of us to God, for those who were “enemies he reconciled in the body of
his flesh through death.” These aspects answer to each other. Everyone to whom he reconciles God he
also reconciles to God, since peace made on only one side leaves the relation unreconciled. The proposed
division will bear examination in neither direction. If God is reconciled while man is left at enmity, what
prevents God from reconciling him, given that he has the power to do so? If man is reconciled while God
remains unreconciled, how can God be ready to receive all who come to him? Yet no one will maintain
that God and every person in the world are actually at peace in Jesus Christ. The extent of the effect must
therefore be determined by the persons whose relation of enmity is changed. The gospel gives the
substance of this change, and the usage of the word in civil matters sheds light upon the relation it
expresses. (Colossians 1:21–22)
    [419] Reconciliation renews friendship between parties formerly at variance, so that the offending and
the offended party are both properly said to be reconciled. Sin put God and man at a distance. The offense
was man's and the alienation was mutual, with this difference, that man was alienated in the affections
from which anger and enmity proceed, while God's alienation lay in the effects and issue of anger and
enmity. The New Testament uses καταλλαγή and καταλλάσσω, reconciliation and to reconcile, both
deriving from ἀλλάττω, to change or to turn from one thing or mind to another. Their native sense is
accordingly permutatio and permutare (so Arist. Eth. 3, Τὸν βίον πρὸς μικρὰ κέρδη — καταλλάττονται,).
The extension to reconciliation is intelligible because the affections of the parties commonly change, and
their variance, distance, and effects always do. No affair or pair of persons can be described by the term
until the parties are actually reconciled and the differences arising from former resentment are removed. If
one is well pleased and the other remains ἀκατάλλακτος, unappeased and implacable, the relation of

friendship has not been restored. In the command concerning a person who remembers at the altar that his
brother has something against him, our Savior requires that he go and be reconciled to the brother. The
command directs a mutual return of minds, with particular regard to the appeasing of the party offended.
Common speech likewise denotes by reconciliation a full redintegration of friendship, usually with
reference to some compensation to the offended party. What is effected in either party can be
distinguished, but both effects are necessary to an entire reconciliation. An account must therefore specify
the grounds of the variance and how it is removed in the parties who stood in it. (Aristotle, as identified in
the editor’s note to Owen, The Death of Death III.6)
   [420] Melito's account of captivity under sin and death and Diognetus's account of inability and the
need for justifying righteousness together expose several defects of the fallen condition. Reconciliation
must reach the grounds of the variance and restore the persons who are at variance. The operations by
which those things occur have distinguishable effects even when they are directed to one saving result.
Providing for the removal of one defect while leaving the other wholly unprovided cannot yield the
reconciliation ascribed to Christ. Follow a spoke all the way to the Hub and the question becomes what
Christ has actually done to bring the person to God. The account must reach the enmity and the bondage
together, for both concern the person reconciled. (Melito of Sardis, On Pascha 50–56; Epistle to
Diognetus 9)

    [421] Socinus and his followers make the reconciliation of Scripture consist wholly in our conversion
to God, leaving God's anger unappeased and his wrath unremoved. The result is a reconciliation hopping
on one leg. Equally monstrous is the invention that God is reconciled to every man while only a small
number of those men are reconciled to him. Mutual alienation requires mutual reconciliation, for the
terms are correlata. Before Christ reconciled us, the relation between God and man was enmity. We were
his “enemies”, hating him and employing our utmost powers against him in rebellion. He was an enemy
to us in the sense that his “wrath” lay upon us and remains upon us until we believe. The perfect
reconciliation ascribed to Christ requires the removal of God's anger and of all its effects toward us, and it
requires our being turned from opposition to voluntary obedience. Until both have been accomplished the
relation has not been fully changed. Scripture assigns both operations to the death and sacrifice of our
Savior. Their efficacy consists in bringing the relation to peace, which the announcement of peace alone
could never accomplish. (Colossians 1:21; Romans 5:10; Ephesians 2:3; John 3:36)
    [422] 1. Christ reconciled God to us by his death, turning away wrath and appeasing him toward us.
Scripture says that “when we were enemies, we were reconciled to God by the death of his Son.” This is
the part of reconciliation by which God chiefly commends his love to us, forgiving sin by turning aside
the anger due to it. Its meaning is apparent also from the contrast with deliverance from the wrath to come
at the end of the verse, which comprehends our conversion and the entire reconciliation to God.
Moreover, we are said to receive τὴν καταλλαγήν, this “reconciliation”, rendered “atonement” in our
translation without clear reason. The thing received cannot be our own conversion, which we cannot
properly be said to accept or receive. It is the reconciliation of God to us, received as faith apprehends it.
The judicial relation is addressed by the accepted offering, and its change is distinguishable from the new
disposition and recognition that subsequently arise in the recipient. (Romans 5:10–11)
    [423] 2. Christ turns us from our enmity and reconciles us to God through “the blood of his cross”.
The death effects this meritoriously and satisfactorily, by acquiring and purchasing the benefit, and his
Spirit effectually brings it about in due time. Scripture presents both operations together. God's
reconciliation to us in Christ consists in the non-imputation of iniquities and supplies the matter
announced in the ministry. Our reconciliation to God, in accepting the pardon of our sins, is the end
toward which that ministry is directed. The accomplishment of both constitutes the reconciliation effected
by Christ's death. The procurement occurs “simul et semel,” and the effect on the side of men is brought
to completion over time in the ordinances of the gospel, as the Spirit overcomes their enmity upon the
meritorious ground already established. Scripture therefore says “We are reconciled to God by the death
of his Son” and “And you, that were sometime alienated, has he reconciled in the body of his flesh
through death”. The recurrence of such expressions leaves no ground for denying that reconciliation is the
immediate effect and product of the death of Christ. (Colossians 1:20–22; 2 Corinthians 5:18–20;
Ephesians 2:13–15; Romans 5:10)
    [424] If reconciliation is the proper effect of the death of Christ, as all sides confess, its asserted
universality must answer six difficulties. First, why is God not reconciled to all? His wrath abides upon
some, and reconciliation consists on his part in turning away wrath. Secondly, why are all not reconciled
to him? The apostle says that “by nature all are the children of wrath”, and some spend their whole lives
in rebellion and “treasure up wrath against the day of wrath.” Thirdly, what could a reconciliation
between God and all men consist in if God is not reconciled to all and all are not reconciled to him?
Fourthly, if God was reconciled to all, when did he become unreconciled to those who perish, and through
what change in his will or nature? Fifthly, if all were reconciled by the death of Christ, when did those
who perish become unreconciled, seeing they were born children of wrath? Sixthly, the turning away of
God's wrath and the non-imputation of sin constitute the justification in which we are blessed. Why then,
if God is reconciled to all, are all not justified and made blessed by having their sin no longer imputed?
Those who have invented a redemption without anyone redeemed and a reconciliation without anyone
reconciled may take their time over these questions. The effect which the death and blood of Christ
produce is the restoration of friendship through the destruction of enmity and the making of peace. It

appeases God and turns away his wrath through the non-imputation of iniquity, and on our side turns us to
God through faith and repentance. Christ is said to have died for the people in whom the acts and
properties constituting this effect can truly be affirmed. The reader may judge whether they can be
affirmed of everyone. The exclusion of some from the changed relation is directly contrary to the
universality attributed to the effect. (John 3:36; Ephesians 2:3; Romans 2:5; 4:6–8; 2 Corinthians 5:18–
19)
    [425] The judicial ground of enmity and the person's hostile disposition can be designated separately
because they are different relations. The offering answers the first in its appointed office, and the Spirit
renews the person upon the ground thus supplied. Neither is figurative of the other. Reconciliation in its
completeness consists in their ordained concurrence for the same person.
                                              [426] ,     . (38)
    [427] Terms. J denotes judicial standing in the relation at issue, and W the person's disposition. The
accepted offering o acts in its meritorious and judicial office, while N designates the efficient operation of
renewal. The covenant determines the temporal mode in which the judicial benefit is applied.
    [428] Consequence. The announcement of reconciliation discloses the operations that constitute it.
The judicial act and the renewal of the person's inward disposition occur through their respective causal
offices, which cannot be supplied by the mere naming of them. One redemption secures both actual
changes because each does its own work within the whole.
    [429] Arg. XIII. Christ's death for sinners is also expressed as satisfaction, since he answers to the
justice of God for the sins of those for whom he dies in order that they go free. Neither the Latin nor the
English Bible uses the word satisfaction of Christ's death. It occurs nowhere in the New Testament and
twice in the Old, but the thing meant by it is everywhere attributed to the death of our Savior, and the
original languages have equivalent expressions. That he made satisfaction for the sins of all for whom he
died is confessed by everyone outwardly called by his name, so far as the dispute shows, apart from the
wretched Socinians, whose position is not in view here. Whether the admitted satisfaction can be
universal depends upon what obligation it answers, who undertakes it in the debtor's place, and on whose
behalf that performance is accepted. (Numbers 35:31–32)
    [430] Satisfaction is a legal term applied properly to things and from there accommodated to persons.
It denotes full compensation to a creditor from a debtor. A person becomes another man's creditor insofar
as that man owes him something, and the debtor remains obliged to pay or restore it until the obligation is
lawfully annulled through satisfaction of what the creditor can require under it. If I owe a man a hundred
pounds by a bond, I remain his debtor until something recompenses him and induces him to cancel the
bond. This is satisfaction. In the extension of the term from things to persons, injuries and faults are debts
because the person who commits them becomes liable to punishment. The person charged with inflicting
the punishment or seeing it inflicted can in this relation be called the creditor, and must act unless
satisfaction is made. Two kinds must be distinguished. First, the very thing in the obligation may be paid
by the person bound or by another in his stead. If I owe twenty pounds and my friend pays them, the
creditor is fully satisfied. Secondly, the payment may consist in another kind of thing which the creditor
accepts in place of what is owed. Release follows also in that case, by an act of favor in accepting a
different consideration. The amount of a payment alone cannot determine its juridical effect, which
depends upon the authority receiving it, the obligation it answers, and the relation in which the debtor is
discharged.
    [431] In the present matter, first, the debtor is man, who owes the ten thousand talents. Secondly, the
debt is sin, as we confess when we say “Forgive us our debts.” Thirdly, death is the satisfaction required
for sin. “In the day that you eat thereof, you shall surely die” states the sentence, and “The wages of sin is
death.” states its ground. Fourthly, the obligation binding the debtor is the law expressed in “Cursed is
every one,” and the rest, together with the justice and truth of God. Fifthly, God requires the debt as

creditor, being the offended party, the severe Judge, and the supreme Lord of all. Sixthly, the intervention
that destroys the obligation is the ransom Christ pays. “God set him forth to be a propitiation through faith
in his blood.” The debt and its debtor, the authority who receives payment, the surety, and the acceptance
of his performance belong to distinguishable relations. The consequence of the whole cannot be derived
from the name of any one of them in isolation. (Matthew 18:24; 6:12; Genesis 2:17; 3:3; Romans 6:23;
1:32; 3:25; Galatians 3:10; Deuteronomy 27:26)
    [432] Two questions concerning satisfaction bear directly upon the extent of redemption. The first is
whether Christ made satisfaction and in what his performance consisted. The second is what act toward
the human debtor properly follows from a satisfaction thus made and accepted. The name itself does not
occur in Scripture in this application, but the thing meant by it, the compensation Christ renders to God
for our debts, occurs repeatedly. The thing required is determined by the kind of debt that stands in the
obligation. Where the debt is sin, what the justice of God requires is that the punishment due to that sin be
undergone. It is in undergoing that punishment, therefore, that Christ makes satisfaction. Scripture
ascribes the act to him in these terms. It says that “his own self bore our sins in his own body on the tree”
and “By his knowledge shall my righteous servant justify many, for he shall bear their iniquities.” The
