word ( ‫ָנ ָׂש א‬nasa) signifies the removal of the punishment of sin from us and its transfer to himself, which
expresses the whole relation meant by satisfaction. Peter's ἀνήνεγκεν answers to it, since to bear iniquity
in scriptural speech is to undergo the punishment that is due to it. Scripture identifies how Christ bore it
when it says he was “wounded for our transgressions, and bruised for our iniquities” and adds that “the
chastisement of our peace was upon him.” Chastisement is either νουθετική, directed to instruction, or
παραδειγματική, directed to example, punishment and correction. The Son of God had no need to be
instructed through such thorns and briers. His chastisement therefore belongs to the second kind, and the
sins for which it came upon him were the sins laid upon him. What he underwent as punishment procured
our peace as freedom from punishment. The answer to the second question must accordingly follow from
the acceptance of a performance of this kind for the persons whose debts were laid upon him. (1 Peter
2:24; Isaiah 53:5, 11–12; Leviticus 5:1)
    [433] The New Testament describes the same performance through several expressions that converge
upon its representative and judicial character. It calls the death προσφορά·, Παρέδωκεν ἑαυτὸν
προσφορὰν καὶ θυσίαν, an oblation or sacrifice of expiation, as the type with which it is compared makes
clear. The Hebrew word ( ‫ָא ָׁש ם‬ascham) has the same force in “He made his soul an offering for sin,”
which speaks of a piacular sacrifice for sin's removal. The apostle likewise says that he was made
ἁμαρτία, “sin” itself, placing the name of sin upon its adjunct, the punishment due to it. The designation
ἱλασμός answers to the Hebrew chitte used in “,‫ָאֹנִכ י ֲא ַח ֶּט ָּנ ה‬Ego illud expiabam,” where the act is the
undertaking of a debt and the making of compensation, the office of Job's goël. These and the other
expressions to be considered signify Christ's taking upon himself the full punishment due to sin, and his
rendering in his self-offering what pleased the offended God more than all the sins of all those for whom
he suffered had offended him. Nothing can more completely satisfy an offended party than a performance
in which he takes greater pleasure than he had displeasure in the offense. The obedience, offering, and
sacrifice of God's Son therefore pleased him more than the rebellions of all the elect had displeased him.
Suppose the subjects of a good king rebel because they will not have him reign over them, moving him to
destroy them, and the king's only son intercedes for their pardon. He might tender an excellent conquest
lately achieved, asking his father to accept it and return his subjects to favor. Or, more nearly to the case,
he might offer to undergo the punishment justice had assigned to the rebels and actually undergo it. He
would thereby make satisfaction for the offense, and strict justice would require their pardon. Christ is the
one hircus, ἀποπομπαῖος, the goat sent away bearing all the sins of God's people and falling himself under
them, assured of breaking the bonds of death and living forever. The distinction already made between
solutio ejusdem, payment of the same thing contained in the obligation, and solutio tantidem, payment of
something different and equivalent only by the creditor's gracious acceptance, now determines the
inquiry. Which of these did our Savior perform? (Ephesians 5:2; Hebrews 9:13–14; Isaiah 53:10;
Leviticus 7:2; 2 Corinthians 5:21; 1 John 2:2; Genesis 31:39; Job 19:25)

    [434] The writer many regard as having handled this question most exactly denies that Christ's
payment for us was solutio ejusdem, payment of the thing directly contained in the obligation. Payment is
used here by analogy for undergoing the punishment owed for sin. He offers two reasons. The first is that
a payment of the same thing is accompanied by actual release from the obligation. The second is that it
leaves no place for remission or pardon. In his words, “It is true,” “deliverance follows upon it; but this
deliverance cannot be by way of gracious pardon, for there needs not the interceding of any such act of
grace. But now,” and “that satisfaction whereby some other thing is offered than that which was in the
obligation may be admitted or refused, according as the creditor pleases; and being admitted for any, it is
by an act of grace; and such was the satisfaction made by Christ.” Neither reason establishes the
conclusion. What counts as the same satisfaction and how discharge follows from it must first be
determined, for the objection assumes both relations in order to deny one of their consequences. (Grotius,
Defensio Fidei Catholicae de Satisfactione Christi contra F. Socinum)
    [435] The first reason assumes the very point at issue, namely that actual freedom from the obligation
does not follow Christ's satisfaction. By death he actually delivered us from death, in such a sense that the
elect are said to die and rise with him. By becoming a curse for us he delivered us from the curse actually,
or ipso facto, and the handwriting against us, which contained the whole obligation, was taken away and
nailed to his cross. All those for whom he did this cannot perceive and apprehend it at once. They
nonetheless have actual right to the fruits of his death before possessing them in actuality, and actual
possession requires at least that the matter be made known to them. A captive held abroad has a right to
liberty on the very day his ransom is paid and accepted, although he cannot enjoy liberty before the news
arrives and a warrant for his release is produced. To infer from later enjoyment that no discharge was
secured is a begging τοῦ ἐν ἀρχῇ. The distinction between the secured discharge and its historical
enjoyment, established in paragraphs 252–273, gives the temporal relation required for the answer.
    [436] Secondly, payment of the very thing required under the obligation leaves intact the free and
gracious pardon Scripture repeatedly affirms. God's pardon comprises the whole dispensation of grace
toward us in Christ. He first lays our sin upon Christ, making him sin for us by an act of pure grace done
for his own sake. He then imputes Christ's righteousness to us, making us the righteousness of God in
him, an act no less gracious, since Christ's own merit rests upon a freely established compact and
covenant. The remission that God grants sinners excludes their merits, and Christ's merit is the ground
upon which they receive it. God forgives us everything. He spared his only Son nothing and abated him
not one farthing. The freedom of the pardon thus consists in God's free appointment of the satisfaction, in
his gracious acceptance of it in the stead of the specified persons and no others, and in his free application
of Christ's death to us. Its freedom follows from who provides the satisfaction and who bears it. Full
satisfaction by the surety gives the debtor no part in redeeming himself. (John 3:16; Romans 5:8; 1 John
4:9)
    [437] Remission excludes the debtor's own payment of the thing in the obligation. It therefore leaves
full satisfaction by another entirely consistent with pardon to the debtor. Christ in his death supplied the
thing required. He removed the curse by “being made a curse” and delivered us from sin by being “made
sin.” He underwent death so that we should be delivered from death. Our whole debt was contained in the
curse of the law, which he underwent in full. Scripture gives no relaxation of the punishment, only a
substitution of the person, so that “God condemned sin in the flesh of his Son”, with Christ standing in
our stead. God received reparation and satisfaction for all the detriment arising from the sin and rebellion
of the persons represented. His justice was violated, and he “sets forth Christ to be a propitiation” for our
sins “that he might be just, and the justifier of him which believes in Jesus.” His justice was never more
clearly shown than in causing “the iniquity of us all to meet upon him.” His law was broken, and Christ
came to be “the end of the law for righteousness.” Our disobedience offended him, and in Christ's
obedience he took full pleasure. Gratuity to the recipient and full satisfaction to the offended party
describe different relations of the same judicial undertaking. (Galatians 3:13; 2 Corinthians 5:21; Romans
8:3; 3:25–26; 10:4; 5:17; Matthew 3:16)

    [438] The satisfaction made by Christ was a full and valuable compensation to God's justice for all the
sins of all for whom he made it. He underwent the punishment that they were bound under their
obligation to undergo. Its sameness consists in its essential weight and pressure, although the accidents of
duration and the like differ, for death could not hold him. The authority who receives satisfaction
establishes the admissibility of the substitution, so that sufficiency belongs with appointment and
acceptance for the represented persons. The first question about the result is therefore whether God's
justice can permit the eternal destruction of someone on whose behalf he has accepted a satisfaction so
full, perfect, and complete.
    [439] The effect upon the obligation follows from the representative appointment under which the
suffering was accepted. Satisfaction answers a specified obligation and changes its status. The
consequence depends on whose obligation it is, on whose performance is received in its place, and on the
manner in which the result is appointed to take effect.
                      [440] Represent(o,x,ℓ)∧Accepted(o,ℓ)⇒Answered(ℓ,x). (39)
    [441] Terms. The obligation ell concerns the sins and judicial relation in question. Represent
identifies the appointed suretyship, Accepted identifies the reception of the performance by the authority
as satisfaction, and Answered identifies the status of the obligation under that provision. The actual
enjoyment of the benefit remains temporally distinct.
    [442] Consequence. The implication concerns a judicial effect arising from God's appointment and
acceptance of the representative performance. It furnishes no arithmetic comparison between the surety's
suffering and a measurable quantity of human pain. The subsequent objections bear on the argument only
insofar as they preserve or expressly deny the office through which the act produces its effect.
    [443] Secondly, the acts God exercises toward us and toward our Savior must be distinguished by
their offices. Everyone confesses that God is the offended party, since sin breaks his law, impairs his
glory, and abases his honor. “If I be a father,” he says, “where is mine honor?” Natural law and universal
right require recompense to the party injured by another's fault. In relation to us God is the creditor and
we are the miserable debtors who owe the “ten thousand talents.” Our Savior teaches us to call our sins
our “debts”, and God exacts the payment of that debt. In relation to Christ, upon whom God laid the
punishment of us all and made our iniquity meet, he is the supreme Lord, Governor, and sole Lawgiver.
As the one who has power of life and death, he alone could so far relax his own law as to put a surety's
name where it had not stood in the obligation, and then require the whole debt of the surety. He laid it
upon his Son to the last farthing and did not spare him. Two acts are eminent in this business. As creditor
God exercises severe justice in exacting the debt at the debtor's hand, which where the debt is sin means
punishment, repairing his justice where it was violated. As sovereign he transfers punishment from the
principal debtor to the surety, whom he freely gave to the debtor. “He spared not his own Son, but
delivered him up to death for us all.” The sovereign appointment and the judicial exaction can thus be
distinguished, along with the reception of the appointed satisfaction. Two consequences follow from the
distinction. (Malachi 1:6; Matthew 18:24; 6:12; James 4:12)
    [444] 1. God accepts Christ's punishment as a creditor receives what is due, requiring the uttermost
farthing and sparing the debtor nothing. Punishment considered simply as punishment has no creditor in
the proper sense, for “Delicta puniri publicè interest.” Considered also as a price, however, it is paid to a
creditor. In this case that creditor is God, which is why Christ is said to come to satisfy him and to do his
will. Some argue that God can inflict punishment neither as creditor nor by supreme dominion. The
arguments have little weight. The author's knowledge of law and its terminology might ordinarily shield
him from an examiner of Owen's modest pretensions, but one who has so foully betrayed God's truth
elsewhere and corrupted his word deserves assent only where the evidence of reason compels it.
Acceptance of what answers the obligation establishes the juridical consequence, while application to
those represented depends upon the conditions proper to that office. The arguments against the authority

to inflict punishment must accordingly be tested on their own grounds. (1 Corinthians 6:20; Hebrews
10:9; John 6:38)
    [445] The first argument is “The right of punishing in the rector or lawgiver can neither be a right of
absolute dominion nor a right of a creditor; because these things belong to him, and are exercised for his
own sake, who has them, but the right of punishing is for the good of community.” Its classification of
rights can settle the matter only if it accounts for the authority actually exercised. A definition which
denies that authority by restriction at the outset gives no ground for the theological conclusion drawn
from it.
   [446] Applied to God, as the argument intends, the reason loses its force. We deny that anything in
him or done by him has primarily in view the good of any other. His αὐτάρκεια, or self-sufficiency,
excludes an ultimate regard in his action for anything other than himself. Hence the “bonum universi,” the
good of the community to which punishment is said to be directed, consists wholly in the glory of God.
The proposed division cannot be made in him, since the relations incident to the exercise of a merely
human office do not determine the exercise of divine authority.
    [447] Secondly, the objection proceeds “Punishment is not in and for itself desirable, but only for
community’s sake. Now, the right of dominion and the right of a creditor are things in themselves
expetible and desirable, without the consideration of any public aim.” Whether punishment is desirable
for its own sake concerns the end in view, while the right of punishment concerns the authority exercised
in appointing it. A difference between those things establishes no opposition between the sources of the
rights.
    [448] First, the proper comparison is between the right of punishing and the right of dominion.
Punishment is the fact in which one right is exercised, and comparing that fact with the other right puts
dissimilar predicates on the two sides. Once the terms are brought into correspondence, the conclusion
must follow from the authority and obligation in view.
    [449] Secondly, nothing is desirable to God except in relation to himself, and it is intolerable to
suppose that any other good is desirable to him for its own sake. The ultimate end orders the intermediate
exercise of right. The hierarchy of ends established in paragraphs 167–175 thus governs the present
juridical relation.
     [450] Thirdly, some acts of supreme dominion are considered in themselves and for their own sake as
little desirable as punishment. The annihilation of an innocent creature is one such act, and Grotius will
grant that God has power to do it. The character of the authority exercised cannot be inferred from the
desirability of the act considered in isolation. It is determined through the entire relation in which the act
is appointed.
    [451] Thirdly, he says “Any one may, without any wrong, go off from the right of supreme dominion
or creditorship; but the Lord cannot omit the act of punishment to some sins, as of the impenitent.”
Whether the omission of punishment is consistent with justice depends upon the appointment, promise,
and obligation under which punishment is in question.
    [452] By supreme dominion God may omit punishment without wrong or injury to his justice. To
impute sin where it is absent and to punish upon that imputation is as great a thing as to withhold
imputation where sin is present and to omit or remove punishment upon that non-imputation. God did the
first toward Christ, and therefore can do the second. His absolute power is distinguishable from the
certainty of the undertaking he has in fact ordained. The latter follows from what he has appointed and
promised.
   [453] Secondly, if the omission of punishment for any sin or sins is unjust, the injustice arises from the
positive act of God's will in which he determined to punish. The obligation is consequent upon that

determination. Its necessity is the necessity of fidelity to the order God establishes, and no natural
obligation stands over him as a superior.
    [454] Fourthly, he says “None can be called just for using his own right or lordship; but God is called
just for punishing or not remitting sin.” The attribution of justice depends on the act and the authority
under which it is performed. One act of God may truly be described through several relations, whose
distinctions remain in force. (Revelation 16:5)
    [455] First, whatever may hold in other cases, God can be said to act justly in exacting what is due or
in exercising his dominion, because his will is the sole rule of justice. The rule is therefore found in the
divine source of the act itself, and the juridical consequence follows through the representative provision
appointed under that rule.
   [456] Secondly, the act of punishment here exacts a debt that is due. The act of supreme dominion
comes into the account as the appointment under which God requires that debt from Christ in our stead.
Because it is exacted from a surety in this relation to those he represents, acceptance of his performance
has consequences for them.
    [457] Fifthly, his final reason is “Because that virtue whereby one goes off from his dominion or
remits his debt, is liberality; but that virtue whereby a man abstains from punishing is clemency: so that
punishment can be no act of exacting a debt or acting a dominion.” The names of virtues cannot
determine in advance the operation to which they are applied. The juridical act and its effect must be
identified before the corresponding virtue can be assigned.
    [458] Liberality is too narrow a name for every refusal to exact what is due. Grotius himself allows
that a debt can arise from injury to a man's reputation, credit, or name by a lie, slander, or other wrong. If
the injured party declines to exact reparation, his virtue is clemency or that grace of the gospel for which
the moralists have no name. The same holds wherever an offended party abstains from requiring
punishment that he has a right to require. The description of an act therefore changes with the kind of debt
and the ground of the right, and the proposed distinction between the virtues fails to displace the account
of satisfaction. In it God as creditor requires the exact payment of the debt by punishment.
    [459] 2. The second act is one of supreme sovereignty and dominion, whereby God requires the
punishment of Christ to answer the obligation completely and fulfill the law. The appointment of the
surety and the acceptance of his performance together establish the relation in which the recipient is
delivered, and both belong to God's sovereign provision. (Romans 8:3; 10:4)
    [460] Six consequences follow from the relations thus established. First, the debt of everyone for
whom Christ was responsible was paid to God in full, to the utmost extent of the obligation. Secondly, a
just creditor in all equity cancels the bond and ceases every suit, action, and molestation against the
debtor whose debt he has received. Thirdly, what Christ paid was the debt of all the sins of all in whose
name he paid, as already proved, rather than the debt of this or that sin. Fourthly, a second payment, or its
exaction, is inconsistent with the justice God displayed in setting forth Christ as propitiation for our sins.
Fifthly, when a debt has been paid, discharge from further trouble is equitably due. Having accepted
payment from Christ in the place of those for whom he died, the Lord therefore grants them a discharge in
justice, according to the obligation he freely laid upon himself. Sixthly, the supreme Lawgiver relaxed the
law in respect of the person undergoing the punishment, and the satisfaction actually rendered under that
appointment is such that the law can charge those for whom Christ died with nothing beyond what it
could charge had they fulfilled by obedience all that it required. One liability cannot be both fully
answered and wholly unanswered in the same relation of representation and acceptance. (1 John 1:7;
Romans 3:25; 8:32–34)
    [461] The doctrine of universal redemption encounters these consequences at every point. First, if
everyone's debt has been paid to the utmost extent of the obligation, why are so many imprisoned
eternally without ever being freed from their debts? Secondly, if the Lord as just creditor cancels the

obligations and ceases every action against those whose debt is paid, why does his wrath smoke against
some forever? To answer that they fail to walk worthy of the benefit is to instance another part of the debt
already paid, since the third inference includes all their sins. Thirdly, does God demand satisfaction from
persons for whom, by his own acknowledgment, Christ made satisfaction full and sufficient, as though
they owed a second payment? Has God an after-reckoning that he did not think of, having spared Christ
nothing for what was before him? Fourthly, why are innumerable souls never discharged if their debts
have been paid? Fifthly, how does even one soul live and die under the law's condemnation, never
released, if satisfaction on his behalf leaves him as though he had done everything the law requires?
Those who can reconcile these things may do so. I am no Œdipus for them. The poor and beggarly
distinctions offered for this purpose have already been examined. The universal account still owes an
explanation of final condemnation that preserves the satisfaction it asserts for the persons condemned.
(Romans 8:32)
     [462] Double exaction concerns the identity of the obligation. The occurrence of two temporal evils
does not establish it, and neither does the difference between an accepted answer by the surety and a
debtor who has yet to enjoy release. The decisive assertion would be that the very same final penal
liability was wholly answered by the representative satisfaction and yet finally exacted without
qualification.
                          [463] FinallyDischarged(ℓ,x)⇒¬FinallyDue(ℓ,x). (40)
    [464] Terms. The predicates apply to one person and obligation, under the same juridical respect and
with the same final issue. The conclusion follows from the meaning of final discharge in this account. The
substantive premise, established by the arguments on satisfaction, is that the offering secures such a
discharge.
    [465] Consequence. The principle bears on the extent of the offering once the premise concerning its
effect is established. After that, to retain the name of discharge and yet assert that the identical liability is
finally due is to contradict the premise. The dispute thus concerns whether the offering effects the
discharge in question.
    [466] While Owen was composing the argument from Christ's satisfaction, a visitor came to the place
where he lived and gave a private discourse on the sufferings of Christ. Those who heard it understood it
to weaken and even to overthrow satisfaction. Owen withheld the man's name and other particulars out of
respect for his abilities and modesty. Because he judged the consequences dangerous and wanted to
prevent the error from spreading, he answered briefly and plainly, and shortly afterward willingly
accepted the speaker's invitation to discuss the point. The discussion proceeded with the quietness and
sobriety befitting men who sought the truth. It made clear both the position the speaker held and the
inference upon which it rested. The speaker held that God's eternal and unchangeable love had already
placed the elect in a condition that admitted no satisfaction on their behalf. The purpose of satisfaction is
to remove wrath and make atonement for sins. Since God's love for the elect was already established, he
concluded that they stood in need of neither. What they lacked was a clear manifestation of that love to
their souls, to free them from the dread, darkness, guilt, and fear that afflicted their consciences. Since
Adam's fall, on this account, they had misunderstood God's love and consequently mistaken their own
condition. Jesus Christ was sent to manifest God's love and declare his eternal goodwill toward them. By
removing the misapprehension that sin had produced in their consciences, he bore and took away their
sins. He made no satisfaction to God's justice, for God was eternally well pleased with them. Thus
election is asserted to the overthrow of redemption. Owen left what followed in the exchange and
whatever success attended it by God's blessing to the judgment of those who heard it, for whose sake
alone it was held. The question concerns all Christians, both because of the weight of the matter itself and
because the inference combines certain truths with errors no less certain. True propositions are made to
yield false consequences, and truths it is necessary to affirm are put forward to the exclusion of others
equally necessary. The argument bears directly on satisfaction, beginning with the eternal love of God

and the condition in which that love places its objects. Its resolution depends upon distinguishing God's
purpose, his execution of it in time, and the recipient's recognition of what he accomplishes.
   [467] The supposed new doctrine of free grace repeats an old objection to it. The Pelagians more than
once objected in substance to Augustine, in the course of his defense of election, that an eternal choice
made satisfaction by Christ unnecessary. Calvin answered the objection when others renewed it, and
several schoolmen, including Thomas Aquinas, had already put it to themselves in their own way.
Although its absurdity had long been exposed by solid answers, the Arminians at Dort eagerly took it up
again and set it at the head of their arguments against the effectual redemption of the elect by Jesus Christ.
Some among us now claim as truth what the Arminians proposed as an objection. They accept the
inconsequent inference as sound and think election necessarily entails denial of satisfaction. Yet the
purpose is the reason the means are appointed to accomplish its effects. It cannot by its mere affirmation
abolish the means it appoints. (Calvin, Institutes II.16; Thomas Aquinas, Summa Theologiae III, q. 49, a.
4)
    [468] Secondly, election and reprobation have the same structure in the respect in which they stand
opposed. “Jacob have I loved, but Esau have I hated.” In the one case persons are “ordained to eternal
life” and in the other “before of old ordained unto condemnation.” If God's decree that the elect shall be
justified, sanctified, and saved gives them those benefits already, so that only a manifestation is needed,
then the reprobate, as soon as finally impenitent, are already damned and burning and need only to have it
disclosed to them. The whole dispensation of God toward them can be consulted to judge that
consequence. The purpose determines the final relation, and the present condition of a person is
determined by the stage actually reached in the appointed course. (Romans 9:13; Acts 13:48; Jude 1:4)
    [469] Thirdly, the eternal love of God belongs to his will. To conceive of it as an affection in the
divine nature in the way that love is an affection in ours would be blasphemy, for his pure and holy
nature, in which there is no change or shadow of turning, cannot be subject to passion. Scripture calls it
his “good pleasure”, his “purpose according to election”, and the “foundation of God.” An eternal act of
the divine will is immanent in God and is not really distinguished from him, for whatever is thus in God is
God. It therefore places nothing in the creature and changes none of its conditions until an outward act of
God's power produces the effect. God decreed the creation of the world from eternity, although the world
was made only about five thousand five hundred years before Owen wrote. “It was made in God’s
purpose.” That is, he purposed to make it. He also purposes a universal day of judgment, which does not
make the day already actual. His purpose to justify and save particular persons in and through Christ
establishes that they shall certainly be justified, not that they already are. The inference from purpose to
futurity and certainty is sound, but it cannot establish present existence. When the Lord undertook at the
beginning to make the world, there was no world. Likewise a person whom God is to bring to faith and
actually justify remains unjustified until God does so. Changes of this kind occur in the creature, and none
requires the attribution of a changing affection to God. (Matthew 11:26; Romans 9:11; 2 Timothy 2:19)
     [470] First, God's eternal love for the elect is his purpose and good pleasure, a pure act of will
determining what he will do for them in his time and way. Secondly, such an immanent act makes the
event necessary in relation to the purpose. The outward effect and the change in the nature or condition of
its object belong to the execution of that purpose. Thirdly, the anger and wrath under which sinners lie are
no passion in God, but consist in outward effects such as guilt and bondage. Fourthly, the act of eternal
love immanent in God leaves the creature under those effects until an act of free grace in time actually
changes its condition. God has the mass of mankind in his power as a potter has clay, and purposes to
make some vessels to honor, for the praise of his glorious grace, and others to dishonor, for the
manifestation of his avenging justice. To that end he allows all to fall into sin and guilt, and all
accordingly become liable to wrath and curse. His purpose to save some gives them no exemption from
the common condition as it exists in themselves. Something must actually be accomplished to bring them
near to him. His wrath in its effects therefore remains upon them until the eternal purpose is realized in a

distinguishing act of free grace. The certainty that a benefit will be conferred is one thing, and its
possession after the appointed application is another, as the ensuing arguments show.
    [471] The purpose appoints a history in which the creature actually passes from one condition into
another. The certainty that the whole history will be accomplished concerns the purpose, and the
creature's present condition concerns the stage of that history already reached. The creature therefore need
not already possess every purposed good for the certainty of its appointed bestowal to remain entire.
                            [472] Ex⇒∃t1 Ax(t1),         t0<t1,     ¬Ax(t0). (41)
     [473] Terms. E denotes election and A the relevant application. The earlier moment in the person's
life is t zero, and the time appointed for realization is t one. The implication uses the efficacy of election
already established, while the final predicate gives the creature's real prior condition. Eternity is no date
preceding t zero.
    [474] Consequence. The elect can be certain to receive the benefit while still standing in need of
conversion, justification, and deliverance from wrath. These events belong to the history appointed by the
purpose. The reality of the purpose thus supplies no argument for removing the means which it appoints.
    [475] 1. If a sinner needs only a manifestation of eternal love in order to have acceptance and peace,
evangelical justification consists in apprehending God's decree. Scripture nowhere makes God's justifying
a person consist in his disclosing election to that person, or the person's justification in apprehending the
decree, purpose, or love. Such a discovery is indeed attainable by the saints, through “God shedding
abroad his love in their hearts by the Holy Ghost which is given unto them”, but comes after they are
“justified by faith,” and have “peace with God.” Believers are to give “all diligence to make their calling
and election sure;” which gives no warrant for defining their justification by their assurance of it. The
scriptural act is God's pronouncing an ungodly person, upon believing, absolved of the guilt of sin and
invested with the all-sufficient righteousness of Christ. God “justifies the ungodly” “by the righteousness
of God which is by the faith of Jesus Christ unto them”, making Christ righteousness to those who in
themselves were sin. The believer's improvement of justification includes the perception of God's love,
though no foundation exists for making that perception its form. An alteration in what someone
apprehends leaves the object as it was. Justification and reconciliation must be understood through the
actual relations ascribed to them in Scripture. (Romans 5:1, 5; 4:5; 3:22)
    [476] Clement excludes our wisdom, godliness, and holy works as grounds upon which we might
justify ourselves, and directs us to faith and the divine calling. The reception of the good God purposes is
one relation, and the discovery that he has purposed it is another. Discovery may assure us of a relation
already established. It cannot supply the righteousness by which the ungodly are justified, and acceptance
cannot therefore consist in the discovery of a fact about ourselves. (Clement of Rome, First Epistle to the
Corinthians 32)
    [477] 2. Scripture assigns all men the same real condition before actual reconciliation, while God
reserves to himself the distinguishing purpose of the change that his free grace will afterward effect.
“There is none that does good, no, not one”, for “we have proved both Jews and Gentiles that they are all
under sin.” Their relation to the eternal decrees does not alter the truth of their condition considered in
themselves. Thus “every mouth is stopped, and all the world is become guilty before God”, and all are
ὑπόδικος, liable to his judgment. “Who makes you to differ from another? and what have you that you did
not receive?” Whatever difference occurs in actual condition is produced by actual grace, for even
believers are “by nature children of wrath, even as others.” During their unregeneracy all occupy the same
state, and the purpose of a future distinction remains in God. Do the reprobate then lie under the effects of
wrath? If the answer is “No,” who will believe it? If the answer is yes, why do the elect escape while in
the same condition? The same state has the same properties, and no actual distinction has been shown.
The adversary must produce a difference that actually exists or give up the cause. The purpose particular

to the elect does not remove the transition by which they come to possess its fruits. (Romans 3:9, 12, 19;
1 Corinthians 4:7; Ephesians 2:3)
    [478] 3. Scripture shows what it is to lie under the effects of wrath, which determines whether the
elect can be said to have escaped it before their actual calling. (1.) Alienation from God makes their
services unacceptable. “The prayer of the wicked is an abomination to the Lord.” (2.) Their outward
enjoyments are unsanctified, so that all things are unclean to them. (3.) They are under Satan's power,
who rules at will in the children of disobedience. (4.) They are in bondage to death. (5.) They lie under the
curse and condemnation of the law. (6.) They are liable to God's judgment and guilty of eternal death and
damnation. (7.) They are under the reigning power of sin. Wrath has these and other actual effects, and
their removal in time requires the saving operations by which the recipient's standing and condition are
changed. (Proverbs 28:9; Titus 1:15; Ephesians 2:2; Hebrews 2:15; Galatians 3:13; Romans 3:19; 6:19)
    [479] What further effect lies upon the reprobate in this life, and which of these effects is absent from
the elect before their actual reconciliation in and by Christ? (1.) Their prayers are abominable to God,
since without faith they cannot please him, and to suppose them already believing is to suppose them
already reconciled. (2.) Their enjoyments have no sanctified relation to them without faith. (3.) They are
under Satan's power, as Christ's destruction of the devil's works for them and in them makes evident. He
came to deliver his people from the devil who had the power of death. (4.) The apostle expressly places
them in bondage to death throughout their lives until Christ actually frees them. (5.) The curse of the law
held them, for they are freed through Christ's becoming a curse for them. (6.) They are liable to judgment
and guilty of eternal death. Paul makes no difference, pronouncing all guilty before God and subject to his
judgment, and ascribes to Christ their deliverance from the wrath they had merited. (7.) They are under
the dominion of sin, for “God be thanked,” says Paul, “that you were the servants of sin, but you have
obeyed,” and so forth. Scripture is nowhere more abundant than in charging upon the elect the misery and
wrath of an unreconciled condition until they actually partake of Christ's deliverance. The comparison
concerns the persons' present conditions. A distinction in eternal appointment cannot make every
corresponding difference in their histories actual at an earlier moment. (Hebrews 11:6; 2:14–15; 1
Corinthians 7:14; Ephesians 2:2; Galatians 3:13; Romans 3:9; 5:9; 6:17; 1 Thessalonians 1:10)
    [480] Some would wipe away all of this by saying that the effects exist only in the persons'
apprehension. If the elect are under them only in apprehension, on what ground are the rest of the world
under them in reality? Scripture gives no such distinction. If all are held captive only by a mistaken
understanding of their condition, then let everyone rid himself of the mistake as fast as he can and the
whole miserable world will be well. Let them call Scripture a fable and the terror of the Almighty a
scarecrow for children, say that sin exists only in conceit, and bring their conduct into agreement with
their blasphemous fancies. Some men's words eat as a canker. What a recipient knows and the reality he
knows have distinct causes, and mere recognition supplies no objective change.
     [481] A person's understanding of a state and the state itself can change independently, whether the
person is judging another or knows himself. Someone may learn what is true without causing it to be true,
and may also believe that a change has occurred when none has. The account of reconciliation and
justification must therefore distinguish knowledge from its object and identify the operation Scripture in
fact ascribes to the saving work.
                                       [482] =T(),     =Q(,ex). (42)
    [483] Terms. T changes the actual condition at issue, and Q changes the person's knowledge upon
evidence e. K gives his warranted cognitive condition when the evidence is adequate. If the claim extends
only to belief, the relation must be qualified accordingly. The equations give distinct operations.
    [484] Consequence. A change of apprehension supplies no explanation where Scripture ascribes a
judicial or inward transformation to Christ's saving work. Where Scripture does speak of recognition, that

operation cannot efficiently produce its own object. The same distinction governs the account of
assurance that follows.
    [485] 4. Among the particular passages that could be given, one suffices. “He that believes not the
Son, the wrath of God abides on him.” To abide is to have been there already and to remain there while
unbelief continues. Upon believing the wrath is removed. “But is not God’s love unchangeable, by which
we shall be freed from his wrath?” Granted, but an apprentice is no more free before his seven years are
up because he will be free at their end. God has purposed to free his people and will do it in his own time.
That does not put them in liberty before he frees them. “But are we not in Christ from all eternity?” We
were chosen in him and in that sense were in him. Actual participation in Christ requires a person who
actually exists to participate. From eternity God purposed that we should exist. We are nevertheless of
yesterday, and his purpose gives us no eternal being. Our eternal being in Christ refers to the same kind of
purpose and warrants the same kind of inference. Scripture's connection between unbelief and abiding
wrath establishes a determinate temporal relation, which is unaffected by whether the recipient knows
another relation that God has purposed. (John 3:36)
    [486] Election furnishes no ground for the forced consequence by which Christ's merit and
satisfaction are overthrown. Redemption by Jesus Christ is the principal means of bringing the purpose
into execution, as the Lord's pleasure prospers in his hand. The argument can be turned back, κατὰ τὸ
βίαιον, with undeniable force. God's purpose to save sinners entails the satisfaction of Christ for those
sinners. In the same act by which he set us apart from eternity to enjoy all spiritual blessings in heavenly
places, God set apart Jesus Christ to procure them and to satisfy for all our sins. The decree establishes
the appointed course of accomplishment, and thereby includes the means whose necessity has been
denied. The following proofs establish that Christ actually made the satisfaction in question.
    [487] I. If God laid our sins upon Christ and he took them in such a way that he underwent their
punishment in our stead, he thereby satisfied God's justice for them and the sinners go free. He did bear
our sins as laid upon him by God, underwent what they deserved, and did so in our place. He therefore
made satisfaction to God's justice for them. The consequence was established above. The premise that
concerns Christ's actual performance requires three things to be shown separately, that God laid our sins
upon him and he bore them, that in bearing them he underwent their punishment, and that he underwent it
in our stead. Each states a relation that the conclusion requires.
    [488] First, Scripture says that he took and bore our sins. Its terms are Ὁ αἴρων, as in “Who takes
away the sin of the world”, and Ὁς ἀνήνεγκεν, as in “Who his own self bore our sins in his own body”. It
also says “ —,‫הּוא ִיְס ֹּבל‬He shall bear their iniquities;” and “ —,‫ָנ ָׂש א‬He bore the sin of many.” The
imposition of the sins by God is as plain, both in “The Lord, ַ,‫ִה ְפ ִּג יע‬made to meet on him the iniquity of us
all” and in Ἁμαρτίαν ἐποίησε, “He has made him to be sin for us.” The passages jointly specify the
operation. The sins that Christ bears are those God lays upon him, and that representative act gives the
term its force in the proof. (John 1:29; 1 Peter 2:24; Isaiah 53:6, 11–12; 2 Corinthians 5:21)
    [489] Secondly, Christ bore the sins laid on him by undergoing their punishment. Death and the curse
of the law comprehend the whole of that punishment. The threat was “,‫מֹות ָּת מּות‬Dying you shall die,” and
death entered by sin. In this use death comprises all the misery due to our transgressions, as does the curse
expressed in “Cursed be he that confirms not all the words of this law to do them.” Christ underwent both
while bearing our sins. Scripture says that “by the grace of God he tasted death”, so that by death he
delivered from death. He was not “spared, but given up to death for us all.” He also underwent the curse
of the law. Γενόμενος κατάρα means that he “was made a curse for us;” and ἐπικατάρατος that he was
“cursed.” The death and curse actually came upon him as punishment, for by them “it pleased the Lord to
bruise him, and put him to grief”, and οὐκ ἐφείσατο, “he spared him not”, but “condemned sin in his
flesh.” The connection between the punishment and the sins he bore supplies the juridical ground of
deliverance. The remaining point is that he underwent it in our stead. (Genesis 2:17; Romans 5:12; 8:3,
32; Deuteronomy 27:26; Hebrews 2:9, 14; Galatians 3:13; Isaiah 53:10)

    [490] Our Savior himself establishes the substitution. He came δοῦναι τὴν ψυχὴν αὑτοῦ λύτρον ἀντὶ
πολλῶν, “to give himself a ransom for many.” The word ἀντί always supposes the substitution of one
person or thing for another, as will be shown afterward. Scripture likewise says “He suffered for us, the
just for the unjust;” and “I restored” or paid “that which I took not away.” The debt thus paid was ours,
and the payment discharges us precisely because he died in our stead. The three premises are therefore
established, and the relation in which he paid must remain fixed when the effects of his payment are
inferred. (Matthew 20:28; 2:22; 1 Timothy 2:6; 1 Peter 3:18; Psalms 69:4; Romans 8:34)
    [491] II. If Jesus Christ paid a valuable price and ransom for our sins into his Father's hands as our
surety, discharging our debt so that we might go free, he bore the punishment due to our sins and made
satisfaction for them to God's justice. To pay such a ransom is to make that satisfaction. Christ paid such a
price into his Father's hands as our surety, and the conclusion therefore follows. Suretyship and
acceptance in this relation make the price an answer to the obligation, with the temporal application
already distinguished in paragraphs 252–273.
    [492] The second proposition includes four claims. First, Christ paid such a price and ransom.
Secondly, he paid it to his Father. Thirdly, he paid it as our surety. Fourthly, its payment was to the end
that we should go free. Each identifies a necessary dependence, and proof of the payment by itself would
leave the conclusion without the other premises on which it rests.
    [493] First, our Savior expressly says that he “came to give his life λύτρον,” the price of redemption
“for many”. The apostle calls it ἀντίλυτρον, a ransom received in the place of others. Thus our
deliverance comes διὰ τῆς ἀπολυτρώσεως, “by the ransom-paying of Christ Jesus.” Scripture also says
“He bought us with a price”, and the price is his blood, compared with silver and gold and declared
superior to them for this work. The reality and value of the price are therefore evident from the life
offered and from whose life it is, under the office in which it was appointed to be offered. (Matthew
20:28; Mark 10:45; 1 Timothy 2:6; Romans 3:24; 1 Corinthians 6:20; Acts 20:28; 1 Peter 1:18)
    [494] Secondly, Christ paid the price into his Father's hands. Release by ransom presupposes a party
to whom the ransom is paid. In the case at issue it would have to be paid either to the judge or to the
jailer, to God or to the devil. To say it was paid to the devil is the highest blasphemy. Satan was an enemy
to be conquered. The obligation was owed to God, whose “wrath” was on us and who had “shut us all up
under sin.” He is the great King to whom the debt belongs and the only “law-giver, who is able to save
and to destroy.” The manner in which Scripture describes the payment confirms the same thing. His death
and shedding of blood are προσφορά and θυσία, “an oblation and sacrifice”, and his soul is ‫ָא ָׁש ם‬, a
sacrifice or “offering for sin.” Sacrifices and offerings are directed to God alone. The authority who
receives the price determines the obligation it answers, while the conquest of the hostile power is its
connected consequence in a different relation. (John 3:36; Galatians 3:22; Matthew 18:23–34; James
4:12; Ephesians 5:2; Isaiah 53:10)
    [495] Thirdly, Scripture establishes that Christ acted as surety. He was made ἔγγυος, a “surety of a
better testament;” and, in the execution of the duty belonging to that office, “he paid that which he took
not away.” Fourthly, the end of that performance could only be that we go free. Suretyship identifies the
persons whose obligation is undertaken, and the act accepted on their behalf explains why its
consequences reach them. (Hebrews 7:22; Psalms 69:4)
    [496] The reality of the offering, its acceptance by the Father, the suretyship of the one who makes it,
and the obligations and persons represented are distinct premises. The inference depends on their
conjunction. Proof of one therefore cannot do the work of all four, and an objection to one has force only
through the effect it actually has upon the inference as a whole.
                          [497] Γ={Po,Pa,Ps,Pr},       Γ⊢Satisfaction(o,x). (43)

    [498] Terms. P(o) states the offering and P(a) its appointed acceptance. P(s) states the suretyship, and
P(r) designates the persons and obligations represented. Gamma conjoins the established premises. The
turnstile marks derivability from them, and their truth rests upon the scriptural argument.
    [499] Consequence. The opponent can answer the proof by denying an actual premise, showing that
one changes meaning, or demonstrating a failure of consequence. The number or impressiveness of
otherwise true statements supplies no answer unless they bear on the particular relations established by
the argument.
    [500] III. To make atonement for sin and thereby reconcile God to sinners is to make satisfaction to
his justice in the sense established above. Jesus Christ by his death and oblation made that atonement and
reconciled God to sinners. He therefore made satisfaction. The offering addresses the grounds on which
judicial enmity subsists, and the reconciliation effected through atonement consists in their removal.
Disclosure of an unchanged relation cannot supply that effect.
    [501] The first proposition follows from the terms, and Scripture confirms the second. We are freely
justified through the ransom in Christ, whom God set forth as ἱλαστήριον, a propitiation, an atonement, a
mercy-seat, and a covering of iniquity, εἰς ἔνδειξιν τῆς δικαιοσύνης, for the manifestation of the justice
declared in its accomplishment. Christ is called a “merciful high priest, εἰς τὸ ἱλάσκεσθαι τὰς ἁμαρτίας
τοῦ λαοῦ,” “to make reconciliation for the sins of the people.” The meaning is ἰλάσκεσθαι τὸν Θεὸν περὶ
τῶν ἁμαρτιῶν τοῦ λαοῦ, to reconcile God who was offended by the people's sins. We are said to
“receive,” that reconciliation. The word καταλλαγή is there rendered “atonement,” in our translation and
elsewhere “reconciliation,” being the only word used for it in the New Testament. All of this is
accomplished δι’ ἑνὸς δικαιώματος, by one righteousness or satisfaction, that of Christ. The usual
rendering “By the righteousness of one,” would require διὰ δικαιώματος τοῦ ἑνός, which is not the text.
The one satisfaction delivers us from what could not otherwise be escaped. The justice displayed in the
satisfaction and the mercy shown in the remission belong together, for the sinner is justified through the
righteousness God has provided. (Romans 3:24–25; 5:11; 8:3; Hebrews 2:17)
    [502] IV. Whatever constitutes the exercise of Christ's priestly office on earth cannot be denied
without damnable error. In exercising that office Jesus Christ bore the punishment our sins deserved,
made atonement with God by undergoing his wrath, and reconciled him to sinners through satisfaction to
his justice. These things therefore cannot be denied without damnable error. The office has its determinate
content in these operations, and affirming the office while denying them leaves nothing determinate of its
exercise.
    [503] Three grounds establish that Christ exercised his priestly office in these things. First, the types
and sacrifices that prefigured it had propitiation and atonement as their chief end. Secondly, the office
itself is appointed for sacrifice, and Christ had nothing to offer but his own blood, offered through the
eternal Spirit. Thirdly, innumerable passages of Scripture affirm the same thing. The several kinds of
testimony are contained in the contrast expressed in “If the blood of bulls and of goats,” and so forth, and
“how much more shall the blood of Christ, who through the eternal Spirit offered himself without spot to
God?” and so forth. Christ's death is compared with the sacrifices of expiation by the blood of bulls and
goats, placed above them, and shown as their fulfillment. It must therefore effect spiritually what they
accomplished in a bodily manner and prefigured, which is deliverance from the guilt of sin by expiation
and atonement. In those sacrifices the life and blood were accepted in place of the offerer, who deserved
death for breaking the law under its rigor. Here Christ's blood is accepted as our atonement and
propitiation, with Christ himself the priest, altar, and sacrifice. Where the old bodily sacrifices could not
perfect those who approached, Scripture expressly sets Christ offering his own body for sins, through
which offering their remission and pardon are effected. The types give evidence of the office through this
correspondence between the sacrifice and its accomplished effect. We affirm also that in carrying it out
Christ underwent the wrath of God due to us, which requires separate confirmation because some have
questioned it. (Hebrews 9:13–14; 10:10–12, 19)

    [504] First, the punishment due to sin is the wrath of God, as Scripture shows in “The wrath of God is
revealed against all ungodliness”, “The day of wrath and revelation of the righteous judgment of God”,
and “Children of wrath.” Jesus Christ underwent the punishment due to sin, being “Made sin for us”, and
Scripture says “Iniquity was laid upon him” and “He bore our sins in his own body on the tree.” Therefore
he underwent the wrath of God. Wrath here is the judicial consequence of sin, rather than a temporal
change of knowledge or passion in God. Its bearing is determined by the obligation and the representative
performance established in the proof. (Romans 1:18; 2:5; Ephesians 2:3; John 3:36; 2 Corinthians 5:21;
Isaiah 53:6; 1 Peter 2:24)
    [505] Secondly, the curse of the law is the wrath of God considered passively. Jesus Christ underwent
the curse in being “Made a curse for us,” which is the curse that lies upon those outside him who are “of
the works of the law.” He therefore underwent the wrath of God. The law's judicial consequence reaches
the surety under his appointment, and that relation explains its saving consequence for those he
represents. (Deuteronomy 29:20–21; Galatians 3:10, 13)
    [506] Thirdly, the death sinners owe is the wrath of God, and Jesus Christ tasted that very death, dying
in our stead as “our surety”. Hence his fear, agony, astonishment and amazement, his dereliction, sorrow,
heaviness, and inexpressible pressures. The death has this character through the relation in which he
undertook it. What it effects cannot be inferred from the outward event considered apart from the
appointment under which he died. (Hebrews 7:22; 5:7; Matthew 20:28; 27:46; 26:37–39; Luke 22:44;
Mark 14:33)
    [507] V. A doctrine that removes the ground of faith and the strong consolation God is so willing to
give us cannot agree with the gospel. The denial that Christ satisfied the justice and underwent the wrath
of his Father does just that. A soul driven out of its own righteousness becomes like Noah's dove, finding
nowhere to rest the soles of its feet. It looks over heaven and earth for a place of rest and finds wrath
disclosed from heaven against all ungodliness, like an inundation covering the world. Its own flight
cannot carry it to heaven and it is unwilling to fall into hell. If Christ does not appear in the midst of the
waters as the ark upon which the flood fell and which nevertheless rose above it as a refuge, where shall
the soul go? When the flood came, the mountains were glorious to the eye and stood far higher than the
ark, but the mountains were drowned while the ark continued upon the waters. So the high places of self-
righteousness and general mercy first seem more considerable to the soul than Jesus Christ, until the flood
of wrath rises and covers them. It falls also upon Christ, yet he rises above it and gives safety to those
who rest upon him. The consolation God promises requires a refuge adequate to the danger, and a
doctrine that changes what Christ accomplishes changes the ground on which faith can rest.
     [508] Let the souls that have been tossed about by fear of the wrath to come consider where they first
found rest. God spared not his only Son, but gave him up to death for us all. He made him sin for us and
put the sins of all the elect into the cup Christ had to drink. The flood they feared fell upon him, and he is
now above it as the ark in which they are safe. The storm was his and the safety is theirs. As the waters
that would have fallen upon those inside the ark fell upon the ark, leaving them dry and safe, the wrath
that would have fallen upon his people fell on Christ. On this ground alone their souls dwell in safety. If
this in substance has not been their foundation, I fear the foundations they have chosen are rotten. What
would they say to the man who pulls away the ark and gives them a rotten post to swim on in the flood? It
is too late to tell them that no wrath is due. The word of truth and their own consciences have taught them
otherwise. They know that the “wages of sin is death,” and that whoever is found with sin must die. The
soul can therefore say “Bereave me of the satisfaction of Christ, and I am bereaved. If he fulfilled not
justice, I must; if he underwent not wrath, I must to eternity. O rob me not of my only pearl!” A doctrine
denying Christ's satisfaction removes the answer to the liability the conscience knows. The comfort
promised to the person depends upon the relation of his deliverance to the satisfaction already made.

    [509] VI. A few particular passages establish the representative undertaking in place of the many that
could be adduced. Each gives a determinate premise, and their evidence converges only if the specific
relation expressed in each is kept in view.
    [510] The first passage says “He made him to be sin for us, who knew no sin.” How could this be? It
says “He made him to be sin for us;” and then “He knew no sin?” He was a Lamb without blemish and
spot, for “he did no sin, neither was guile found in his mouth.” In what sense then can it be asked “God
made him to be sin?” God could not have made him sinful by any sin inherent in him, which would be
contrary to God's justice and to the holiness of the person of our Redeemer. What then does it mean that
“He made him to be sin who knew no sin?” By dispensation and consent God charged him with what he
had not committed, imputing to him all the sins of all the elect and proceeding against him accordingly.
The entire debt was really charged to him as our surety, so that he was to pay to the utmost farthing. A
surety may have borrowed nothing and received no penny of what is due under the obligation, yet if the
creditor proceeds to execution against him, he must pay it all. The Father's justice, so to speak, proceeded
to execution against Christ, and he underwent all that sin deserved, which as already proved is death,
wrath, and curse. The imputation concerns his representative office and its judicial effect, and his personal
innocence remains entire. (2 Corinthians 5:21)
    [511] The objection is “That God was always well pleased with his Son, — he testified it again and
again from heaven, — how, then, could he lay his wrath upon him?” The Father was always well pleased
with his Son, yet it “pleased him to bruise him and put him to grief.” The holiness of his person, the
perfection of his righteousness, and the sweetness of his obedience were always pleasing to the Father.
The sins charged upon him were displeasing to the Father, who was therefore pleased to bruise and put to
grief the one in whom he was always well pleased. His delight in the Son and the Son's undertaking of
punishment answer to different relations and are both true of the same undertaking.
     [512] The further objection has no greater force. “That Christ underwent no more than the elect lay
under; but they lay not under wrath and the punishment due to sin.” Its proposition is altogether false and
its premise is no truer. First, Christ underwent the wrath, considered passively, that lay upon the elect and
that which they would have undergone had he not borne it. He “delivered them from the wrath to come.”
Secondly, in their several generations the elect do lie under all of God's wrath in respect of merit and
procurement, being guilty of it without yet enduring the punishment. The claims about their present
condition and about the surety's full suffering have different objects. Both objections therefore leave it
established that “he was made sin for us, who knew no sin.”
    [513] “He was wounded for our transgressions, he was bruised for our iniquities: the chastisement of
our peace was upon him; and with his stripes we are healed.” The passage was considered above, and its
further expressions determine precisely what it is to bear sin. “The chastisement of our peace was upon
him;” means that he was punished that we might have peace and go free, our transgressions causing his
wounds and our iniquities his bruises as all our sins met upon him. In Peter's interpretation he “bore our
sins.” He bore them through the wounds, bruises, and chastisement they deserved, undergoing death,
wrath, and curse, and thus making his soul an offering for sin. The explanation that he merely declared us
never to have been truly sinful cannot be drawn from the text. On the words “He bore our sins;” some say
that he declared us to have an eternal righteousness in God because God eternally purposed our good.
This corrupts the word instead of interpreting it. Scripture itself identifies the bearing of sin with being
“stricken” for our transgressions and “cut off” for our sins. Nor does the expression bear another meaning
elsewhere. “If a soul hear the voice of swearing, if he do not utter it, then he shall bear his iniquity.” The
person in that case does not declare himself or another free from sin. He undergoes its punishment, as our
Savior did in bearing our iniquities. It takes a cunning gamester to cheat a believer of that foundation. The
passage connects the peace of the persons concerned with the chastisement undergone for them, and that
relation determines the extent at issue. (Isaiah 53:5–6, 8; Daniel 9:26; Leviticus 5:1)

    [514] The cause would supply abundant arguments and texts to the least skillful defender, but the
present digression has gone as far as its place in the larger argument permits. A full treatment of Christ's
satisfaction would bring forward and answer every objection, which is beyond the scope of this inquiry.
Owen also declined to reproduce the objections raised in the debate that occasioned the digression, since
their weakness might make readers suppose that he had devised them to obtain an easy victory over a man
of straw of his own construction. They had little force against the fundamental truth in question. The
premises established here suffice for the conclusion concerning satisfaction and bear directly on merit and
the application of what Christ procures.
    [515] Arg. XIV. Merit is the worth of Christ's death in virtue of which he purchased for us every good
that Scripture says is bestowed through that death. The thing itself has been considered under impetration,
and the point here is its bearing upon the extent of the purchase. No New Testament translation known to
Owen uses merit as a rendering of an original term. The Vulgate has promeretur once, and the Rheimists
retain its sound in promerited. Both are uncouth and barbarous, and neither answers to εὐαρεστεῖται,
which gives no color to the name or thing. It would be difficult to find a word in either scriptural language
whose first and native sense directly signifies merit. The thing is evident in both Testaments. “The
chastisement of our peace was upon him, and with his stripes we are healed.” Our peace and healing are
what the chastisement and stripes procured, and their procurement is his merit. Διὰ τοῦ ἰδίου αἵματος
αἰωνίαν λύτρωσιν εὑράμενος, “Obtaining by his blood eternal redemption,” expresses the same relation.
The nearest word is Περιεποιήσατο, as in “Purchased with his own blood;” Purchase, impetration, merit,
and acquisition are equivalent for this purpose, and the last occurs in other passages. By merit we
understand an act so performed that the good aimed at becomes due to the person who performs it,
according to the equity and equality of justice. “To him that works, is the reward not reckoned of grace,
but of debt.” Christ's death has such merit, as the foregoing argument makes evident and the adversaries
themselves seem to allow, unless they join the Socinians here as well. Its worth must be considered in
relation to the agent, the appointment under which he acts, the performance, and the fruit thereby
procured. (Hebrews 13:16; 9:12; Isaiah 53:5; Acts 20:28; 1 Thessalonians 5:9; Ephesians 1:14; 1 Peter
2:9; Romans 4:4)
    [516] The things Christ merits and purchases for everyone for whom he dies are the fruits and effects
that Scripture assigns to his death. Among the privative effects are (1.) deliverance from the hand of our
enemies and from the wrath to come, (2.) the abolition of death in its power, (3.) the destruction of the
works of the devil, (4.) deliverance from the curse of the law, (5.) from our vain manner of life, (6.) from
the present evil world, and (7.) from the earth and from among men, together with (8.) the purging of our
sins. The positive effects are (1.) reconciliation with God, (2.) the appeasing of God by propitiation, (3.)
peacemaking, and (4.) salvation. Christ has so procured all these benefits from his Father for the persons
on whose behalf he died that, in respect of his merit and according to the equity of justice, they ought to
be bestowed upon those persons. It was of absolutely free grace that God would send his Son to die for
anyone and that he chose the people for whom he sent him. It is likewise free grace that any of the
persons receive what his death has procured, considered in relation to them. In relation to Christ the same
benefits are due, because God himself appointed and constituted his death as the means by which he
should merit grace and glory for those for whom he died. Their communication is therefore required, and
withholding them would be unjust. What is due to Christ in virtue of his merit comes to them in the
appointed application, without any second meritorious work supplied by the recipient. (Luke 1:74; 1
Thessalonians 1:10; Hebrews 2:14; 1:3; 1 John 3:8; 2:2; Galatians 3:13; 1:4; 1 Peter 1:18; Revelation
14:3–4; Romans 5:10; 3:25; Ephesians 2:14, 16; Colossians 1:20; Matthew 1:21)
    [517] Merit relates the accepted act to each good it procures under the undertaking. Faith, pardon,
sanctification, and glory have different offices and times of bestowal, while their procurement has one
ground in the one accepted offering. The variety of the effects therefore requires no succession of
meritorious payments.
                         [518] Bx={Fx,Jx,Hx,Gx},         Rx⇒Procure(o,Bx). (44)

     [519] Terms. B is the collection of benefits under consideration. F denotes faith, J justification, H
sanctification, and G glory, in the scriptural senses established here. Procure states the meritorious
relation. The operation and time by which each benefit is bestowed are specified separately.
     [520] Consequence. The work has unity of ground and purpose across effects that remain
distinguishable. Each must be followed into the operation by which it occurs. The past offering need
exercise no immediate physical action upon the present recipient for its merit to be efficacious in the
benefits presently conferred.
    [521] The procurement of grace and glory for the persons in whose stead Christ died puts the general
ransom to a decisive question. If Christ merited both for everyone for whom he died, and died for all, why
are grace and glory not bestowed upon all? Does the deficiency lie in Christ's merit or in God's justice?
To say that the benefits were procured and are bestowed conditionally merely revives the difficulty
already answered, because the very condition was itself merited and procured. The extent of merit follows
the efficacy affirmed of the purchase. The term cannot signify an effectual purchase in the premise and a
benefit never received in the conclusion. (Ephesians 1:3–4; Philippians 1:29)
    [522] Polycarp joins Christ's bearing of sins with the purpose that his people live in him. Suffering
and saving benefit belong to one confession, and the premises established here carry the inquiry from the
merit of the undertaking to the goods attributed to it. If the adversary excludes necessary grace from the
purchase, he owes an account of the operation sufficient to produce it. If he includes it, he must follow its
bestowal through the people represented. Neither account is made good by letting the meaning of
purchase lapse at the point where its consequence becomes inconvenient. The spokes of paragraph 0 bring
this question of purchase into focus. Whatever is procured for the represented persons must be followed
into the goods they receive. (Polycarp, Epistle to the Philippians 8)
    [523] Arg. XV. Fifthly, the expressions “dying for us,” “bearing our sins,” and being our “surety,” are
inconsistent with a ransom paid for all. To die for another in the scriptural sense is to die in his stead so
that he goes free. Judah asked Joseph to receive him as a bondman instead of Benjamin and so release
Benjamin, that Judah might fulfill the engagement in which he had become surety to his father. Christ's
suretyship carries the same relation of one person undergoing the danger for another's deliverance. When
David wished that he had died for Absalom, he meant to exchange his life for his son's so that his son
might live. Paul supposes that one person might be found willing to die for another, doubtless alluding to
the Decii, Menœceus, Euryalus, and others in the histories of the heathen, who voluntarily gave
themselves to death for their country or friends. They took upon themselves a death to which they were
not directly liable and thereby preserved the liberty and lives of those who would have undergone it. The
declaration “Christ died for us;” thus signifies that in undergoing death he was substituted for us. Some
object that ὑπέρ in “That he by the grace of God should taste death for every man,” signifies only the
good of those for whom he died and requires no exchange of persons. But the same preposition in
comparable cases is admitted to signify such an exchange. Paul says he “could wish himself accursed
from Christ ὑπὲρ τῶν αδελφῶν,” “for his brethren,” meaning that he would be accursed in their stead so
that they might be united to Christ. So also, Ὑπὲρ Χριστοῦ πρεσβεύομεν, “We are ambassadors in
Christ’s stead.” Again Paul strongly denies a thing by asking Μὴ Παῦλος ἐσταυρώθη ὑπὲρ ὑμῶν. “Was
Paul crucified for you?” He could have been crucified for the church's good, but abhors the thought that
he might be crucified in its stead. Thus the preposition as used of Christ's crucifixion signifies the
exchange of persons. In ἀντί the substitution is beyond dispute and concerns both things and persons.
Ὄφις ἀντὶ ἰχθύος is “A serpent instead of a fish”, Ὀφθαλμὸς ἀντὶ ὀφθαλμοῦ is “An eye for an eye”, and
Archelaus reigns ἀντὶ Ἡρώδου τοῦ πατρός, “instead of his father.” The same word occurs of our Savior's
death in “The Son of man came δοῦναι τὴν ψυχὴν αὑτοῦ λύτρον ἀντὶ πολλῶν,” a declaration repeated
elsewhere. His life is given as ransom in the stead of the lives of many. In dying as our surety, “bearing
our sins in his own body” and being made a curse for us, Christ underwent death, punishment, curse, and
wrath in our stead and for our good. God allowed and accepted the substitution of his person for ours.
First, did he die in that way for all, including Cain and Pharaoh and the others already under the power of

the second death long before he died, never to be delivered? Secondly, is it just that any for whom he died
in this sense and whose iniquities he bore should themselves die and bear their sins forever? Thirdly, what
rule or example of equity permits proceedings against the persons represented once the surety has
satisfied to the utmost the obligation for which he undertook? Fourthly, did Christ hang on the cross in the
place of the reprobate? Fifthly, did he undergo all that was due to the persons for whom he died? If he did
not, what does it mean to say that he died in their stead? If he did, why are they not all delivered? To
affirm that he died for everyone is the readiest way to prove that he died for no one in the sense Christians
have believed, and to hurry poor souls into the depths of Socinian blasphemy. The representative phrases
carry their consequences through the very obligation the surety fulfills and God accepts. (Genesis 44:33;
Hebrews 7:22; 2:9; 12:16; 2 Samuel 18:33; Romans 5:7; 9:3; 2 Corinthians 5:20; 1 Corinthians 1:13;
Luke 11:11; Matthew 5:38; 2:22; 20:28; Mark 10:45; 1 Peter 2:24)
    [524] Argument XVI. Particular passages of Scripture supply the remaining proof, since they declare
distinctly the truth already established through the relations of the saving undertaking. A few of these
passages will suffice to complete the demonstration. Each contributes its own premise, while the
definitions established in the preceding argument determine how its testimony belongs to the whole. Their
convergence completes the proof without requiring every text bearing upon the question to be collected
here.
    [525] 1. The first announcement of Jesus Christ already distinguishes his people from his enemies, “I
will put enmity between you” (the serpent) “and the woman, and between your seed and her seed.” The
seed of the woman comprises the entire body of the elect, with Christ first as its head and the others
united to him as members. The serpent’s seed comprises the devil and the whole multitude of the
reprobate, whose malignant community stands opposed to Christ’s kingdom and body. Enmity is
therefore appointed between communities constituted under opposed heads. The promise introduces
Christ through this distinction, and its saving content unfolds through the representation and deliverance
which belong to the people whose head he is. (Genesis 3:15)
    [526] The properties assigned to the woman’s seed establish its identity, for those in whom the
predicted operations concur are the persons whom the description designates. These operations concur in
the elect as believers united with Christ — for them, within them, and through them, the serpent’s head is
broken, Satan is trodden beneath their feet, his temptations are defeated, and the undertakings of his
agents are frustrated. Christ himself possesses the principal place in this victory, while his whole body
participates collectively in what he accomplishes as its head. Its continuing hatred of the serpent and his
seed belongs to that participation. The text thus identifies membership by predicates fulfilled in the
people’s union with Christ. Their reception of his victory follows that actual relation, rather than an
independent designation which leaves the predicted properties absent from those designated.
    [527] Secondly, the serpent’s seed comprises the reprobate — the unbelieving and impenitent people
of the world. The designation expresses their hostility and final exclusion, and its force in this argument
arises from the scriptural properties through which that opposed relation becomes identifiable. Two
considerations establish the referent.
    [528] First, the serpent’s enmity operates in these persons. Their hatred and opposition continually
direct themselves against the woman’s seed, so that the properties attributed to his seed find their proper
exercise in them. This historical operation manifests a disposition. The connection between disposition
and membership does not confer upon human observers an unrestricted knowledge of each person’s final
condition.
    [529] Secondly, Scripture repeatedly gives them this designation. “O generation of vipers,” identifies
them as the serpent’s seed, as does the corresponding denunciation elsewhere. Christ tells the reprobate
Pharisees, “You are of your father the devil, and the lusts of your father you will do.” The same relation
appears in “Child of the devil”, since “he that commits sin is of the devil.” The descriptions agree through
the persons and contexts to which they belong. Their figurative expression carries an actual assertion

concerning moral relation and saving condition. The figure does not dissolve the distinction which
supplies the argument. (Matthew 3:7; 23:33; John 8:44; Acts 13:10; 1 John 3:8)
    [530] The conclusion follows from the promise governing Christ’s undertaking. His death extends to
those for whom God promised that he should die. Yet God did not promise him to every person under that
appointment. The first promise does not give the woman’s seed to the serpent’s seed, or appoint Christ as
the Redeemer of the reprobate. It establishes enmity against them. The woman’s seed therefore did not die
for the serpent’s seed. Both the persons represented and the effects accomplished receive their
determination from the promised undertaking, whose fulfillment cannot be judged by replacing its actual
appointment with a universal purpose the promise does not contain.
    [531] 2. “I will profess unto them, I never knew you.” At the last judgment Christ declares that some
never stood within the relation of knowledge which he acknowledges toward his own, the people for
whom he lays down his life. He certainly knows both what he has purchased and whom he has purchased.
A supposed purchase of persons whom he finally refuses to own would divide the payment from the
ownership it establishes — if they are “bought with a price,” they belong to him. If his precious blood
purchased them, his declaration that he never knew them would expose the general ransom to this
unanswered plea, “Ah, Lord! was not your soul heavy unto death for our sakes? Did you not for us
undergo that wrath that made you sweat drops of blood? Did you not bathe yourself in your own blood,
that our blood might be spared? Did you not sanctify yourself to be an offering for us as well as for any of
your apostles? Was not your precious blood, by stripes, by sweat, by nails, by thorns, by spear, poured out
for us? Did you not remember us when you hung upon the cross? And now do you say, you never knew
us? Good Lord, though we be unworthy sinners, yet your own blood has not deserved to be despised.
Why is it that none can lay any thing to the charge of God’s elect? Is it not because you died for them?
And did you not do the same for us? Why, then, are we thus charged, thus rejected? Could not your blood
satisfy your Father, but we ourselves must be punished? Could not justice content itself with that
