sacrifice, but we must now hear, ‘Depart, I never knew you?’ ” Once a ransom for every person is
conceded, no answer to this plea preserves the force of Christ’s purchase. The knowledge denied is the
saving acknowledgment specified by the passage. Divine knowledge of every fact about every creature is
not under dispute. The purchase, ownership, and acknowledgment concern one saving relation, and the
conclusion requires their conjunction. (Matthew 7:23; John 10:14–17; 1 Corinthians 6:20)
    [532] 3. “I thank you, O Father, Lord of heaven and earth, because you have hid these things from the
wise and prudent, and have revealed them unto babes. Even so, Father: for so it seemed good in your
sight.” The sovereignty here confessed belongs to the Lord of heaven and earth, who according to his own
good pleasure withholds the gospel from some, whether by withholding its outward proclamation or its
powerful inward revelation. Christ did not die to redeem persons whom the Father had determined should
remain everlasting strangers to that redemption, never receiving even the revelation of its power.
Otherwise the Father would send his only Son to secure an end while appointing that its intended
recipients should never participate in it. Christ expressly affirms that such withholding occurs, and thanks
the Father for the dispensation against which many now complain. Divine purpose governs the
distribution of temporal means as well as their inward reception — what is disclosed, to whom it is
disclosed, and the reception granted belong to the same saving account. (Matthew 11:25–26)
    [533] 4. The testimony concerning Christ’s sheep suffices in itself to overthrow the general ransom.
Its earlier consideration permits a more concentrated statement here. First, the sheep do not comprise all
humanity. Christ expressly says, “You are not of my sheep.” The final separation of sheep and goats
confirms the distinction. And hearing his voice and knowing him, the properties assigned to his sheep, are
not found in everyone. Secondly, these sheep comprise all the elect, including those already called and
those still awaiting their calling. Those outside the gathered fold are nevertheless sheep — election
precedes the believing through which they enter it. Thirdly, the relation under which Christ gives his life
excludes the others. He dies for them as sheep, and what belongs to persons precisely under that
description belongs only to persons so described. Goats, wolves, and dogs are not included through the

sheep’s identity. He likewise dies as shepherd, an office ordering him toward his sheep, while his relation
to wolves concerns their destruction. When he distinguishes sheep from others and assigns his death to
the sheep, the distinction gives the assignment its restrictive force. Fourthly, he identifies those
represented through the Father’s gift, “My Father gave them me”. “Yours they were, and you gave them
me.” This gift cannot comprise everyone, since “all that the Father gives him shall come to him”, and he
“gives unto them eternal life, and they shall never perish.” The sheep’s inheritance rests upon this
evidence and cannot be taken from them by the world’s opposition. The additional passages confirm the
same conjunction — life given, the voice heard, the shepherd followed, and the people preserved belong
to the same persons under one appointment. (John 10:11, 15–16, 26–29; 17:6; 6:37; 11:52; Matthew
20:28)
    [534] 5. The apostle grounds the consolation of afflicted believers in God’s assured presence and

assistance. That assistance suffices to conquer every opposition, and his lovingkindness, which is better
than life itself, makes every difficulty contemptible in comparison with the good they are assured of
possessing. “If God be for us, who can be against us?” The singular act by which God makes his presence
and kindness manifest is the gift of his Son. He sent him to die for them and, rather than sparing him,
required their debt at his hand. The apostle reasons from the greater act to the lesser. Having given his
Son to death, will God withhold the other things that the people for whom he was given require? The very
greatness of the gift is the ground for expecting that he will freely give them every further good necessary
to their salvation. Two consequences follow. First, sending his Son to die for believers, and refusing to
spare him for their sake, is the highest expression of God’s love toward them. If the Son was sent to die
for everyone, God would have done as great an act of love for those who perish as for those who are
saved, and the greatest manifestation of that love would belong to both alike. Second, all those for whom
God gave his Son and did not spare him will assuredly receive all good things with him. Yet faith, grace,
and glory are not bestowed upon everyone. The Son was therefore not given to die for everyone. The
persons for whom God gave his Son and to whom he freely gives these other benefits are the very persons
to whom the consolation is addressed. The apostle calls them the “elect,” those chosen before the
foundation of the world to be holy. That designation confirms the restriction of Christ’s death to them. He
further declares that they are freely justified and freed from condemnation, assigning as reasons that
Christ died for them and that he rose and intercedes for those for whom he died. These yield two further
proofs. The first concerns the infallible effect of the death itself. Who can bring a charge against them,
and who can condemn them? The reason the apostle gives is “It is Christ that died.” His death therefore
actually frees from condemnation all those for whom he died. The second concerns the connection of
death and intercession. Christ intercedes for the people for whom he died and saves to the utmost those
for whom he intercedes. The gift, justification, and intercession ground the consolation through their
secure conjunction in the same people. Christ’s death and all its fruits therefore belong to the elect of God
alone. (Romans 8:31–34; Hebrews 7:25)
    [535] The persons addressed remain the same throughout the apostle’s connected demonstration of
consolation. The Father’s gift of the Son carries the further gift of saving goods. Justification carries
deliverance from condemnation. And Christ’s death is joined to his resurrection and continuing
intercession. Certainty belongs to the conjunction of these relations. To remove one link while claiming
the assurance furnished by the whole would retain the conclusion after abandoning part of its ground.
                                 [536] Dx⇒Bx,      Jx⇒¬Cx,      Ox⇒Ix. (45)
      [537] Terms. D denotes the saving gift specified in this passage, B the further benefits promised with
it, J justification, and C condemnation in the opposed final judicial sense. O denotes offering and I
intercession. The discourse fixes these meanings, while x retains the same personal reference throughout
the relevant chain of relations.
    [538] Consequence. Assurance proceeds from a saving relation already provided and carrying
determinate effects. The believer’s desire for safety supplies neither its cause nor its certainty. He is

directed to the work accomplished for him, the office presently exercised on his behalf, and the
completion promised by that same faithful undertaking.
    [539] 6. “In whom we have redemption.” Those for whom the blood was shed participate in the
benefits procured through it. Redemption here includes the forgiveness of sins. Yet forgiveness does not
belong to everyone. Its possessors are “blessed”, and their blessedness endures eternally, belonging to the
seed of righteous Abraham rather than to all humanity. The redemption and its fruits cannot be detached
while the passage’s account of participation is retained. Whoever extends the shedding of blood to every
person must therefore extend to those same persons the forgiveness and blessedness which the passage
assigns to it. (Ephesians 1:7; Romans 4:7, 16)
    [540] 7. “He has made him to be sin for us, that we might be made the righteousness of God in him.”
Christ was made sin, or constituted an offering for sin, in his death. The persons for whom he underwent
this appointment are the persons made God’s righteousness in him, “By his stripes we are healed”.
“Greater love has no man than this, that a man lay down his life for his friends.” Nor can intercession, or
any other act performed for the elect, exceed in love the gift of his life. If he performed this greatest act
for everyone, the withholding of the remaining acts, including the intercession through which he saves to
the uttermost, would sever the lesser completion from its greater foundation. The representative offering
carries the saving result through the purpose expressly assigned to it. Its fulfillment must retain the same
persons on both sides — those represented in the sacrifice are those in whom its appointed righteousness
becomes actual. (2 Corinthians 5:21; Isaiah 53:5; John 15:13)
    [541] 8. “I pray for them: I pray not for the world, but for them which you have given me; for they are
yours.” Again, “For their sakes I sanctify myself.” The offering by which Christ sanctifies himself and the
intercession he exercises retain the common object established in paragraphs 91–113. His express
restriction identifies whose benefits the joined operations secure and supplies the particular premise
required by the present argument. (John 17:9, 19)
    [542] 9. “Husbands, love your wives, even as Christ also loved the church, and gave himself for it.”
The corresponding testimony gives the same account of his purchase. Christ’s bride, the church, is the
expressly appointed object of both his love and his death, under a relation as proper and exclusive as a
husband’s conjugal affection toward his own wife. To extend the love expressed in this death to others
would extend the corresponding exhortation, allowing husbands a conjugal affection for women besides
their wives. The self-giving has its determinate completion in cleansing and presenting the church. Those
finally presented are therefore the people first loved and represented in the offering. (Ephesians 5:25; Acts
20:28)
    [543] The arguments already established complete the demonstration, although further proofs could be
added to a discussion of the whole controversy. Their convergence supplies enough evidence for anyone
willing to receive evidence. An obstinate refusal would not be corrected merely by enlarging their
number. The positive case therefore ends here. What follows concerns the objections, whose force must
be judged against the dependencies now established together. Each answer will identify the premise
actually challenged, since an objection to one relation cannot overthrow the whole by silently substituting
another.
    [544] The objections to the particular and effectual redemption of the elect arise from three classes of
scriptural expression. Some passages describe Christ’s ransom and propitiation through general or
indefinite terms. Others seem to suggest that his death proves fruitless for some persons through their own
fault. Still others extend offers, promises, and exhortations to persons who never actually embrace its
fruits. From these sources the defenders of universal redemption draw their arguments, often insisting that
no contrary account can possibly agree with Scripture. Their inference nevertheless forces these passages
to express an equal ransom for every person. Before examining the individual texts whose interpretation
has been pressed into this service, the principles underlying their general language must therefore be
established. These principles arise from Scripture itself, agree with the particular redemption defended

here, and explain why the word speaks generally without appointing a universal ransom. The truth of
those expressions belongs to the relations they actually declare. Appropriate distinctions will further
expose the false consequences and imputations attached to this doctrine, whether through error or malice.
Worth, extent, application, proclamation, and judgment possess distinct objects and operations. Their
differences must remain explicit through the exposition, because the worth sufficient for an undertaking
does not itself determine everyone for whom the undertaking was appointed, and a command addressed
universally does not by its address identify the persons in whom its saving result is accomplished.
     [545] 1. The blood and death of Jesus Christ possess infinite dignity, preciousness, and worth.
Christian judgment must begin here — any position apparently diminishing them deserves suspicion, and
any position found actually to do so must be rejected as injurious to Christ’s merit and honor. Scripture
repeatedly exalts his sacrifice. Through the unity of his person it names his blood “God’s own blood”. It
places that sacrifice infinitely above every other, deriving its operation from “the eternal Spirit,” and
describing the offering itself as “without spot”. Its preciousness exceeds silver, gold, and every
corruptible thing, and it justifies from the sins from which the law could never justify. The Father
intended the sacrifice to possess the very worth it actually possesses. His purpose was therefore that the
Son should offer a sacrifice sufficient in itself to redeem every person, had he appointed it to that end.
The same sufficiency would extend to other worlds, had he freely created them and willed their
redemption. The offering is thus sufficient in worth for the expiation of every sin of every person in the
whole world. Two causes establish this sufficiency — the dignity of the person who both offered and was
offered, and the greatness of the suffering through which he bore the whole curse of the law and the wrath
due to sin. These constitute the blood’s intrinsic perfection and real internal value. Its application to
particular persons, its appointment as their price, and the benefits they receive are further relations,
dependent upon God’s intention and will. Infinite worth makes the offering sufficient to purchase
everyone. The divine appointment constitutes it the actual purchase of those whose redemption God
intends. The offerer’s and accepter’s intention determines for whom the payment stands as a price. That
determination is external to the intrinsic value by which the offering is fit to serve as a price. This
distinction explains the older scholastic formula, accepted by some Protestant divines and rejected by
others, “That Christ died for all in respect of the sufficiency of the ransom he paid, but not in respect of
the efficacy of its application;” or “The blood of Christ was a sufficient price for the sins of all the
world.” Some correct the latter expression to “That the blood of Christ was sufficient to have been made a
price for all,” and in this form it expresses the truth already established. Appointment as a price for some
or all does not arise from intrinsic dignity alone, but from the purpose for which God and Christ employ
the offering. To deny that Christ’s blood was a sufficient price and ransom for every person therefore
denies its appointment as everyone’s ransom, without denying its sufficiency in worth. The earlier
formula can likewise be received if it means only that the blood possessed value sufficient for everyone’s
redemption, and that Christ intended to offer a payment possessing that sufficiency. But “To die for
them,” ordinarily asserts more — it assigns to Christ the intention of redeeming every person through that
payment. This is denied. Such an intention, once joined to the actual payment of an adequate ransom,
would require every represented person to participate in the eternal redemption purchased. Otherwise
God’s design would fail because the ransom proved deficient, or divine justice would refuse release after
receiving the appointed price. Infinite value supplies the offering’s fitness. Divine appointment identifies
its beneficiaries. One cannot enumerate the beneficiaries merely by restating the value, because value and
personal appointment are distinct predicates with distinct grounds. (Acts 20:28; 13:28; Hebrews 9:14; 1
Peter 1:18)
   [546] Universal redemption diminishes the very value it claims to enlarge. The number of persons
toward whom the offering is appointed is external to its intrinsic worth. That worth concerns the effects it
produces and the saving operations it is fitted to accomplish. Yet the universal account weakens or nearly
abolishes these effects. First, it reduces the death to the opening of a door of grace, without explaining
where this door stands, and expressly denies that the sacrifice effectually brings anyone through it.
Secondly, it makes the result a bare permission for God to save, if he wishes and upon whatever condition

he chooses, while denying that Christ purchased a right to salvation for anyone. Its further concessions
reveal the consequence — God could, after the death, impose a legal condition again. Every person could
perish while the sacrifice nevertheless achieved its entire intended effect. And neither faith nor
sanctification was purchased through it. No benefit secured for anyone would therefore exceed what a
person might possess while proceeding to hell. Such descriptions disclose impoverished judgments
concerning the blood’s innate sufficiency. The honor of Christ our Mediator, God and man and all-
sufficient Redeemer, requires the opposite confession. His death possesses such dignity, preciousness,
and infinite fullness that it is perfectly sufficient to redeem, justify, reconcile, and save every sinner in the
world. To satisfy divine justice for every human sin. And to bring every person to everlasting glory. Its
merit contains no internal defect requiring its beneficiaries to supply what the offering could not procure.
The issue of extent must therefore be settled by appointment, while the saving efficacy remains the
efficacy of a sacrifice adequate to accomplish salvation. To enlarge its alleged reach by removing the
results belonging to it diminishes the very predicate being extended. This fullness and sufficiency
establish two further consequences.
    [547] First, the gospel may be proclaimed generally to “all nations,” and possesses a rightful address
to “every creature”, because the salvation it announces is sufficient for everyone who comes. The remedy
contains enough to heal every disease and deliver from every evil. A thousand worlds could hear this
gospel upon the same ground — Christ contains sufficient salvation for all, received through the touch of
faith, which draws refreshment from this fountain. The objection that preaching to everyone becomes
useless unless Christ died for everyone therefore mistakes what proclamation requires. It equally mistakes
the command of faith when it accuses God of requiring belief in the false proposition that Christ died for
each hearer. Three considerations already answer it. First, among the nations receiving the gospel are
people appointed to salvation—“I have much people,”—whose salvation, under the appointed means,
requires preaching among others as well as themselves. Secondly, the new covenant removes the external
privileges and distinctions of peoples, languages, and nations, so that the word of grace is preached
without such discrimination and everyone everywhere is called to repent. Thirdly, the initial demand of
faith concerns the sole saving name given under heaven, Jesus Christ, through whom salvation is
proclaimed. It does not first require each hearer to affirm that Christ died for him personally. These truths
dispose of the objection independently, while the sufficiency presently considered supplies a further
adequate ground for the general commands. The proclamation extends to every hearer because the
salvation offered is adequate for everyone who receives it. No prior identification of the elect is required
of the preacher. The promise remains true for every person who comes, and actual reception proceeds
through the causal order already established. (Matthew 28:19; Mark 16:15)
    [548] Secondly, ministers may address every person in their congregations with the command to
believe and the assurance that whoever believes will be saved. Their ignorance of God’s secret counsel
neither invalidates this address nor supplies a reason to investigate what they are forbidden to search.
They know that Christ’s death possesses enough saving worth for every person who believes, while God
retains the purpose determining whom he will give faith and for whom Christ particularly died. The
distinction belongs to their office — the revealed command and promise authorize the minister’s speech.
The hidden decree is not an object he must first inspect. His creaturely limitation of knowledge governs
his duty without imposing a corresponding limitation upon the divine knowledge directing salvation.
(Deuteronomy 29:29)
    [549] The minister’s observation has a real boundary. Confessions, conduct, instruction, and
responses provide evidence without giving him possession of every person’s inward history or final
condition. His commission therefore appoints a field of action governed by revealed rules, within which
he performs his duty under limited knowledge. The ignorance belongs to the creature exercising the
office. It does not narrow the saving purpose governing those whom he addresses.
                [550] yx(t)=Om(Xx(t)),         Om(X)=Om(X′) can hold while X≠X′. (46)

    [551] Terms. O(m) denotes the minister’s observational access to a person’s actual condition X, while
y denotes the testimony and conduct available to him. Different inward states may yield identical outward
evidence. The equation expresses that possible non-uniqueness without assigning election a numerical
measure.
    [552] Consequence. Outward evidence may warrant a pastoral judgment while leaving the inward
condition underdetermined. The minister obeys his office by acting upon the revealed rules and entrusting
unrevealed particulars to God. Proclamation occurs within this actual limitation of knowledge, which also
governs the later examination of charitable descriptions and apparent counterexamples. (Ashby, An
Introduction to Cybernetics, ch. 6)
   [553] This distinction removes much of the opponents’ empty display, as the particular answers will
demonstrate. The truth of the minister’s proclamation and the particular efficacy of redemption stand
together because they concern distinct operations — his declaration supplies the appointed address, while
saving grace produces in its recipients the condition to which the promise belongs.
     [554] 2. The administration of the new covenant enlarges Christ’s manifested dominion after his
appearance in the flesh. The former external divisions fall away — Gentile exclusion ceases, the partition
is broken, and Abraham’s promised inheritance of the world receives its accomplishment through his
relation as father of the faithful. This administration differs fundamentally from the earlier order, in which
a single people and family stood as God’s peculiar possession while the remaining nations were excluded.
The contrast explains Scripture’s general expressions. Their universality removes the restrictions
previously imposed. It does not thereby collect every individual into the same saving appointment. Their
intended end determines both their meaning and the people whom they include. Christ’s visible kingdom
extends rightfully to all nations and actually to many, since within those nations God has elect persons
who are brought forth in the generations during which the means of grace operate among them. The
distribution therefore crosses every former difference without requiring the inclusion of every person
under every difference. This is precisely what the enlargement requires. Objections founded merely upon
all, all men, all nations, the world, or the whole world consequently exceed the expressions’ intended
work. The Holy Spirit employs them to declare the removal of national and personal exclusions, the
opening of Old Testament boundaries, and the extension of Christ’s kingdom beyond Judea and
Jerusalem. Through the abolition of those restrictions the elect among every people, called “The fullness
of the Gentiles,” receive entrance, since there is now “neither Greek nor Jew, circumcision nor
uncircumcision, Barbarian, Scythian, bond nor free, but Christ is all, and in all.” The promise to “pour out
his Spirit upon all flesh” receives its explanation in Peter’s account of the apostles’ gifts — the Spirit
equips them to preach among the nations, “having received grace and apostleship for obedience to the
faith among all nations”, extending the commission beyond the Jews to persons within all nations, “the
gospel being the power of God unto salvation to every one that believes, to the Jew first, and also to the
Greek”. Salvation still concerns the peculiar people whom Christ purchased, whom he “redeemed out of
every kindred, and tongue, and people, and nation”. That explicit distribution interprets the general
expressions elsewhere. Preaching is commanded among these nations so that the purchased people
dispersed through them may be brought home to God. The apostle’s extended exposition declares the
same enlargement. The passages ordinarily pressed into the service of universal grace and redemption
therefore bear this sense, as their later examination will establish individually. National extension
removes an ethnic restriction. It does not, merely by removing it, confer identical saving effects upon
every individual. The changed administration retains its own actual domain and predicates. (Colossians
3:11; Joel 2:28; Acts 2:17; Romans 1:5, 16; Revelation 5:9; Matthew 28:19; John 11:52; Ephesians 2:14–
17)
    [555] 3. Human duty and divine purpose require an exact distinction, because no inference passes
immediately from one to the other. God’s decree does not constitute the rule by which we discover our
duty, and our performance of a command does not disclose what God has decreed to accomplish. The
distinction governs especially the ministry of the word. Exhortations, invitations, precepts, and threats

continually declare what people ought to do, manifest God’s approval of the commanded act, and
establish the true connection between that act and its appointed consequence. They do not disclose his
secret counsel concerning every individual receiving the address. A minister therefore has no commission
to investigate whom God particularly intends to save or for whom Christ was particularly sent to die. His
directions arise from the revealed will in which his commission is given. Universal precepts concerning
acts do not consequently disclose a universal purpose concerning persons. Ministers summon everyone to
repent and believe while remaining ignorant of those to whom God will grant saving repentance and in
whom he will effect faith with power. Their offers in God’s name do not tell every hearer “It is the
purpose and intention of God that you should believe.” No authority for such a declaration has been
given. They declare God’s command and thereby the hearer’s duty, without claiming to reveal what God
himself will accomplish in that particular hearer. Every recipient may infer his own duty from the
outward offer. None may infer God’s hidden purpose from that address alone, although performance of
the duty may afterwards furnish knowledge of his saving relation. The claim that God has given Christ for
everyone to whom Christ is offered therefore confuses what the offer declares. Gospel proclamation does
not announce to every individual the particular divine act already performed or still to be performed
toward him. It announces what he ought to do in order to receive God’s approval and the promised
benefits. Command appoints an act as obligatory. Purpose appoints an event as certain to occur. Passing
from the former to the latter requires a further premise which the command itself never supplies. Two
consequences follow.
     [556] First, wherever God sends the gospel in its power, he intends the salvation of some who receive
its proclamation. Ministers neither inspect that particular purpose nor receive permission to neglect others
— they must seek everyone’s good as far as they can and exercise the hopeful judgment proper to their
calling. They may therefore offer Christ, together with life and salvation in him, to every person, although
the Son was given to the elect alone. Their hope and diligence answer the duty assigned under incomplete
personal knowledge. The saving operation answers the divine purpose whose particular recipients remain
hidden from them.
    [557] Secondly, the offer has a true and fruitful office, since it declares the duty acceptable to God
when performed as required. If it is asked what divine will is revealed when persons for whom Christ did
not die receive the command to believe, the answer has three parts. First, the command discloses the act
they ought to perform if they would do what pleases God. Secondly, it announces the sufficiency of the
salvation in Christ for everyone believing in him. Thirdly, it declares the certain and inviolable
conjunction of faith with salvation — whoever believes will obtain the promised benefit, because Christ
never casts out anyone who comes. The later exposition will develop this further. A promise connecting
faith with salvation does not assert that every hearer already believes. Its truth belongs to the secured
dependence of the benefit upon the condition specified.
    [558] The gospel promise remains true in the conditional form it actually possesses — Christ receives
every person who comes to him. The command gives the hearer his duty, and the promise warrants the
consequence of performing it. Neither depends for its truth upon the minister’s advance knowledge of
which hearers will come.
                      [559] ∀x [Fx⇒Sx],        Commandm(Fx)⇏Knowm(Ex). (47)
    [560] Terms. The universal conditional expresses the saving connection. Command denotes the
minister’s appointed proclamation, and Know his knowledge of a person’s election. The second
expression denies that exercising the former office confers the latter knowledge.
    [561] Consequence. The command’s sincerity follows its actual object, the duty imposed, and the
promise attached. Grace supplies the further causal account of the faith eventually received. Confusing
these relations either requires the minister to know what he cannot know or converts the hearer’s
obligation into an allegedly revealed list of divine decrees.

    [562] 4. Many general expressions correcting the extent assigned to redemption arise from the Jews’
deeply rooted belief that the promised Messiah’s salvation belonged exclusively to Abraham’s
descendants according to the flesh. This inherited error affected even the apostles for a time. Its correction
enlarges the declared object beyond Israel without establishing an unlimited universality of individuals.
The Jews commonly treated the promises as universal within their own community and exclusive of
everyone outside it, calling the others “dogs, uncircumcised,” and pouring curses upon them. Their
reaction to the Gentiles gathering for Paul’s preaching manifests this pride — they became “filled with
envy, contradicting, blaspheming, and raising up persecution against them”. The apostle describes the
same disposition elsewhere, “They please not God,” he says, “and are contrary to all men; forbidding us
to speak to the Gentiles that they might be saved;” Nothing in the Savior’s preaching enraged them more
than his declaration that the vineyard would be entrusted to others. The proclamation encountered hearers
whose inherited account of its domain already excluded those whom it now addressed. General language
corrects that received restriction by declaring the people whom the hearers had wrongly supposed to stand
outside the promise. Its breadth must be understood through the error it overturns. (Acts 13:45–50; 1
Thessalonians 2:15–16)
    [563] The apostles’ own conduct shows how deeply this ancestral teaching had shaped their
understanding. Their question about restoring Israel’s kingdom already reveals it. Their practice after
receiving the universal commission reveals it more plainly. Though commanded to teach and baptize all
nations or every creature, and furnished with the promised power from above, they initially acted as
though their commission concerned only Israel’s lost sheep, preaching to Jews alone. When the contrary
became evident, their praise expressed astonishment at something previously unknown, “Then has God
also to the Gentiles granted repentance unto life.” Such difficulty is intelligible because Gentile
participation was the great mystery disclosed by the Spirit to the holy apostles and prophets with a clarity
not granted in earlier ages, “That the Gentiles should be fellow-heirs, and of the same body, and partakers
of his promise in Christ by the gospel.” The change concerns the apostles’ creaturely apprehension of an
already appointed commission. Events and instruction communicated through revelation corrected that
apprehension. Their former limitation of understanding therefore explains the general expressions by
which they subsequently announced the commission’s breadth. It does not establish that every individual
within every nation receives the same saving appointment. (Acts 1:6, 8; 11:18–19; Matthew 28:19; Mark
16:15; Ephesians 3:5–6)
    [564] Once the Spirit made this truth known, the appointed time had arrived for the little sister’s
reception, the prodigal’s return, and Japheth’s dwelling in Shem’s tents. The apostles accordingly sought
to remove the inherited error from their brethren according to the flesh, toward whom they bore a special
care, while assuring the eunuch that he was no dry tree and the Gentile that he was not excluded from
God’s people. Their general expressions directly oppose the former restriction, which would destroy the
enlargement belonging to Christ’s kingdom. World, all men, all nations, and every creature declare that
redemption and its proclamation are no longer confined to the particular nation and family previously
imagined to possess them exclusively. They extend to the whole people of God dispersed throughout the
earth. John especially employs this language. Having lived to witness the Lord’s first coming in the
fearful judgment executed upon the Jewish nation roughly forty years after his death, he repeatedly
affirms the world’s benefit through Christ against the Jewish claim of national exclusivity. His own
statement furnishes the interpretive rule, “He signified that Jesus should die for that nation; and not for
that nation only, but that also he should gather together in one the children of God that were scattered
abroad;” Accordingly, he tells believing Jews that Christ’s propitiation concerns more than themselves,
“but for the sins of the whole world”: the people of God scattered through the earth, whose saving
participation is not bound to one nation. The historical opposition supplies substantial light upon world
and all in the disputed passages. Their universality distributes salvation across all sorts of people against
the former national exclusion. It does not collect every human individual into the same saving result. The
commission’s field is enlarged while the relation between saving operation and its recipients remains

particular. Geographic breadth and individual extent are therefore different determinations, and the former
cannot settle the latter by itself. (John 11:51–52; 1 John 2:2)
    [565] 5. The general terms used indefinitely of redemption’s object require careful examination of
their nature and actual extent. The opposing case places its principal weight, sometimes its entire weight,
upon expressions whose apparent breadth seems adequate to universal redemption — the world, the
whole world, all, and similar terms. Having seized them, its advocates proceed “Io triumphe,” as though
the words themselves had completed the victory. Repetition of world, whole world, all, and all men
becomes their answer to every question. Context, interpretive rules, the passage’s scope and
circumstances, and the uses of the same expressions elsewhere are then set aside in favor of the bare
written letter, “Away with the gloss and interpretation; give us leave to believe what the word expressly
says;” Yet a general refusal of interpretation according to the proportion of faith would establish errors
which these advocates themselves reject. The Anthropomorphites could assign bodily form to God, who
has none. Transubstantiation could overthrow Christ’s body, which he truly possesses. And other
pernicious doctrines would claim equal protection under the isolated letter. Affection for a preferred
conclusion conceals the consequence of that interpretive rule. Empty clamors may unsettle weak hearers,
but their force cannot require silence where truth is concerned. The general terms will prove unable to
sustain universal redemption, whether absolute or conditional. Their breadth always possesses a domain
supplied by the discourse. Identical grammatical form can therefore extend across different populations
without contradiction, because the subject actually designated changes with the context. The issue is
whether the asserted saving predicate belongs to every person without exception in the passage under
examination, and that question cannot be decided merely by repeating the general word.
    [566] Two expressions carry the principal dispute — world and all. Their individual occurrences, and
the arguments drawn from them, will receive examination later. For the present it is enough to establish
that scriptural usage does not invariably make either expression collect every individual of whom it might
grammatically be spoken. Their meanings vary, and the passage’s scope and subject determine the
applicable sense. Lexical possibility supplies the question. Actual use supplies the answer. Before an
inference can rest upon either word, the discourse must identify the domain and relations that the word
actually denotes.
    [567] The New Testament word relevant here is κόσμος. Another word sometimes rendered world,
αἰών, concerns duration rather than the object continuing within that duration and does not govern this
dispute. Anyone denying that κόσμος is πολύσημον displays his unfamiliarity with Scripture’s actual
usage. Its different meanings are sufficient to establish that no argument can proceed from the bare
occurrence of such an equivocal expression. The sense belonging to the particular passage must first be
distinguished and demonstrated. The classification that follows separates the kinds of object to which the
term applies. A physical totality, a population inhabiting it, and a moral condition are not interchangeable
subjects. Their common written designation does not constitute them a single logical term within an
inference.
     [568] Mundus sumitur,
1. Subjectivè,
1. Ὀλικῶς.
2. Μερικῶς, idque vel pro
1. Cœlo aspectabili.
2. Terra habitabili.
2. Adjunctivè, ratione
1. Incolarum, idque
1. Collectivè, seu κατὰ πάντας.

2. Multis.

3. Signanter, pro
1. Bonis, seu electis.
2. Malis, seu reprobis.

1. Præcipuis.
2. Romanis.
2. Accidentum

1. Ipsa corruptione.
2. Sede corruptionis.
3. Terrena conditione.
2. Maledictionis.
    [569] The classification articulates distinct uses within one ordered account. Its divisions separate the
containing world from the populations inhabiting it, and distinguish moral conditions from communities
qualified by other relations. The same written expression thus designates different objects. An argument
remains sound only when each occurrence retains the object actually intended.
                 [570] ⟦world⟧c1=D1,         ⟦world⟧c2=D2,          D1≠D2is possible. (48)
    [571] Terms. Brackets identify an expression’s referent under context c. D denotes that referent or
domain. The formula registers variation of meaning without deciding which interpretation belongs to any
particular occurrence.
    [572] Consequence. The classification refutes the claim that the written word invariably identifies an
unrestricted population. It does not independently establish a particular restricted reading. That reading
requires proof from the audience, subject, predicate, and relations of the passage—the work undertaken
by the individual expositions below.
    [573] The following observations substantiate the distinctions already classified. Their purpose is to
retain the actual object asserted in each proposition. Where that object changes, repetition of the same
written word no longer supplies the common term necessary to an inference. The wheel in paragraph 0
can be named as a whole while its spokes and its rim have each their proper place. So when Scripture
speaks of the world, attend to what it declares of that world, for the predicate may identify the very people
whose extent is disputed.
    [574] Smyrna’s church confesses that Christ suffered for “the world of them that are saved.” The
expression contains its own qualification and thereby identifies its referent through an actual saving
relation. This ancient Christian usage prevents the bare word world from deciding its extent before the
sentence is read. An interpreter insisting that every occurrence includes every individual must explain the
qualification actually present. His prior definition cannot erase the words through which the proposition
receives its determination. Interpretation answers to that proposition as a whole. (Martyrdom of Polycarp
17.2)
   [575] Scripture’s principal uses of world fall into five divisions. Each designates its own semantic
domain, which must first be identified before the expression can contribute to a conclusion concerning
redemption’s extent.
    [576] First, Pro mundo continente — world designates the containing creation. This occurs generally,
ὅλως, of the entire fabric of heaven and earth with everything God created within them. The use is
frequent. It also occurs distinctively, either of the heavens and what belongs to them as distinguished from
earth, or, very commonly, of the habitable earth itself. Such propositions concern the created whole or one
of its divisions. They do not enumerate the persons participating in a saving benefit, and transferring their

domain to that question changes the object of the assertion. (Job 34:13; Acts 17:24; Ephesians 1:4; Psalms
90:2; 24:1; 98:7; Matthew 13:38; John 1:9; 3:17, 19; 6:14; 17:11; 1 Timothy 1:15; 6:7)
   [577] Secondly, world designates what is contained, especially its human inhabitants. Six distinctions
occur within this use. First, it can comprise every person universally. Secondly, it can refer indefinitely to
people without specifying restriction or enlargement. Thirdly, and most commonly, it expresses many.
Fourthly, a comparative use denotes a great portion of humanity. Fifthly, a restricted historical use
denotes the Roman Empire’s inhabitants. Sixthly, people receive designation through their qualifications
— either the good, God’s people by appointment or present possession, or the evil, wicked and rejected
inhabitants of the world. Collective, comparative, historical, and moral relations thus determine different
human domains. The predicate asserted of the term identifies the relation under which that population is
meant. (Romans 3:6, 19; 5:12; 1:8; 10:18; 4:13; 11:12, 15; John 7:4, 7; 4:42; 12:19; 16:8; 17:21, 25; 3:16;
6:33, 51; 14:17, 22; 15:19; Isaiah 13:11; Matthew 18:7; 24:14; 26:13; 1 Corinthians 4:9; 6:2; 11:32;
Revelation 13:3; Luke 2:1; Psalms 22:27; 2 Corinthians 5:19; Colossians 1:6; 1 John 2:2; 5:19; Hebrews
11:38; 2 Peter 2:5)
   [578] Thirdly, world designates the creation under corruption, or the corruption pervading its affairs.
The object is then a moral condition, whose effects and opposition to grace follow from that condition.
The expression does not independently supply a complete enumeration of human persons. (Galatians 1:4;
6:14; Ephesians 2:2; James 1:27; 4:4; 1 John 2:15–17; 1 Corinthians 7:31, 33; 1:20–21; 3:18–19;
Colossians 2:8; 2 Timothy 4:10; Romans 12:2)
    [579] Fourthly, world denotes an earthly estate or the worldly condition of persons and things, a
frequent use beyond the examples collected here. The designated state of life and action cannot simply be
transferred to every person inhabiting the earth. (Psalms 73:12; Luke 16:8; John 18:36; 1 John 4:5)
   [580] Fifthly, world designates the accursed order under Satan’s power. Scripture contains further
uses which need not be catalogued here. In this use the object receives its determination through the
hostile power actually governing it. The predicates of the particular passage establish whether that
condition, or persons finally delivered from it, is intended. (John 7:7; 14:30; 16:11, 33; 1 Corinthians
2:12; 2 Corinthians 4:4; Ephesians 6:12)
    [581] The catalogue exposes the emptiness of an argument which terrifies unstable hearers merely by
repeating how often Scripture uses world concerning redemption. “Parvas habet spes Troja, si tales
habet.” If the general ransom’s strongest defense rests upon an equivocal word treated as though its
meaning never varied, the whole defense rests upon sophistical ambiguity. The preceding examples
establish the word’s several uses. The later examination of particular objections will show, with God’s
assistance, that no occurrence concerning redemption denotes every person without exception. Even
outside that subject, this unrestricted meaning occurs in very few places. Several further observations
complete the interpretive account. Ambiguity defeats an inference when its meanings actually change,
and its presence requires the intended sense to be established precisely. It provides no license to select
meanings arbitrarily. The point is therefore neither to evade a universal assertion nor to impose a
restriction in advance, but to preserve the object which each actual proposition supplies before using that
proposition as a premise.
    [582] First, Scripture frequently employs ἀντανάκλασις, repeating one word while changing its sense.
In “Let the dead bury their dead,” the first dead denotes those spiritually dead in sin, while the second
denotes bodily death through separation of soul and body. Christ came εἰς τὰ ἴδια, “to his own,” the things
he had made. καὶ οἱ ἴδιοι, “his own,” the greater part of his people, “received him not.” Again, “That
which is born of the Spirit is spirit.” The first Spirit names God’s almighty Spirit, while the second names
the spiritual life of grace received from him. An inference imposing the first sense upon the second would
therefore distort the Holy Spirit’s meaning. World exhibits the same variation, “He was in the world, and
the world was made by him, and the world knew him not.” Its three occurrences cannot bear one identical
signification. The first concerns a portion of the habitable earth, subjectivè μερικῶς. The second concerns

the whole created frame of heaven and earth, subjectivè ὁλικῶς. And the third concerns unbelieving
people inhabiting the earth, adjunctivè. The same discrimination applies to “God sent not his Son into the
world to condemn the world, but that the world through him might be saved.” The first world denotes the
inhabited region in which the Savior conversed. The second may, as some propose, include all humanity
— even then the statement is true, because condemnation was not the principal aim of his coming, and his
purpose to save his own people excludes an intention to condemn everyone. The third denotes the elect,
or believers living through successive generations, whom he actually intended to save. Extending that
intended salvation beyond its recipients would make the divine purpose fail and Christ’s undertaking
insufficient for its appointed end. One sentence can thus employ the same expression with different
referents. Causal and logical explanation must follow the actual subjects asserted, since identical spelling
does not make a created region, all humanity, and the believing people one object. (Matthew 8:22; John
1:10–11; 3:6, 17)
    [583] Secondly, no argument can impose upon a phrase in one passage a meaning demonstrably
absent from its other scriptural uses unless the present context or subject requires that meaning. God loves
the world and sends his Son. He reconciles the world to himself in Christ. Christ is the propitiation for the
whole world’s sins. If these passages require every person without exception, their scope must be
accepted. But that requirement must be shown from the passages themselves. Otherwise there is no reason
to assign them a universality absent from the same word elsewhere. “The world knew him not,” cannot
mean that every person disbelieved, because the corresponding passage expressly identifies believers.
“That all the world should be taxed,” concerns the principal inhabitants of the Roman Empire. “I speak to
the world those things which I have heard of him,” concerns the Jews whom Christ addressed during his
earthly mission, rather than every human being, to whom he was not then sent. “Behold, the world is gone
after him!” designates a large gathering within one small nation. “The whole world lies in wickedness,”
excludes believers from the world so described. And whether “All the world wondered after the beast,”
can include every individual without exception is evident to anyone considering the assertion. All nations,
whose grammatical breadth equals world, likewise receives its determination through the discourse. When
world, all the world, or the whole world denotes human inhabitants, its usual and almost invariable
reference is to some or many persons qualified by the predicate — good or evil, believing or unbelieving,
elect or reprobate. No interpretive warrant makes the disputed passages exceptions merely because
universal redemption requires them to be exceptions. Their individual exposition must settle their actual
sense. A contextual reading preserves the coherence of what is asserted of its subject. It cannot import an
unrestricted population from an unrelated use of the same expression and then force the saving predicate
upon that imported domain. (John 1:10, 12; 8:26; 12:19; Luke 2:1; 1 John 5:19; Revelation 13:3; 18:3, 23;
Romans 1:5; Psalms 118:10; 1 Chronicles 14:17; Jeremiah 27:7)
    [584] The argument drawn from all depends on the same confusion that attends the argument drawn
from world, since the breadth of either expression receives its determination from the relation in which
Scripture employs it. As already shown, Scripture nowhere says that Christ died for all men or gave
himself a ransom for all men, still less that he did so for every individual. It expressly affirms that he
“gave himself a ransom for all”. Whether the persons comprehended are all believers, all the elect, some
of every sort, or every member of every sort is the proposition under dispute. Our adversaries assert the
last of these and place the weight of their case upon the bare word, as though its generality already settled
what it comprehends. The circumstances of the passage and the analogy of faith furnish no such grounds,
as the exposition of the texts urged for their position will establish. The prior question is whether
Scripture uses the word in such a manner that the unrestricted reading follows of necessity. The domain of
the assertion is fixed by what is said of the persons concerned and by the relations within which the
passage places them. (1 Timothy 2:6)
    [585] All has two familiar uses in human discourse, both of which occur in Scripture. It can
comprehend every individual in the relevant domain collectively, or it can extend a proposition
distributively to persons or things of every sort, without excluding any sort from the domain. No one

disputes the former use. The error lies in claiming that it is the only one, when scarcely one occurrence in
ten throughout Scripture can bear the unrestricted sense and a few ordinary instances establish the
propriety of the other. Christ declares, “And I, if I be lifted up from the earth, will draw all unto me.” The
rendering “all men,” introduces a noun absent from the original, which has only πάντας, even if the sense
is otherwise preserved. If this all comprised each individual, each individual would be drawn to Christ,
made a believer, truly converted, and certainly saved, since of those who come by his and the Father’s
drawing he says that “he will in no wise cast out.” The extent of that drawing is declared by the persons
who say, “You have redeemed us out of every kindred, and tongue, and people, and nation.” Christ draws
the many whom he has redeemed out of every people. The exposition supplied by Scripture itself is worth
more than a thousand glosses that detach the word from the saving operation predicated of its objects. The
translators follow the same usage when they render πᾶν λάχανον, which ῥητῶς is “every herb”, as “all
manner of herbs,” giving the expression its proper distributive force. Their business is to render the proper
sense, and they rightly understand the tithe to extend to herbs of every sort, since the Pharisees neither
tithed nor could tithe each individual herb. The Pharisee who says, “I give tithes of all that I possess;”
likewise speaks of the several kinds of his possessions, for the assertion cannot concern every individual
thing in his keeping. The same restriction follows from the terms of the promise, “I will pour out of my
Spirit, ἐπὶ πᾶσαν σάρκα·” The Spirit is poured out on people of diverse sorts, and the event itself
determines whether the words comprehend each human being. So πάντα τὰ τετράποδα, literally “all
beasts or fourfooted creatures,” is rendered “all manner of beasts,” because the creatures are
comprehended under their several sorts. The statement “One believes that he may eat all things;” grants
liberty to choose among things fit to eat, as the object of the liberty makes plain. The very chapter in
which our opponents insist, falsely and to no purpose, that “God will have all men to be saved,” must
concern every individual supplies an instance which even they concede to our reading. Paul says, “I will,
therefore, that men pray ἐν παντὶ τόπῳ·” No one understands the direction to extend to each individual
place in heaven, earth, and hell. In the same manner Christ is said to cure πᾶσαν νόσον because his cures
extended to every sort of disease, and no one imagines that every disease of every person was thereby
healed. The difference is therefore in the proposition itself. Where all members of a specified class are
comprehended, the predicate attaches to each member. Where the persons or things are drawn from every
class, no class is excluded, yet the predicate can be true of the persons or things drawn from it without
attaching to each of its members. The two uses cannot have the same saving consequences merely
because Scripture employs the same word for both. (John 12:32; 6:37; Revelation 5:9; Luke 11:42; 18:12;
Acts 2:17; 10:12; Romans 14:2; 1 Corinthians 1:5; 1 Timothy 2:4, 8; Matthew 9:35)
     [586] A proposition about every individual attributes its predicate to every member of the domain. A
proposition about persons drawn from every sort asserts that the domain contains no relevant sort without
a participant and leaves the number of participants in each sort undetermined. The breadth of the latter is
real, but its distribution has different consequences for the individual.
                         [587] ∀x∈D P(x)         and      ∀K∈K ∃x∈K P(x). (49)
    [588] Terms. D denotes the domain of individuals, K the collection of relevant sorts, and P the
predicate. The second expression presupposes that each relevant sort has members and asserts a
participant from each. These conditions can hold without every individual in D satisfying P.
    [589] Consequence. A passage that removes an ethnic or social restriction gives the saving relation a
breadth which the interpretation must preserve. Inclusion of every individual would be a further
determination of that relation. It cannot be derived from the first expression of breadth and must be
established through the terms of the particular passage.
     [590] The same use recurs throughout Scripture, and the instances already established are sufficient to
defeat the claim that the word itself carries an unrestricted universality of individuals. They do not
determine beforehand the meaning of every place in which the word occurs, for the truth asserted there
still receives its extent through the relations which constitute the particular proposition. The passages

urged for universal redemption will therefore be examined in their own terms. Three observations
conclude the account of these general expressions by making plain the grounds on which their application
in those passages depends.
    [591] First, all can be restricted to the whole of a determinate class even when the qualification which
fixes that class is unexpressed. Thus the statement “The free gift came upon all men to justification of
life:” concerns all believers. The “all men,” who are actually justified are exactly those who are Christ’s,
since justification is received by faith and no one is justified apart from it. What the passage attributes to
the persons concerned therefore supplies the qualification that is absent from the bare expression. The
absence of a modifying word does nothing to establish that the class is unrestricted when the benefit
predicated of its members determines who they are. (1 Corinthians 15:22; Ephesians 4:6; Romans 5:18)
    [592] Second, all can signify some of every sort, so that the breadth of the proposition lies in the
classes it represents. Paul renders ‫ כּוָּל ם‬by πάντες, and the express mention of “kings,” singles out a sort
of person whom the word comprehends. Distribution of this kind requires participation from the sorts
designated. It does not assign the predicate to each of the individuals who belong to them. The particular
proofs will establish that all, wherever it occurs in the matter of redemption, must have either this
distributive sense or the restriction to a determinate class just described. (Jeremiah 31:34; Hebrews 8:11;
John 12:32; 1 Timothy 2:1–3)
    [593] Third, the general expressions of the New Testament receive their extent through their
correspondence with the predictions of the Old, since the Lord declares in the Gospel the accomplishment
of the very works he had foretold. The prophets say that all nations, all flesh, all people, all the ends,
families, or kindreds of the earth, the world, the whole earth, and the isles will be converted, look to
Christ, and come to the mountain of the Lord. The truth of these promises consists in their
accomplishment in the elect of God throughout the nations. The extent of the nations named is real, and
the persons in whom the prediction is fulfilled are those whom God brings to Christ within them. The
same expressions in the Gospel often declare that fulfillment directly. To press them beyond the relation
given in the promise, until they comprehend each individual of whom no such event was promised, is to
make the account of the fulfillment contradict the mind of the Holy Ghost in the prediction. The Lord’s
wiping tears from all faces is compatible with the reprobate being cast out forever where there is weeping
and wailing. In the same manner Christ’s death for all is compatible with the eternal perishing of the
reprobate for their sins, since the remedy can be proposed on occasion to some of them without being
intended effectually for them. The places from which the participants are drawn and the persons in whom
the promise takes effect are distinct determinations of its extent.
    [594] 6. Scripture often speaks according to the appearance under which persons and things enter
public judgment, or according to the standing accorded them by those to whom it speaks. Men are called
wise, just, and righteous because they are so esteemed among men, though the Lord knows them to be
foolish sinners. Jerusalem is called “The holy city” according to its acknowledged appearance, even when
in fact it was a “den of thieves.” Of Ahaz, the wicked king of Judah, it is said that “he sacrificed to the
gods of Damascus that smote him.” The Lord alone smote him. The gods to whom he sacrificed were
stocks and stones made by human hands, unable to help themselves or strike their enemies. Yet in giving
the account of his idolatry the Holy Ghost speaks in accordance with the persuasion by which Ahaz acted
and says, “They smote him.” So also Christ is said to have broken the Sabbath, though the transgression
consisted wholly in the corrupt opinion of the blinded Pharisees. A public description can be warranted by
the standing under which the person appears to those who judge or can state what their judgment reckons
to be the case. The relation in which the description is given must therefore be determined before it is
treated as a disclosure of what the person inwardly is or of what actually occurred. (Matthew 27:53; 2
Chronicles 28:23; John 5:18)
    [595] Those who share the outward communion of the children of God receive real privileges through
their membership in it, and Scripture frequently describes them under predicates proper to the children of

God, though they remain strangers to participation in the grace of the promise. This fact and the ordinary
use of descriptions according to appearance answer the passages which seem to allow the eternal
destruction of persons said to be redeemed by Christ’s blood. A person can be called redeemed κατὰ τὴν
δόξαν, not κατὰ τὴν ἀλήθειαν, κατὰ τὸ φαίνεσθαι, not κατὰ τὸ εἷναι, according to appearance rather than
reality, as Scripture speaks in the other instances already established. The benefits of outward
participation are genuine in their own order. They do not give everyone who shares them the inward
saving condition proper to the children of God. Accordingly an account of what befalls a member of that
communion supplies no example against the effectual redemption of God’s elect unless the person is
shown to have participated in the saving relation itself.
    [596] 7. A judgment of charity is right when it proceeds according to the rule given to the person who
judges. Its warrant is established by the evidence accessible to that person, and does not extend to facts
that have been withheld from human cognizance. The apostles frequently address those to whom they
write as “holy,” “saints,” and even “elected;” but their terms give no warrant to conclude that each of the
persons so addressed was inwardly what the description affirms. Peter calls the strangers dispersed
through Pontus, Galatia, Cappadocia, Asia, and Bithynia “elect according to the foreknowledge of God
the Father,” and no one will affirm that we are therefore warranted to hold de fide that all of them were
elect. Paul says to the Thessalonians, the whole church addressed in his epistle, that he “knew their
election of God” and blesses God “who had chosen them to salvation.” The rule by which such a
judgment proceeds is expressed when he says, “It is meet for me to think so of you all”. The judgment is
proper according to that rule, while the conclusion that each Thessalonian was infallibly elect is
unwarranted. If some who received the address afterward abandoned the Gospel and perished, their
apostasy would not establish that the elect may perish. It would show that the description accorded to
them in the judgment of charity did not disclose their actual condition. The very same answer disposes of
the argument from the perishing of some who were called redeemed according to the judgment of charity.
If an apostolic description of election does not transform the outward profession on which it proceeds into
certain evidence of eternal election, a description of redemption given on the same terms cannot establish
actual redemption. The conduct warranted in the observer and the concealed condition of the person
observed are distinct objects of judgment. (1 Peter 1:1–2; 1 Thessalonians 1:4; 2 Thessalonians 2:13;
Philippians 1:7)
    [597] Under incomplete observation the evidence leaves a collection of inward conditions compatible
with what the observer knows. Further evidence can reduce that collection without reducing it to one
member. Charity determines how the observer is to judge and act within the limits of the evidence, while
the actual condition of the person judged remains determinate independently of that judgment.
                  [598] Cm(e)={X:Om(X)agrees withe},           e+⇒Cm(e+)⊆Cm(e). (50)
    [599] Terms. C denotes the states compatible with the minister’s evidence e, and e plus denotes
additional information compatible with that evidence. The inclusion follows from refinement by this
addition. If evidence is retracted or shown false, the collection must be determined again.
     [600] Consequence. A profession can supply the minister with sufficient grounds for his conduct,
while subsequent events disclose that the stronger saving predicate was never established. The actual state
of the person has no dependence on the classification warranted in the observer. Charitable apostolic
address therefore furnishes no exhaustive knowledge of the eternal condition of its several recipients.
    [601] 8. God has established in his counsel and declared in his Word an inseparable connection
between faith and salvation, by which “he that believes shall be saved”. This connection is the substance
of the Gospel in its outward proclamation. God testifies that eternal life is in his Son. The person who
believes receives the testimony and thereby sets his seal to God’s truthfulness, while the unbeliever, so far
as his refusal can do it, makes God a liar. To innumerable hearers the proclamation conveys this
appointment of the means and the end, and its commands, offers, and promises disclose the certain
salvation of believers without disclosing God’s intention concerning each person addressed. The promise

is a divine truth sufficient for supernatural faith to rest upon, and refusal of it is itself sufficient ground of
condemnation. Christ makes the consequence explicit when he says, “If you believe not that I am he”
(that is, “the way, the truth, and the life”), “you shall die in your sins.” The connection is universally true
of those who meet its condition. The certainty of what follows upon believing is one determination of
God’s will, and the actual production of faith in particular persons has its own explanation. The first is
proclaimed to the hearer as a ground on which he is bound to rest, whether or not the second is disclosed
to him. (Mark 16:16; 1 John 5:9–11; John 8:24)
    [602] The command and promise of believing can be addressed to a man who will certainly perish,
because the condition belongs to the relation between the things proposed. God’s will establishing that
relation is absolute. The imagination that he conditionally intends the salvation of each hearer confuses
the contingency of the event in the hearer with a condition imposed upon God’s will. It is the old
Arminian sophism, offered in broken pieces and acclaimed as a discovery of grace by men who take the
novelty of a thing to themselves for evidence of its novelty to the world. Their eagerness to stand on their
own legs while they can hardly crawl would better be spent taking hold of those with strength enough to
direct them. Their argument is “As is God’s proffer, so is his intention; but he calls to all to believe and be
saved: therefore he intends it to all.” The inference takes a command given to the hearer and treats it as
though God had communicated his private purpose concerning the salvation of that hearer. The duty
disclosed by the command concerns an object of faith given publicly, whose truth supplies sufficient
grounds for the duty. The conclusion concerning the intention of God requires a different proposition, and
the argument provides none.
    [603] First, a great part of mankind has passed without any offer of life upon condition of faith, since
the proclamation in which that offer consists was never made to them. The actual distribution of the
Gospel is therefore already fatal to the asserted universality. A proposition about each person who hears
does not thereby comprehend every human being.
    [604] Second, if the offer means God’s command and promise, the form of these declarations
furnishes no ground for identifying them with his intention concerning what each recipient will do. God
commanded Pharaoh to let his people go, having foretold that he would so order things that Pharaoh
would refuse. The command was a declaration of what Pharaoh was bound to do, while the foretelling
disclosed what God would do in the ordering of the event. Promises, when particularly applied, do reveal
God’s mind to the persons to whom they are applied. In their indefinite proposal they declare the
determination already established, namely that faith and salvation are infallibly joined. A hearer therefore
receives both a true command concerning his duty and a true promise concerning the benefit that follows
upon believing. Neither, merely by being proposed, names each person in whom believing will actually
be produced or turns the appointed connection of the things into a revealed intention concerning all the
persons addressed.
    [605] Third, if God intends the salvation of every person to whom salvation on condition of faith is or
may be offered, election and reprobation cannot stand, since neither is consistent with this universal
intention to save. And since salvation does not follow in every case, the supposed intention must fail.
“Dum vitant stulti vitia, in contraria currunt.” The Scylla thus reached is more certain and more
destructive than the Charybdis feared. Our opponents answer, “He intends it only upon condition; and the
condition being not fulfilled, he fails not in his purpose, though the thing be not conferred.” That answer
requires a further account of God’s foreknowledge. If he did not know whether those to whom the
condition was proposed would fulfill it, omniscience has been surrendered. If he knew that they would
never fulfill it, their salvation cannot be the event he intended, the more so since he knew that fulfillment
depended upon his own gift and had determined not to give it. They ascribe to the only wise God a
purpose they would not knowingly attribute to a wise man. A father who promises his daughter in
marriage to a man on condition that he bring a certain jewel cannot be said to intend the marriage when he
knows the man has no such jewel, knows that the man can receive it only from him, and has resolved
never to give it. The determination touching the necessary means is part of the account of what is

intended, and a man must mistake blindness for light before he can regard that determination as
immaterial. What the promulgation of the Gospel chiefly declares, in respect of unbelievers, is the strict
connection between the commanded duty of faith and the promised benefit of life. Its truth extends to all
who shall believe, and is grounded in the plenary sufficiency of the death of Christ for them. The Gospel
can therefore be preached to all without the death of Christ being intended for all. M S, page 202, calls the
contrary proposition the lowest part of the mystery of the Universalists. This and the higher part already
mentioned are the same old, carnal sophism long since confuted, arising from ignorance of Scripture and
right reason, which agree in the matter. A true promise gives the benefit to everyone who fulfills its
condition. To convert that relation into an intention that every hearer shall actually receive the benefit, the
argument must supply a ground connecting the proposal with the intended event. It furnishes no such
ground, and its conditional answer leaves the prior determination about the means exactly where it was.
     [606] 9. God has distributed the elect and reprobate, believers and unbelievers, among the several
places of the world in such a way that they stand together in all or most particular congregations. This
mixture supplies another ground for the proposal of Christ’s blood to persons for whom it was never shed,
as the ineffectual event of the proposal in them makes plain. The ministers to whom the word of
reconciliation is committed are stewards of the mysteries of Christ. They know what God has revealed,
while his purposes toward particular persons remain in his own counsel and are no object of their inquiry.
Their commission obliges them to admonish and warn everyone to whom they are sent. They give the
same commands, propose the same promises, and offer Christ in the same manner to all, so that the elect,
who are known to them only by the event, may obtain and the rest be hardened. Two determinations of
Him who disposes all things govern this manner of proclamation. The wheat and the tares remain mingled
to the end of the world, and Christ and reconciliation through him are preached by men who do not know
God’s eternal purposes of distinction. It follows from the first and the second together that the promises
must have a generality suited to the mixed congregation and must reach persons for whom God never
intended the good things promised. Such persons have a share in the proposal because they live among
the elect. Its extent consequently has a sufficient ground in the constitution of the public ministry and the
assembly to which that ministry is sent, and gives no ground to infer that Christ died for everyone to
whom he is proposed. The word of reconciliation is preached to persons of diverse conditions in the
purpose of God, by messengers who have no access to that distinction in advance. God brings his own
home by exhortations, entreaties, promises, and like means suited to the rational nature common to those
who hear. Those same means serve other ends among the rest, whom they convict, restrain, harden, and
leave without excuse. The conditional offer must therefore reach some who have no right to the promised
benefit in the intention for which God appointed it. The offer has never been absolutely universal, since
its indefinite form disregards outward distinctions without extending the preaching to every individual.
Christ is received only through faith, and God bestows faith on whom he pleases. Where God determines
not to bestow faith, he does not intend the person to receive Christ. Thus the generality proper to the
public promise follows from the office of the speaker and the constitution of the congregation. What is
disclosed in the hearing belongs to the minister’s charge, and the final condition of those addressed is
known to him only as it becomes manifest in the event.
    [607] 10. The faith commanded in the Gospel has several acts and degrees, because its exercise
follows the order in which its several objects are made known. Our adversaries make the exhortation to
faith depend on a proposition the Gospel nowhere puts at its entrance. If Christ did not die for every
person, they say, innumerable persons are exhorted in vain since there is no proper object for their faith.
For this objection to have force, the Gospel would have to begin by declaring that Christ died for
everyone, elect and reprobate, or by requiring each person who lives under the means of grace to believe
first that Christ died for him in particular. Both propositions are notoriously false, as the close of the
undertaking will make apparent. The order of the acts of faith, already indicated, is sufficient to exhibit
why the exhortation is warranted in every case. Every person whom the Gospel commands or invites to
believe has an object sufficiently true to ground the particular act required, and sufficiently ample for the
full exercise of that act. A later act of reliance has intelligible content because the object on which it rests

has first been made known. The efficient production of faith, in its turn, must be understood as the
production of acts directed toward these determinate objects in their appointed order. To require
assurance of the particular application before the person has apprehended the object offered would
dissolve the dependence through which that assurance becomes intelligible.
    [608] First, the Gospel commands and persuades sinners to believe that they cannot find salvation in
themselves, since all have sinned and come short of the glory of God, and cannot obtain it by works of the
law, since no living flesh can thereby be justified. The apostle dwells upon this saving truth to prepare the
way for the exposition of justification by Christ. The apprehension of guilt and inability concerns what
the hearer actually is under the law, and supplies a determinate need to which the subsequent
proclamation of deliverance answers. Innumerable hearers never come so far as to believe this much, and
almost the whole nation of the Jews can be counted among them. Their refusal requires no supposition
about a further proposition which they have not reached. Contempt of the first object already proposed to
their faith is itself the sin of unbelief. (Romans 1; 2; 3; 9; 10:3–4)
     [609] Second, the Gospel requires belief that salvation is available in the promised seed, whom God
ordained beforehand as captain of salvation to those who believe. This apprehension concerns the remedy
in its relation to the need already established, since acknowledgment of guilt does not itself communicate
that God has provided a way of saving sinners. Here again millions of those outwardly called fall away
from the order of belief. They never receive with true divine faith the proposition that such a way has
been provided, even though it furnishes an adequate object of faith to every person to whom it is
proposed.
    [610] Third, Jesus of Nazareth, crucified by the Jews, is the Savior promised beforehand, and no other
name under heaven is given by which men may be saved. This identification is the point at which the
Jews refused the message, rejecting Christ as the Savior of men and proceeding against him as an enemy
of God. The unbelief for which they are repeatedly condemned concerned whether he was the Messiah
promised to them. The question whether he died for them all was not the question between them and
Christ. They denied that he was the promised one and perished in their unbelief. The proposition gives
reliance its particular object, since the salvation promised is received through this person and trust has no
saving content when severed from him.
   [611] The three preceding acts furnish the grounds on which each subsequent act depends. To exhort a
soul to the highest exercise while it has yet to receive these first truths is to require an inference from
propositions it does not hold. The later act lacks the object and grounds by which it is an exercise of faith.
    [612] Fourth, the Gospel requires the sinner to rest upon the Christ already apprehended as the
promised Redeemer. Redemption is plenteous in him, who is able to save to the utmost those who come
to God by him and to bear the burden of every weary and laboring soul that comes to him by faith. This
proposition is infallibly true, since the oblation in itself has a superabundant sufficiency however many or
