few the persons for whom it is intended. Resting on Christ in this manner involves much knowledge of
oneself and deep conviction of sin, of the justice of God, and of his free grace. Thousands of souls within
the outward bounds of the church never come to this act. Without the aid of God’s Spirit none of the three
preceding acts can be performed, much less this one. The Spirit works freely when, how, and in whom he
pleases. Resting is accordingly the genuine act of the renewed person and the instrument by which he
receives Christ. Its efficient cause is the operation of the Spirit producing the disposition from which that
act proceeds.
    [613] Fifth, when the preceding truths are firmly received in the soul, the person is called to believe in
particular the efficacy of redemption in the blood of Jesus for his own soul. Every person in whom free
grace has wrought the earlier acts and works this act also may do so with assurance, for the object of this
belief is true. Christ died for everyone in whose heart the Lord by almighty power effectually works faith
to apprehend Christ and assent to him in the order in which the Gospel makes him known. The assurance
follows through the relation between the promise and the faith which the person actually exercises. It does

not make recognition of that relation the cause of the saving provision which makes it true. The ancient
summary of Christian religion commonly called the apostles’ creed observes the same order, as has been
remarked, since it places the remission of our sins and eternal life last. The other articles must first be
firmly rooted before the believer comes to these in their particular application. The charge that faith has
no object unless Christ died for everyone is therefore a senseless vanity. Every proposition to which any
hearer is called to assent has an absolute truth in the order of the Gospel. What can be known about a
person’s own redemption through the acts God works in him need not have been known when the
propositions which make those acts intelligible were first proposed.
     [614] Hearing can enter a course in which the hearer receives instruction, answers it, encounters
correction, and afterward understands and acts differently. The earlier answer returns as a condition of the
instruction subsequently given. The course is therefore intelligible through the real acts of the recipient
and his teacher, while its saving character depends upon the efficient grace and the received object of
faith already distinguished.
                    [615] mt+1=Q(mt,ct,rt),        ct+1=P(ct,rt),     rt=G(Wt,mt). (51)
     [616] Terms. m denotes the content apprehended by the recipient, c the instruction proposed, r the
answer disclosed in confession or conduct, Q the change in understanding, and P the subsequent
instruction given in response. W retains its meaning as the disposition of the recipient, whose renewal has
a distinct efficient account.
    [617] Consequence. The answer tells the minister what the hearer understood and so enters into the
determination of what he teaches next. The hearer then receives further content and correction. This
recurrence actually operates within the appointed means, explaining how the recipient’s acts enter the
course in which the saving object is understood and trusted. Its occurrence alone does not establish
regeneration.
    [618] The grounds of the answers have now been established in the nature of the operations and
relations themselves. Their application to the ensuing objections requires in each case the determination
of what the objection assumes, whether a domain of persons, a saving operation, a promised condition, or
a relation between the evidence and the judgment it warrants. The particular replies will make that
application plain.
    [619] The doctrine established from the Word of God is commonly assailed with more noise than
argument. Its objections can be judged when they are stated in the propositions they affirm and the
conclusions they claim to derive, for their consequences can then be compared with the relations already
established. Three considerations govern the examination and explain why the objections are reproduced
and what weight belongs to them.
    [620] First, there is no good in giving currency to an objection merely because it appears to deface a
truth of God. Owen would have preferred the objections to remain buried, and his reluctance concerned
the injury done by circulating error rather than any difficulty in answering it. Yet many readers have
already encountered them, or can expect to do so, since men lying in wait to deceive use the liberty of the
times to propagate their errors. When this is the condition in which the truth must be defended,
concealment would leave the poison in circulation without its antidote. The asserted content of the
objection is therefore given openly. Its repetition serves the reader who has already heard it or will hear it
from men who employ it for their own ends, because the answer can then be directed to the consequences
the objection actually claims. A reply which changed the subject would leave the injury untouched.
   [621] Second, the sound of words cannot carry a consequence that the proposition itself does not
contain. A hearer should withhold assent to an objection until the passages have been weighed, the
arguments considered, and the answers given, remembering the many demonstrations and passages of
Scripture by which the truth under attack has been established. The impression made by a general term is
no measure of its operation within the proposition which employs it, nor can the force with which a claim

is delivered supply the missing relation between its premise and conclusion. Once these have been
judged, may the Lord direct you to “prove all things, and hold fast that which is good.”
    [622] Third, the weight of an objection lies in what it establishes concerning the point in dispute. A
flourish has no weight merely because it enlarges the sound of the argument, and even a true observation
leaves the conclusion intact when it touches none of the premises on which it depends. The controversy is
judged by the bearing of the proposition asserted.
    [623] The objections proceed either from Scripture perverted or from reason abused. These are
distinct failures. The interpretation of a passage must establish what it asserts before a further conclusion
can be tested as a consequence of that assertion.
    [624] The strongest scriptural case urged by our adversaries comprises three arguments,
corresponding to the passages on which they rely. The first rests on passages which affirm that Christ died
for the world or otherwise use world in the matter of redemption. The second rests on passages which
speak of all and every man, whether in relation to Christ’s death or to God’s will that they should be
saved. The third rests on passages said to affirm that Christ bought or died for persons who perish. These
are the three principal sophisms, and they derive their apparent strength from claims about different
relations. The first two argue from the extent of a scriptural expression, while the third turns upon the
condition of persons to whom a saving act is attributed. By the Lord’s help each will be examined in its
order and with the texts brought to support it, so that the distinctions already established can be applied to
the actual demand of the particular argument. (The Remonstrants, Synodical Writings)
    [625] I. The first argument turns on “world,” and is proposed in its strongest form by men of whom
our poor pretenders are scarcely more than children. The extent of the term and the saving act predicated
of the persons it denotes must both be established. The word by itself supplies neither determination.
    [626] The argument opens with “He that is given out of the love wherewith God loved the world; that
gave himself for the life of the world; and was a propitiation for the sins of the whole world” and
concludes, “he was given and died for every man in the world; — but the first is true of Christ, as appears
by the places before alleged: therefore he died for all and every one.” Its authors boast that no answer has
even the appearance of force. The inference joins love, self-giving, life, and propitiation as though their
subjects and saving import had already been determined. A universal conclusion would require the same
persons to be comprehended at each point and the same saving sense to hold across the premises which
are supposed to connect them. (Arminius, 530–531, as cited by Owen; Corvinus, Ad Molin. 29, 442;
Remonstrants, Acta Synodi, 300; John 3:16; 6:51; 1:29; 4:42; 1 John 2:2; 2 Corinthians 5:19)
    [627] We deny the consequence of the first proposition and will, with the Lord’s help, submit the
denial to trial at any time. The question is how all and every man follows from world in the sense borne
by the passages alleged. Our opponents offer two grounds, one drawn from reasoning about the word
itself and one from their interpretation of the particular passages. Both can be examined. To deny the
proposed consequence leaves the words of Scripture in full force and requires only that the relation
claimed as a consequence be demonstrated. Boasting adds nothing to the force of the argument while the
connection between its terms is still the very thing in dispute.
   [628] First, the argument from the word itself can be stated as a syllogism. Its form makes plain which
premise is required, so that the claim made for it can be judged independently of the force with which the
universal conclusion is announced.
    [629] The whole world contains every man in the world. Christ died for the whole world. The
proposed conclusion is that he died for every man. The inference requires the world named in the minor
to have the same meaning as the world that contains every man in the major.
   [630] The syllogism has four terms, since “world,” which supplies its apparent middle, changes its
sense between the premises. In the first it denotes the world which contains the men, while in the second

it denotes what is contained, the men who inhabit it. There is consequently no common middle through
which the population named in the major can enter the saving predicate of the minor. To maintain a single
middle on those terms would require the assumption that Christ died for the containing world, the fabric
of the habitable earth, with the conclusion that he died for the thing which contains all men. The
supposition is a frenzy, and the syllogism as actually advanced is sophistically false. If the inference has
any ground in “world,” it must be furnished by the signification of the word in Scripture. The thing that
contains the men can furnish no account of which of them receive the benefit attributed to Christ in the
second premise.
    [631] The revised major affirms that the word “world” signifies all and every man in Scripture. The
minor affirms that Christ is said to die for the world. The conclusion is that he died for every man. The
force of the major depends on whether it concerns every scriptural occurrence or only some.
    [632] If the major purports to give the sense of world in every place where Scripture uses it, the
contrary instances already established prove it false. One contrary use is sufficient against that
proposition. If the major concerns only some occurrences, the conclusion must depend on the further fact
that the passage now in question belongs to them. Stated in its particular form, the premise leaves that fact
open.
    [633] In some places of Scripture “world” signifies all and every man in the world, of every age,
time, and condition. Christ is said to die for the world. The proposed conclusion is again that he died for
every man. A use found in some places can furnish that conclusion only if it is also established for the
place now adduced.
    [634] The new syllogism is as defective as the first, for it draws a universal conclusion from a
particular proposition. Once the major is properly restricted, the scope of the minor also comes into
question. Does every passage which mentions Christ’s death say that he died for the world, or do only
some say it? The first proposition is plainly false in view of the many passages already produced which
restrict his death to his elect, his sheep, and his church. The passages alleged for the contrary are few by
comparison. If the minor also concerns only some places, its particularity must be expressed in the
argument. The defect in scope remains even if all the examples cited for the major bear the sense claimed
for them, since their correctness still tells us nothing about a use occurring elsewhere.
    [635] One occurrence can bear the unrestricted sense while another does not. That assignment is
sufficient to make the premise true, since there is a use of the kind claimed, and to make the universal
conclusion false, since that use is not found in every occurrence. The intended inference therefore
depends upon a further premise identifying the present passage as an occurrence with the unrestricted
sense.
                        [636] ∃c U(c) ⇏ ∀c U(c),    U(c1)=true, U(c2)=false. (52)
    [637] Terms. c denotes an occurrence in its context, and U affirms that the occurrence has the
proposed unrestricted domain. The final assignment satisfies the existential premise and falsifies the
claimed universal conclusion, exhibiting their logical independence.
    [638] Consequence. The demonstration establishes the insufficiency of the inference. The sense of
either particular occurrence must still be determined by its passage, since nothing in the failure of the
universal argument supplies the theology of the text by itself. The opponent has failed to prove the
universal reading, and the affirmative exposition proceeds on its own scriptural grounds.
   [639] In some few passages the word “world” signifies every man in the world. In some few passages
Christ is said to die for the world, in equivalent terms though not in express words. The conclusion is
again that he died for every man. The two claims can both be true without concerning any of the same
passages.

    [640] The argument is manifestly sophistical. It infers a universal from particular affirmatives and
employs four terms unless the places named in the major can be shown to be the very places named in the
minor. Their identity is the point to be proved. No additional force can be extracted from the word itself,
since the number of occurrences of either sort gives no ground for placing any occurrence in both. What
would give the argument force is the identification of a passage in which Christ’s death is predicated of
the world and the world has the unrestricted meaning. Any argument that places weight on the word must
therefore affirm the overlap directly rather than leave it to be inferred from the two particular premises.
    [641] If “world” signifies every man who ever lived or shall live in the places where Christ is said to
die for the world, then he died for every man. The minor asserts that “world,” in all those places, does
signify every man in the world. The conclusion follows if the minor is granted, but the major has merely
lodged the whole dispute in its antecedent. The unrestricted sense must be established in the very texts
where the death of Christ is at issue.
    [642] First, only one place says that Christ gave his life for the world or died for it, and this place
concerns his intention. The other places appear to declare the sufficiency of the oblation for all, which we
also maintain. Second, we absolutely deny the minor and appeal to the passages themselves. Their
agreement in using world cannot decide their sense in advance of examination, since their subjects,
contexts, and the operations they ascribe to Christ must determine what is meant in each.
    [643] Brought to measure, the argument derives its apparent strength from the very equivocation in
which its weakness consists. Men take hold of the word world and run away with the bait as though
universal redemption had been proved. Asked to state the ground of their inference, they repeat world and
the whole world without determining what either expression signifies in the propositions they assert. Quid
dignum tanto? The boast with which the argument began is out of all proportion to what rational men
have brought forward in so weighty a cause. The formal examination has nevertheless done something
necessary. It has isolated the premise upon which the entire inference depends, and prevented that
premise from passing under the cover of a repeated word as though it had already been granted. The
scriptural proof can now address it directly. The several passages alleged will be considered in order, and
each will be judged by what it attributes to Christ and by the persons whom its own terms bring within the
operation it declares.
     [644] 1. The first passage alleged is also the one on which our opponents lay the greatest weight. Yet
it is as ready to overthrow their opinion as Goliath’s sword was to take off his own head, for the words
furnish several unanswerable arguments against universal redemption. May the great and peaceable King

of his church guide the vindication of the truth here, both in the exposition of the words and in the
judgment of the consequences drawn from them. The passage reads, “God so loved the world, that he
gave his only-begotten Son, that whosoever believes in him should not perish but have everlasting life.”
The love of God is declared through the giving of the Son, and the giving is ordered to the life of those
who believe. These are determinate relations within the passage. The claim that every individual is
included must be sustained in the account of that love, the act through which it operates, and the salvation
to which the act is directed. Their order supplies the saving content against which the rival interpretations
must be judged. (John 3:16)
    [645] The Universalists boast of this text as though its relations secured their whole position. With the
Lord’s assistance it will be shown that those very relations overthrow it. The difference becomes plain
when both meanings are put into connected form, so that the nature of the love, the persons whom it
concerns, and the means by which its fruit is attained can each be judged. The first paraphrase gives their
interpretation. “ ‘God so loved,’ had such a natural inclination, velleity, and propensity to the good of ‘the
world,’ Adam, with all and every one of his posterity, of all ages, times, and conditions (whereof some
were in heaven, some in hell long before), ‘that he gave his only-begotten Son,’ causing him to be
incarnate in the fullness of time, to die, not with a purpose and resolution to save any, but ‘that
whosoever,’ what persons soever of those which he had propensity unto, ‘believes in him should not

perish, but have everlasting life,’ should have this fruit and issue, that he should escape death and hell,
and live eternally.” The several determinations of this account must be considered in turn, since an
agreement about the words leaves the dependence of salvation upon the asserted love undecided.
    [646] Five determinations make up their account. First, the love which causes the sending or giving of
Christ is a natural propensity toward the good of all. Second, the persons loved are every man of every
generation. Third, the giving is left indeterminate between the appointment of Christ to recover sinners
and his actual exhibition in the flesh to accomplish his ministry. Fourth, whosoever is made a distribution
over the persons included in the world, so that God’s intention is restricted to no part of them. Fifth,
everlasting life is a fruit obtained by believers, but is no end intended by God in the giving. The first
determination governs what may follow from the others. A natural inclination toward a possible good has
a different relation to the actual salvation of the persons concerned than a free act of will directed to
securing that good.
    [647] The object of the passage is to magnify God’s free love to lost sinners in sending Christ to
procure eternal redemption for them. The meaning must therefore be set out through the dependence of
the gift on the love from which it proceeds, the place of faith in the reception of that gift, and the end to
which the giving is directed. The second paraphrase expresses the relation as a whole.
     [648] “ ‘God’ the Father ‘so loved,’ had such a peculiar, transcendent love, being an unchangeable
purpose and act of his will concerning their salvation, towards ‘the world,’ miserable, sinful, lost men of
all sorts, not only Jews but Gentiles also, which he peculiarly loved, ‘that,’ intending their salvation, as in
the last words, for the praise of his glorious grace, ‘he gave,’ he prepared a way to prevent their
everlasting destruction, by appointing and sending ‘his only-begotten Son’ to be an all-sufficient Savior to
all that look up unto him, ‘that whosoever believes in him,’ all believers whatsoever, and only they,
‘should not perish, but have everlasting life,’ and so effectually be brought to the obtaining of those
glorious things through him which the Lord in his free love had designed for them.” The giving proceeds
from God, whose antecedent provision supplies the ground upon which the recipient subsequently
believes.
    [649] The expansion just given expresses the mind of the Holy Ghost by keeping together the persons
and effects which the text connects. Its several determinations can be distinguished without separating
them, since the saving purpose is expressed through the agreement of the love, the giving, and the end
attained in those who believe.
    [650] First, the “love” of God is the act of his will from which the sending of his Son Jesus Christ
proceeds. Love is velle alicui bonum, “to will good to any.” God has never willed a greater good to the
creature than when he appointed his Son for its redemption, and the love here declared is therefore the
most eminent act of favor toward it. The order in which we apprehend the love and the sending does not
establish that the purpose of giving Christ is absolutely subordinate to the love of the elect as its ultimate
end. Both are coordinate with respect to their supreme end, the manifestation of God’s glory by mercy
tempered with justice. As apprehended by us, the free willing of the good stands as the ground of the gift
by which that good is attained. This is the love than which there is none greater. The condition expressed
in the passage belongs within the saving order governed by that love. Its presence alone cannot decide
whether the purpose which appoints the end also provides the fulfillment of the condition.
     [651] Second, by “world,” the text comprehends the elect of God alone, under a description which
magnifies the love shown to them. They are miserable and lost creatures, in and of the world, scattered in
all its places. Their dispersion reaches beyond Jews and Greeks and is confined to no nation, kindred, or
language under heaven. What the term brings before us is their misery and dispersion, which are true of
the elect and exhibit the greatness of the favor shown them. The gift must suffice for the end assigned to
it, in accordance with the relation between purpose and appointed means established in paragraphs 14–32.

    [652] Third, Ἵνα πᾶς ὁ πιστεύων, “that every believer,” declares God’s intention in giving or sending
the Son by designating the persons whose good is intended. It supplies no distribution of the world loved.
The love is an unchangeable intention of the highest good, and each proposed enlargement of its domain
brings with it the saving predicates attributed to the persons in that domain. Those consequences must be
owned wherever the predicate is retained.
    [653] Fourth, “Should not perish, but have life everlasting,” expresses the particular end for which
God gave Christ, the certain salvation of believers through him. This interpretation supplies several
arguments, each sufficient to overturn the general ransom, because the clauses establish a relation whose
parts cannot be retained in their grammatical form after the saving dependence between them has been
removed. The force of the arguments becomes clearest when the words under dispute are set alongside the
rival determinations given to them. The reasons for rejecting one sense and affirming the other concern
the coherence of what the passage asserts throughout. An interpretation which names the same love and
the same gift while depriving them of the end expressly joined to them has changed the proposition on
which its conclusion must rest.
   [654] The first difference concerns the cause of the sending, which the passage calls love. The second
concerns its object, which it calls the world. The third concerns the intention of God in giving the Son,
expressed as the salvation of believers. The act meant by love must be determined first, since its nature
governs what can be asserted of those to whom it extends.
    [655] First, by “love” our adversaries agree in understanding a natural affection in God for the good of
the creature lost in sin, a general propensity moving him to provide some way in which the loss might
possibly be remedied. We understand the act of his will by which he eternally purposes to do good to
man. The love belongs to the will, and in giving the Son it is exercised in its most transcendent and
eminent act toward the creature. The dispute concerns the dependence of the giving and the salvation
upon that act. Agreement in naming love does nothing to settle whether the good is merely made possible
or is the intended fruit of the gift.
    [656] The two interpretations are judged by their consequences for the giving of the Son and the
salvation of those for whom he is given. The grounds against the account of natural propensity come first,
followed by the positive grounds for the act of free purpose. The question is whether this love appoints
and secures its saving fruit in the persons whom it concerns.
    [657] First, the word cannot signify a natural affection that necessarily carries God toward something
outside himself, unless such an affection can be affirmed of God at all. The interpretation requires the
conjunction of a necessary inclination with the completeness of the divine being and operation. But an
inclination toward an effect that never comes to pass would bring into the divine life a want which its own
action leaves unfulfilled. That such an affection neither is nor can be in God is evident from several
demonstrations, of which the following are sufficient for the question at hand.
    [658] First, no imperfection can be assigned to Almighty God, who is all-sufficient, whose work is
perfect, and who is himself our rock. A natural affection in him toward the good and salvation of all, if it
never attains its end, introduces both weakness in the operation and a defect in the blessedness of its
subject. So far as a thing lacks the fulfillment of that toward which its desire carries it, whether the desire
is natural or voluntary, it lacks the blessedness which consists in that fulfillment. The infinite blessedness
of the ever-blessed God consequently excludes a natural affection directed toward an effect which will
never be accomplished. Such is the affection our opponents affirm under the name of a general love to all.
Its decisive cause remains unprovided, and the explanation thus assigns to God an inclination which his
own power never brings to its proper completion. The all-sufficiency of God governs what can be
ascribed both to his being and to his saving operation.
    [659] Second, if God is naturally moved to love all so far as to send his Son to die for them, why does
the affection remain without its effect? The power to accomplish it belongs to the very subject in whom

the desire is placed. Our opponents say, “It does not seem good to his infinite wisdom,” “so to do.” Their
answer therefore assigns to God an affection toward an end which his wisdom cannot prosecute. Even
among men, who are worms of the earth, an affection thus divided from wisdom would be called brutish.
A universal saving love which secures what it intends would issue in the salvation of every person it
comprehends. The exceptions are fatal to the proposed universality unless the operation itself is changed,
and the change their answer introduces puts the alleged affection in opposition to the wisdom of God.
     [660] Third, Scripture assigns no natural propensity of this kind to God, and what it does assign
excludes it. The passage which teaches that he is naturally inclined toward the good of the creature has
yet to be produced. In contrast, Scripture declares the freedom with which he exercises mercy, saying that
“he has mercy on whom he will have mercy.” Every act of mercy proceeds from the free distinguishing
will of God and is bestowed as he pleases. If he were instead moved to mercy by the misery of the
creature operating upon a natural affection, the same affection would move him toward devils and men
alike, and toward the damned as much as toward the saved. A natural affection would operate equally in
all the cases that come under it, and what is natural to God would be eternal. The difference in the effect
therefore cannot arise from an affection of the kind asserted. Our divines furnish many further reasons
against it in their examination of the distinction between God’s antecedent and consequent will, a
distinction properly called Arminian in the form in which it is now abused. The learned reader may
consult them for a fuller account. For the present question it suffices that neither the general assertion of
Scripture about mercy nor the difference between those who receive it and those who do not allows the
definition our opponents require. A meaning which has no place in the subject cannot be intended by the
word. The love declared in this passage must be understood through the effects Scripture actually ascribes
to it, whose dependence on a freely distinguishing will gives them a ground that the supposed natural
propensity cannot supply.
   [661] Second, the love declared here is the special love of God to his elect. Its nature becomes
apparent through the benefit which it purposes for them and the gift by which it brings that benefit to
pass. The proofs which establish that connection exclude the velleity or natural inclination toward the
good of all which our adversaries would place in its stead.
    [662] First, the passage sets forth the most eminent and transcendent love God has ever borne toward
a miserable creature. That is the Savior’s intention in the emphatic form of the declaration, for the
particles “so,” “that,” set the act above every other act of the same kind. Expositors have justly found its
eminence expressed in nearly every word. “So,” signifies a degree whose height calls for astonishment.
“God,” names the glorious and all-sufficient God, who could have manifested his justice eternally in the
condemnation of all sinners and had no need of them to participate in his blessedness. “loved,” signifies
the earnest intensity of an eternal and unchangeable act of his will, whose purpose is the effectual
bestowal of the highest good. “the world,” names men in and of the world, subject to its iniquities and
miseries, lying in their blood and having nothing to commend them before him. In “that he gave,” the
nature of the act is given through the thing bestowed. God could speak the world into being at its first
creation, but here he undertook a greater and longer work in which there was more to be done than the
exercise of almighty power by a word. He gave “his Son;” and no treasure of creation could have
expressed the love signified by that gift. No favored creature, no sun, moon, or stars, and no riches of the
world he had made could answer to it. The further determination “begotten Son,” distinguishes the Son
from those called sons through their relation and obedience to God, as the angels are, and from believers
who are sons by adoption. Even the giving of an angel would have testified to an intense love, but the Son
he gave was begotten of his own person from eternity. The words “his onlybegotten Son;” make plain that
the Father had no other such Son to give. He gave the one who was always in his bosom, his Isaac.
Infinite wisdom could supply no higher testimony of love, especially when the end to which the Son was
given is understood. Though the time had not yet come to express it openly, the giving already
comprehends what was to be endured. The Son was given first to suffer rather than to reign and be
worshiped, since God “spared him not, but delivered him up” to death “for us all.” The intention that

governs the whole is expressed in the good actually appointed for believers, whom God thus loved. They
“might not perish,” that is, they escape the utmost misery and wrath they had deserved to endure
eternally, “but have everlasting life,” entering the glory of life with God which they could never attain of
themselves. In the cost of the gift and the good which it actually accomplishes we see the truth that
“greater love has no man than this.” No love compatible with the eternal perishing of those it concerns
can equal the love by which God actually and eternally saves its objects. The common affection already
examined is therefore excluded by the very eminence which the Savior attributes to this love. The greatest
gift expresses the saving provision as a whole, because the further benefits it secures belong to the same
purpose that gave the Son. (Romans 8:32)
    [663] Second, Scripture singles out this very love as God’s chief act of love and directs believers to
take notice of it above all others. Paul declares, “God commends his love toward us, in that, while we
were yet sinners, Christ died for us;” and John gives the matter at length. “In this was manifested the love
of God toward us, because that God sent his only-begotten Son into the world, that we might live through
him. Herein is love, not that we loved God, but that he loved us, and sent his Son to be the propitiation for
our sins.” Both passages magnify the love by declaring what God bestows through it. The death of Christ
concerns the sinners toward whom the love is commended, and the Son is sent to be the propitiation for
their sins so that they might live through him. These determinations belong to the love itself as it is made
manifest to believers. They cannot be accommodated to a natural velleity for the good of all, since the
good by which the love is identified is the effectual life and reconciliation of those who receive it.
(Romans 5:8; 1 John 4:9–10)
    [664] Third, divine love is velle alicui bonum, the willing of good to the beloved. The objects of a
given act of love are therefore those for whom God intends the good which constitutes its issue. Here that
good consists in deliverance from perishing and in the attainment of eternal life through Christ. It belongs
to elect believers alone, who alone receive it. They alone are consequently the objects of this love. The
good willed determines which act of love is being asserted, and the efficacy of God’s will connects that
good with its actual bestowal through the means appointed to bring it about.
    [665] Fourth, the love from which God gives Christ is also the source from which he bestows all the
other good things that come with him. Paul asks, “He that spared not his own Son, but delivered him up
for us all, how shall he not with him also freely give us all things?” The giving named in the present
passage proceeds from that love and therefore entails the giving of those further benefits. Its objects are
accordingly those to whom the things are actually given, namely the elect who believe. Grace here and
glory hereafter belong to no others. The benefits remain distinct in their reception, but the cause which
gives the Son gives them with him and supplies what is necessary for their enjoyment. The relation
cannot be divided by extending the first gift to persons for whom the same love provides none of the rest.
(Romans 8:32)
     [666] The love under examination gives Christ and gives with him the further saving benefits which
the passage joins to that gift. Any enlargement of its objects must carry this whole relation with it. If the
fruits necessary to that love are removed to accommodate a universal domain, the new proposition
concerns a different love rather than a greater number of recipients of the same provision.
                         [667] Loved(x)⇒Gived(o,x),          Gived(o,x)⇒◇Bx. (53)
    [668] Terms. The predicates concern the particular effectual saving love under examination, and B
denotes the conjunction of benefits established by the text and preceding argument. The temporal operator
expresses their realization in the recipient at the appointed time.
    [669] Consequence. The extent proposed for the love must retain the saving good which defines it. A
universal love with another issue constitutes a different proposition and requires its own grounds. The
dispute concerns the good actually willed and secured, the persons to whom it is given, and the operations
through which they attain it.

    [670] Fifth, ἠγάπησε has the force of valde dilexit, a love so strong that the one who loves rests in it. It
is for our opponents to show how such love can stand in the same subject toward the same persons with
hatred and an eternal determination to withhold effectual grace, which God does withhold from some.
The Christian reader may now judge whether the love of God in this place is a natural inclination or
velleity for the good of all, elect and reprobate together, or the peculiar love to the elect which is the
source of the greatest good ever bestowed on men. The force of the word agrees with the operation and
the result established through the passage. Intensity considered by itself does not determine a domain
beyond those relations. This concludes the first difference in the interpretation of the words.
    [671] Second, the object of the love is expressed by “world;” the dispute turns on who is
comprehended in it. Our opponents take the term to mean every man, while we understand the elect of
God scattered throughout the world. The expression tacitly opposes that dispersion to the former privilege
of the Jewish nation. Before Christ’s actual exhibition in the flesh, the benefits of the promises were
appropriated to that nation alone, a few proselytes excepted, whereas the privilege was now to extend
equally to all nations. This enlargement concerns the peoples among whom the benefits are enjoyed. The
Universalists produce nothing of weight for the further proposition that every individual is intended. Their
case rests upon the word alone, though neither the love which begins the verse nor the design which
concludes it will agree with the sense they impose upon the word between them. The varied uses of world
and the weakness of the inference drawn from the term by itself have already been established at length.
The proper object must be capable of receiving both the love and the saving effect which the passage
ascribes to it. (Romans 9:4)
    [672] The champions of the universal reading produce three evasions against the claim that world
signifies the elect. They had undertaken to establish that it includes every individual, yet they offer no
reason for that assertion, as though conscious that none can be given. The position they attack is one no
one has undertaken to prove, namely that the elect are here described as elect. The persons designated are
all and only the elect, while the description under which they enter the passage concerns men in and of the
world, scattered throughout it and subject in themselves to sin and misery. Whoever would refute this
exposition must establish either that world necessarily includes every individual here or that the term
cannot be used indefinitely of men in the world who are in fact elect though election is not the formal
character under which the passage describes them. Their practice of substituting elect for world and
inventing absurd consequences therefore misses the proposition they must answer. Even the substitution,
however, yields no absurdity or untruth. Suppose the verse read, “God so loved the elect, that he gave his
only-begotten Son, that whosoever believes in him should not perish,” and ask what follows. They what
follows and answer, “Why,” “that some of the elect, whom God so loved as to send his Son for, may
perish.” Nothing in the sending of the Son so that they might not perish supports that consequence. They
reply, “No; but because it is said, that whosoever of them believes on him should not perish; which
intimates that some of them might not believe.” The alleged intimation is absent from the words. God
expressly designs the salvation of all those for whom he sends his Son, and everyone who shall be saved
shall believe. Their case consequently rests on taking whosoever to distribute the world into those who
believe and those who do not. First, if the word distributes the object in their sense, the love intending
salvation must be restricted to the believing part, for the other part is excluded from the benefit to which
the giving is directed. If it effects no restriction of the love intending salvation, then it does not distribute
the object of that love as alleged. If it restricts the love, it gives up the proposition that all are intended by
the love which moved God to give the Son. Second, the word declares the end which God intends in
giving Christ in pursuance of his love, namely the salvation of all believers. It does not divide the persons
loved into a part for whom salvation is intended and a part for whom the same love intends nothing of the
kind. The sense is “God so loved his elect throughout the world, that he gave his Son with this intention,
that by him believers might be saved.” Apart from worthless cavils, this is the whole objection from the
place itself. It mistakes the description given to the persons for a denial that they are in fact elect and
treats an alleged awkwardness in a substitution as proof that a wholly different referent is intended. The

positive and negative grounds for the proposed reading must be drawn from the actual terms of the
passage.
    [673] First, the nature of the love already proved determines who can constitute the world said to be
loved. It is the most transcendent and remarkable love, an eternal act of God’s will, the cause of his
sending Christ, the source of all good things given in and with him, and the assured fountain of salvation
to every person who is its object. Those determinations belong to a single act of love. Every person
included within the domain must therefore receive the consequences which the passage establishes for
that act. Our opponents cannot extend it to every individual while conceding that many never receive
those consequences. The domain of the term is fixed by the love under which it is comprehended, and the
world which receives this love cannot possibly consist of every man in the world.
    [674] Second, the following verse continues the same matter and makes known God’s intention in
giving his Son. It therefore supplies the sense in which world is used here. World occurs three times in the
next verse with different senses, an inversion of the kind already shown to be familiar in Scripture. It is
the last occurrence that corresponds to the world said to be loved in the present passage, as the words
“That the world through him might be saved,” make plain. The clause ἵνα σωθῇ expresses the purpose,
“that it should be saved.” God intends the salvation of the world to which this love is directed. The
continuity of the matter establishes continuity in the relevant referent, since the people intended in the
purpose are those for whom the giving proceeds from the love already named. The world in this clause
must therefore consist of the elect and believers, or at least of those actually saved in the event. If it
comprises anyone else, God fails in the very purpose the verse declares, a consequence we do not grant.
(John 3:16)
    [675] Third, Scripture elsewhere names God’s chosen people as the world, just as it uses all flesh, all
nations, and all families of the earth. The title magnifies the love declared here by bringing before us
those whom it comprehends as a world. Christ is called their Savior because he actually saves them, for a
Savior of men who are never saved is a strange assertion. The same usage appears where he gives himself
for their life and where he “gives life unto the world:” those to whom the life is given receive it as his
“sheep,” who “shall never perish.” Abraham by faith is “heir of the world;” and in the corresponding
passage he is called the father of the faithful. The fall of the Jews is “the riches of the world;” a world
comprising believers of all sorts, as the apostle says that the word bore fruit “in all the world.” This is the
“world” which “God reconciles to himself, not imputing their trespasses unto them”. The non-imputation
named in that relation carries blessedness to all to whom it belongs. There are several reasons for the
name. First, it distinguishes the object of this love from the angelic nature, which perished in all its fallen
individuals. Scripture expressly distinguishes the two and calls this love of God φιλανθρωπία. Second, it
overturns the boast of the Jews that all the means of grace and the benefits intended through them were
appropriated to their nation. Third, it marks the difference between the old administration of the covenant,
which was confined to one people, family, and nation, and the new, in which those boundaries are broken
down. The fullness of the Gentiles and the farthest corners of the world are brought under the scepter of
Christ. Fourth, it discloses the condition of the elect themselves, that the freedom of grace may appear in
the love shown them. Under this description they have no qualification that commends them, but are
earthly, lost, miserable, and corrupted. Each use shows how a term of such breadth can concern the elect
without narrowing the breadth it actually expresses. The word itself therefore furnishes no objection to the
exposition already given. Its proper application in this passage receives its positive grounds from the love
that bears upon the persons it designates and the saving work carried out for them. The further argument
makes that determination plainer still. (John 4:42; 6:33, 51; 10:27–28; Romans 4:8, 11, 13; 11:12;
Colossians 1:6; 2 Corinthians 5:19; Hebrews 2:16; Titus 3:4)
    [676] Fourth, if God intends every individual under the love declared, why does he never reveal Christ
to innumerable persons so loved? It is strange to ascribe to him a love so great that he gives his only-
begotten Son for them, while holding that he never once signifies that love to them by any means. The
proposed account requires his wise dispensation to order things so that the love remains vain and fruitless,

and requires him to will their greatest good while determining that they shall receive no good from that
act of love. Where hearing is a necessary means of believing, the provision of the communication belongs
to the efficacy of the saving purpose. The universal account must explain the persons left without that
communication, for the absence of the appointed means bears directly upon whether the giving was
intended to save them.
    [677] Fifth, the unrestricted reading can stand only if its defenders grant six consequences. First, some
are both loved and hated from eternity under the same respect. Second, God’s love toward innumerable
persons is vain and fruitless. Third, his Son is given to people who never hear of him and who receive no
power to believe in him. Fourth, God either changes in his love or still loves those who are in hell with
the very love said to intend their salvation. Fifth, he withholds the other saving goods from some to whom
he gives his Son, contrary to the apostle. Sixth, he has no certain foreknowledge of those who will believe
and be saved. Unless these blasphemies and absurdities are granted, world cannot comprise every
individual of mankind. It designates men scattered throughout the world, who are the elect. An appeal to a
difference of respect can do nothing for the argument unless the difference is stated and shown to
preserve the saving relation which the verse actually declares. (Romans 8:32)
     [678] The third difference concerns the way in which the objects of the Father’s love come to
participate in its fruits. The words ἵνα πᾶς ὁ πιστεύων may be rendered “that whosoever believes,” or
“that every believer.” They designate faith in Christ as the appointed way in which the elect receive the
life that is in the Son. God has appointed this way alone for its communication to us. As already
established, whosoever does not distribute the object of God’s love into those who share its intended good
and those for whom it fails. The Father’s gift of the Son is the provision through which that love operates,
while believing describes how the person comes into actual enjoyment of the benefit. Its presence must
therefore be understood within the provision whose end is life, since a condition of reception does not by
itself determine whether the gift of that condition lies within the purpose of the giver. Two considerations
make this plain.
     [679] First, suppose the clause restricts the persons loved so that only some of those for whom Christ
was sent believe and are saved. The difference depends either on God’s will or on the persons themselves.
If it depends on the persons themselves, they make themselves to differ, contrary to the apostle. If it
depends on God’s will, their interpretation gives this meaning. “God so loved all as that but some of them
should partake of the fruits of his love.” For what end, then, did he love the others? “Out with the sword,
and run the dragon through with the spear?” The condition determines who enjoys the fruits, but the
source of the difference between those who believe and those who do not is a further question. The
universal reading must give an account of both propositions, since an appeal to the condition alone leaves
untouched the relation of the professed love to those for whom God makes no provision of faith. (1
Corinthians 4:7)
    [680] Second, the words that whosoever believes express the aim of God in the giving of the Son. If
they restrict the objects loved, the salvation of believers as distinguished from others is confessedly the
intended end. The general ransom would then be an empty sound, with no place in the purpose of God.
His intention in giving the Son would extend determinately to the salvation of believers alone, unless one
denied his knowledge of who would believe. The purpose clause therefore specifies the end of the act,
and the condition identifies the mode in which the persons for whom the act is intended receive what it
secures.
    [681] Whosoever believes designates the means by which the Lord brings us to participate in life
through the Son whom he gave for us. Everlasting life, named in the words that follow, is the saving end
of his whole counsel in the matter, subordinate to his own glory. Faith retains its place as a necessary
means within that counsel. Once its procurement is established, the condition can no longer be treated as
an event left without a cause in the purpose which secures the end. The question at the Hub is how the gift

of the Son brings the persons spoken of into life. The passage names faith as the means of that enjoyment,
which leaves us to trace its provision through the undertaking that secures the end.
   [682] It follows that God did not give his Son for those who never believe, still less for those who
never hear of him and thus plainly lack the appointed means of faith. Nor did he give his Son for those to
whom he has determined not to grant the effectual grace by which they would believe. Both the declared
purpose and the actual provision of its necessary means sustain this consequence, and a reply must answer
one of them to overturn it.
    [683] The two interpretations can now be judged in their full connection. The decisive question is the
nature of the love from which the sending proceeds. If it is a general affection or natural velleity for the
good of all, it is compatible with the eternal perishing of every person it concerns. If it is the Father’s
peculiar and transcendent love to the elect, it gives the Son for the purpose which the passage expressly
declares and provides the means through which its objects attain that end. The reader may weigh which
account preserves the persons, the giving, the condition, and the promised life together, and so determine
whether the words of the Savior establish a ransom fruitless for most of those for whom it was paid, or the
effectual redemption of the elect alone. Our opponents bring this passage forward as the strongest support
of their cause. Both the cause of the sending and the end intended in the sending, as declared by Christ
himself, are incompatible with their conclusion.
    [684] The second passage is urged next to the first with the greatest confidence in support of the
general ransom. The distinctions already established give precision to the question without deciding in
advance whom this passage designates. Its own subject and purpose must supply the grounds for the
extent of the relation it asserts.
    [685] 2. The apostle writes, “If any man sin, we have an advocate with the Father, Jesus Christ the
righteous: and he is the propitiation for our sins: and not for ours only, but also for the sins of the whole
world.” The words have been dressed up in various ways, with observations meant to turn them to the
advantage of the general ransom. The weight of the argument rests on Christ being the “propitiation for
the sins of the whole world;” an expression “which,” they claim, “manifestly appears to be all and every
one in the world,” on two grounds. Both must be judged within the relation established by the apostle,
who joins propitiation to advocacy as the ground of consolation. The extent of the persons who have that
ground cannot be determined after severing the act by which God is appeased from the office through
which its benefit supplies their comfort. (1 John 2:1–2)
    [686] First, they argue “From the words themselves without any wresting; for what can be signified by
the whole world, but all men in the world?” This is the very proposition for which the passage must
supply grounds, since the breadth of the phrase does not of itself determine every person within its
domain.
    [687] Second, they argue “From the opposition that is made between world and believers, all believers
being comprised in the first part of the apostle’s assertion, that Christ is a propitiation for our sins; and
therefore by the world, opposed unto them, all others are understood.” Any further objection of weight
can be answered in the exposition itself. An opposition between two descriptions does not by its form
divide mankind into believers and all other people. The terms of the passage must establish which
community supplies the first description and in what respect the second extends beyond it.
    [688] The objection admits a brief answer sufficient to cut off the cavils drawn from the words
themselves. World elsewhere denotes men living in the world. The whole world can accordingly
designate people throughout the several regions of the world, in opposition to the inhabitants of a single
nation, place, or country, as the redeemed of Christ are expressly said to be drawn from many places. This
answer grants the breadth of the expression and shows how it can be satisfied without an unrestricted
universality of individuals. It therefore meets the objection even before the positive sense of the passage
has been established. Since our adversaries boast of the passage as though their reading were its only

possible sense, a fuller exposition is necessary. With God’s assistance it will show how little ground their
interpretation has, both in the words they rely upon and in the saving relation which those words serve to
express. (Revelation 5:9)
    [689] Three things determine the sense of the passage. The first is the community to whom the apostle
immediately writes. The second is the consolation which he intends to supply. The third is the meaning of
the two expressions upon which the dispute turns, Christ being a “propitiation;” and “The whole world.”
The analogy of faith, the scope of this passage and its parallels, and the proper use of the words all bear
upon these determinations. Taken together, they give grounds sufficient to show that the text cannot be
made to support universal redemption. Its scope must be assigned after identifying the people to whom
the apostle speaks, the end for which he speaks, and the operation in which their comfort consists.
    [690] (1.) The epistle, like every part of divine Scripture, was given for the use, benefit, and direction
of the whole church. That general office is exercised in many passages through words addressed
immediately to particular churches, persons, or classes of people, and intended to teach, reprove, remove,
or establish something with special reference to them. The primary audience therefore contributes to the
determination of the words. No express designation of that audience stands in this epistle, and its identity
cannot on that account be affirmed as infallibly certain and de fide. Yet the grounds available make it
more than probable that John directed it chiefly to Jews who believed, the believers of the circumcision.
That identification is important because the first community supplies the immediate referent of us. The
breadth introduced by the second expression is an enlargement in relation to that group, and the character
of the enlargement cannot be judged without attending to the persons among whom the apostle first
reckons himself.
    [691] First, the Jews were the immediate objects of John’s apostolic care, in accordance with the
division of labor acknowledged by the apostles themselves. “James, Cephas, and John gave to Paul and
Barnabas the right hand of fellowship, that they should go unto the heathen, and themselves unto the
circumcision.” Peter and James exercised that apostleship toward the Jews by writing to them in their
dispersion. The James who wrote was the one of whom Paul here speaks, since John’s brother had already
been slain. Correspondingly, Paul wrote to the principal churches which he had planted among the
Gentiles. It is therefore more than probable that John directed this epistle in the first instance to those who
were in the first instance the objects of his commission. The office identifies the community to which the
apostle’s care was immediately directed, and supplies historical evidence for the persons addressed
without by itself determining what saving acts the passage attributes to them. (Galatians 2:9; James 1:1;
Peter 1:1)
    [692] Second, John repeatedly treats his readers as persons who heard and received the word from the
beginning. Twice in the same passage he marks their relation to that original proclamation. “I write an old
commandment, which you had from the beginning, … which you heard from the beginning.” Acts records
that the Gospel first went out among the Jews and was received among them before the conversion of the
Gentiles, which remained a mystery for a time. The order divinely appointed was “To the Jew first, and
also to the Greek,” and the words of the epistle agree with that order. The temporal relation which John
establishes between his readers and the preaching they received thus furnishes a further ground for
identifying the community directly addressed. (Acts 1–5; 10; 11; 1 John 2:7; Romans 1:16)
    [693] The Smyrna testimony examined in paragraph 574 establishes why the extent of world is
determined by the relation in which it is used. The same distinction governs the present passage. The
community immediately addressed, the peoples newly admitted to the proclamation, and the persons who
receive its saving effects can overlap without being identical. Extension beyond one nation therefore
establishes the removal of a national restriction. To conclude that every individual of every nation is
actually justified requires a further proposition, whose grounds must be supplied by the text itself.
(Martyrdom of Polycarp 17.2; 1 John 2:1–2)

    [694] Third, the apostle’s opposition between us and the world in this very place shows whom he
addresses. As a Jew he reckons himself among the believing Jews and distinguishes that company from
the rest of the believers throughout the world. The same opposition is familiar in his writing, and his
Gospel supplies the terms in which it is understood. The difference lies in the communities to which the
believers belong. It does not place saving faith in the one group and total unbelief in the other, since both
are brought within the same saving relation. (John 11:51–52)
    [695] Fourth, the warnings concerning false teachers, seducers, and antichrists also indicate the
persons to whom the epistle was especially directed. In those first days such men were for the most part of
the circumcision, if not all of them, as Scripture and ecclesiastical history show. The apostle says of them
that “they went out from them,” and his repeated cautions have particular force for readers more exposed
than others to the seductions of their own countrymen. This historical relation between those warned and
those against whom they are warned joins the other evidence for the audience. Its weight arises in
conjunction with the terms of the apostle’s address. (1 John 2:19)
    [696] The purpose of the disputed expression becomes clear in light of the inveterate hatred of the
Jews toward the Gentiles and the settled opinion that the redemption purchased by their Messiah belonged
to their nation alone. “He,” the apostle says, “is the propitiation for our sins,” meaning the sins of us who
are believers of the Jews. Lest the particular assurance become an occasion for strengthening their former
error, he adds, “And not for ours only, but for the sins of the whole world,” extending it to “The children
of God scattered abroad”, of whatever nation, kindred, tongue, or language. The enlargement concerns the
communities from which the redeemed are drawn. What belongs effectually to the believing Jews is
declared to belong in the same manner to all the other believers, the children of God scattered throughout
the world. The contrast therefore gives no ground for placing effectual salvation on one side and
ineffectual redemption on the other. The same redemption supplies the force of both parts of the assertion,
and the expression that enlarges its scope removes the national restriction without changing the operation
predicated of the persons whom it comprehends. (John 11:51–52)
    [697] (2.) The order of the words gives the purpose for which the apostle speaks. He supplies
believers with consolation against the sins and failings under which they might otherwise despair or
utterly faint. “If any man sin, we have an advocate with the Father, Jesus Christ the righteous: and he is
the propitiation for our sins.” The remedy afforded is sufficient and effectual, and three considerations
identify the persons to whom he offers it. First, they alone have an advocate, since it is confessed that
only believers have an interest in Christ’s advocacy. Second, only they are entitled to consolation in this
case. Wrath is to be denounced to those who remain in a state of alienation. Third, the people to whom he
writes are his “little children”, whom he describes as having “their sins forgiven them for his name’s
sake,” and as those who “know the Father.” The purpose of the words is consequently to make the remedy
known to believers in their failings, and the relation which makes it a ground of consolation belongs to
them. A propitiation for every individual in the sense supposed by our adversaries would tell the believer
that Christ died for innumerable persons who will be damned. What assurance against condemnation
could a man receive from a benefit which he holds in common with those who perish eternally? One
might as well offer him a pumice-stone for a breast of consolation. If the question is how every believer
can be comforted unless Christ died for them all, the passage answers that he is both propitiation and
advocate for every one of them. If every individual is meant, including reprobates and unbelievers, there
is no solid spiritual consolation for them in Christ’s death or in the Word of God while they remain in that
state. The children’s bread must not be cast to dogs. The believer’s continuing need is met by a remedy
whose efficacy the apostle declares through the conjunction of advocacy and propitiation. The certainty of
his comfort depends on Christ standing in that operative relation to him. (John 3:36; 1 John 2:1, 12–13)
    [698] (3.) The next determination concerns “propitiation,” which Christ is said to be for “us,” and for
“the whole world,” in the same proposition. The operation signified must remain the same in both cases,
since an interpretation of the extent of the expression must preserve what the passage predicates of
everyone included.

    [699] First, the original word is ἱλασμός, used only twice in the New Testament, here and in the same
epistle. The verb ἱλάσκομαι occurs as often. In one construction it is properly rendered “to make
reconciliation;” and in the prayer of the publican it appears as Ἱλάσθητί μοι, “Be merciful to me.” The
related ἱλαστήριον likewise occurs twice, translated in one place “a propitiation;” and in the other used for
and rendered “the mercy-seat.” The thing called capporeth, from caphar, is thus named by the same word
as that which Christ is declared to be. The mercy-seat was a plate of pure gold, two cubits and a half in
length and a cubit and a half in breadth, like the upper board of a table. It lay upon the ark beneath the
shadow of the cherubim’s wings. ‫ ַּכ ֹּפֶר ת‬derives from ‫ָּכ ַפ ר‬, whose first and proper sense is “to cover,”
although its most common use is “to expiate.” The plate was named for its position over the ark, which it
covered as the wings of the cherubim hovered over it. Its mystical use was to cover the law kept in the
ark, the rigid tenor of the covenant of works. In this figure God declared himself appeased and reconciled
because the cause of anger and enmity was hidden. Hence the second sense which the apostle renders by
ἱλαστήριον, “placamen,” or “placamentum,” the thing by which God is appeased. The cherubim’s wings,
bent over the plate like a hen’s wings over her young, denoted his presence in power and goodness.
David’s prayer to be “hid under the shadow of God’s wings,” and perhaps the Savior’s allusion to the hen
gathering her young, express that favorable protection under mercy which the wings signify. They
embrace the cover over the bill of accusation, which in the type was the golden plate and in truth is Jesus
Christ, as Scripture expressly affirms. The relation is intelligible through what the covering accomplishes.
By covering the accusation it exhibits the removal of judicial enmity, and thus the type supplies the
saving office in which the lexical sense of propitiation must be understood. (1 John 4:10; Hebrews 2:17;
9:5; Luke 18:13; Romans 3:25; Exodus 25:17; Psalms 36:7; 57:1; 61:4; 63:7; 91:4; Matthew 23:37)
    [700] The operation represented by the mercy-seat determines the sense of ἱλασμός and ἱλαστήριον,
both translated “a propitiation,” together with the verb from the same root. They derive from ἱλάω, which
does not occur in the New Testament and which Eustathius derives from ἵεμαι λάειν, “intently and with
care to look upon any thing,” as in the oracle upon the mercy-seat. The related forms signify the act
effected in the type, the appeasing, pacifying, and reconciling of God in respect of his aversion for sin.
Thus Ἱλάσκεσθαι τὰς ἁμαρτίας τοῦ λαοῦ is rendered by the Latinists “Expiare peccata populi,” “To
expiate the sins of the people.” In this matter “Expiare” signifies the turning away of anger by atonement,
as in the historian’s words, “Solere reges ostenta cœlestia cæde aliquâ illustri expiare, atque a semet in
capita procerum depellere,” (Suet. in Neron. 36.) Our rendering is “To make reconciliation for the sins of
the people.” The word will bear either rendering, since both give the satisfaction offered to God for sin,
by which he is appeased toward those to whom it is therefore not imputed. Ἱλάσκεσθαι τὰς ἁμαρτίας τοῦ
λαοῦ has the force of Ἱλάσκεσθαι τὸν Θεὸν περὶ τῶν ἁμαρτιῶν, “To pacify God concerning sin.” The
publican’s use of the word follows from this relation. Ἱλάσθητί μοι, “Be merciful to me;” means “Let me
enjoy that mercy from whence flows the pardon of sin, by your being appeased towards me, and
reconciled unto me.” Thus ἱλασμός, or “propitiation,” when predicated of Christ, signifies that whereby
the law is covered, God is appeased and reconciled, sin is expiated, and the sinner is pardoned. Hence
Scripture so frequently places pardon and remission of sins as the fruit of the shedding of his blood, by
which he was a “propitiation”. The act and the effect belong to the meaning together. Once the effect
essential to that act has been removed, the proposition that Christ actually propitiates has itself been
changed. (Hebrews 2:17; 9:22; Luke 18:13; Matthew 26:28; Ephesians 1:7; Colossians 1:14; Romans
3:25; 5:9; 1 John 1:7; 1 Peter 1:2; Revelation 1:5)
     [701] Christ so expiates sin and reconciles the sinner to God that for his sake the sinner is pardoned,
received to mercy, and never again liable to have the law produced for his condemnation. Let those who
take the whole world for every individual judge whether these effects can be attributed to everyone. If
every man’s sins are expiated and God is reconciled to each, then every sinner is pardoned, and no
transgression of the law shall ever be charged against any man. Why then is every man not saved? What
is true of every believer is true of no other person in the world. The apostle affirms that Christ is a
propitiation for believers in order to establish the ground and principal substance of the advocacy from
which their consolation arises, namely his presentation of the atonement made by his blood. Christ is a

propitiation only through faith, which none but believers have. It follows that believers throughout the
world are the persons for whom alone he is a propitiation. To them alone God says, Ἵλεως ἔσομαι, “I will
be propitious,” which is the great declaration of the new covenant, because they alone are its covenanters.
Their reconciliation follows from the operation attributed to Christ in relation to them. No widening of the
term for the persons concerned can relieve an interpreter of the consequence which the saving predicate
carries with it. (Romans 3:25; Hebrews 8:12)
    [702] Second, the inquiry turns to ὅλου τοῦ κόσμου, which is rendered “of the whole world.” The
varied significations of world need not be rehearsed, both because it has already been shown to be
πολύσημον and because our opponents here insist upon the force it receives from its adjunct. The
question therefore concerns “the whole world:” taken together. The addition of whole expresses
completeness within the domain, but the phrase as it occurs in the passage must determine the domain in
which that completeness is asserted.
     [703] First, the expression, together with its equivalent all the world, occurs seven or eight times in
the New Testament. In none of these places can it clearly and undeniably be made to comprehend every
individual, save perhaps in one where the subject requires it, in re necessariâ. The unrestricted sense
cannot therefore be imposed where the circumstances give no such ground. The later occurrences make
this plain. The promise reads, “I will keep you from the hour of temptation, which shall come ἐπὶ τῆς
οἰκουμένης ὅλης,” that is, “upon all the world,” from which some are promised preservation. The Greek
word for world differs here from the one in dispute, since Scripture uses different words for the thing
according to the aspect under which it is considered. The promise itself excludes a reference to every
person, because some are to be preserved from what comes upon the world. Passing over the disputed
passage, the next instance says, “Which is come unto you καθὼς καὶ ἐν παντὶ τῷ κόσμῳ,” rendered “as in
all the world.” Not every person had then received the Gospel, and the world here comprises believers
dispersed abroad, for the Gospel is said to “bring forth fruit” in those to whom it comes, and there is no
true fruit of it without faith and repentance. Another passage declares, “Your faith is spoken of ἐν ὅλῳ τῷ
κόσμῳ,” that is, “throughout the whole world.” The faith of the Romans was no subject of knowledge and
speech to every individual in the world. The decree of Augustus furnishes a further instance. “There went
out a decree from Cæsar Augustus, ἀπογράφεσθαι πᾶσαν τὴν οἰκουμένην,” requiring “that all the world
should be taxed;” yet its domain was the Roman Empire, which was far short of comprehending all the
individuals of the world. The remaining examples have the same indefinite force and need not be
repeated. It follows that the terms themselves express no such universality as is claimed. The subject and
circumstances could establish it in a given instance, but neither gives any appearance of doing so here.
The ordinary use of all the world and the whole world for men of various sorts dispersed through the
world therefore agrees with the contextual sense already established. The complete body comprehended
by ὅλος ὁ κόσμος here is the ἐκκλησία καθολική. Its completeness concerns the church throughout the
world, and the domain of that whole is given by the saving relation in which the passage names it.
(Revelation 3:10; Colossians 1:6; Romans 1:8; Luke 2:1)
     [704] Second, the whole world is no more comprehensive than all nations, all the families of the earth,
all flesh, all men, or all the ends of the world. Each of these expressions can gather particulars as broadly
as the phrase under dispute, yet Scripture frequently uses them of believers of every sort throughout the
world. Thus it is said, “All the ends of the earth have seen the salvation of our God”, “All the ends of the
world shall remember and turn unto the Lord, and all the kindreds of the nations shall worship before
you”, and “All nations shall serve you”. The persons denoted are the believers of the several nations, for
they alone see God’s salvation, remember and turn to him, and serve him. The same breadth appears in
the promise, “I will pour out of my Spirit upon all flesh;” and in its repetition at the accomplishment.
Luke likewise gives the words of John the Baptist, “All flesh shall see the salvation of God.” What a
conquest our opponents would have proclaimed if Scripture had said that Christ died for all flesh, all
nations, or all kindreds. Yet these expressions are already the dress in which Scripture presents believers,
and they are as ample as the whole world. Believers are called “all nations” and even “all men”, because

the grace that brings salvation is manifest to them alone. Since the children of God may accordingly be
named all flesh, all nations, all kindreds, all the ends of the world, all the ends of the earth, and all men,
no greater difficulty attends their being named the whole world. What these expressions establish is the
extension of the saving relation among the nations. Extension to every individual is formally different and
cannot be put in its place without a premise establishing the stronger claim. (Psalms 98:3; 22:27; 72:11;
Joel 2:28; Acts 2:17; Isaiah 2:2; 66:18; Titus 2:11)
    [705] Third, the whole world can signify the worse part of the world and may by the same synecdoche
signify the better. Scripture says, “The Devil, and Satan, which deceives the whole world, is cast out;”
and others rejoice at his overthrow. The persons deceived are the wicked and reprobate throughout the
world. So Ὁ κόσμος ὅλος occurs in “The whole world lies in wickedness;” where “the whole world” is
opposed to those said to be “of God,” at the beginning of the verse. The contrary restriction appears in the
passage previously cited. These uses establish that the expression can signify one part under the name of
the whole. The grounds for assigning that sense here are furnished by the relations asserted in this
passage. (Revelation 12:9–10; 1 John 5:19; Colossians 1:6)
    [706] Nothing in the words therefore requires that every individual in the world be denoted. The
evidence instead gives the expression its proper breadth as embracing all who believed or would believe
throughout the world, in opposition only to believers of the Jewish nation. This reading brings together
the actual audience, the consolation the apostle gives, the operation he predicates of Christ, and the extent
of the community in which it operates. The reasons that follow establish the conclusion from those
determinations together, in addition to the proofs by which each has already been made plain.
    [707] First, the passage treats the ransom in respect of application rather than impetration. It affirms
Christ to be through his death what he is only through faith, as the cited passage establishes. Consolation
likewise arises from application, and no one has maintained that the application of Christ’s death is
universal. The passage consequently cannot comprehend every individual. Actual propitiation is an
operative saving relation. Its extent cannot be derived from a provision asserted for persons in whom the
effects that constitute the application are absent. (Romans 3:25)
    [708] Second, Christ is said to be a propitiation for the persons the passage intends. Its purpose is to
comfort believers in their failings, and the consolation belongs to them alone. They are therefore the
persons for whom he is said to be a propitiation, though they are believers of every sort, time, place, and
condition. The saving predicate attaches to the population actually described, and every enlargement of
that population would require the same saving consequences for those newly included.
    [709] The third consideration concerns the actual use of such expressions elsewhere in Scripture,
where the alleged inclusion of every individual is plainly impossible. The passages already examined
establish this, and another instance makes the relation especially clear, “Then went out to him πᾶσα ἡ
Ἰουδαία, καὶ πᾶσα ἡ περίχωρος τοῦ Ἰορδάνου,” — “all Judea, and all the region round about Jordan;” yet
the Pharisees, who belonged to that country, rejected his baptism. The whole is therefore named
according to the class constituted by the context. No reason exists for imposing a different extension here,
where the circumstances already examined positively resist it. (Matthew 3:5)
    [710] The fourth consideration is the testimony of the clearest parallel passages, whose assertions
concerning the persons and benefits in question oppose the imposed universal sense. Their agreement
