determines the saving relations within which the disputed expression operates. An interpretation which
detaches the expression from those relations cannot claim their support. (Colossians 1:6; John 11:51–52)
    [711] The fifth consideration follows from the consolation which the assertion is appointed to
communicate to believers. If its subject comprises every person in the world, its alleged universality
contributes nothing to that consolation. A believer receives no assurance from being told that Christ is a
propitiation for persons who nevertheless perish. Calling this propitiation sufficient while withholding its
efficacy leaves the difficulty intact. Jacob and his sons would have obtained little comfort from Joseph’s

possession of enough corn to sustain them if his actual provision for them had remained wholly uncertain.
A promise of sufficient sustenance which might never effectually feed them would have permitted them
to starve beneath its abundance. “The whole world,” accordingly, comprises the whole people of God
dispersed among the nations, in distinction from the Jewish nation alone — persons of every kindred,
tongue, family, and country, some of every sort rather than every member of each sort. General
redemption receives no proof here. Whoever extends the subject universally must also retain the saving
consequence belonging to propitiation. Otherwise the expansion has been obtained by substituting a
different predicate.
     [712] Propitiation carries an asserted effect, and its interpretation stands or falls with the preservation
of that effect. An actual saving non-imputation extended to every person necessarily extends its judicial
consequences to every person also. A provision under which the entire liability remains can be substituted
only upon textual grounds establishing that weaker operation. Its existence cannot be assumed merely to
accommodate the proposed universality.
          [713] ∀x∈D Prop(x)Prop(x)⇒NonImpute(x)                  ⇒ ∀x∈D NonImpute(x). (54)
   [714] Terms. D denotes the disputed domain. Prop denotes propitiation in the saving sense asserted.
NonImpute denotes its relevant judicial consequence under the appointed application. The second premise
depends upon the exegetical argument and the grounds concerning satisfaction already established.
     [715] Consequence. Symbolic expression supplies no proof of that premise, but neither does it
authorize its unannounced removal. The inference requires one predicate to retain its identity throughout
the population claimed for it. The subsequent examination of reconciliation and non-imputation follows
this same requirement — the world alleged to be universal must actually receive the effect which the
passage predicates of that world.
   [716] The usual objections to this interpretation have, for the most part, already received their answers
within the exposition itself. One or two nevertheless deserve explicit consideration, since an objection
becomes determinate only when it identifies the relation already established which it means to deny. The
answer can then address that premise directly.
    [717] The first objection proposes a universal need for consolation as the ground of a universal
interest in its provision, “It is the intention of the apostle to comfort all in their fears and doubts; but every
one in the world may be in fears and doubts: therefore, he proposes this, that they all may be comforted.”
Yet a person’s desire for comfort does not constitute participation in its saving ground. The persons
whom the apostle intends to comfort must be identified before the proposed domain can be accepted.
    [718] The answer follows from the saving relation under which this consolation is addressed. Those
who may suffer fears and doubts in the matter here considered are believers, as the preceding exposition
established. The universal expression must therefore preserve that condition in its domain. The possibility
of fear in others does not confer the believer’s interest upon them.
    [719] The second objection treats the added clause as the complement of an allegedly exhaustive first
class, “All believers are comprehended in the first branch, ‘For our sins;’ and, therefore in the increase
and extension of the assertion, by adding, ‘For the sins of the whole world,’ all others are intended.” Its
conclusion depends entirely upon the claim that the first branch has already included every believer. That
claim requires proof. The added words do not themselves supply it.
    [720] The answer has two parts. First, the initial branch concerns believing Jews, among whom John
himself belonged. Its enlargement therefore adds other believers to those believers, without carrying
Christ’s propitiation beyond the believing people. Second, even if all believers then living and capable of
presently receiving this truth were conceded to belong to the first branch, the corresponding accession
would comprise believers in later ages and in places which the name of Christ had not yet reached. These
are all who, according to the Savior’s prayer, would believe upon his name until the end of the world.

Both principal passages offered for the first argument are thus freed from the imposed gloss, and the
remaining passages admit the same discrimination. An enlargement from one believing community to
believers beyond it extends the geographical and communal reception of the benefit while preserving the
saving predicate. No inclusion of each unbelieving individual follows from that extension. (John 17:20)
    [721] The third passage concerns the Savior’s promise to give his “flesh for the life of the world.” His
giving is the sanctification and offering of himself as an acceptable oblation for the sins of those for
whom he suffered, with the intention that through that offering they should possess eternal life. Because
the intended recipients comprise the elect of God everywhere, beyond the Jewish people alone, he calls
them “the world.” The operation asserted therefore excludes an extension to every person who ever lived
or would live — the world is the object of Christ’s intention to purchase and bestow life and salvation.
Could spiritual or ordinary judgment admit that his oblation intended the purchase of salvation for
persons whom he knew to have been condemned for ages under an irreversible sentence of wrath? Could
he have given himself for the life of those who, under his own appointment, eternally fail to obtain it?
Even if this passage alone had survived, the very text advanced against the restriction would establish that
world can designate some of every sort, as the elect are, without designating all individuals. Equitable
judgment must follow the life promised through the actual operation of the flesh offered, and must
preserve that saving effect in identifying its recipients. (John 6:51)
   [722] Thomas More assembles further passages in the “Universality of Free Grace,” and the chapter
containing them calls for a brief examination. Each passage asserts its own predicate of its own domain.
Merely placing the texts beside one another cannot make their disputed meanings establish one another.
Each interpretation requires grounds within the passage concerned. (Thomas More, The Universality of
God’s Free Grace 14)
    [723] The first of these passages declares, “God was in Christ reconciling the world unto himself, not
imputing their trespasses unto them.” Non-imputation constitutes an actual judicial relation, so that every
person included within a universal reading of world must also receive the judicial consequence asserted of
that world. The extent cannot be enlarged while this operation is left behind. (2 Corinthians 5:19)
    [724] A universal redemption cannot be established from this passage without disregarding the
account which surrounds and explains it. First, those designated the “world,” are also designated “us” and
identified in the apostle’s own account. “He has reconciled us to himself by Jesus Christ”. Christ is said to
have been “made sin for them,” and they are said to be “made the righteousness of God in him.” These
predicates do not belong to every person in the world. If the antecedent and subsequent statements may
interpret the expression they directly expound, its persons are elect believers. Second, God’s
reconciliation of this world either consists in or necessarily carries the non-imputation of its sins, further
explained by the imputation of Christ’s righteousness. These constitute the blessedness of justification in
Christ. The reconciled world is consequently a blessed and justified world, whereas the greater part of
mankind lies in evil. Third, the reconciliation described is effectual, and therefore cannot be indefinitely
suspended between an absolute and a conditional operation. If absolute, its alleged universal recipients
must all be actually reconciled, pardoned, and justified. If conditional, four difficulties follow. First, an
actual reconciliation cannot simply be exchanged for one awaiting a condition. Second, the text mentions
no such condition. Third, if faith is proposed, the alternatives are “God was in Christ, reconciling a
believing world unto himself,” which makes the operation unnecessary because believers are reconciled,
or “God was in Christ reconciling an unbelieving world unto himself, upon condition that it do believe;”
which amounts to requiring it to cease being unbelieving, and thus to be reconciled, as the condition of
reconciliation. Such a construction cannot express the Holy Spirit’s meaning. Fourth, the non-imputation
must concern either all sins or some. The latter makes Christ a Savior from only some sins. The former
includes unbelief, unless unbelief is no sin, and would therefore save every person whose unbelief is
pardoned. The world is accordingly the world of blessed, pardoned believers, “made the righteousness of
God in Christ.” Its identification does not depend upon awaiting a later restriction — reconciliation and

non-imputation already constitute the saving relations which determine it. (2 Corinthians 5:18–19, 21;
Romans 4:6–7)
    [725] More’s elaborate defense of the contrary interpretation yields little beyond a distinction which
this account itself affirms. He discovers two reconciliations in the passage — God’s reconciliation to us
by Christ and our reconciliation to God by the Spirit. These are distinguishable operations within one
complete reconciliation, whose former branch governs the latter. Every person to whom God is reconciled
in and through Christ will certainly be reconciled to God by the Spirit, since God’s reconciliation consists
in the non-imputation of their sins and their reconciliation consists in receiving that non-imputation in
Jesus Christ. The former is also the principal motive of the latter — it supplies the matter of the gospel
message through which the latter is effected. The distinction, therefore, establishes the restriction of world
to the elect instead of dissolving it. The actual saving predicates decide the extent. An insistence upon the
bare word cannot explain how persons whose trespasses remain imputed belong to the world of which
non-imputation is asserted.
    [726] More next appeals to the surrounding description, “For,” says he, “those of the world here are
called ‘men’; men that must ‘appear before the judgment-seat of Christ’; that were ‘dead’; that ought to
live unto Christ: therefore, all men.” The missing premise is exposed by the question, “homini homo quid
interest?” Such reasoning would permit any desired conclusion unless it first established that the elect are
not men, that they will not appear before Christ’s judgment-seat, that they were never dead, or that they
ought not to live to Christ. Until one of these is proved, the descriptions remain entirely consistent with
the persons already identified. The operations surrounding reconciliation refine its meaning through their
connection with it. They cannot be isolated from that operation and then employed to substitute a
different population. (2 Corinthians 5:10–11, 14–15)
    [727] The further claims fail at the same point. First, “Of these, some are reconciled to God.” The text
supplies no restriction to a reconciled subset of the persons under consideration. Reconciliation is
extended to them all. Second, “But some are not reconciled.” No statement in the passage supports this
division, nor can any plausible inference obtain it from the words. “Many corrupt the word of God.” A
distinction between the persons reconciled and those appointed to minister reconciliation concerns their
relation and office. Difference of office does not divide the saving object of the reconciliation already
asserted. (2 Corinthians 5:11, 18)
    [728] The second passage is “That was the true Light, which lights every man that comes into the
world.” More argues, “This world,” says he, “is the world of mankind, made by Christ; which was his
own by creation, mercy, and purchase, yet ‘received him not’: therefore, it is manifest that there is life,
and that Christ died for all.” The conclusion first requires an identification of the light attributed to Christ,
since creaturely understanding and saving renewal possess distinct causal contents. (John 1:3–5, 9–11)
    [729] The world into which one comes here designates the habitable earth. Coming into it ordinarily
signifies birth and the beginning of breathing the common air, rather than a class of men, whether all or
some. Within the apostle’s discourse, however, the more consonant construction connects ἐρχόμενον,
“coming,” with φῶς, “light,” instead of ἄνθρωπον, “man,” to which it is commonly attached. The
resulting reading is “That was the true Light, which, coming into the world, lights every man.”
Comparable expressions confirm it, “Light is come into the world;” and “I am come a light into the
world.” On either construction, world supplies no proof of universal grace or ransom. The entire burden
falls upon “every man,” an expression which More does not examine. Whoever undertakes that
examination must retain the actual illumination predicated, whose subject can comprise only those who
are indeed illuminated. The place entered and the recipients of a benefit are different objects within the
statement, and confusing their referents supplies no evidence concerning the benefit’s extent. (John 3:19;
12:46)
    [730] Christ’s coming into the world is thus the coming of the sole true light and fountain of
illumination, from whom every person possessing light receives it. He enlightens everyone who is

enlightened. The text asserts this, and the assertion is undisputed. Whether each person before and after
the incarnation actually receives the knowledge of Christ through that coming is another proposition, to
be judged by Scripture, experience, reason, and sense. The passage therefore cannot sustain universal
redemption. This conclusion leaves unopposed the further interpretation which takes the illumination to
be reason and understanding communicated to all, and regards Christ in his divine nature as the light of
all and the eternal wisdom of his Father. A source universally responsible for one capacity does not
thereby produce every spiritual benefit universally. The effect named in the proposition must retain its
identity.
    [731] The third passage announces, “Behold the Lamb of God, which takes away the sin of the
world;” More takes this world to be universal. Yet taking sin away asserts an operation of the sacrifice
upon the persons delivered from it. The removal itself supplies the consequence by which the alleged
extension of the persons must be tested. (John 1:29)
    [732] Two considerations answer this inference. First, even a reference to the world generally would
not establish the inclusion of each individual. Second, Christ truly is ὁ αἴρων: he takes away, bears,
purges, and pardons, as the term is used. Justification removes sin’s condemnation, sanctification its
reign, and glorification its existence. The object is τὴν ἁμαρτίαν, “the sin,” great sin and original sin, τοῦ
κόσμου, “of the world,” common to all. Yet neither the text nor the truth of the matter permits the
assertion that he bears, pardons, or purges that sin for every individual in the world. The collective
designation does not settle individual extension independently of the causal content belonging to the
removal. (2 Samuel 24:10)
    [733] The next passage declares, “God sent not his Son into the world to condemn the world, but that
the world through him might be saved.” The sending is ordered through its purpose clause to a saving
issue. World as the place entered and world as the object saved must therefore be interpreted according to
the relations in which each occurs. (John 3:17)
    [734] The repeated world undergoes the ἀντανάκλασις already observed, an eminent inversion of
reference comparable to the sequence “He was in the world,” meaning upon earth, “and the world was
made by him,” meaning the whole creation and its contents, “and the world knew him not,” meaning the
greater part of mankind. Here also the first occurrence assigns the name of the whole to the part in which
the Savior lived. The second may, if desired, include every individual, although the text does not compel
this, since his principal end was to save his own, rather than condemn any, and still less to condemn all
out of whom his elect were to be saved. The third designates those whom God expressly purposed to save
by sending his Son. Four considerations establish that these are believers among Jews and Gentiles
throughout the world. First, all are not saved, whereas God declares that “he will do all his pleasure, and
his purpose shall stand.” Second, many were already actually condemned when the Son was sent. Their
salvation cannot have constituted the purpose of his sending. Third, Christ was appointed for the fall of
some, an appointment incompatible with an intention to save every individual. Fourth, his historical
exhibition in the flesh cannot oppose the eternal decrees concerning the condemnation of some for their
sins. God neither acts against his own purpose nor fails in its execution. The saved world is his people
dispersed throughout the world. Its identification requires that each occurrence of the repeated term be
followed through its own object and operation. (John 1:10; Luke 2:34)
    [735] More also adduces the passages already examined which name Christ the “Savior of the world.”
Savior names an office, and the operation of that office determines the force of the designation. Universal
significance may concern its sole source, its worldwide exercise, or its actual recipients. Those are
different propositions, each requiring its own grounds. (John 4:42; 6:51; 1 John 4:14)
    [736] The title permits two proposed senses. Christ is Savior of the world because no one in the world
has another Savior, and because he saves every person who is saved, the people of God throughout the
earth beyond the Jews alone. Or he bears the title because he actually saves the entire world and each
person within it. Upon the latter interpretation, vicisti, Mr More. Upon the former, μένομεν ὥσπερ ἐσμέν,

— “we are still where we were.” The interpretation must declare what the office effects for its particular
recipients before any universal saving result can be inferred.
    [737] The appeal to “I am come a light into the world,” contributes so little to the present question that
acknowledgment of its occurrence suffices. The other cited passages have received their examination
already. Further texts are accumulated through such strained applications and such complete irrelevance
to the disputed conclusion that repeating them would consume the reader’s patience without advancing
the argument. Light must be understood through the operation actually asserted of it. An inference to
redemption requires the connection of that operation with the saving predicate in dispute. Mere repetition
of the designation supplies no such connection. (John 12:46; 3:1, 16–17; 2:1–2)
   [738] The first principal argument has therefore received its answer through the examination of the
passages upon which it depends. Their alleged force proceeds from the ambiguity of one word. The
Christian reader’s duty remains, “Prove all things, and hold fast that which is good.” Across these
passages the distinctions are consistent, but each conclusion rests upon its own domain and the causal
content of its own assertion.
    [739] The second principal argument appeals to passages naming all men or every man in connection
with redemption. Their advocates commonly treat the bare expressions as a victory already obtained,
surrounding them with assertions rather than establishing their interpretation. Since the appeal claims the
express words of Scripture, no separate formal construction of the argument is needed. Its force can be
determined by examining the passages themselves together with the strongest defenses of the universal
reading. The principal texts follow in the terminal references. What world required in the preceding
discussion, all requires here — a domain determined within the passage and a predicate preserved
throughout the inference. Universality of expression cannot detach either relation from the assertion it
serves. (1 Timothy 2:4, 6; 2 Peter 3:9; Hebrews 2:9; 2 Corinthians 5:14–15; 1 Corinthians 15:22; Romans
5:18)
    [740] The scriptural uses of all have already received extensive examination, both in the preceding
argument and in the work of others. Those instances establish that the word alone supplies no inference to
the inclusion of every individual. The present task concerns the actual scope of the particular passages
advanced, and the objections drawn from them. Possibility of a restricted domain prepares that
examination without determining each occurrence in advance.
    [741] The first and principal passage states, “God will have all men to be saved, and come to the
knowledge of the truth … Christ gave himself a ransom for all, to be testified in due time.” The argument
drawn from it, Rem. Act. Synod, is “If God will have all men to be saved, then Christ died for all; but God
will have all men to be saved, and come to the knowledge of the truth: therefore, Christ died for all men.”
Saving will, knowledge of the truth, and ransom belong to the connected assertion. The identity of their
predicates and recipients must survive its interpretation. (1 Timothy 2:4, 6)
    [742] The argument turns upon an ambiguity. All possesses several uses, and its sense is determined
by the matter asserted and by the persons of whom it is asserted. According to the sense imposed, the
argument may be granted in full or its particular propositions denied. Scripture supplies nearly five
hundred instances in which all or all men does not comprise every person who has lived or will live.
Taken distributively for some of every sort, the conclusion is granted. Taken collectively for every
member of every sort, the minor premise is denied, since God does not will the salvation of them all. Two
questions establish the grounds of that denial and the meaning of the Holy Spirit here — first, what divine
will is predicated in willing their salvation. Second, who constitute the all of whom the apostle speaks.
The ambiguity identifies the work required, while the context supplies the persons within the quantified
class.
   [743] Divine will is commonly distinguished as intending and commanding, though more exactly the
same expression refers to two relations — God’s purpose concerning what he will do, and his approval of

what we do together with his command of it. The opposing interpretation must specify which relation
constitutes his willing the salvation of all. Command, approval, and effectual purpose have distinct
consequences. A consequence belonging to one cannot be transferred to another without its proper
premise.
    [744] Upon the first interpretation, God wills salvation “voluntate signi,” by commanding, requiring,
and approving. The sense would then be “God commands all men to use the means whereby they may
obtain the end, or salvation, the performance whereof is acceptable to God in any or all;” corresponding
to the declaration “God commands all men everywhere to repent.” Those all can then comprise no more
than the persons to whom the means of grace are granted and revealed, many indeed, yet historically not a
hundredth part of Adam’s posterity. Moreover, upon this account the consequence in the argument’s first
proposition is denied — Christ need not have died for every person whom God thus commands to seek
salvation. The command rests upon God’s decreed connection of faith with salvation, rather than upon a
particular death for every commanded person. Christ’s death is abundantly sufficient for declaring that
connection to all, since it contains enough to save all believers. Obligation and approved conduct are what
the interpretation establishes. The command does not effect or entail its universal performance.
     [745] The second interpretation takes divine will as the efficacious purpose and good pleasure of God,
and the context strongly establishes this sense. His will is made the ground of supplication, so that the
prayer answers “Your will be done,” in the salvation of those intended. The promise grants “whatsoever
we ask according to his will”, and the will furnishing this ground must therefore be the will which is
accomplished. Such a purpose necessarily secures the salvation of everyone whom God wills to save —
what he both can and will do certainly occurs. Apart from consideration of his decree, his ability to save
all is undisputed. The passage affirms his will to save all. If those all comprise each individual, each
individual must consequently be saved. “Let us eat and drink, for tomorrow we shall die.” “Who has
resisted God’s will?”. “He has done whatsoever he has pleased.” “He does according to his will in the
army of heaven, and among the inhabitants of the earth.” A universal population therefore entails either
universal salvation or the failure of divine purpose. The effectual purpose cannot be retained while its
appointed issue is denied. The remaining question is accordingly the identity of the all men to whom this
saving will belongs, a question to be answered together with the result which that will secures. (1 John
3:22; 5:14; Romans 9:19; Psalms 115:3; Daniel 4:35)
    [746] All men here comprises persons of every sort living under the gospel, in the latter times of the
enlarged dispensation of grace. Arminius himself, in his treatment of this passage against Perkins,
acknowledges its reference to men of these times. The apostle’s argument concerns the enlargement of
grace in its outward administration, and upon that enlargement establishes prayer for all. The means of
grace and the habitation of the church have passed beyond the narrow bounds of a single nation. Peoples,
tongues, and languages now receive them without that former distinction, as do persons of every rank
among those peoples, high and low, rich and poor. The expression therefore serves the apostle’s purpose
of removing external differences between mankind by including all sorts. It does not predicate identical
saving participation of every individual within every sort. The following considerations further confirm
this distribution among kinds and the abolition of the former restriction to one community.
    [747] The first consideration is ordinary scriptural usage. All most commonly signifies many of every
sort, and nothing in the subject asserted here compels a departure from that sense toward a collection of
every individual. The established use should therefore govern unless the passage supplies a reason
otherwise. Thus the Savior cures all diseases and the Pharisees tithe πᾶν λάχανον. Such uses establish the
available meaning, whose application remains subject to the present context. (Luke 11:42)
     [748] The second consideration is Paul’s own distribution of the persons named. Having commanded
prayer for all upon the ground that God wills all to be saved, he identifies ranks and conditions within this
all, expressly naming “kings and all in authority.” The same relation appears in the account
“Nebuchadnezzar carried away all the people captive to Babylon, Jeconiah the king, and the queen, and

the eunuchs, the princes of Judah and Jerusalem, the carpenters, and the smiths;” where the total
expression receives its interpretation through a distribution among orders and occupations, comprising
some of every sort. Paul likewise explains all men through the conditions and offices he names. “Pray for
all men,” therefore includes magistrates and persons in authority as classes among other classes, because
the time has arrived in which God saves some of every nation and condition beyond the former
distinctions. The enumeration belongs internally to the domain of the exhortation. Its bearing upon prayer
and divine purpose cannot be removed while interpreting either. (Jeremiah 29:1–2)
   [749] The third ground concerns the obligation of prayer. The persons whose salvation God wills fall
within the scope of our duty to pray. Yet there are persons whom Scripture expressly excepts, for it makes
known that some are reprobate and sin unto death, and warns us against praying for such. This exception
belongs to the revealed rule under which prayer is exercised. Its force does not depend on the minister
possessing exhaustive knowledge of the persons comprised in the hidden purpose.
    [750] The fourth ground is the efficacy of the will whose extent is disputed. Everyone whom God
wills to save will be saved, for “who has resisted his will?” Yet the sentence of judgment places some on
the left hand, and the salvation of every person is consequently excluded. Once the will asserted in the
passage is understood according to its saving operation, the question of its extent must be settled in
accordance with that operation. The universality of mankind cannot be its object.
    [751] The fifth ground lies in the common extent of the ends which the passage joins. God wills no
more persons to be “saved” than he wills to “come to the knowledge of the truth.” Yet God has not willed
that every individual in every age should receive this knowledge. Formerly “he showed his word unto
Jacob, his statutes and his judgments unto Israel. He has not dealt so with any nation: and as for his
judgments, they have not known them.” The knowledge in question depends on the word by which the
truth is communicated. If God willed the knowledge in every person, why did he give its appointed means
to some while withholding it from others? “He suffered all nations” in former ages “to walk in their own
ways”, and “winked at the time of this ignorance”, concealing the mystery of salvation from those
generations. The same dispensation has continued with respect to some, because “so it seems good in his
sight.” The historical distribution of the word thus makes visible a distinction already present in the
saving purpose which appoints it. God wills the knowledge of the truth for persons of all sorts without
distinction, and it is these same persons whom he wills to save. A reading which extends either end to
every person in all ages divides what the passage joins or posits the end wherever its appointed means has
been withheld. (Psalms 147:19–20; Acts 14:16; 17:30; Colossians 1:26; Matthew 11:25–26)
     [752] The purpose which brings a person to knowledge of the truth also provides the means by which
the truth reaches that person and is received. Historical provision therefore has an internal relation to the
end purposed. The necessity of hearing for the operation here described does not make hearing its
sufficient efficient cause, since the truth can reach the ear without producing faith in the hearer.
                   [753] Fx⇒Hx,        Hx⇏Fx,       Secure(d,Fx)⇒Provide(d,Hx). (55)
     [754] Terms. H is the hearing or receipt of the saving content relevant to the case, and F is saving
faith. The final implication takes as its premise the established relation between the purpose and its effect,
which the purpose secures through the necessary means it appoints. That relation imposes no independent
limit on divine power.
    [755] Consequence. The absence of a necessary means excludes the effect within the operation here
described. The presence of the means does not by itself establish the effect. The saving undertaking
consequently provides for the communication of the word and for the efficient renewal by which the
recipient believes it. Their union in one undertaking gives neither provision the operation proper to the
other.
    [756] The grounds which establish that God wills the salvation of persons of all sorts determine the
extent of the same expression when Christ gives himself “a ransom for all;” and the nature of the ransom

confirms their conclusion. The equity already established requires that everyone for whom a ransom is
paid should participate in its benefit and, once it is accepted as sufficient, be set at liberty. Payment and
acceptance are the acts by which the exchange is effected and the persons for whom it is made obtain
their freedom. The all whom the passage assigns to Christ are therefore his ransomed people, who by
virtue of the price of his blood are brought into the glorious liberty of the children of God. The express
account of their redemption from every sort of people interprets this passage, and the denial that every
individual is thus set free is common ground. The persons intended are identified through the saving will

and the ransom by which its end is accomplished, so that the extent of the word follows from the very
operations the passage predicates. (1 Timothy 2:4, 6; Revelation 5:9)
    [757] The objection gains its apparent force by moving between two extensions of the word all. If all
men are the persons the text designates, namely persons of all sorts, the proposition that Christ died for all
is granted entire. If all men are every individual without exception, the minor premise is denied, since the
preceding grounds establish that no such population is designated in the text. The concession under the
first meaning cannot support a conclusion which presupposes the second.
    [758] Thomas More devotes an entire chapter of his “Universality of Free Grace,” to developing the
objection from this passage. It supplies one of the two foundations of his whole argument and is the place
to which he returns when his other supports fail. The size of the chapter cannot give its construction a
premise which the passage withholds. Three considerations make a separate examination of the chapter
unnecessary.
    [759] First, a full answer already exists, and repeating it would be actum agere in a controversy which
scarcely warrants the duplication. Samuel Rutherford’s work on the death of Christ and the drawing of
sinners by it appeared while Owen was treating this point and answered the whole of More’s chapter. The
reader can consult that demonstration for the disputed proposition, whose terms retain the senses already
established here. (Samuel Rutherford, Christ Dying and Drawing to Himself, 375–410)
    [760] Second, More does not undertake to answer any of the reasons which establish that all men in
the passage are men of all sorts. Those reasons concern the actual relations of prayer, saving purpose, and
redemption to the persons designated. An objection which leaves those relations unexamined leaves the
inference they support intact.
    [761] Third, apart from More’s unsupported assertions about the meaning of the passage, his
argument proceeds everywhere by one fallacy. He establishes, at most, that the persons for whom we
ought to pray include more than those who are already believers. No one of sound judgment will deny
this. It requires no less a failure of judgment to conclude that the removal of this restriction includes every
individual of mankind. The enlargement needs its own warrant, and the saving relations already examined
supply a different one. The next passage urged for a universal ransom from the word all turns on the same
requirement.
     [762] The second passage declares, “The Lord is long-suffering to us-ward, not willing that any
should perish, but that all should come to repentance.” “The will of God,” say some, “for the salvation of
all, is here set down both negatively, that he would not have any perish, and positively, that he would have
all come to repentance; now, seeing there is no coming to repentance nor escaping destruction, but only
by the blood of Christ, it is manifest that that blood was shed for all.” The objection correctly joins
repentance and deliverance to the blood of Christ, but it can extend the blood to every person only if the
patience here described has every person for its saving object. That patience has its own temporal
operation, and the persons toward whom it is exercised are named in the assertion itself. (2 Peter 3:9)
    [763] An indefinite expression takes its extent from the persons or things of which it is predicated, and
the corruption of the apostle’s words is plain once this rule is applied. “The Lord,” says he, “is long-
suffering to us-ward, not willing that any should perish.” Ordinary judgment supplies the subject of the
succeeding clauses from the one expressly given, yielding “Not willing that any of us should perish, but

that all of us should come to repentance?” The apostle writes to persons who have received “great and
precious promises”, whom he calls “beloved” and sets in opposition to the “scoffers” of the “last days”.
They are those for whose sake the Lord orders these days and who are said to be “elect.” To infer from
God’s will that none of these perish and that all these repent an identical will for every person in the
world comes little short of madness, particularly when the proposed enlargement includes those to whom
God never discloses his saving will or addresses a call to repentance, and who never hear the way of
salvation. Nor is this inference assisted by the possibility that some actual readers of the epistle were not
elect. Peter addresses them as such under the judgment of charity, urging them “to give all diligence to
make their calling and election sure”. His former epistle likewise addresses its recipients as “elect”, a
“chosen generation,” and a “purchased people.” The contrary construction imposes contradictions of its
own. It makes God will the repentance of persons whom he cuts off in infancy outside the covenant,
whom he has hated from eternity, from whom he conceals the means of grace, and to whom he withholds
repentance while knowing that they cannot possess it except by his gift. The elect are consequently the
persons, all and only, whom he does not will to perish in this passage. The passage in Ezekiel alleged to
be parallel will receive separate consideration. (2 Peter 1:4, 10; 3:1, 3; Matthew 24:22; 1 Peter 1:2; 2:9;
Ezekiel 18:23, 32)
   [764] The third passage says, “That he by the grace of God should taste death for every man.” The
phrase every man applies distributively to a class. The account of the persons who receive the benefit of
Christ’s death must therefore settle the extent of that class. (Hebrews 2:9)
    [765] The expression ὑπὲρ παντός, “for every one,” is acknowledged by both sides to stand for ὑπὲρ
πάντων, “for all,” through a substitution of grammatical number. No decision concerning the persons
intended is contained in that substitution. The question remains whether all designates every individual or
every one of the persons with whom the apostle is concerned. Scripture regularly uses every man within
an already constituted class. In “Warning every man, and teaching every man” the apostle is speaking of
the persons to whom he preached the gospel, and warning and teaching reach each person within that
relation. “The manifestation of the Spirit is given to every man to profit withal” likewise concerns every
recipient of the gifts under discussion, whether at Corinth or elsewhere. It is possession of the gift which
determines the class throughout which the expression distributes the benefit. Owen repeatedly
encountered the passage in Hebrews adduced in favor of universal redemption without finding an attempt,
from the text or from any other ground, to prove the proposed extent. This silence is especially
remarkable because the proponents cannot be unaware that the ordinary use of the expression is against
them. More spends a whole chapter on the passage, and even a close search for anything that might tend
to establish that the apostle intended every individual discovers no such undertaking. His abundant
fluency leaves the disputed proposition exactly where it was. He begs for the concession he was bound to
earn, and however earnest the petition, the operative relation asserted of those for whom Christ tasted
death supplies three reasons to refuse it. (Colossians 1:28; 1 Corinthians 12:7)
    [766] First, to taste death for sinners is to drink the cup due to them in their stead. The Savior left no
drop for those whose cup he took, since the very purpose of his undergoing death was that it should pass
from them although it did not pass from him. For every person whose death he tasted he swallowed it up
into victory. Yet the cup passes only from the elect, who are believers. A universal extension of the act
would therefore require a universal extension of the liberty it procures.
    [767] Second, the apostle has a definite reason for designating the persons for whom Christ died as all.
He writes to Hebrews among whom the belief had taken deep root that the benefits purchased by Messiah
belonged exclusively to their own nation. That pernicious restriction had to be removed by declaring the
extent of free grace under the gospel, which gathers the whole of God’s elect throughout the world. The
breadth of the expression answers the national exclusion which occasioned it.
    [768] Third, the designations actually given to those for whom Christ tastes death apply only to the
elect of God. They are “many sons to be brought to glory”, the “sanctified,” his “brethren”, the “children

that God gave him”, and those “delivered from the bondage of death;” whereas others are born, live, and
die the “children of the wicked one.” Christ exercises the office of captain of salvation for those who
“obey him”, and righteousness comes through him “unto all and upon all them that believe.” These
descriptions give the persons their place within the saving operation of the passage. The universality
claimed by the objection would require that the persons whom those predicates expressly exclude
nevertheless belong to the class constituted by them. The petition must therefore be refused. Every one
means all and only the elect, in whose stead Christ by the grace of God tasted death. (Hebrews 2:10–11,
13, 15; 5:9; Romans 3:22)
     [769] The fourth passage joins the love by which the apostle is constrained to the death and risen life
of Christ. “For the love of Christ constrains us; because we thus judge, that if one died for all, then were
all dead: and that he died for all, that they which live should not henceforth live unto themselves, but unto
him that died for them.” The contrary argument runs, “Here,” say they, “, you have two alls, which must
be both of an equal extent. If all were dead, then Christ died for all, — that is, for as many as were dead.
Again; he died for all that must live unto him; but that is the duty of every one in the world: and therefore
he died for them all. Farther; that all are all individuals is clear from the cited passage, where they are
affirmed to be all that must ‘appear before the judgment-seat of Christ;’ from which appearance not any
shall be exempted.” Each inference depends on the asserted connection between death, resurrection, and
life unto Christ retaining the same subjects as the objection moves between them. (2 Corinthians 5:10,
14–15)
     [770] The answer distinguishes three claims. First, even under the understanding of death adopted by
some of the opponents, the apostle’s argument gives no ground for assigning the proposed equal extent to
the two occurrences of all. “If he died for all, then were all dead,” establishes that the persons for whom
Christ died were all dead in the sense at issue. The proposition therefore takes the extent of the latter class
from that of the former. To recover from it the claim that Christ died for every dead person is to reverse
its direction and use as the determining term what the text expressly determines by the other. Second, we
deny that every individual is bound to live unto Christ virtute præcepti. That command concerns those to
whom he is revealed and, in the proper sense of the passage, those who live by him and have a spiritual
life in and with him. Others remain under prior obligations. Third, every person must indeed appear
before Christ’s judgment seat, since he is appointed judge of the world. The apostle nevertheless speaks
here of us all, that is, believers and particularly preachers of the gospel, and neither description belongs to
every person. None of the three claims extends the passage beyond elect believers. Its positive account of
their relation to Christ supplies the following grounds for that conclusion. (2 Corinthians 5:10, 14)
    [771] First, the apostle joins Christ’s resurrection to his death for the same persons. “He died for them,
and rose again.” Whoever receives the saving benefit of his rising receives it for “justification”, and must
accordingly be justified. The opponents themselves have always acknowledged that the fruits of the
resurrection belong to believers alone. The personal unity of death and resurrection here is therefore the
same unity established in paragraphs 91–113. (Romans 4:25; 8:34)
    [772] Second, the persons treated are those who by virtue of Christ’s death “live unto him”, are “new
creatures”, are persons “to whom the Lord imputes not their trespasses”, and “become the righteousness
of God in Christ”. These effects constitute the life described and belong only to believers. A class wider
than the believers cannot be constituted by operations whose effects its additional members never receive.
(2 Corinthians 5:15, 17, 19, 21)
    [773] Third, the article οἱ joined with πάντες expressly qualifies the extent of all. “Then were they all”
(or rather all these) “dead.” The grammatical force of these directs the assertion to the persons already
under consideration. The preceding descriptions identify them as all those believers of whom the apostle
treats.
   [774] Fourth, the apostle infers the death of all the persons under consideration from Christ’s death for
them. “If one died for all, then were all dead.” The death inferred is spiritual rather than natural, and

within the spiritual order it is death unto sin rather than death in sin. Two grounds establish this. Vorstius
and Grotius, among the principal defenders of the Arminian cause, acknowledged the force of the
evidence and expressly interpreted the passage as death unto sin by virtue of Christ’s death. The text itself
establishes why this reading is necessary. The persons for whom Christ died are so dead unto sin that they
should no longer live to it but to the one who died for them. The fuller treatment of the same relation
describes believers as “dead unto sin,” through being “planted together in the likeness of the death of
Christ;” and therefore calls them to “newness of life.” The conditional “If Christ died for all, then were all
dead,” accordingly concerns the death unto sin of the persons for whom Christ died, at least of those
whom the apostle is here treating. It affords no proof of a general ransom, since death in sin, however
widely it extends, cannot be substituted for the death which the apostle actually infers from Christ’s death.
(Romans 6:5–8)
    [775] Fifth, the death of Christ is treated here under its application. Its efficacy in the persons for
whom he died is precisely what causes them to live unto him. No defender of the opposing position has
yet affirmed that Christ died for every person in respect of application. Such an affirmation would require
every person to live unto him and with him forever, so long as the oblation retains any efficacy for the
end to which it is applied. The only persons here said to receive Christ’s death are therefore those who are
dead unto sin and live to him. Mere availability cannot stand in for the operation that produces this life.
    [776] The fifth passage urged from the word all is “For as in Adam all die, even so in Christ shall all
be made alive.” The two assertions place their subjects under distinct representative heads. The
comparison consequently requires the relation expressed by in Adam and in Christ to remain operative in
determining the scope of each all. (1 Corinthians 15:22)
     [777] The ordinary exposition suffices here, since the passage to be examined next contains the full
strength of the argument usually drawn from this comparison. The all of this verse are the persons whom
Paul treats throughout the chapter, members implanted into Christ their head and receiving through his
resurrection a glorious resurrection of their own. The grounds of Paul’s argument disclose its subject. The
resurrection of Christ, the faith and hope of Christians, their customs, and the rewards they expect carry
unconquerable force for the confirmation of believers in the resurrection. To address those same grounds
to the world as a proof of the general resurrection would render every one of them ridiculous, since the
persons addressed would have no share in what makes them persuasive. The word ζωοποιηθήσονται itself
denotes a rising to blessed life and glory. It cannot denote the awakening of persons raised to a second
death. The Son is said ζωοποιεῖν, to “quicken” and make alive “whom he will.” The same word occurs in
“It is the Spirit, τὸ ζωοποιοῦν, that” (thus) “quickens;” and nowhere designates the common resurrection
at the last day. Those made alive by virtue of Christ’s resurrection are therefore those who participate in
his nature, expressly designated “they that are Christ’s,” for whom he is the “first-fruits;” for he is
certainly no firstfruits of the damned. It is true that every person died in Adam, but the apostle here
speaks of believers, and even if the first all were conceded to be universal, the express limitation of the
second clause would prevent the proposed inference. If both occurrences were granted the universality
demanded, the argument would still lack a middle term connecting the comparison to a general ransom.
The kind of life and death asserted, and the representative relation under which each is received, remain
decisive. (John 5:21; 6:63; Romans 4:17; 1 Corinthians 15:20, 23)
    [778] The sixth and final passage in this class is the one on which some place the greatest weight. “As
by the offense of one judgment came upon all men to condemnation; even so by the righteousness of one
the free gift came upon all men unto justification of life.” The latter all men are the persons upon whom
the free gift actually comes unto justification. They “receive abundance of grace and of the gift of
righteousness,” thereby “reign in life by one, Jesus Christ;” and are “made righteous” through his
obedience. Nothing is more certain in the truth of God than that these predicates do not belong to every
person. Some do not believe, since “all men have not faith;” and on some “the wrath of God abides”.
Grace does not reign in them through righteousness unto eternal life by Christ, whereas it reigns in all
upon whom the free gift comes to justification. That account is sufficient to answer the objection. A fuller

consideration is nevertheless warranted because the opponents have exerted unusual effort and displayed
unusual confidence in pressing the passage into the service of the error. The apostle compares the efficacy
of Adam’s sin to condemn his natural seed, who belong to him by propagation, with the efficacy of
Christ’s righteousness to justify and give life to his spiritual seed, who belong to him by regeneration. The
comparison of the judicial effects must therefore preserve the respective relations through which the acts
of the two heads reach those they represent. (Romans 5:17–19; John 3:36)
    [779] Adam is called τύπος, the type and “figure of him that was to come;” because his standing, his
act, and its consequences bear a resemblance to Christ’s, although Adam was never instituted solely to
serve as a type. The resemblance enables the apostle to set forth the obedience of Christ and the efficacy
of his death through their opposition to Adam’s act. In developing it he also specifies differences, every
one of which exalts Christ above his figure. The point of comparison is a like efficacy, without equality of
merit or power, for there is more required to save one than to lose ten thousand. Adam’s transgression
brings the guilt of condemnation upon every person in whose place he stood publicly. He is their natural
head and fountain, and by divine institution they are all comprised in the condition which his
disobedience incurs. Christ’s righteousness, obedience, and death effect absolution, justification, and
salvation for every person whose spiritual head he is by divine institution and in whose place he appears
publicly. The several particulars by which the apostle establishes this correspondence contain no
suggestion that the second population comprises every member of the first. The comparison is intensive,
since it concerns how effectually each head’s act reaches those he represents. The extent of each
population is determined by its own representative relation. Adam’s all may therefore be called his many
and Christ’s many his all, for they are the whole seed given to him. (Romans 5:14)
    [780] Melito’s account of humanity under sin and death identifies the condition to which the exchange
described in Diognetus answers. These accounts supply material for distinguishing the two representative
relations, but representation as such cannot determine who belongs to either head. The apostle establishes
that each act has efficacy throughout the population constituted under its respective head. Numerical
identity of those populations would require a further premise and cannot take the place of the
correspondence which his argument establishes. Bring the two headships back to the wheel of paragraph
0. The question is who stands under each head, and the comparison must follow the persons constituted
under each before their numbers can be compared. (Melito of Sardis, On Pascha 50–56; Epistle to
Diognetus 9; Romans 5:12–19)
    [781] Thomas More makes the apostle’s comparison of Adam and Christ one of the principal
foundations of universal redemption in his “Universality of Free Grace”. After mixing truths and errors in
a preliminary discussion which need not detain us, he proposes four particulars in which the comparison
consists. Their force depends upon what the respective public offices have in common, and neither office
by itself establishes the identity of the persons represented. (Thomas More, The Universality of God’s
Free Grace 8, 41)
    [782] His first particular is “That Adam, in his first sin and transgression, was a public person, in the
room and place of all mankind, by virtue of the covenant between God and him; so that whatever he did
therein, all were alike sharers with him. So also was Christ a public person in his obedience and death, in
the room and place of all mankind, represented by him, even every one of the posterity of Adam.”
Adam’s natural relation to his posterity identifies the persons who participate in his act. The
corresponding participation in Christ requires its own saving relation and cannot be derived from natural
descent from Adam.
    [783] Adam stood as a public person for all who were to proceed from him by natural propagation,
and he prefigured Christ insofar as Christ also stood publicly in the place of his own. But the alleged
extension of Christ’s representative obedience, death, and sacrifice to every person in every age is
monstrous. It places among those in whose stead he acted the reprobate whom God hated from eternity,
persons whom Christ never knew, those for whom he refused to pray, and those concerning whom he

thanked his Father, in the days of his flesh, for concealing the mysteries of salvation. Most of the persons
thus included were already damned in hell and irrevocably beyond redemption before he actually
rendered his obedience. The claim entails that persons may perish in whose stead the Son of God
appeared before his Father with perfect obedience. It makes him mediator and advocate, king, priest, and
prophet, sponsor, surety, and undertaker for persons who can nevertheless be taken from him and plucked
out of his arms through a refusal of his satisfaction and advocacy on their behalf. Such a doctrine can
scarcely be owned by those who seek to preserve the testimony of the Lord Jesus. The correspondence of
the public offices cannot turn the natural relation which constitutes Adam’s posterity into the saving
relation which constitutes Christ’s people.
    [784] More’s grounds for the assertion, as they can be gathered from page 44, are seven. First, Christ
did not stand only for the elect because Adam never lost election, with which he had never been entrusted.
Second, Christ would fall short of his figure if he did not stand for all. Third, he is said to restore all
whom Adam lost. Fourth, he took flesh, was subject to mortality, came under the law, and bore the sins of
mankind. Fifth, he did these things in the place of all mankind, once given to him. Sixth, he is called the
“last Adam;” and seventh, he is said to have been a public person in the place of all ever since the “first
Adam”. These grounds must establish that common extent which the comparison is alleged to prove. The
assertion of an identical population cannot be its own ground. (Hebrews 2:9; Romans 14:9; 5; Philippians
2:8–11; 1 Corinthians 15:45, 47; 1 Timothy 2:5)
    [785] Few conclusions so rotten have rested upon grounds so loose, and the word of God has never
been more boldly corrupted to defend an error since the Christian name was known. The hay and stubble
are nevertheless easily removed. First, Adam did not lose election, for the eternal decrees of the Almighty
are not committed to the keeping of men. But in Adam all the elect were lost, and Christ came to seek and
find these very persons, in whose stead he stood publicly. Second, the comparison nowhere concerns the
extent of the persons for whom Christ died. It concerns the efficacy of his obedience. Third, the assertion
that he came to restore all is false, as the foregoing examination of the passage shows. Fourth, the
assumption of flesh and the other things named were necessary for the salvation of the elect. He took
flesh and blood because the children shared in them. Fifth, nowhere does Scripture affirm that all the sons
of men were given to Christ to redeem, so that he should stand in their place. Christ expressly affirms the
contrary. Some are given to him out of the world, those he saves, and not one of them perishes. The
passages urged affirm nothing of the kind More asserts and will receive further consideration. Sixth, the
designation “last Adam” concerns the efficacy of his death for the justification of the seed promised and
given to him, corresponding to the efficacy of the sin of the “first Adam” in bringing condemnation upon
the seed propagated from him. It gives no ground for including those to whom his death was never known
and never proved profitable. Seventh, his public standing is granted, but neither More’s reasoning nor the
texts alleged, all already considered, establish a public standing in the place of every person. The seven
grounds show the weight of the inferences More habitually draws from premises incapable of sustaining
them. Two further assertions in the discussion deserve notice. He says that Christ by his death brought all
out of the death incurred in Adam. That death is death in sin with the consequent guilt of condemnation.
If all were freed from it, every person would possess the spiritual life obtainable only through Christ. The
gospel has already declared whether life by Christ is the privilege of believers, and God will determine it
openly in due time. More also treats Christ’s presentation of himself alive and just before the Father as
though it were the ultimate end of his death, whereas the Holy Ghost expressly declares that “he loved the
church, and gave himself for it, that he might present it to himself a glorious church.” The comparison
cannot bear a conclusion which requires a representative relation stronger than the one the apostle
establishes. (Hebrews 2:9; John 17:6, 9; Ephesians 2:1–3; 5:25–27)
   [786] The remaining parallels More draws between Adam and Christ do nothing to establish the
proposition for which they are offered, that Christ obeyed as a public person in the place of every person
implicated in Adam’s disobedience. They mingle some truths with several unsavory heresies, and the
presence of truths does nothing to rectify the particular inference. A comparison can instruct where the

offices correspond while furnishing no proof of the identity of the persons whom each office represents.
A few instances will enable the reader to judge the rest. There is no advantage in transcribing every vanity
or imposing the labor of reading it when it contributes nothing to the matter in hand. What gives the
comparison inferential force is the particular feature which the two offices actually share.
     [787] More’s second parallel concerns the execution of what Christ undertook. He affirms, “That
when Christ finished his obedience, in dying and rising, and offering himself a sacrifice, and making
satisfaction, it was, by virtue of the account of God in Christ, and for Christ with God (that is, accepted
with God for Christ’s sake), the death, resurrection, the sacrifice and satisfaction, and the redemption of
all, — that is, all and every one;” and under this description compares Christ with Adam. The
contradictions which follow ought to astonish anyone who still supposes that faith, reason, and reverence
for God impose some restraint upon what a Christian may publish and propagate. The righteous God is
said to receive Christ’s death as the death of all and yet adjudge most of those same persons to eternal
death in their own persons. Every person is said to rise in and with Christ although most remain dead in
their sins and die for sin eternally. God is said to have received satisfaction for persons who are never
spared a farthing of their debt, and to have received atonement by sacrifice for persons who remain
forever under wrath. Cain, Pharaoh, Ahab, and the other reprobate already under death and torment in hell
when Christ died, suffered, made satisfaction, and rose, are thus alleged to have died, suffered, made
satisfaction, and risen with him in the account of God. To press such blasphemous and senseless
contradictions upon Christians without even the appearance of proof, on no better authority than that of a
man who has already embraced them, would be inexplicable were not the judgments of God often hidden
and far beyond our sight. Completed obedience reaches those represented in its performance with the
consequences of the appointed office. The inclusion of persons in whom those consequences are absent is
the very assertion for which More owes a proof. (2 Thessalonians 2:11–12)
    [788] The third parallel advances from the performance of obedience to its efficacy, effect, and fruit.
More affirms, “That as by the sin of Adam all his posterity were deprived of life, and fell under sin and
death, whence judgment and condemnation passed upon all, though this be done secretly and invisibly,
and in some sort inexpressibly”. Calling the operation secret and invisible explains nothing, since there is
no reason to suppose it could have been an object of sense, and the texts that express all this and more
with great fullness can judge of its alleged inexpressibility. He continues, “so,” says he, “by the efficacy
of the obedience of Christ, all men without exception are redeemed, restored, made righteous, justified
freely by the grace of Christ, through the redemption that is in Jesus Christ, the ‘righteousness that is by
the faith of Jesus Christ’ being ‘unto all’,” where he corrupts Scripture as the devil does by cutting off
“and upon all that believe,” although both occurrences of all concern believers. The inevitable
consequence of his assertion is already expressed in the question “What remains now but that all also
should be saved? the Holy Ghost expressly affirming that those ‘whom God justifies, he also glorifies,’ ”.
“Solvite mortales animas, curisque levate.” More would impose the claim without a show of proof. The
restored are on his account still lost, the righteous still detestably wicked and wholly abominable, and the
freely justified still under the law’s condemning sentence. The righteousness of God through faith in
Christ becomes the righteousness of all unbelievers. These propositions contradict the gospel and one
another too directly for his subsequent cautions to reconcile them. There is little profit in raking further in
the dunghill. Those who cannot receive the truth in the love of it may content themselves with husks
obtained by corrupting Scripture and denying common sense. The weakness of the argument that Christ
obeyed unto death as a public person in the place of every individual is already established. The positive
grounds now show that his obedience unto death, the redemption he wrought, the satisfaction he made,
and the sacrifice he offered do not make him the representative of elect and reprobate, believers and
unbelievers alike. The effect of each representative act reaches its own members, and likeness in efficacy
cannot confer membership under the other head. (Romans 5:12; 3:22; 8:30; Matthew 4)
   [789] First, the seed of the woman does not stand publicly in the place of the seed of the serpent. Jesus
Christ is the seed of the woman κατ’ ἐξοχήν· and all the reprobate, as already established, are the seed of

the serpent. In the oblation and suffering by which he broke the head of their father, Christ therefore did
not stand in their place. The operation by which the two communities are distinguished supplies no
ground for making him their common representative.
    [790] Second, the persons Christ represents are those for whose sake he sets himself apart to the office
in which he represents them. His own testimony gives this consecration a determinate object, namely the
persons given him out of the world. Its object does not comprise every individual. The undertaking
consequently does not make him a public person for every individual, since its extent belongs to the
appointment and purpose which constitute it. (John 17:19)
    [791] Third, Christ is a “surety,” in virtue of the public office he exercises, and the suretyship cannot
be extended to every person. The covenant of which he is surety does not take in everyone, for its
conditions, as already shown, are effected in every party to it. Further, no one for whom Christ is surety
can perish unless he proves unable to discharge the debt. The persons and obligations actually assumed
therefore determine the extent of his public representation. (Hebrews 7:22)
    [792] Fourth, Christ suffered in the place of those whom he represented and made satisfaction for
them. Some must nevertheless undergo the suffering themselves, which shows that he did not undergo it
for them, and divine justice requires satisfaction from those same persons to the utmost farthing. The
persons represented in his performance are the persons who receive its judicial effect. A population from
whom the debt is still exacted cannot be made the population for whom he satisfied it. (Isaiah 53:5–6;
Romans 8:33–34)
    [793] Fifth, nothing Christ performed as a public person was vain for anyone whom he represented.
Yet his representative acts could bear no fruit for the greater part of mankind, namely those already under
actual damnation and therefore incapable of receiving any good by what he did. Redemption,
reconciliation, and satisfaction in their case could only be empty names. To include such persons within
his representative undertaking would divide its actual performance from the fruit which the undertaking
secures.
    [794] Each of the two universal propositions is constituted by a relation between a representative head
and the persons who belong to him. What is true of everyone represented by Adam and what is true of
everyone represented by Christ can therefore each be universal throughout its own population. Parallel
quantification establishes no identity between the populations quantified over.
             [795] ∀x∈A D(x),         ∀x∈C J(x),       these do not alone entailA=C. (56)
     [796] Terms. A denotes the population under the specified Adamic relation and C the population
under the specified relation to Christ. D denotes the death or condemnation asserted and J the life or
justification asserted. Their meanings are determined in each case by the passage which predicates them.
     [797] Consequence. The parallel establishes a correspondence of effects, while identity of
membership remains a separate proposition requiring proof. The common word all marks the
completeness of each effect within its own population. Even the equal certainty with which each head’s
act reaches his members supplies no ground for an equality of populations.
    [798] Sixth, the Father’s good pleasure in what his Son performed as a public person necessarily
reaches the persons whom the Son represents, either absolutely or conditionally. Yet the Father was
neither absolutely nor conditionally well pleased in the representation of his Son with Cain, Pharaoh,
Saul, Ahab, and others who were dead and damned before. Christ’s public representation therefore cannot
extend to all. The acceptance of the performance and its promised issue belong together for the very
persons represented in it. (Ephesians 5:2)
    [799] Seventh, the scriptural testimonies identify the persons in whose place Christ acts by naming the
saving relation they receive through his work. Their cumulative force lies in that common relation across
the several offices and benefits asserted. (John 17:9; 10:15; 11:51–52; Matthew 20:28; 26:26–28; 1:21;

Mark 10:45; Hebrews 6:20; 13:20; 2:17; Isaiah 53:12; Acts 20:28; Ephesians 5:2, 23–25; Romans 8:33–
34)
    [800] III. The third and last scriptural argument by which the Arminians and their successors defend
the fiction of universal redemption takes its force from passages alleged to show that some for whom
Christ died nevertheless perish, and that his blood accordingly bears no fruit for many for whom it was
shed. On this subject their invention is remarkably fertile. They amplify by every rhetorical means the
supposed failure of Christ’s blood for the greater part of its intended recipients and the perdition of
sinners alleged to have been bought, purged, and reconciled. What consolation is this for afflicted souls,
when the strongest defense of the doctrine requires the precious blood of the Lamb to be made vile,
trampled upon, and counted a common thing? In the Father’s sight that blood was of no such little value
that he should have it poured out in vain for a single soul. The defense is welcome insofar as it vindicates
the honor of the Savior whose name the proponents claim while working to deprive his death of efficacy.
The passages they bring must establish that the person alleged to perish really received the very operation
whose efficacy they deny. A description which belongs to another office or relation cannot serve in its
place.
    [801] Their argument is “If Christ died for reprobates and those that perish, then he died for all and
every one, for confessedly he died for the elect and those that are saved; but he died for reprobates, and
them that perish: therefore,” etc. Even if a single exception were proved within the reprobate class, the
universal conclusion would still require a premise extending the asserted death to every member of that
class.
    [802] The elect have every ground for denying the second proposition outright. Christ did not at his
Father’s command lay down his life with the intention of making satisfaction for the sins of reprobates
and those who perish. His death is here considered according to the persons for whom the Father and the
Son intended it, so the denial concerns its representative and satisfying operation. Its intrinsic worth and
sufficiency, and the external privileges which others may receive, do not settle that intention.
    [803] The passages adduced for the minor premise will be considered in the order in which they are
offered. To establish what is pretended, a passage must assert the saving relation and the final loss of the
same person. The proposed inference stands or falls with their conjunction. (Romans 14:15; 1 Corinthians
8:11; 2 Peter 2:1; Hebrews 10:29)
    [804] The first passage says, “But if your brother be grieved with your meat, now walk you not
charitably. Destroy not him with your meat for whom Christ died.” The warning directs the conduct of the
strong by making the injury to a brother present to their judgment. The description of the injured person
has its warrant within the pastoral and evidential relations in which the warning is addressed. (Romans
14:15)
    [805] Only the ingenuity with which the opponents draw conclusions in favor of their cause could
make anyone expect the following inference from this passage. “The apostle exhorts strong and sound
believers to such a moderate use of Christian liberty that they do not grieve the spirit of the weak ones,
that were believers also (professors, all called ‘saints, elect, believers, redeemed,’ and so in charity
esteemed), and so give them occasion of stumbling and falling off from the gospel: therefore, Jesus Christ
died for all reprobates, even all those that never heard word nor syllable of him or the doctrine of the
gospel.” It takes an extraordinarily quick eye to discover that dependence. “Is it not affirmed that he may
perish for whom Christ died?” The apostle affirms no such thing here. He forbids conduct which tends
directly to destroy a brother by grieving him through an uncharitable use of liberty. “But why should the
apostle exhort him not to do that which he could no way do, if he that Christ died for could not perish?”
The act may sinfully supply an occasion for ruin although the ruin never occurs. A prohibition properly
reaches the attempt, including attempts which cannot achieve their intended effect. The soldier who thrust
the spear into the side of the dead Redeemer deserves no thanks for breaking none of his bones. Nor does
every person whom another attempts to destroy by uncharitable conduct necessarily incur damnation.

These are men of straw. Yet many may perish utterly whom we are bound to regard as redeemed in our
conduct and converse with them. That obligation concerns those whom Scripture directs us to esteem
“saints and brethren,” as it speaks of professing Christians. No passage mentions such persons as bought
or redeemed by the Savior without the qualification of visible membership in the church, which falls
infinitely short of every person. An infallible account of inward condition requires another warrant than
the judgment of charity proper to the community.
    [806] The second passage says, “And through your knowledge shall your weak brother perish, for
whom Christ died.” The expression has a greater appearance of favoring the objection, but supplies it no
greater force. The act by which the weak brother is said to perish is eating food offered to an idol with
conscience of the idol, following those who claim knowledge that the idol is nothing and therefore eat
freely. This is a sin damnable in its own nature, as every sin is. So far as anything in the sinner is
concerned, every sin ruins him, and in that respect the person who eats incurs destruction. God has
revealed himself otherwise in the blood of Christ than as one who invariably punishes every sin with
actual damnation in its subject. Actual eternal ruin in each such case therefore requires proof beyond the
fact that the sin merits it. Further, the person is called a brother. Brotherhood in the proper sense belongs
to believers, who through faith have one Father. Christ is said to die for him under the same judgment by
which he is said to be a brother. A true believer cannot finally perish, and the departure of the one who
perishes shows what he was all along. “They went out from us, because they were not of us.” His
brotherhood is the brotherhood of profession as it appears to our judgment, which Scripture requires us to
exercise charitably. We cannot count him a brother while withholding the judgment that Christ died for
him, since we have no brotherhood with the reprobate. Christ died for all believers, and we esteem all
who walk in due profession as believers while they give us no contrary evidence. Many so esteemed may
nevertheless perish. The threatened ruin also concerns the sin of the one who lays the offense, since so far
as his conduct tends, it ruins his brother irrecoverably. The objection is thus “The apostle tells persons
walking offensively, that by this abusing their liberty, others will follow them, to the wounding of their
conscience and ruin, who are brethren, acknowledged so by you, and such as for whom Christ died:
therefore, Christ died for all the reprobates in the world. ‘Is it just and equal,’ says the apostle, ‘that you
should do such things as will be stumbling-blocks in the way of the weak brother, at which he might
stumble and fall?’ therefore, Christ died for all.” Eternal perdition is possible for some whom we ought to
judge redeemed while they live and converse with us according to the gospel. The injury to conscience,
the occasion of stumbling, and the final event remain distinct despite the gravity of the warning. (1
Corinthians 8:11; John 17)
    [807] The next passage, on which much weight is placed, is “There shall be false teachers, denying
the Lord that bought them, and bringing upon themselves swift destruction.” Its usefulness for the
proposed proof depends on three uncertain identifications. The original calls the Lord Δεσπότης, a title
seldom if ever assigned to Christ, so the identity of the purchaser is itself in question. The buying may be
eternal redemption through Christ’s blood, or it may be a deliverance through divine goodness from
worldly defilements such as idolatry through knowledge of the truth. The latter benefit is expressly
asserted. It is also uncertain whether the apostle describes the purchase in its inward reality or according
to the teachers’ apprehension and profession. The saving procurement cannot be inferred before its agent,
benefit, and mode of predication are established. (2 Peter 2:1)
    [808] Two points are certain on the other side. First, the apostle assigns the false teachers no
distinguishing spiritual fruits of redemption. Their light and knowledge are common gifts which Christ
