has purchased for many for whom he did not make his soul a ransom. Second, if redemption by Christ’s
blood belongs to every person as the opponents maintain, it cannot peculiarly aggravate the sin of these
teachers. The apostle nevertheless names their buying precisely as an aggravation of their sin. The
benefits actually ascribed are the evidence for the kind of participation they possessed.
   [809] Even if the contested expressions all bore the senses the opponents give them, universal
redemption could by no means be drawn out of the passage. The three uncertainties nevertheless require

an account before the passage’s own intention can be stated, since the proposed counterexample depends
on holding all three identifications together in the same persons.
    [810] First, the passage gives no necessity for understanding Christ as mediator by the Lord, and its
indications point the other way. The verses which follow describe the dealings of God as God with
persons of this sort and say nothing of Christ. The title Δεσπότης, properly “Herus,” denotes a lord in
possession of dominion and sovereignty. The New Testament constantly calls the Savior Κύριος and
nowhere clearly calls him Δεσπότης, whereas it uses the latter for the Father in several places. Even an
instance of its application to Christ elsewhere would not determine its application here. Its fitness for
Christ’s redeeming office would still require proof, since Δεσπότης designates a master in relation to
servants and subjection, while Scripture consistently expresses the end for which Christ purchases
persons by his blood in terms of greater endearment. The title alone therefore settles neither the identity of
the Lord nor the office under which he bought the teachers. (Luke 2:29; Acts 4:24)
    [811] Second, even if Christ were intended by the title, the purchase of the teachers with the ransom
of his blood would remain unproved. The apostle first compares the false teachers with false prophets
among the ancient people and throughout the following chapter supports the comparison by examples
from the Old Testament. The word ἀγοράζω primarily denotes the buying of things and is transferred to
the redemption of persons, while the corresponding Old Testament word ‫ ָּפ ָד ה‬is used for deliverance of
any kind in innumerable places. That use supplies a sufficient account of the deliverance here. Second,
the passage mentions neither blood, death, price, nor the offering of Jesus Christ, as passages concerning
redemption properly so called ordinarily do. Some such expression accompanies ἀγοράζω when it
denotes that redemption, for by itself it may express deliverance from trouble generally. Third, when the
apostle expressly identifies the deliverance and its means, he describes the “escaping of the pollutions of
the world,” such as idolatry and false worship, “through the knowledge of the Lord and Savior Jesus
Christ;” the purchase therefore concerns their separation from the world through knowledge of the truth.
The apostle says nothing of their washing in the blood of the Lamb. God delivered them through the
knowledge of the gospel from Jewish or pagan blindness and bought them as servants subject to him as
their supreme head. The opponents’ remarkable chemistry reduces to this argument. “God the Lord, by
imparting the knowledge of the gospel, and working them to a professed acknowledgment of it and
subjection unto it, separated and delivered from the world divers that were saints in show, — really
wolves and hypocrites, of old ordained to condemnation: therefore, Jesus Christ shed his blood for the
redemption and salvation of all reprobates and damned persons in the whole world.” The deliverance
which the text specifies has its own benefits and consequences, and none establishes the eternal
redemption which the inference requires. (Deuteronomy 7:8; 15:15; Jeremiah 15:21; 1 Corinthians 6:20;
Revelation 5:9; 2 Peter 2:20)
    [812] Third, it is equally uncertain whether the purchase of these wolves and hypocrites is asserted as
an inward reality or under the judgment which others rightly formed on the evidence of their outward
profession. The teachers themselves professed to have been purchased by the one whom they pretended to
preach. Scripture similarly says of Ahaz, “The gods of Damascus smote him,” because this was what he
imagined and professed. The latter reading is further supported by Scripture’s constant practice of
ascribing to those within the fellowship of the church what belongs properly to its true spiritual members,
calling them saints, elect, and redeemed. Even by the opponents’ account the apostle could make the
teachers’ own profession that Christ bought them a special aggravation of their sin. The bare fact of
purchase, if universally shared, could be urged no more against them than against heathens and infidels
who never heard Jesus Christ named. The apostle’s description must therefore be read according to the
evidence under which it is pronounced. Public standing has its own truth without disclosing every inward
condition professed under it. (2 Chronicles 28:23)
    [813] If the opponents can establish universal redemption here, they need never despair of succeeding
in any enterprise, however foolish. They must first show that the Lord designates Christ as mediator.
Second, the buying must mean spiritual redemption by the blood of the Lamb. Third, the teachers must be

shown to have received that redemption really and effectually, beyond being accounted redeemed on
grounds of their church standing. Fourth, they must prove against the express Scripture that the persons so
redeemed can perish. Fifth, they must establish the inference, “Some in the church who have
acknowledged Christ to be their purchaser, fall away to blaspheme him, and perish forever: therefore,
Christ bought and redeemed all that ever did or shall perish;” and sixth, they must show that something
common to everyone can be a peculiar aggravation of one person’s sin. When they have completed these
tasks, they will find more work waiting in places they already know. Even the strongest reading of a
single apparent case leaves the asserted universality to be proved. (Revelation 14:4)
    [814] The final passage offered for the argument asks, “Of how much sorer punishment, suppose you,
shall he be thought worthy, who has trodden under foot the Son of God, and has counted the blood of the
covenant, wherewith he was sanctified, an unholy thing, and has done despite unto the Spirit of grace?”
“Nothing,” say our adversaries, “could be affirmed of all this concerning apostates, — namely, ‘That they
have trodden under foot,’ etc. unless the blood of Christ was in some sense shed for them.” The sanction
establishes the aggravated guilt of despising the covenant under a stated condition. Whether that condition
requires the saving application of the blood depends upon the person to whom sanctification is ascribed
and the kind of sanctification asserted. (Hebrews 10:29)
    [815] The warning serves the purpose of the whole epistle by urging Jews who have embraced the
gospel to persevere in it. Elsewhere the apostle does this through several arguments, principally through a
sustained comparison of the gospel in its administration with the legal shadows under whose bondage
they lived before their profession. Here the argument proceeds “ab incommodo, seu effectu pernicioso,”
from the destructive consequences of backsliding and willfully renouncing the truth once known and
professed, whatever the motive for the renunciation. It leaves the apostate without hope or means of
recovery, under the horror of conscience which expects the coming judgment. The apostle establishes
this, as is his practice in the epistle, through a provision familiar to his hearers from the administration of
the covenant under which they had lived in Judaism. The lesser instance is the punishment which God
appointed for a transgressor of the law of Moses who offended in the manner in which apostates offend
against the gospel, namely “with an high hand,” or “presumptuously:” and was consequently to die
without mercy. Since the gospel and the grace manifested in it stand so far above the ceremonies of the
law, those who willfully violate the gospel and despise its declaration of grace necessarily deserve a much
sorer punishment, whose severity he leaves his hearers to judge. To make that consequence plain, he
describes the quality of the sin in one who, having professed redemption and deliverance through Christ’s
blood, willfully casts himself into it. “It is,” says he, “no less than to tread under foot or contemn the Son
of God; to esteem the blood of the covenant, by which he was set apart and sanctified in the profession of
the gospel, to be as the blood of a vile man; and thereby to do despite to the Spirit of grace.” This
acknowledged purpose determines how the grammar and condition of the warning are to be understood,
and supplies the following grounds against the use made of it. (Hebrews 10:26–27; Numbers 15:30–31)
    [816] First, the apostle speaks of persons who professed the gospel, had been separated from the
world and brought into church fellowship, professed sanctification by Christ’s blood, received and
acknowledged him as the Son of God, and possessed gifts of the Holy Spirit. These are actual public
privileges, peculiar to relatively few among the sons of men. A predicate asserted under qualifications
which the multitude lacks cannot be extended to the multitude on the strength of this passage. The
exemption of even one person suffices to overthrow universal redemption. Nor does real public separation
of itself establish every inward transformation which belongs to saving participation. (Hebrews 6:4–5)
    [817] Second, the apostle neither reports an event nor asserts that it can occur. He declares the
sanction on the supposition of the act, and the misery which would follow is communicated in order to
deter it. When Paul told the soldiers that they could not be saved if the sailors escaped in the boat, he had
already received God’s assurance the previous night and told them that they would not drown. The
conditional warning directed them to prevent conduct which of itself led toward their ruin. All the rhetoric
of the Remonstrants cannot make it vain to forewarn men of an evil and turn them from the course which,

according to the actual order of things, incurs it, even when the purpose of God excludes the evil from
coming to pass. The threat of judgment for apostasy is itself appointed to preserve the saints from that sin
and can accordingly be addressed to them although the elect cannot be seduced. The apostle shows the
connection between apostasy and condemnation so that the saints may “take heed lest there should be in
any of them an evil heart of unbelief in departing from the living God”. The opening words make his
supposition plain, “For if we sin willfully.” He says nothing of believers being able to do it, but tells them
what the event would be if they did, just as Paul told the soldiers what would follow if the appointed
means of prevention were neglected without telling them that they would perish. The first person of “If
we sin willfully,” supports this reading of the apostle’s intention. Under it the passage establishes neither
the universality of redemption nor the apostasy of saints, for “suppositio nil ponit in esse.” A conditional
sanction, however certain its consequence, supplies no report that the act has been completed. (Acts
27:31; Hebrews 10:26)
    [818] Polycarp urges perseverance and seeks the repentance of members who stray. Ignatius warns
against error while directing prayer toward the repentance Christ can effect. These admonitions
participate in the government of the community by giving its members grounds on which to persevere or
return. Their presence gives no reason to conclude that the government under which they operate has
failed. A prescribed means, a consequence threatened under a condition, and a declared event each require
their own evidence. The opponent manufactures an accomplished apostasy by treating the operation
appointed to restrain it as testimony that it occurred. The place of a spoke in the wheel of paragraph 0 is
considered here together with the means of its preservation. An admonition belongs among those means,
and its effect must be judged by what it is appointed to do. (Polycarp, Epistle to the Philippians 7–8; 11;
Ignatius, Epistle to the Smyrnaeans 4)
     [819] A warning enters the history to which it is addressed. The consequence it communicates can
change what the hearer attends to, what he deliberates upon, what he refuses, and whether he repents or
appeals for help. The truth of the warning does not depend on the threatened event occurring in every
hearer. Its appointed operation can consist precisely in producing the response through which the danger
is avoided.
                          [820] wt→rt→Xt+1,         If(a,ℓ)⇏Occurred(a∧ℓ). (57)
     [821] Terms. w denotes the communicated warning and r the response actually made by the hearer.
The conditional identifies the act a and the consequence ell which the warning declares. Its sanction
follows from the supposition of the act and says nothing by itself of the act’s occurrence.
    [822] Consequence. Preservation through warnings is intelligible because a person may hear the
threat and respond to it as part of the course by which he is preserved. The appointed means and promise
establish that operation. Requiring the preserved person to be incapable of receiving a real warning would
exclude in advance one of the means by which his preservation can be effected. (Hebrews 3:12–14;
10:23–25)
     [823] Third, the persons addressed had professed all the things mentioned. They confessed Jesus
Christ as the Son of God, their sanctification by the blood of the covenant, and their enlightenment by the
Spirit of grace. The parallel passage ascribes many gifts of illumination to them, and their initiation by
baptism openly demonstrated their profession. To renounce these things with public detestation and curse
the name of Christ, as apostates did, was therefore an abomination aggravated by every privilege they had
professed. It warranted the remarkable threat even if the apostates never possessed any effectual interest
in the blood of Jesus. The profession supplies evidence on which the community can act without making
all the inward claims it expresses true. (Hebrews 6:4–5)
    [824] Fourth, the saints and the apostles themselves regarded persons who had been baptized and
incorporated into the church as sanctified. The apostle could therefore speak of backsliders according to
the standing commonly and properly assigned to them at the time in the judgment of charity. Whether

those to whom the argument was addressed were true believers or only temporary professors, the Holy
Ghost’s usual manner of speech gave them the same description. The rule established in paragraphs 594–
600 grounds that judgment in the appointed evidence and gives it no power to inspect the whole inward
condition of another.
    [825] Fifth, a reading which affirms as accomplished realities both the true sanctification of these
persons and their possible total perdition entails two things. Faith and sanctification would no longer be
fruits of election, and believers could fall finally from Christ. The old Arminians contended for both
propositions, but the new Universalists have yet to own either. Such a reading would have to retain the
same person and the same operation throughout. A sanctification predicated under a different relation
would leave the alleged counterexample unproved.
    [826] Sixth, nothing in the text establishes that the persons addressed are justified and regenerated
believers, much less that Christ died for them. Only one expression lends the inference any appearance of
force. They are said to be “sanctified by the blood of the covenant.” Five considerations determine its
meaning. First, the apostles address churches by calling all the called “saints”, thereby ascribing to each
what belongs properly to some. Second, these persons were baptized, an ordinance which the ancients
sometimes called φωτισμός, “illumination,” and sometimes ἁγιασμός, “sanctification,” because by the
solemn sprinkling of the symbol of Christ’s blood they were externally separated and set apart, and were
esteemed as saints and believers. Third, among the several scriptural senses of ἁγιάζω, the consecration of
a person or thing to a holy use is one of the most frequent. Fourth, the epistle repeatedly employs temple
expressions, applying to the ways of the Christian church terms drawn from legal observances. Fifth,
supposed and professed holiness is often named and esteemed as holiness itself. The sanctification here is
consequently the common outward separation, carrying the repute of real holiness, from the ways of the
world and the customs of the old synagogue to the enjoyment of Christ’s ordinance representing the blood
of the covenant. It does not assert the inward and effectual sanctification proper to the elect. The warning
addressed to all who were outwardly sanctified declared to the truly sanctified the certain consequence of
apostasy and thereby directed them to avoid it, as Joseph was warned to flee into Egypt lest Herod kill the
child, an event excluded by God’s purpose. To the merely outwardly sanctified it disclosed the odiousness
of the sin and the certain destruction which would follow if they fell into it, as they indeed might. An
inward saving predicate would require grounds beyond those which the warning actually supplies before
it could be used to disprove preservation. (2 Chronicles 29:33; Leviticus 16:4; Septuagint, Exodus 29:33)
    [827] The arguments formerly drawn from Scripture in support of the Arminian cause have thus been
answered by examining the persons and operations their passages actually assert. The preservation at
issue retains the same saving relation with which the argument began. Other sophisms remain, and a
recent multiplication of objections, some new at least in form, can trouble readers unpracticed in these
distinctions. Thomas More, in his “Universality of Free Grace,” has collected his objections against the
proposition that Christ died only for the elect into a single bundle of what he calls reasons. These are the
next claims to be examined. (Thomas More, The Universality of God’s Free Grace 26)
    [828] The title of More’s collection promises satisfaction to those who would have their reason
satisfied. The undertaking is large, and its performance is another matter, “hic labor, hoc opus.” Christian
reason instructed by the word of God could scarcely find satisfaction in a doctrine so contrary to that
word and so full of contradictions both within itself and against its own principles. The promise will
accordingly fail with anyone who has reason enough to use it and grace enough to prefer light to
darkness. More seems to call his collection reasons, distinguishing it from his earlier and more extensive
treatment of the same arguments and passages, because he supposes that here he has given them a logical
form. Their presentation in that form allows his skill in framing them to be tested, since in each case the
precise relation between the premises and their asserted consequence can be examined.
    [829] I. The major premise is “That which the Scripture oft and plainly affirms in plain words is
certainly true and to be believed. Scripture’s authority settles what is due to its actual assertion. Whether

More has stated that assertion correctly remains a question about the text. (Proverbs 22:20–21; Isaiah
8:20; 2 Peter 1:19–20)
    [830] The minor is “But that Jesus Christ gave himself a ransom, and by the grace of God tasted death
for every man, is oft and plainly affirmed in Scripture, as is before shown — This is the proposition
which introduces the disputed extent. A quotation cannot invest its interpretation with the authority
granted in the major. (Thomas More, The Universality of God’s Free Grace 7–13)
   [831] The conclusion is “Therefore, the same is certainly a truth to be believed.” It follows if the
minor has been established. A valid form does not establish the truth of its own premise. (John 20:31;
Acts 26:27)
    [832] First, every professor of the name of Christ acknowledges the major, provided that the plain
words of Scripture are understood according to the plain sense which the rules of interpretation establish.
Belief concerns the thing signified. The words have authority as its signs, so what they actually mean is
the object of inquiry and what is intended when Christians speak of believing the plain words of
Scripture. If plain words instead designate the bare literal import of expressions which may be figurative
or admit several senses, each capable of contextual enlargement or restriction, the assertion is utterly
false. Scripture repeatedly mentions God’s eyes, hands, and ears. What prevents the blasphemous folly of
the Anthropomorphites, who assign God a body and human shape, if those expressions are compelled to
yield their gross carnal conception, when even a child can see that their true import is otherwise? By the
same rule transubstantiation, or its younger brother consubstantiation, could be made an article of our
creed. With the meaning thus determined the major and its accompanying testimonies can pass, though
none of the latter bears on the matter in dispute and their irrelevance may be left to the reader’s judgment.
The authority of what Scripture asserts and the identification of its assertion perform distinct offices in
the proof.
    [833] Second, the minor must be denied outright in part. Scripture says nowhere that Christ gave
himself a ransom for every man. It says it neither often nor once, neither plainly nor obscurely, and the
place to which More refers affords it no proof. This part of the premise is empty flourish. The words
“tasting death for every man,” are indeed found, but every man does not invariably mean every person in
the world. Νουθετοῦντες πάντα ἄνθρωπον, καὶ διδάσκοντες πάντα ἄνθρωπον, — “Warning every man,
and teaching every man.” It would be false and impossible that Paul warned and taught every individual.
In Scripture every man may be distributive for some of every sort or collective with a restriction to every
member of one sort, as in Paul’s account of those to whom he preached. It does not of itself gather all
persons of all sorts. Moreover, the original says only ὑπὲρ παντός, for every, without the substantive man.
The omitted substantive might equally be supplied by elect or believer. The actual recipients of the saving
act must therefore be identified from the text instead of being inserted with the supplied noun. (Hebrews
2:9; Colossians 1:28)
    [834] Third, the earlier examination established that every one in the passage is restricted to the
members of Christ, the children he brings to glory. These descriptions identify the persons of whom the
expression is asserted. The passage therefore affords no support for the minor in the sense More intends,
for which no clear or even probable testimony will be found.
    [835] More appends further proofs to his conclusion to display his skill in the argumentative method
he has undertaken. He seems aware how little support it receives from the premises, and the reader who
consults the passages with the actual question in view can judge the success of his new supports. This
new logic attaches testimonies to the conclusion which suit neither premise, as though the connection
missing from both could be supplied by adding evidence at the end. It deserves notice in an age of learned
writers. “Heu quantum est sapere.” The logic is fit for the divinity it maintains. No further form of the
first argument can give it the premise its terms require.

    [836] II. More’s major is “Those whom Jesus Christ and his apostles, in plain terms, without any
exception or restraint, affirm that Christ came to save, and to that end died, and gave himself a ransom
for, and is a propitiation for their sin, he certainly did come to save, and gave himself a ransom for them,
and is the propitiation for their sins. The saving purpose must have the persons and effect actually
ascribed to it. The description of a class does not of itself determine the number of its members to whom
the assertion applies. (Matthew 26:24; John 6:38; 8:38, 45; 1 Corinthians 15:3–4; Hebrews 10:7; 2:3–4; 2
Peter 1:16)
    [837] The minor is “But Jesus Christ and his apostles have, in plain terms, affirmed that ‘Christ came
to save sinners’. The ‘world’. That he died for the ‘unjust’. The ‘ungodly’. For ‘every man’. ‘gave himself
a ransom for all men’. And is the ‘propitiation for the sins of the whole world’. And every one of these
affirmations without any exception or restraint, all being unjust, ungodly, sinners, and men, and of the
world. To give the conclusion unrestricted universality, the minor would have to give each description
that extent in its own passage. (1 Timothy 1:15; 2:6; John 3:4, 6, 17; 1 Peter 3:18; Romans 5:6; 3:10, 19–
20, 23; Hebrews 2:9; 1 John 2:2; Ephesians 2:1–3; Titus 3:3)
   [838] The conclusion is “Therefore, Jesus Christ came to save, died, and gave himself a ransom for all
men, and is the propitiation for their sins.” The authority of the passages can support the conclusion only
under the interpretation which they establish. The universal claim requires that interpretation to be
proved. (John 1:29)
    [839] The major requires a distinction between excepting persons from an established assertion and
identifying the persons about whom it was made. We ascribe no such contradiction to Scripture as that
one place affirms Christ died for all men and another excludes some of those very persons. One passage
instead interprets another by disclosing a sense which, taken by itself, might remain doubtful. We believe
Scripture when it says Christ gave himself a ransom for all, and we believe its account of that all as his
church, his elect, his sheep, and all believers, some from every sort, kindred, nation, and tongue under
heaven. The second declaration establishes who the all of the first declaration are, so that both stand in
their full force. Under that understanding the major may pass, though the pomp of More’s citations gives
it no additional support. Their irrelevance suggests that the references were put down at a venture, in
confidence that no one would trace them and that the number alone might dazzle some readers. Let the
reader turn to the places and give More credit another time if a single one concerns the question. The
word of God is not to be corrupted in this fashion. Perhaps a fault in printing has displaced the passage
which actually says Christ shed his blood for many. In the account of his mission, More gives the
preceding verse for the following one, where Christ says that he came to save what his Father gave him so
that none should be lost, which certainly identifies the elect. His citation of the account of Christ’s death
and resurrection is nearer the matter, for the apostle joins both and says they are for us, with the
consequences already explained for More’s argument. The reference to the will of God perhaps intends
the later verse, which ascribes our sanctification to the offering of the body of Jesus in fulfillment of that
will. Sanctification is not effected in everyone, although More may find comfort in the words “once for
all,” until someone explains the meaning of ἐφάπαξ. The other passages likewise have nothing to say for
the proposition under dispute, and their vicinity often supplies enough to overthrow it. The major
consequently amounts to “All those for whom the Scripture affirms that Christ did die, for them he died;”
which is true and granted. A description of these persons as sinners supplies their condition without
enumerating every person in that condition. (Matthew 26:24, corrected to 26:28; John 6:38, corrected to
6:39; 1 Corinthians 15:3–4; Hebrews 10:7, corrected to 10:10)
    [840] The minor affirms that Christ died to save sinners, the unjust, the ungodly, the world, and all. Its
conclusion could therefore read “Therefore Christ died for sinners, unjust, ungodly, the world, and the
like.” Four considerations answer the further claim. First, this argument is substantially the same as the
preceding one, as are some which follow it. A change of words gives the appearance of a greater number
without multiplying the grounds. Second, its whole force is obtained by turning indefinite propositions
into universal ones. From Christ’s death for sinners it infers his death for every sinner, from his death for

the unjust, the ungodly, and the world his death for every unjust and ungodly person in the world, and
from his death for all his death for every person of every sort. The same reasoning yields whatever
conclusions one wishes. God “justifies the ungodly”, so he must justify every ungodly person, and
because “whom he justifies, them he also glorifies;” every ungodly person must be glorified. When Christ
came, “men loved darkness rather than light”, so all must have loved darkness and none believed. “The
world knew not Christ”, so nobody in the world knew him. “The whole world lies in wickedness”, so
each individual in it must lie in wickedness. Third, the whole argument can be granted if its conclusion
retains the terms in the senses they bear in the passages cited. Christ died for sinners and the world, for
sinful persons in their several generations. If a collective universality is imposed, the syllogism becomes
false. No passage adduced affirms it, and the indefinite descriptions in which Scripture assigns the object
of his death receive their determination in passages which name his own people and the children of God
scattered throughout the world. Fourth, the texts cited at the beginning of the minor do not establish the
disputed point, and the pertinent passages have already been examined. Those added at the end establish
that all are sinners and children of wrath, without the least intimation that Christ died for all such persons.
This answers the first two arguments, which could readily be turned upon their author, since Scripture is
full and plain in establishing the very position he opposes. The predicate common to the recipients does
not make every person who bears it a recipient. That converse requires its own ground. (Romans 4:5; 5:6;
3:10, 19–20, 23; John 3:4, 6, 17, 19; 1:10; 1 John 5:19; 2:2; 1 Timothy 1:15; 1 Peter 3:18; Hebrews 2:9;
Ephesians 2:1–3; Titus 3:3)
     [841] An inclusion is not converted by extending the relation proper to its narrower class to the whole
class containing it. Every person represented in the saving office may be a sinner, although it does not
follow that every sinner is represented in that office. The wider description can belong to each recipient
without identifying the full population of recipients. Each spoke belongs to the wheel of paragraph 0. The
work of identifying the spokes begins with their particular relation within that whole, and the wider
description by itself leaves that work unfinished.
                       [842] ∀x [R(x)⇒Sinner(x)] ⇏ ∀x [Sinner(x)⇒R(x)]. (58)
    [843] Terms. R denotes the representative saving relation already defined. Sinner denotes the broader
predicate which holds of its recipients. The implication from left to right fails unless a further premise
establishes the converse inclusion.
    [844] Consequence. Common sin identifies the condition in which salvation is needed. The persons
for whom the saving undertaking is appointed must be identified through the undertaking itself. The
proclamation therefore retains its full force in addressing sinners, while the claim that every sinner is
savingly represented remains a proposition requiring proof.
   [845] III. The major is “That which the Scripture lays forth as one end of the death of Christ, and one
ground and cause of God’s exalting Christ to be the Lord and Judge of all, and of the equity of his
judging, that is certainly to be believed. The fact that one event has several effects gives no ground for
passing between them without first identifying the end and office which the passage actually asserts.
(Psalms 12:6; 18:130; 119:4)
    [846] The minor is “But the Scripture lays forth this for one end of the death and resurrection of
Christ, that he might be the Lord of all. And for that cause (even his death and resurrection) has God
exalted him to be the Lord and Judge of all men, and his judgments shall be just. The jurisdiction asserted
here includes authority to judge everyone. The saving representation proposed in the conclusion requires
a relation beyond the one established by this jurisdiction. (Romans 14:9, 11–12; 2:16; 2 Corinthians 5:10,
14–15; Philippians 2:7–11; Acts 17:31)
    [847] The conclusion is “Therefore, that Christ so died, and rose again for all, is a truth to be believed,
” and introduces a saving relation stronger than anything entailed by universal lordship. The inference
needs a premise which joins those two relations. (1 Timothy 2:6)

    [848] First, the awkward expression and ill construction of the argument belong to the person who
frames it and need not detain the cause. Two instances are sufficient. More places the minor, Christ’s
appointment as Lord and Judge of all, within the major itself, carrying one term through all three
propositions and making the whole nearly unintelligible. He also interprets “For this cause God exalted
Christ,” to mean his death and resurrection, though the resurrection by which he was “declared to be the
Son of God with power” was itself a glorious part of his exaltation. It would squander time to expose the
innumerable defects of this sort which occur throughout. Anyone with a little learning or practice in
reasoning can see their vanity. For those who admire these cloudy sophisms the appropriate words are
neither “Quoniam hic populus vult decipi, decipiatur,” nor an indulgence of their error, but “God give
them understanding and repentance, to the acknowledgment of the truth.” The formal defects matter here
because the ordering of the terms conceals the theological premise which the conclusion needs. (Romans
1:4)
    [849] Second, if the misery of the times should again call More to write, he would do better to speak
in his customary manner and leave syllogisms alone. The confusion of his innumerable repetitions may
perplex a reader, but this manner of arguing is ridiculous, since want of logic deceives More and delight
in sophistry enables him to deceive others. Even a first reading exposes a conclusion which neither
premise so much as contemplates, and which he therefore tries to reinforce with new testimony. The
premises concern Christ’s exaltation to judge all, which belongs to his own glory. The conclusion
concerns his dying for all, which necessarily intends their good. To abolish human learning and establish
this procedure in its place would be a noble design indeed. “Hoc Ithacus velit et magno mercentur
Atridæ.” The conclusion cannot be repeated into a demonstration of the connection it needs.
   [850] Third, reduced to its substance the argument is “Christ died and rose again that he might be
Lord and Judge of all. Therefore, Christ died for all.” The whole treatise explains what More intends by
dying for all, namely the payment of a ransom for all so that they might be saved. Yet the dominion and
power of judgment from which he extracts this payment extend to angels for whom Christ did not die. Let
those who can perceive the inference enjoy their quickness. Judgment reaches those who are subject to his
authority, and ransom reaches those for whom its price is paid. The first extent does not establish the
second.
    [851] Fourth, the abundance of texts distributed among the propositions adds no weight where the
reasoning has none. The passages accompanying the major, with the verse number corrected as it
evidently should be, speak of God’s precepts and the purity and perfection of his word. None bears on the
question. The text attached to the conclusion is brought out on every occasion as a supposed foundation
for the whole assertion, without cause, as already repeatedly shown. Among the passages appended to the
minor is one already examined, whose meaning excludes the imposition of universal redemption. It says
nothing of Christ’s power to judge all and therefore contributes nothing to the proposed middle term. The
other passages do indeed speak of his exaltation and his judgment of all at the last day. The inference
from universal judgment to a universal saving death would cost More more labor than he intends to spend
on it. A passage that establishes the appointment of his authority does not on that account establish every
benefit alleged to belong to those under it. (Psalms 12:6; 18:30, correcting the printed verse 130; 119:4; 2
Timothy 2:6; 2 Corinthians 5:14–15; Philippians 2:7–11; Acts 17:31; Romans 2:16)
    [852] The whole weight therefore falls on the passage which alone gives the argument any appearance
of force. The apostle sets forth Christ’s dominion over all so that believers may walk blamelessly and give
no offense to one another. They know the terror of the Lord and know that they, no less than others, must
appear before his judgment seat, where an account of scandals and offenses would be a sad account to
give. To fix this in their minds he explains how Christ obtained that dominion and the exercise of
judgment, all things being put under his feet. He also discloses what Christ intended in this respect when
he undertook mediation, here expressed by “dying, rising, and reviving,” namely to have the execution of
universal judgment committed to him. This was part of the “glory set before him,” for which he would
“endure the cross and despise the shame.” The relation here established runs from his mediatorial

undertaking to his possession of authority. It does not describe his death in the place of every person
under that authority. (Romans 14:9, 11–12; Hebrews 12:2)
    [853] The passage therefore concerns the end and issue of Christ’s death for Christ himself, without
specifying his intention toward those for whom he died. To die for others denotes at least their good, and
in Scripture denotes dying in their stead. His death opened the way to the exercise of power under which
he judges all and casts the greater part into hell by a righteous sentence. That office gives no ground for
saying he died for their good or in their stead. If the opponents nevertheless maintain that his obtaining
the power to judge all means that he died for all, three consequences remain. First, innumerable persons
will be judged for failing to walk according to the light of nature which directed them to seek the eternal
power and Godhead of their Creator, without a rumor of the gospel ever reaching them to direct them to a
Redeemer. What good have they received from the supposed death for them? Second, on the same
reasoning he died for the devils, since by his death and resurrection he obtained the power to judge them
also. Third, the assertion leaves the actual question unanswered. We inquire whom the Savior intended to
redeem and save by his blood, and More tells us whom he will judge. “quæstio est de alliis, responsio de
cepis.” A consequence for the office Christ exercises and a consequence for the salvation of every person
require a further premise to join them. (Romans 2:12)
    [854] IV. The major is “That which the Scripture so sets forth in general for the world of mankind, as
a truth for them all, that whosoever of the particulars so believe as to come to Christ and receive the same
shall not perish, but have everlasting life, is certainly a truth to be believed. The promise has the form of a
universal conditional. Its effect is affirmed of whoever believes, without making belief a condition
actually fulfilled in everyone. (Acts 5:20)
    [855] The minor is “But that God sent forth his Son to be the Savior of the world is in Scripture so set
forth in general for all men, that whosoever of the particulars so believe as they come to Christ and
receive the same, they shall not perish, but have everlasting life — The title specifies a saving office, and
the proclamation offers its promise to whoever believes. Their conjunction supplies no assertion that the
benefit is applied to all. (John 3:16–18, 36; 1:4, 11–12)
    [856] The conclusion is “Therefore, that God sent his Son to be the Savior of the world is a certain
truth.” What it establishes depends on the meaning under which the premises declare that office. Any
further claim about the complete enumeration of its recipients needs further evidence. (1 John 4:14)
    [857] A reader acquainted with the point to which the controversy has come, or with the ordinary
manner of reasoning, can scarcely expect much time to be spent on this confusion of flourishes,
repetitions, and defective inferences. The pretended new argument is the same in substance as the first
two and nearly all those which follow. Reviewing such things would be intolerable were it not that
“eundum est quo trahunt fata ecclesiæ.” The reader can see at a glance what a recital of particulars would
only repeat, that More cannot express his own conceptions tolerably or infer one thing from another in a
regular way. Four points dispose of the argument. First, whatever Scripture sets forth as a truth to be
believed certainly is so and ought to be embraced. Second, it sets forth Christ’s death to everyone who
hears the gospel as a means wholly sufficient to bring sinners to God, so that whoever believes and comes
to Christ will certainly be saved. Third, the consequence is the infinite value of Christ’s death, which can
save to the utmost everyone to whom it is made known if that person obtains an interest and right in it
through true faith. Many scriptural testimonies have already established this innate sufficiency as the
ground of its common proposal to elect and reprobate. Fourth, to give the inference even the appearance
of an argument the conclusion ought to retain the whole assertion of the major, namely “That Christ is so
set forth to be the Savior of the world, that whosoever of the particulars believe,” etc. Under this form it is
granted entirely. It establishes the fullness and sufficiency of the satisfaction, without proving the
universality of redemption. The relevant senses of world have already been established. The truth of
Christ’s saving office is common ground, while the supposed extension of his redeeming act remains to
be demonstrated.

    [858] V. The major is “That which God will one day cause every man confess to the glory of God is
certainly a truth, for God will own no lie for his glory. Universal confession gives a ground for the truth
of what is confessed. A saving consequence can be inferred only when the confession has the character
proper to a saving relation. (John 3:33; Romans 3:3–4)
   [859] The minor is “But God will one day cause every man to confess Jesus (by virtue of his death
and ransom given) to be the Lord, even to the glory of God. Compelled acknowledgment of lordship and
saving faith concern the same Christ, but each has its own disposition and effects. The title confessed
does not erase that difference. (Philippians 2:7–11; Isaiah 45:22–23; Romans 14:9, 11–12; Psalms 86:9)
    [860] The conclusion is “Therefore, it is certainly a truth that Jesus Christ has given himself a ransom
for all men, and has thereby the right of lordship over them; and if any will not believe and come into this
government, yet he abides faithful, and cannot deny himself, but will one day bring them before him, and
cause them to confess him Lord, to the glory of God; when they shall be denied by him, for denying him
in the days of his patience.” The universal ransom enters the conclusion as a relation of its own. The
universality of a confession about authority does not prove that relation. (2 Timothy 2:12–14; Matthew
10:32–33; 2 Corinthians 5:10)
    [861] The conclusion to which these premises give any strength is “Therefore, that Jesus Christ is the
Lord, and to be confessed to the glory of God, is certainly a truth.” The argument can go no further unless
a syllogism may be replaced by three independent assertions, each left to stand on its own strength. The
insertion about a ransom for all and the ensuing account of the conviction and condemnation of those who
neither believe nor obey the gospel, with the passages cited in support, are foreign to the question. If
More imagines that opponents of universal redemption doubt the proper conclusion, he has found an easy
occupation in refuting mistakes of his own creation. Charity may separate him from the shadow with
which he is so violently at odds. First, we ascribe no fruitless redemption to Christ. He loved his own
church with the entire love which moved him to lay down his life, and he effectually redeemed all his
elect. He will nevertheless judge the reprobate, including those who know nothing of his gospel, those
who deny and disobey it, and those who corrupt its truth. Everyone will be convinced at the last day that
he is Lord of all, and More may spare himself the labor of proving what no one questions. Indignation
could say more and must be restrained. Second, the cause inserted in the minor, “By virtue of his death
and ransom given,” is unsupported. Scripture nowhere identifies the ransom paid for us in his death as the
cause of his exaltation to universal lordship. It identifies his obedience to his Father in death as antecedent
to that exaltation. The distinction between obedience as the ground of his exaltation and satisfaction as the
saving procurement for his people exposes the missing premise. (2 Corinthians 5:2, 10; Timothy 2:12–14;
Philippians 2:7–11)
    [862] VI. The major is “That which may be proved in and by the Scripture, both by plain sentences
therein and necessary consequences imported thereby, without wresting, wrangling, adding to, taking
from, or altering the sentences and words of Scripture, is a truth to be believed. The rule requires the
alleged consequences to be exhibited. A declaration that proof is possible does not complete it. (Matthew
22:29, 32; Romans 11:2, 5–6)
    [863] The minor is “But that Jesus Christ gave himself a ransom for all men, and by the grace of God
tasted death for every man, may be proved in and by the Scripture, both by plain sentences therein and
necessary consequences imported thereby, without wresting, wrangling, adding, or taking away, or
altering the words and sentences, as is already showed, which will be now ordered into several proofs —
This restates the proposition requiring demonstration. Its placement under a general rule gives it no
evidence of its own. (Thomas More, The Universality of God’s Free Grace 7, 13)
    [864] The conclusion is “Therefore, that Jesus Christ gave himself for all men, and by the grace of
God tasted death for every man, is a truth to be believed.” Its validity depends upon the minor in the sense
under dispute. Syllogistic arrangement cannot establish the required interpretation. (Mark 1:15; 16:15, 18;
1 John 4:14)

    [865] First, the argument amounts to an assertion that universal redemption can be proved by
Scripture. Since this is the very thesis More has undertaken to establish, its repetition serves only to
increase the number of arguments. It might pass without any other answer were the things included in that
number unworthy of separate consideration. Their actual dependence on Scripture must be made available
for judgment before the claim of proof can have force.
    [866] Second, since this must pass for an argument, both propositions can be considered on their own
terms. Stripped of unnecessary language, the major is “That which is affirmed in the Scripture, or may be
deduced from thence by just consequence, following such ways of interpretation, of affirmation, and
consequences, as by which the Spirit of God leads us into the knowledge of the truth, is certainly to be
believed;” and is granted by all, though the passages cited do not prove it. It is the sole foundation of the
article of faith More opposes. As to the minor, Scripture says that Christ gave himself a ransom ὑπὲρ
πάντων, for all, and tasted death ὑπὲρ παντός, for all. Neither expression has ever been denied. All men
and every man in the unrestricted sense are More’s additions, and he may spare himself the labor of
establishing the expressions which everyone already grants. If he means to prove that all comprises every
person in all ages and of all kinds, elect and reprobate, instead of all Christ’s children, elect, sheep, and
people given him by God, gathered from every sort, nation, tongue, and language, that is an issue we may
gladly join with him or any living man, with the Lord’s assistance. It must be decided by the proportion of
faith, by the essential content of redemption, by the scope of the passages containing the assertions and
their comparison with other passages, and by humble inquiry after the mind of God according to his own
appointment. The confidence with which Owen undertook that trial, apart from failings incident to
personal weakness, followed serious examination of what the most celebrated Lutheran and Arminian
advocates of universal redemption could produce. The proposition for which the reader should require
proof is “That Jesus Christ, according to the counsel and will of his Father, suitable to his purpose of
salvation in his own mind and intention, did, by his death and oblation, pay a ransom for all and every
man, elect and reprobate, — both those that are saved and those that perish, — to redeem them from sin,
death, and hell, [and] to recover salvation, life, and immortality for them; and not only for his elect, or

church, chosen to an inheritance before the foundation of the world.” The passages produced for it can
now be taken in order, requiring of each the true premises and preserved senses without which no
consequence is necessary. (Matthew 22:29, 32; Romans 11:2, 5–6)
    [867] Proof 1. “God so loved the world, that he gave his Son to be the Savior of the world; and sends
his servant to bear witness of his Son, that all men through him might believe; that whosoever believes on
him might have everlasting life. And he is willing that all should come to the knowledge of the truth, and
be saved. Nor will he be wanting in the sufficiency of helpfulness to them, if, as light comes, they will
suffer themselves to be wrought on and to receive it. And is not this plain in Scripture?” The assembled
expressions concern divine love, the saving office, and the extent designated by world. Their force
depends upon preserving the relation and population proper to each assertion. (1 John 4:14; John 1:4, 7;
3:16–17; 1 Timothy 2:4; 1:15; Proverbs 1:23; 8:4–5)
    [868] First, the single thing the proof must establish is that the indefinite scriptural propositions
concerning the object of Christ’s death require an unrestricted universal reading. All and world would
have to designate every person in the world wherever they here designate that object. Without such a
demonstration the remaining labor bears no fruit. A few ambiguous passages are recited and a false
inference drawn which they do not even appear to warrant. The proposed extent still depends on the
interpretive grounds examined in paragraphs 544–618.
    [869] Second, God sends his Son as the “Savior of the world,” and sends his servant to testify to him
as such. The designation means that he saves persons living in the world, as the elect are, and a hundred
such passages could not establish more against the interpretation which other passages make clear. The
next text calls Christ the “life of men;” which is wholly true because life comes to no one except in and
through him, but it does not decide the question. More adds “That all men through him might believe;” to
a fragment from elsewhere and thus makes it appear that Christ was sent so that all men through Christ

should believe. This joins Scripture as the devil joined it, cutting away what stood in the way of his
argument that the Savior should cast himself from the temple pinnacle. Once the serpent’s sophistry is
removed, the words bear against the very thesis they are brought to establish. “There was a man sent from
God, whose name was John. The same came for a witness, to bear witness of the light, that all men
through him might believe.” The referent of δι’ αὐτοῦ, “through him?” is therefore John the witness,
rather than Christ the light, as almost all expositors agree apart from certain Papists and Grotius, that
Ishmael. The Syriac renders it “By his hand or ministry.” The usage establishes the distinction. Scripture
does not say that we believe διὰ Χρίστου, “by Christ,” or διὰ τοῦ φωτός, “by the light;” but εἰς τὸ φῶς,
“in the light,” and ἐπι τὸν Κύριον, “believed in the Lord;” and Καὶ πᾶς ὁ πιστεύων ἐπ’ αὐτῷ, “Every one
that believes on him.” So also ἐν Χριστῷ denotes belief in him in several places. Believing through
someone denotes the instrument of faith, as the ministry of the word is its instrument, whereas Christ is its
object. Now John was sent “that all through him might believe.” This all has every qualification More
demands for an invariably collective universal, including being spoken of God. Yet John preached only
for a few years in Judea. Did his ministry intend to bring to faith every person alive in every country then,
everyone dead before his birth, and everyone born after his death in any place under heaven until the end
of the world? Those who can believe it may enjoy their persuasion without a rival here. The all are some
of all sorts to whom his word came, and that necessary sense overthrows the rule on which More’s
proposition depends. The Savior’s sending and the witness’s ministry therefore remain distinct
operations, with no ground for enlarging the saving domain by confusing them. (1 John 4:14; John 1:4, 7;
12:36; Acts 9:42; Romans 9:33)
   [870] Third, the assertion that God sent his Son so that “whosoever believes on him might have
everlasting life,” is not in dispute among Christians as to its sense. The believing condition identifies the
persons who enjoy the promise, while the source of the faith they exercise remains as established in the
account of its procurement. (John 3:16–17)
     [871] Fourth, the expression of God’s will that all should be saved must be considered without the
unnecessary word added for show or the neighboring passage brought in from another purpose. If all men
signifies every individual, two questions follow. First, in what act of God does this willingness consist? If
it is his eternal purpose to save everyone, why is it unaccomplished? “Who has resisted his will?” If it is
an antecedent desire which fails of its end, the blessed God would be most miserable, unable to
accomplish his just and holy desires. If it is a temporal act declaring himself to them, grant that salvation
is obtainable only in the Redeemer Jesus Christ and produce a single act in which God declared to all
persons in all times and places his willingness to save them through that Redeemer. That would end this
dispute. Second, does the will bear equally on everyone intended? If so, why are its effects unequal, and
what reason can be assigned? If not, where does the text distinguish its operation? It contains no
intimation of such a diversity. All men means some of all sorts throughout the world, as the context and
circumstances already show to any fair reader, and the will in question is the one expressed by the Savior.
The assertion added at the close, of God’s “not being wanting in the sufficiency of helpfulness to them
who, as light comes, suffer themselves to be wrought upon and receive it,” is the poisonous sting in the
serpent’s tail. It contains the Pelagian poison of free will, the Popish merit of congruity, and the entire
universality of Arminian sufficient grace, none of which the passage gives the least ground to affirm. The
distinction between command and effectual purpose determines what follows from the will asserted. (1
Timothy 2:4; 1:15; John 6:40)
    [872] The claim amounts to a universal grant of sufficient subjective grace which enables obedience
in every person and increases by degrees as its recipients make use of what they already possess. Few
inventions of a decaying Christianity are more contrary to innumerable places of Scripture, more
derogatory to the freedom of grace and destructive of its efficacy, or more plainly set the old idol of free
will upon the throne of God. Far from being “plain and clear in Scripture,” the position contradicts the
whole revealed dispensation of the new covenant. A full demonstration would be fitting if the Lord gave
occasion, but the point does not immediately belong to the present question. The alleged assistance would

in any case require evidence of its universal distribution and of the adequacy claimed for it. The name
sufficient supplies no account of the performance. The second proof now follows.
    [873] Assistance which is adequate only when the recipient supplies a further decisive act has
conditional adequacy. The actual response still depends on the occurrence of that act and on whatever
brings it about. A cause sufficient to produce the actual response must account for that remaining
dependence. To ascribe assistance universally settles nothing about the source of the act which makes it
adequate.
                [874] Gx∧Vx⇒FxGx⇏Vx          ⇒ Gx⇏Fxfrom these premises alone. (59)
    [875] Terms. G denotes the assistance proposed, V the additional voluntary condition which the
assistance does not secure, and F saving faith. The last statement concerns what follows from these
specified premises. A further true premise could still establish the result.
     [876] Consequence. The doctrine must say whether the assistance produces the willing required for
faith or accompanies that willing whenever it occurs. This is the difference pursued since paragraphs 252–
332. The words grace and sufficiency obscure it for as long as the operations they designate remain
unspecified.
    [877] Proof 2. “Jesus Christ, the Son of God, came into the world to save the world; to save sinners; to
take away our sins, and destroy the works of the devil; to take away the sins of the world: and therefore
died for all; and gave himself a ransom for all; to save that which was lost. And so his propitiation was
made for the world; the whole world. And all this is full and plain in Scripture.” These descriptions
disclose the conditions under which salvation reaches its recipients. None becomes an enumeration of
every individual who shares the condition merely by being placed beside the others. (John 12:47; 1:29; 1
Timothy 1:15; 2:6; 1 John 3:5, 8; 2:2; 2 Corinthians 5:14–15, 19; Matthew 18:11)
    [878] All the passages in which this proof invokes all or world have already received their
examination. The reader can consult those places without a repetition of their reasoning and see that they
overthrow the proposition rather than support it. The other passages supply no appearance of a universal
redemption, and what they establish against it has already been declared. The same expressions and
saving predicates require the same interpretations when introduced into a further inference. The third
proof accordingly remains. (John 12:47; 1:29; 2 Corinthians 5:14–15, 19; 1 Timothy 2:6; 1:15; 1 John
2:2; 3:5, 8; Matthew 18:11)
    [879] Proof 3. “God in Christ does, in some means or other of his appointment, give some witness to
all men of his mercy and goodness procured by Christ; and there-through, at one time or other, sends forth
some stirrings of his Spirit, to move in and knock at the hearts of men, to invite them to repentance and
seeking God, and so to lay hold on the grace and salvation offered: and this not in a show or pretense, but
in truth and good-will, ready to bestow it on them. And this is all fully testified in Scripture.” A witness
can communicate only the determinate content which it bears. Knowledge of goodness establishes
knowledge of the redemption here alleged only where the witness supplies what makes that particular
redemption known. (Psalms 19:4; Romans 10:18; Acts 14:17; 17:30–31; Genesis 6:3; Isaiah 45:22; John
1:19)
    [880] “Parvas habet spes Troja, si tales habet.” If universal redemption needs such proofs, its need is
great and its hope of support small. More invokes universal vocation to maintain universal redemption,
although he often invokes the latter to support the former. “Manus manum fricat,” or rather, “Muli se
mutuo scabiunt;” for these are the two legs of the idol of free will, and when one stumbles the other
comes forward to uphold the Babel. A full examination of that gross fiction would carry us beyond the
controversy. Three distinctions suffice here. First, from creation God has at all times called persons to
know him as Creator through what the visible creation makes known of him, “even his eternal power and
Godhead”. Second, after the death of Christ God extended the preaching of the gospel far and wide to call
home his children scattered throughout the world, whereas his elect had formerly been confined almost to

one nation. The gospel was authorized to be preached to “every creature”. Third, the claim that in every
age and place God grants means of grace, calls everyone to Christ as Redeemer and to the mercy
manifested in him, and accompanies those invitations with strivings of his Spirit to bring everyone to
accept them, is a groundless imagination. It contradicts God’s distinguishing mercy, express Scripture,
and the experience of all ages. Its assertion is bold enough, and producing it as a proof of a still grosser
untruth is bolder. A separate demonstration could be given if the Lord permitted and the present inquiry
did not set the matter aside. Meanwhile the cited passages give the reader the means to judge what each
witness makes known. A general disclosure of divine goodness cannot establish the saving propositions
for which a particular revelation is required. (Romans 1:19–20; 10:14–15, 18; Psalms 19:1–2; 147:19–20;
Acts 14:16–17; 17:30–31; 16:7; Mark 16:15; Isaiah 45:22; Matthew 11:25; 22:14)
    [881] Proof 4. “The Holy Ghost, that comes from the Father and the Son, shall reprove the world of
sin (even that part of the world that refuses now to believe that they are under sin), because they believe
not on Christ, and that it is their sin that they have not believed on him. And how could it be their sin not
to believe in Christ, and they for that cause under sin, if there were neither enough in the atonement made
by Christ for them, nor truth in God’s offer of mercy to them, nor will nor power in the Spirit’s moving in
any sort sufficient to have brought them to believe, at one time or other? And yet is this evident in
Scripture, and shall be by the Holy Spirit, to be their great sin, that fastens all other sins on them.” To
convict of unbelief and to procure saving faith are distinct operations. Their occurrence in connection
with the same proclamation supplies no proof of the dependence More asserts. (John 3:18–19; 8:24;
12:48; 15:22, 24; 16:7–11)
     [882] More proposes that men cannot be condemned for unbelief unless Christ made an atonement
sufficient for them, God truly offered them mercy, and the Spirit gave them at some time sufficient will
and power to believe. Nothing in these conditions establishes universal redemption, but each requires an
answer. First, “Enough in the atonement for them,” can mean that the atonement actually made on their
behalf has enough in it. The denial concerns the assertion that it was made for them. Its intrinsic merit is
sufficient to save them if they believe, and that sufficiency is the chief ground of its proposal to those to
whom the gospel is preached. Second, God’s offer of mercy is true wherever he makes it, as are all his
ways and words. If the command to believe and the promise of life upon believing are called an offer, the
offer contains the eternal truth that God assuredly bestows life and salvation on every believer. It
immediately declares our duty and the connection between faith and life. It does not disclose God’s
intention toward the particular person addressed. “For who has known the mind of the Lord, and who has
been his counselor?” Third, by placing will before power More puts the cart before the horse. No inward
assistance is required to make a person inexcusable for not believing when the object of faith is set before
him, even though he has neither the power nor the will in himself, having lost both in Adam. Let More
explain in his next controversy how a person can receive a will to believe and nevertheless not believe.
The only form in which the proof can display its strength is “If the Spirit shall convince all those of sin to
whom the gospel is preached, that do not believe, then Christ died for all men, both those that have the
gospel preached unto them and those that have not; but the first is true, for their unbelief is their great sin:
ergo, Jesus Christ died for all.” If any inference is “a baculo ad angulum,” this is, “from the beam to the
shuttle.” The passages establish that unbelief condemns the soul and that persons are condemned for it in
its privative sense when the gospel has been preached to them. But quid ad nos? The hearer’s
responsibility for refusing a revealed command cannot establish an intention to redeem those who heard it
and those who never did. (John 3:18–19; 8:24; 12:48; 15:22, 24)
    [883] One passage is pressed more than the rest and is consequently more abused. “I will send the
Comforter to you. And when he is come, he will reprove the world of sin, and of righteousness, and of
judgment: of sin, because they believe not in me; of righteousness, because I go to my Father, and you see
me no more; of judgment, because the prince of this world is judged.” First, does More understand this as
the action of the Spirit with Christ at the last day or as the action of the Spirit in the present ministry of
the word? The first reading is grossly mistaken. The second makes the conviction concern those to whom

the gospel is preached, which leaves it incapable of proving a redemption of everyone before and after
Christ’s death. Second, does he restrict the Spirit’s conviction to the preaching of the gospel or include
strivings and motions in people who never hear the word? If he means the latter, proof is still wanting.
Third, does he hold that this conviction and the efficacy of grace attending it actually convert and bring to
faith those who are convicted? The passage must answer these questions through the subjects and purpose
it identifies for the operation it describes. (John 16:7–11)
     [884] The purpose of the Savior’s last sermon determines what the promise of the Spirit means. The
apostles were grieved to hear that he must leave them and go to the Father, and he knew how much their
sorrow would increase when they saw the ignominious manner in which their Lord and Master would be
taken away and endured the reproaches and persecutions which would follow his departure. He tells them
to refuse fear and sorrow because what he will do and bestow after the removal of his bodily presence
will more than make up their loss, shame, and reproach. The principal offense would be his rejection and
removal from the world as a false teacher and seducer. In answer he promises ἄλλον παράκλητον,
“another Comforter,” who, in Tertullian’s phrase, will “vicariam navare operam,” taking his place with
them, filling them with the consolation his absence would otherwise take away, and accompanying them
in greater things than any in which he had yet employed them. He now reminds them of that promise. The
promised ὁ παράκλητος is properly “an advocate,” one who pleads the cause of an accused or guilty
person before a tribunal and is opposed τῷ κατηγόρῳ. The word is so translated elsewhere. Christ will be
their advocate with the Father and will send them an advocate to plead his own cause, which they profess,
before those in the world who slandered and condemned him as a seducer and cast that reproach on his
followers. Although the operation continues in a measure through the ministry of the word in every age,
its principal reference is to the abundant outpouring of the Spirit on the apostles at Pentecost after Christ’s
ascension. The three acts assigned to the advocate make this plain. First, “He shall reprove,” or more
properly “convince, the world of sin, because they believed not on him;” and this the Spirit did
abundantly in Peter’s sermon. The very enemies and haters of Christ were so pressed by conviction of
their sin that they cried, “Men and brethren, what shall we do to be saved?” The world was thereby
brought to confess voluntarily the sin of murdering Jesus Christ. Second, the advocate likewise convicts
the world of “righteousness, because he went to his Father;” which is Christ’s righteousness, since the
world possesses no righteousness of its own for him to establish. Those who condemned Christ as a
seducer will be convinced that he was the Son of God as he testified, rather than the blasphemer they
alleged. The pouring out of the Spirit upon his apostles makes it evident that he has gone to the Father and
been received and owned by him, as the centurion owned him immediately upon his death. Third, he will
“convince the world of judgment, because the prince of this world is judged;” for the one whom they
despised as the carpenter’s son and challenged to come down from the cross is shown to be exalted to
God’s right hand with all judgment committed to him. In his death he had already judged, sentenced, and
overcome Satan, the prince of this world, who had the power of death and was the chief instigator of his
crucifiers. This is the sense of the passage. It preserves the efficacy of the Spirit in the majesty of the
word until the end of the world, working in the same manner and for the same end though in a lesser
degree. Whoever thinks this establishes universal redemption may keep his discovery to himself, for he
will never make the connection good to others. The act and effect at issue require evidence of their own,
and the Spirit’s advocacy supplies no premise extending the intended recipients of the ransom. The
conviction in this proof may reach as far as the rim. Follow the act actually ascribed to the Spirit and we
find the grounds on which he convicts the hearers, while the extent of the ransom remains to be proved
from its own undertaking. (John 14:16; 16:7; Revelation 12:10; 1 John 2:1; Acts 2)
    [885] Proof 5. “God has testified, both by his word and his oath, that he would that his Son should so
far save as to work a redemption for all men, and likewise that he should bring all to the knowledge of the
truth, that there-through redemption might be wrought in and upon them, with the cited passage. So he
wills not, nor has any pleasure in, the death of him (even the wicked) that dies, but rather that he turn and
live. And dare any of us say, the God of truth says and swears that of which he has no inward and serious
meaning? O far be such blasphemy from us!” An oath establishes the certainty of the proposition sworn.

It cannot supply a different proposition, or extend the persons and relations declared beyond the actual
terms of the declaration. (1 Timothy 2:4; John 3:17; Ezekiel 18:23, 32; 33:11)
    [886] The first assertion, “That God testifies, by his word and oath, that he would that Christ should so
far save us,” calls God as witness to something he has never affirmed, nor entertained in his heart. His
revealed will is that Christ should save completely those who come to him. It is neither a partial salvation
nor an indefinite distance toward salvation, such as this bold and ignorant formulation falsely attributes to
him. God will not attest the inventions of false hearts. To invoke him in their defense is to provoke the
very judgment his oath is supposed to avert. The second assertion, “That Christ should so bring all to the
knowledge of the truth, that there-through redemption might be wrought in and upon them,” likewise
corrupts his word and bears false witness in his name. Seducing and wearying men does not become
innocent when the same violence is offered to God's testimony. The texts themselves expose the
corruption. God is said to “send his Son, that the world through him might be saved;” the salvation
intended is salvation “from their sins” and “to the uttermost”, not salvation to some unspecified degree.
The world is therefore the world of God's elect, since they alone receive the salvation described, as the
previous argument has established. The passage concerning God's will that all sorts of men should be
saved has also received its explanation. It supplies nothing to the assertion now placed upon it. The
remaining texts declare that God has no “pleasure at all that the wicked should die;” and “no pleasure in
the death of him that dies.” They say nothing of Christ or his death, and therefore cannot establish
universal redemption. At most their language might appear to concern a universal calling. Yet because the
passages are repeatedly entangled into arguments which ensnare inexperienced readers, their actual
subject requires a fuller examination. The authority of God's oath is certain. Whether the universal
interpretation belongs to its content is the distinct question which the objection has left unproved. (John
3:17; Matthew 1:21; Hebrews 7:25; 1 Timothy 2:4; Ezekiel 18:23, 32)
    [887] The persons addressed determine the first boundary of the proposition. These words are spoken
to and concerning the house of Israel, “Hear now, O house of Israel.” God had revealed his mind to this
people and required their repentance and conversion. His declaration that he takes no pleasure in their
death does not, by its own force, become a declaration concerning every person, including those who had
received neither this revelation of his will nor this summons to repent. The inference has therefore lost its
foundation before the meaning of willing has even been examined. That meaning supplies a second
distinction. “God wills not the death of a sinner,” must mean either “God purposes and determines he
shall not die,” or “God commands that he shall do those things wherein he may live.” If it expresses
determination, the continued death of sinners would require an explanation, since God's counsel is
immutable, “His counsel shall stand, and he will do all his pleasure.” If it expresses command, the Lord
requires those whom he calls to perform the duty in which they may live, although he knows that without
his assistance they cannot perform it. A command of this kind establishes no universal redemption. The
prophet's whole controversy supplies the third and decisive consideration. God answers the Jews'
complaint embodied in their proverb, “The fathers have eaten sour grapes, and the children’s teeth are set
on edge.” They employed it “concerning the land of Israel”, their own habitation, which the sword had
laid waste, as they alleged, for their fathers' sins while they themselves were innocent. The dispute thus
concerns the temporal judgments by which their land and nation were overthrown. God vindicates those
judgments by showing that their own sins deserved them, the very abominations for which the land
devoured and expelled its inhabitants. Their death would be just. Their blood would rest upon themselves
when the sword and the calamities they had earned cut them off. Yet the shedding of their blood and the
exposure of their corpses were not pleasures desirable in themselves, pursued merely because God willed
them. Let them abandon their abominations, and they could test whether peaceful life would follow. This
immediate and evident purpose of the passage gives no warrant for the general purpose of mercy,
universal vocation, and universal redemption which have been extracted from it. Still less does it support
the collection of blasphemous assertions presented as the fifth proof. Extension beyond the community
addressed requires grounds in the text. The mere presence of an oath does not create them. (Ezekiel 18:2,
13, 25; Psalms 147:19–20; Hebrews 6:17; Isaiah 46:10)

    [888] Proof 6. “The very words and phrases used by the Holy Ghost in Scripture, speaking of the
death of Christ, and the ransom and propitiation, to whom it belongs, and who may seek it, and in
believing find life, implies no less than all men. As to instance: “All nations”. “the ends of the earth”.
“every creature”. “all”. “every man”. “the world”. “the whole world”. “that which was lost”. “sinners”.
“unjust”. “ungodly”. And that whosoever of these repent and believe in Christ shall receive his grace.
Now, all these so often and indifferently used, were it not pride and error to devise glosses to restrain the
sense the Scripture holds forth, so full and large for all men?” The saving consequence belongs to the
entire proposition in which these terms occur. A class named without restriction does not thereby
establish every relation which the objection wishes to predicate of its members. (Matthew 28:19–20; 9:13;
Isaiah 45:22; 49:6; Mark 16:15; 2 Corinthians 5:1, 14–15; Timothy 2:6; Hebrews 2:9; John 3:2, 16–18;
Corinthians 5:19; 1 John 2:2; Luke 19:10; 1 Peter 3:18; Romans 5:6; Acts 10:43)
    [889] The accumulation of expressions does not resolve the meaning disputed in them. This argument
occupies both pages of More's book and returns continually through the same citations, but its repetition
contributes no proof, since whether all and the world must be read universally is precisely the question
between us. To affirm that they must, and then affirm it again, supplies the conclusion in place of its
ground. The descriptions of those for whom Christ died as “sinners,” “ungodly,” and “that which was
lost,” have already been shown incapable of establishing universal redemption. The passages containing
“all,” “every man,” “the world,” and “the whole world,” have likewise received repeated consideration.
The expressions governing the field of preaching require their own distinctions. “all nations” and “every
creature” do not describe an accomplished proclamation to every individual, nor even to every nation at
every time. The gospel has never in fact reached all persons, although its dispensation possesses the
fitness and suitability for universal proclamation already explained. Moreover, a command to preach
everywhere does not establish an intention in Christ to redeem everyone, because the command has
grounds and ends of its own, which have been stated. Nor does the ransom belong to everyone to whom it
is proposed. Its proposal serves further purposes, and among its hearers are those to whom Christ will
declare that he never knew them. He certainly did not lay down his life for those whom he never knew.
Finally, “The ends of the earth,” in the cited passage, are those who look to God from every region and
receive salvation, a company which cannot be identified with every individual. When Christ is appointed
a “salvation unto the end of the earth,” his work among the Gentiles corresponds to the work promised for
Israel in the same place, to “gather the preserved of Israel;” he carries God's salvation abroad by gathering
