the preserved remnant of his elect to the earth's extremities. A description common to recipients, such as
their being sinners, establishes their condition. It does not establish that all who share the condition
participate in this redemption. (Luke 19:10; Matthew 9:13; 28:19–20; 1 Peter 3:18; Romans 5:6; Mark
16:15; Isaiah 45:22; 49:6)
    [890] The final citations consequently overthrow the thesis they were assembled to confirm. If all and
the world signify no more than “all the ends of the earth,” as these passages are offered to show, their
referents are the elect and believers expressly identified in both places. Interpretation of the former
expressions by the latter therefore establishes the very limitation More denies. “If pride and error” did not
possess men's minds, they would not oppose their own reasoning and the plain text in order to maintain a
corrupt opinion. The repeated error consists in taking a predicate whose persons are determined by their
relation to salvation and treating it as an enumeration of everyone whatsoever. More examples of the
predicate cannot provide the missing premise that would justify the change. (Isaiah 45:22; 49:6)
    [891] Proof 7. “That whereas there are certain high and peculiar privileges of the Spirit contained in
the New Testament, sealed by the blood of Christ, which belong not to all men, but only to the saints, the
called and chosen of the Lord, and when they are alone distinctly mentioned, they are even so spoken of
as belonging to them only; yet many of these peculiar privileges are so spoken of as joined together with
the ransom and propitiation, which belongs to all. Then are they not spoken of in such a restraining and
exclusive manner, or with such appropriating words, but so, and with such words, as room is left to apply
the ransom to all men, in speech; and withal, so hold out the privileges to them that believe that are proper

to them, that they may both have their comfort and especial hope, and also hold forth the ransom and keep
open the door for others, in belief and receipt of the propitiation, to come in and partake with them. And
so it is said for his “sheep,” and for “many;” but nowhere but only for his sheep, or but only for many —
which is a strong proof of the ransom for all men, as is shown” Gifts and privileges may differ in their
distribution. Participation in one therefore establishes only that gift's proper relation, not a common
saving relation among everyone who receives anything. (Thomas More, The Universality of God’s Free
Grace 3, 10; Matthew 13:11; John 14:17, 21–23; 16:13–15; 17:19–20; Acts 2:38–39; 1 Corinthians 2:9,
14; Hebrews 9:15; 8; 1 Peter 2:3, 9)
    [892] This observation supplies no intelligible proof of universal redemption, because its one
potentially relevant assertion is neither true nor supported. Peculiar and exalted privileges do indeed
belong to God's saints and called people. Among them stands Christ's death on their behalf, which
belongs to them as elect, rather than presupposing that they are already saints — the benefit of his blood
makes them holy and constitutes them God's holy people. Thus “he redeemed his church with his own
blood”. He “loved and gave himself for it”. The persons redeemed are “us”. Other privileges cited here
are likewise expressly assigned to the elect, and among them, in the same order, stands Jesus' “sanctifying
himself for their sakes,” his appointment of himself as an offering. Every peculiar saving privilege
belongs to the elect alone and was purchased for them alone through Jesus Christ's blood. The second half
of the observation, that joining these privileges to the ransom leaves the ransom available for extension to
everyone, is merely asserted. A bare word cannot establish a matter of such consequence. The supposed
“leaving of room for the application,” in practice means making room even by displacing Scripture's
actual sense. Nor does the verbal distinction hold. The ransom alone, in More's own terminology, is
expressed where “many” occurs and where “sheep” occurs, just as it is where “all” occurs. The
expressions furnish no such difference as the objection needs. In several passages, moreover, the ransom
and the other peculiar privileges which flow from it are united so closely that they cannot be divided
between different populations. Those redeemed through Christ's blood are also made kings and priests.
The ransom is specifically assigned to these saved and crowned persons, distinguished from the
remaining peoples, languages, and nations out of which they were taken. Those passed over in the
payment of that ransom cannot be included by separating its fruits from its procurement. This directly
contradicts the observation's only discernible meaning. The contention concerning “sheep, and sheep
only,” has already received sufficient answer. Difference among gifts proves nothing until the objector
establishes how each gift belongs to the particular purchase whose extent is disputed. (Acts 20:28;
Ephesians 5:25; 1:3–4; Titus 2:14; John 17:19–20; Revelation 5:9–10)
    [893] Proof 8. “The restoration wrought by Christ in his own body for mankind is set forth in
Scripture to be as large and full for all men, and of as much force, as the fall of the first Adam, by and in
himself, for all men; in which respect the first Adam is said to have been a figure of Christ, the second
Adam: as is before shown.” The extent claimed for this parallel depends upon the representative relation
under each head. The identity of those participating cannot be established by assuming that the parallel
already includes them. (Thomas More, The Universality of God’s Free Grace 8; Romans 3:22–25; 5:12,
14, 18; 1 Corinthians 15:21–22, 45–47)
    [894] Adam and Christ are indeed compared through what each communicates to those who belong to
him — the first communicates disobedience and transgression, the second righteousness. Yet the
comparison concerns the efficacy of these communications, not an identical extension of their objects.
The apostle sets Christ's righteousness unto justification against Adam's sin unto condemnation, showing
that each actually produces its respective effect. As Adam's transgression brought condemning guilt upon
all his natural offspring, Christ's righteousness procured the free gift of grace unto justification for all his
spiritual offspring, the children entrusted to him by the Father. The populations are constituted by
different relations to their heads. To preserve the efficacy asserted in both cases is therefore to preserve
those relations, not to infer that both heads represent the same population merely because each effect
reaches all who belong to its source. (Romans 5:12, 18)

    [895] The next passage concerns resurrection from the dead, and specifically the resurrection of
believers. Although the apostle says, “In Christ shall all be made alive,” he immediately determines these
persons as those who are “Christ’s.” The remaining dead will also rise, but the subject here is resurrection
to glory through the efficacy of Christ's own resurrection, and this plainly does not belong to everyone.
The nature of the life bestowed and the identification of its recipients together establish the scope within
which the word all operates. Neither can be removed while the universal expression is retained as proof.
(1 Corinthians 15:21–23)
    [896] The further comparison, leaving aside the variant reading of the passage, concerns the principles
entrusted to Adam and Christ for communication to others. “Adam a living soul,” or a “living creature;”
possesses natural life as a principle communicable to his posterity. “Christ a quickening Spirit,”
communicates spiritual life and grace to those who are his. Every comparison between the two converges
upon this relation — they stand as common roots or stocks from which those engrafted in them receive
what each contains. Generation establishes natural participation in Adam. Regeneration establishes
spiritual participation in Christ. From Adam come sin, guilt, and disobedience. From Christ come
righteousness, peace, and justification. The number participating under each head lies outside the apostle's
purpose in instituting these comparisons. His concern is their respective communication, whose distinct
sources and modes of participation must be retained before the persons belonging to either can be
identified. (1 Corinthians 15:45)
    [897] The declaration “All have sinned, and come short of the glory of God,” completes the apostle's
preceding demonstration of the universal need for salvation through Jesus Christ alone. It does not leave
the recipients of Christ's righteousness and redemption undefined. Their extent is expressly given, “unto
all and upon all them that believe”, Jews and Gentiles alike, “for there is no difference.” Sin is predicated
universally. Justification is predicated of those specified by faith. Because the predicates express different
relations, the scope of the first cannot determine the scope of the second independently of the
qualification the apostle supplies. (Romans 3:22–23)
    [898] Proof 9. “The Lord Jesus Christ has sent and commanded his servants to preach the gospel to all
nations, to every creature, and to tell them withal that whoever believes and is baptized shall be saved:
and his servants have so preached to all. And our Lord Jesus Christ will make it to appear one day that he
has not sent his servants upon a false errand, nor put a lie in their mouths, nor wished them to dissemble,
in offering that to all which they knew belonged but to some, even to fewest of all, but to speak truth.”
Christ's commission appoints the field in which his ministers proclaim the gospel. It does not disclose to
them a separate account of his hidden saving purpose toward every hearer within it. (Matthew 28:19–20;
Mark 16:15–16; 2 Corinthians 5:19; Romans 10:13, 18; Isaiah 44:26; 61:8; 1 Timothy 1:12)
    [899] The propositions affirmed here are true without establishing the consequence sought from them.
Christ commanded the apostles to “preach the gospel to all nations and every creature,” and to declare
“that whosoever believes shall be saved”. Their summons to repentance and faith was to recognize no
distinction of nation or person wherever providence directed them, subject to those restraints which the
Spirit himself imposed upon their journeys. They obeyed by beseeching men throughout the nations to be
reconciled to God, withholding their ministry only where they were forbidden, and carrying the gospel
toward the ends of the earth instead of confining it to Jewry. Equally certain is the truthfulness of their
commission — Christ supplied no lie, and did not authorize an offer whose stated promise was false.
These truths need no defense. What requires proof is the proposed connection between such proclamation
and a purchase intended to save every person. Its existence cannot be assumed as the ground of the
command and then derived from that command as though it had been demonstrated. (Matthew 28:19–20;
Mark 16:15–16; Acts 16:6–7; 2 Corinthians 5:19–20; Romans 10:18)
    [900] The intended inference may be that ministers cannot offer Christ to every hearer unless he died
for every hearer. This inference fails at both its scope and its account of the offer. The gospel has never
been preached to every individual, nor do the passages cited affirm that it has. Universal redemption

would still require proof concerning those who never hear as well as those who do. What ministers
actually offer is life and salvation through Christ upon faith and repentance. The offer is true because
everyone who believes will be saved, and its truth remains inviolate while Christ possesses all sufficiency
to save all who come to him. Ministers are entrusted with God's commands and promises, not an
inspection of his intentions, purposes, and counsels toward each individual. To tell a man that the believer
will be saved consequently remains true even if Christ did not die for some of those addressed. An
argument which calls that declaration a lie assumes precisely what it must establish. The revealed
condition and the promise constitute the truth of the offer without requiring the preacher to determine
beforehand the saving state of every hearer.
     [901] Those addressed by the commission stand before the preacher as hearers of a true invitation and
promise. The command establishes the breadth of his address. The further operations of grace establish its
saving issue. His appointed act thus belongs to an order larger than his own knowledge of its personal
extent, and he can faithfully perform that act without first inspecting whom the whole order will bring to
faith.
                             [902] ∀x∈Dm,Preachm(c,x),           c: Fx⇒Sx. (60)
     [903] Terms. D(m) denotes the field appointed to the minister's address and extended by the
commission. C denotes the saving content communicated, including here the conditional promise. The
formula states both the duty to address everyone within that field and the truth of the content proclaimed
to them.
     [904] Consequence. The universal quantifier attaches in the first instance to the commanded address.
Its transfer to the distinct predicate of representative procurement would require a further premise.
Proclamation can therefore extend throughout its appointed field while the actual believing of those who
hear continues to depend upon the effectual provision previously established. The herald may carry his
commission as far as the rim of the wheel. His commission tells him whom he is to address, and the
saving work already examined tells us what brings a hearer to faith.
    [905] Proof 10. “The Lord wills believers to pray even for the unjust and their persecutors; yea, even
‘for all men;’ yea, even ‘for kings and all in authority,’ when few in authority loved Christianity. Yet he
said not, some of that sort, but, ‘For all in authority;’ and that on this ground, — it is good in the sight of
God, ‘who will have all men saved, and come to the knowledge of the truth.’ Surely there is a door of life
opened for all men; for God has not said to the seed of Israel, ‘Seek you me in vain.’ He will not have his
children pray for vain things.” Prayer belongs to the disciple's revealed obligation toward others. Its
commanded performance establishes that obligation, while the representative intention of Christ remains
a distinct matter whose content requires its own testimony. (Matthew 5:44, 48; Luke 6:28; 10:5; 1
Timothy 2:1–4; 2 Timothy 1:10; Isaiah 44:19)
    [906] The apparent force of this argument depends upon six suppositions, each of which fails. It first
treats indefinite assertions as equivalent to universal assertions, although no such equivalence exists. It
next assumes that “all,” cannot mean all sorts of men distributively, despite the apostle's enumeration of
different sorts, which plainly determines the distribution intended. Third, it assumes an obligation to pray
for the salvation of every single person. No warrant, rule, command, or example establishes this. The
apostolic precept, the Savior's own example, and God's counsel and purpose so far as generally revealed
oppose it. Prayer for all extends to all sorts without excluding any sort, and asks that those appointed to
eternal life may believe. Fourth, the argument assumes that salvation through Christ is the only good we
may seek for others in prayer, which is manifestly false. Fifth, it makes certainty that Christ died for a
particular person the ground upon which prayer for him rests. This also is untrue. Sixth, it measures our
duty by conformity to God's secret purpose and counsel. Unless all six suppositions can be established,
there is no strength in the argument, “We must pray for all; therefore God intends by the death of Christ
to save all and every one.” Its weakness and sophistry remain evident. God's command certainly
determines our duty. Our duty does not, by a reverse inference, disclose the content of his secret purpose.

The indefinite assertion, the conditional promise, and the effectual intention must therefore retain the
distinct logical relations proper to each. The breadth of prayer thus raises its own question at the rim. The
petitions commanded for men of every station must be read according to what is sought for them, and the
price paid by Christ according to the persons and benefits assigned to it. (Romans 4; 5; 9:11–12, 15; 11:7;
1 Timothy 2:1; 1 John 5:16; John 17:9; Jeremiah 29:7; Acts 8:22, 24)
    [907] Proof 11. “The Lord has given forth his word and promise to be with his servants so preaching
the gospel to all, and with his people so praying for all where they come, that they may go on with
confidence in both. The assistance promised to ministers belongs to their faithful performance of the work
appointed to them. The promise receives its determination from that office and its duties. (Matthew 28:20;
1 Timothy 2:3, 8; Luke 10:5; Isaiah 54:17)
    [908] God's promised presence with his people in preaching and prayer follows their obedience to his
will and the duties of their calling. This is certain, and equally certain is its failure to establish universal
redemption. Assistance within an appointed duty secures the relation declared in the promise. It does not
thereby communicate a saving benefit to every person whom the obedient minister encounters.
    [909] Proof 12. “The Lord has already performed and made good his word to his servants and people,
upon some of all sorts of men and all sorts of sinners, showing them mercy to the very end, that none
might exclude themselves, but all be encouraged to repent, believe, and hope thereby.” Particular
instances show that salvation actually operates among the different classes named. They establish the
reality of that operation where it occurs, while leaving unproved the claim that every individual receives
the same saving issue. (Acts 2; 3; 8–11; 16; 19; 28; 1 Corinthians 6:10–11; 1 Timothy 1:13–16)
    [910] A demonstration that God had fulfilled his word by saving every individual would indeed
establish the opinion at issue. The evidence actually offered is different — he has shown mercy to some
from every sort, including every sort of sinner, so that others of the same sorts, the elect still awaiting
their calling, might be encouraged to believe. In that form the proof abandons its own cause and confirms
the opposing account. God's acts declare that he saves through Jesus' blood some of every sort, as the
doctrine of particular redemption affirms, rather than every person, as More's universal proposition
requires. The distinction is decisive because the evidence concerns a distribution among classes and the
conclusion an exhaustive distribution among individuals. The latter cannot be obtained by merely
restating the former.
    [911] Proof 13. “The blessing of life has streamed in this doctrine of the love of God to mankind; yea,
in the tender and spiritual discovery of the grace of God to mankind (in the ransom given and atonement
made by Christ for all men, with the fruits thereof) has God, in the first place, overcome his chosen ones
to believe and turn to God.” The fruits of grace as proclaimed belong to the truth and operation by which
they are produced. Their presence attests that actual cause. It cannot establish every further interpretation
that a preacher attaches to it. (Acts 13:48; Titus 2:11, 13; 3:4–5)
    [912] The freedom of God's grace, the surpassing greatness of his eternal love, and his sending of the
Son to recover men from sin and Satan are indeed powerful motives to conversion. When applied by the
Spirit of grace they are a certain operative principle of the conversion of the elect. Our hearts rejoice in
that love, are bound to God through it, and desire every moment to answer it in thankful obedience. Yet
neither the effectiveness of this truth nor any part of its effectiveness arises from extending that love
indiscriminately to everyone. Such an extension is false and corrupts God's word, as already shown. A lie
cannot supply the good consequence claimed for it. It also removes the force of the motive itself. God's
intense and incomparable love for his elect is reduced to a common inclination or desire, contrary to the
perfection of his nature, which fails of its own end and could coexist with the eternal destruction of the
whole human race. The further controversy concerning the cause of Christ's sending would disclose this
consequence at greater length. Nor do the texts cited contain the alleged common love. The “grace” in the
first passage certainly brings salvation, which common love does not, and causes the sending of Christ,
“that he might redeem us from all iniquity, and purify to himself a peculiar people, zealous of good

works.” Redemption and sanctification are there the immediate purpose of his offering, a conjunction
already shown destructive of universal redemption. The “love and kindness” in the next passage likewise
brings the “washing of regeneration and renewing of the Holy Ghost”, justification, and adoption to the
inheritance of eternal life. Its fruits determine whether this love is common or peculiar. The further
citation describes God's mercy in Christ extending through gospel preaching to Gentiles as well as Jews,
according to Isaiah's prophecy. The passage cited within it restricts the love to the elect as clearly as could
be desired. Its enlargement among nations gives no warrant for universal grace, but affirms what is
elsewhere expressed by “gathering together in one the children of God that were scattered abroad.” The
saving efficacy belongs to grace in its actual content and operation. Enlarging its proposed domain while
removing what it certainly effects does not preserve the same grace. (Titus 2:11, 13; 3:4–5, 7; Acts 13:47–
48; Isaiah 49:6; John 11:52)
    [913] Clement's urgent exhortation to good works immediately follows his exclusion of works as the
ground of justification. This conjunction exposes the objection that grace destroys obedience, because the
command to act and the ground upon which grace is received occupy distinct places within the saving
order. An act proceeding from grace does not become the meritorious cause of the grace from which it
proceeds. The earnestness of the command establishes the seriousness of obedience. Whoever makes it
establish that reversed causal relation must supply a further proof. (Clement of Rome, First Epistle to the
Corinthians 32–33)
    [914] Proof 14. “Those that, when the gospel comes, and any spiritual light therein, to them, when
they refuse to believe, and suffer themselves to be withdrawn by other things, they are affirmed to love or
choose ‘darkness rather than light’, (which how could it be, if no light in truth were for them?) in
following lying vanities; to forsake their own mercies; to harden their own hearts; to lose their souls; and
to destroy themselves. And they being from Adam fallen into darkness, hardness, and their souls [lost],
and death passed on them, how could these things be if by Jesus Christ no life had been attained, no
atonement made, no restoration of their souls, nor means procured and used, that they might be saved?
God is no hard master, to gather where he has not strewn.” The hearer's rejection of the message is his
own actual deed and carries its proper moral responsibility. That responsibility establishes the guilt of
refusal without independently defining the persons for whom saving procurement was made. (John 3:19;
Jonah 2:8; Romans 2:5; Matthew 16:26; Hosea 13:9)
    [915] The argument passes from the truth that unbelievers destroy themselves by rejecting the
preached gospel to the conclusion that Christ died for every individual in the world. Although such a
consequence scarcely merits an answer, several distinctions remove whatever difficulty it might leave
with an inexperienced reader. First, the gospel has not reached everyone. Since the beginning, the greater
part of mankind has been passed over in the distribution of the means of grace, “winked at.” Those
persons are therefore excluded even from the proposed inference, which makes it useless for universal
redemption, since the exclusion of a single soul from the intention of the ransom overturns that
universality. Second, destruction does not arise from refusing to believe that Christ died for every person
who ever lived or will live. Scripture nowhere commands that belief. It arises from refusing the all-
sufficiency of his suffering and offering for sinners, and thus failing to receive the mercy procured
through it upon the terms disclosed in the gospel. Those terms concern the sufficiency and efficacy of
Christ's death for all who duly receive him, not an inspection of God's intention concerning the persons
for whom he should die. He alone is the true way, life, and light. No other name under heaven gives
salvation. The cited passage calls the refusal a “loving darkness rather than light.” Its μᾶλλον, “rather,”
opposes love of light to a full affection for darkness. It does not compare two concurrent loves as though
darkness were merely preferred while light was also loved. The persons said to do this are “men”. Were
More's rule applied consistently, this indefinite expression would mean every person. It is sufficient
instead to understand the greater part of those who heard Christ, since some “received him,” to whom he
“gave this privilege, that they should become the sons of God.” Responsibility for refusing and the

effectual cause of believing remain distinct inquiries, each determined by its proper subject and operation.
(Romans 2:14; Acts 14:16; 17:30; John 3:19; 1:12)
    [916] The substitution of “choose,” for “love” has no foundation either in the relation between the
words or in the original term. Loving and choosing are different acts. To translate ἠμάπησαν as “they
choose.” would reproduce in translation the same error committed in interpretation. Loving darkness
more than light consists in an affection and practice which cling to the ways already followed — men
alienated from God's life continue in darkness's unfruitful works and refuse the heavenly doctrine offering
peace and reconciliation through Christ, with life and immortality. The fact that most who heard Christ
refused this gospel cannot establish that he died for everyone in the world. The contrary inference has far
greater force, since it is not “given unto them, for his sake, to believe on him.” Election is not identical
with an expressed attitude toward a message. The relation between them must be the relation the text
actually declares. (Philippians 1:29)
    [917] The parenthesis “Which how could it be, if no light in truth were for them?” does not repair the
inference. If “for” denotes God's intention and purpose, it cannot rightly be said that he purposes the
reception of light by those who never receive it. That would make the Strength of Israel mutable like
ourselves and contradict his declaration, “My counsel shall stand, and I will do all my pleasure.” “The
counsel of the Lord stands for ever”. He is “the Lord, and changing not”. If instead “for them,” signifies
the fullness and sufficiency of light and grace, as sunlight is sufficient for all although blindness prevents
some from seeing, then the gospel does provide such light to all who hear it, and their blindness alone
accounts for their failure to receive it. This establishes nothing further for the objection. The stone rolls
back to its former place. Communicated light determines the provision before the hearer and the
responsibility of rejecting it. It does not by that rejection establish all the saving effects which the
argument appends to the communication. (Isaiah 46:10; Psalms 33:11; Malachi 3:6; James 1:17; 2
Timothy 2:19; Romans 9:11)
    [918] The remaining passages are still further removed from the disputed relation. One concerns those
who desert the true God for idols and thereby forfeit temporal and spiritual mercies formerly received
from him. Another describes Gentiles instructed by God's works and granted time by his patience, who
use both occasions for vile rebellion and acquire further hardness of heart. The references to losing one's
soul and destroying oneself through sin add nothing different to the preceding argument. Each passage
has its own authoritative subject. Their accumulation cannot supply an unexpressed connection between
forfeited mercies, aggravated sin, and the intention of Christ's saving ransom. (Jonah 2:8; Romans 2:5;
Matthew 16:26; Hosea 13:9)
     [919] The conclusion of the argument appears, however, to rest upon a further proposition concealed
at first by its wording. It treats all mankind as actually restored by Christ to grace and favor, not merely as
standing before an open door of mercy, so that salvation follows unless they fall from this restored state.
Its supposed proof is that those already lost in Adam could not lose themselves unless Christ had first
restored them. Those already dark and hardened could not love darkness or harden themselves unless
Christ had first enlightened and softened them all. This plainly appears to be the intended position, and it
requires examination in two respects — the inference by which it is obtained, and the restored condition
itself. A universal change of state introduces actual predicates and an actual operation which cannot
remain hidden within the language of culpable refusal.
    [920] The inference denies that men already liable to death and damnation through original sin can
aggravate and secure that condemnation through actual sins, bringing upon themselves a further death
unto death. It likewise denies that native hardness can be increased by the acquired hardness of continued
rebellion, or that blindness can undervalue light, although blindness is precisely why light is undervalued.
Finally, it assumes that men supplied with time, opportunity, and means for saving their souls cannot be
said to lose them through condemnation unless those souls had previously occupied a saved condition.
This is among the arguments later praised as “plain, and according to Scripture,” although it contradicts

reason, Scripture, and the first principles of God's oracles. For hearers of the gospel, refusal of the remedy
offered, joined to innumerable other offenses against the Lord, suffices to establish responsibility for their
own condemnation. Original liability does not prevent its personal aggravation. Nor does that liability
establish its supposed universal removal. The latter requires positive testimony which this inference never
provides.
    [921] The proposition itself is an actual restoration of all men by Christ to the covenantal condition
they possessed in Adam, though not to his innocence. This understanding is confirmed by More's express
assertion elsewhere that all are justified through Christ, even though he cannot explain how. Nothing in
Scripture gives even a plausible appearance of support to this gross error. It directly contradicts the
declarations that we are “dead in trespasses and sins”, that “except we be born again, we cannot see the
kingdom of God”, and that until faith brings us to Christ, “the wrath of God abides on us”, together with
the innumerable passages describing mankind's universal estrangement until actual peace and
reconciliation are made through him. It also contradicts the constitution of the new covenant of grace.
Proceeding from God's free mercy to his elect, that covenant carries distinguishing promises from its
beginning to its end, separating the woman's seed from the serpent's in the members as well as the Head.
It actually effects every promised good in all to whom it belongs, which it certainly does not accomplish
in everyone. Scripture consistently makes it with God's people, those he owns in distinction from the
world. None of these plentifully attested properties can survive a universal restoration into covenant. The
proposition further opposes the eternal purpose of election and reprobation, since reprobation resolves to
leave men in their fallen state without restoration through Christ. Finally, it entails a series of absurd and
groundless consequences. These consequences disclose what an actually restored condition would have to
communicate to every individual included within it. (Ephesians 2:1; John 3:3, 36)
    [922] [1.] Every infant dying before rational action and actual sin would necessarily be saved,
although the Savior declares “except a man be born again, he cannot see the kingdom of God”, and Paul
calls the children of unbelievers “unclean”, while nothing unclean enters the new Jerusalem. Infants of
Turks, pagans, unbelievers, and persecutors dying in infancy would thus enjoy a condition more secure
than Christ's apostles and the best believers, who remain, according to this doctrine's advocates, exposed
to eternal ruin. [2.] Salvation would require only continuance in one's birth-state, since everyone would be
born actually restored by Christ into covenant. Yet the whole testimony of God's word declares that
continuance in that condition brings everlasting destruction. [3.] Every person who perishes would have
fallen from new-covenant grace, although the covenant promises exclude total falling away among its
members. [4.] Only those who had personally fallen from Christ could come to him, because all others
would already abide in him. The conclusion concerning infants depends upon the stated premise of
universal removal. It exposes that premise's consequence rather than furnishing independent proof that the
removal occurred. (John 3:3; 1 Corinthians 7:14; Revelation 21:27)
    [923] Many further consequences of the same kind necessarily accompany this heretical assertion,
which destroys the freedom of God's grace. Arguments offered as its support should therefore move
attentive readers to investigate the proposition more closely and discover the falsehood governing it.
These consequences are not detachable accidents — they follow from the scope of the restoration
asserted. Its defenders must either accept them or qualify their premise in a manner consistent with the
condition they claim for everyone.
    [924] The final words receive their answer from the provision actually made. God sowed the seed in
Adam and watered it with innumerable temporal blessings toward all, adding spiritual blessings in some.
He will require its fruit from the unbelieving world. That requirement does not rest upon Christ's blood
except where that blood has actually been proposed to hearers and despised by them. Provision through
Adam and saving restoration through Christ belong to their respective appointed orders. The conditions of
the former cannot be transferred into the subsequent covenant without proof.

    [925] Proof 15. “God’s earnest expostulations, contendings, charges, and protestations, even to such
as whereof many perished. As, to instance:— ‘O that there were such an heart in them, that they would
fear me,’ etc. ‘that it might be well with them!’. ‘What could have been done more to my vineyard, that I
have not done in it?’ etc. ‘What iniquity have your fathers found in me, that they are gone far from me?’.
‘Have I been a wilderness unto Israel? a land of darkness? wherefore say my people, We are lords; we
will come no more unto you?’. ‘O my people, what have I done unto you? wherein have I wearied you?
testify against me.’ ‘How often would I have gathered,’ etc. ‘and you would not!’. ‘O that my people had
hearkened unto me!’ etc. ‘I should soon have subdued their enemies,’ etc. ‘Because I have called, and you
refused; I have stretched out my hand, and no man regarded,’ etc. ‘Because, when they knew God, they
glorified him not as God,’ etc. ‘Therefore you are inexcusable, O man,’ etc. ‘You, after your hardness and
impenitent heart, treasure up unto yourself wrath,’ etc. No Christian, I hope, will reply against God, and
say, ‘You never meant us good; there was no ransom given for us, no atonement made for us, no good
done us, no mercy shown us, — nothing, in truth, whereby we might have been saved, nothing but an
empty show, a bare pretense.’ But if any should reason so evilly, yet shall not such answers stand.”
Expostulation declares a norm, passes judgment, and summons a response. Its moral force belongs to
those operations. It does not dissolve the distinction between what God commands and what he
effectually purposes to accomplish. (Romans 9:27; 1:21, 28; 2:1, 5; Isaiah 10:22; 5:4–5; Deuteronomy
5:29; Jeremiah 2:5, 31; Micah 6:3; Matthew 23:37; Psalms 81:13–14; Proverbs 1:24–31)
    [926] Several observations determine the bearing of these expostulations. First, none mentions a
ransom or atonement for those who perish, despite the assertion appended to them. Their subject is
temporal mercy and the outward means of grace. As the preceding answer showed, God does not rebuke
these persons on the proposed ground, nor will they be able to raise that ground against him at judgment.
There is certainly sufficient occasion to plead with sinners concerning Christ's blood and ransom — such
pleading draws the elect toward faith and repentance, binds believers more closely to the abandonment of
ungodliness and worldly lusts for the service of him who died for them, and leaves others increasingly
without excuse. Yet no expostulation here declares an intention in God through Christ to save those who
finally perish. Second, except for the passages which ground guilt in the knowledge of God's eternity,
almighty power, and providence available through creation, without reference to ransom or atonement, all
the citations concern recipients of the means of grace. At the time addressed these formed only a small
portion of mankind. What is said to them cannot establish God's purpose concerning everyone else, and
thus cannot establish a general ransom. Third, all men, especially those enjoying means of grace, receive
sufficient mercy for God justly to charge their ingratitude and their failure to render obedience
proportionate to the light received. Fourth, God could effect in their hearts, through his exceeding power,
every good whose absence occasions the rebuke, if he pleased to do so. The rebukes therefore cannot
simply declare a purpose which he could certainly fulfill, “for who has resisted his will.” Fifth, desires
and wishes cannot be properly ascribed to the all-sufficient and perfect God, any more than bodily eyes,
ears, or hands. Such language must be understood θεοπρεπῶς. Sixth, the texts fervently declare duty
under the means of grace, convict stubborn disobedience, and vindicate the excellence of God's ways in
drawing men to obedience. The appended conclusion that Christ died for everyone is no consequence of
those truths, however confidently presented as ὅπερ ἔδει δείξαι. Seventh, particular passages appearing
weightier than the rest have already been examined. Each rebuke receives its meaning from the duty and
revelation it actually contains. Responsibility before that revelation and God's hidden decree remain
distinct relations. (Romans 1:28; 2:5; 9:19; Psalms 147:19–20)
     [927] Proof 16. “The Scripture’s manner of setting forth the sin of such as despise and refuse this
grace, and their estate, and the persons perishing; as to say they ‘turn the grace of God into wantonness’;
‘tread under foot the Son of God, profane the blood of the covenant, with which they were sanctified,
offer despite to the Spirit of grace’; ‘deny the Lord that bought them’; ‘they perish for whom Christ died’;
‘trees twice dead, plucked up by the roots’; ‘and bring upon themselves swift destruction.’ And how could
all this be if God had given his Son in no sort for them? if Christ had shed no blood to procure remission
for them? if he had not bought them, nor had any grace or life by his Spirit to bestow on them?” Guilt is

aggravated by contempt for the privilege actually bestowed. The character of that privilege determines the
contempt, while the disputed saving relation still requires evidence proper to itself. (Jude 1:4, 12–13;
Hebrews 10:29; 2 Peter 2:1; 1 Corinthians 8:11)
    [928] Three passages in this proof recur continually in the controversy and have already received
separate and extensive examination. Their actual meaning was shown to resist the assertion toward which
they are violently bent. Repetition does not correct that distortion. The remaining citations contribute no
intelligible connection to universal redemption. Those said to “turn the grace of God into wantonness,”
abuse the gospel's doctrine and the mercy disclosed through it as an encouragement to sin. Their abuse
cannot establish that Christ died for everyone. Indeed, the apostle indicates the opposite concerning these
persons by describing them as “before of old ordained to condemnation,” an ordination directly opposed
to the love through which God sent his Son to procure anyone's salvation. Whatever force the argument
possesses must therefore lie in the passages already answered. Their contextual qualifications remain in
place when they are cited again. (Hebrews 10:29; 2 Peter 2:1; 1 Corinthians 8:11; Jude 1:4, 12–13)
    [929] Proof 17. “Jesus Christ, by virtue of his death, shall be their judge, and by the gospel, in which
they might have been saved, will he judge them to a second death; and how can that be, if he never died
the first death for them, and if there were not truth in his gospel preached to them?.” Universal judgment
establishes Christ's authority over every person subject to it. The extension of that jurisdiction cannot by
itself identify the persons represented in his saving satisfaction. (Romans 14:9–12; 2:16; Philippians 2:7–
11; John 12:47–48, 50)
    [930] Christ's appointment as judge of all and the present commitment of all judgment to him are
freely acknowledged. Neither establishes that he died for everyone, as already shown, unless his judgment
of devils is also to establish his death for them. The further assertion that everyone, including those who
never heard the gospel, will be judged by it contradicts its own declaration, “For as many as have sinned
without law shall also perish without law: and as many as have sinned in the law shall be judged by the
law.” Judgment proceeds according to the light and rule which a person possessed or could have
possessed, not according to revelation of which he was invincibly deprived. The expression that Christ
suffered only the first death is also neither scriptural language nor an inference Scripture supplies. He
suffered the death contained in the law's curse. Finally, the suggestion that gospel preaching would be
false unless Christ died for everyone assumes an assertion especially hostile to its truth. The passages
cited establish his lordship over all, exaltation above all, and judgment of all, together with judgment
according to the gospel for those who possess it. They furnish no passage from that jurisdiction to
universal saving representation. The offices remain distinguishable, and the consequences proper to one
cannot be inferred merely from the extension of the other. The authority of the Judge reaches the whole
wheel. The scope of the price is a further question, for the persons summoned to judgment are identified
by one office and the persons represented in the offering by another. (Romans 2:12)
    [931] Proof 18. “Believers are exhorted to contend for the faith of this common salvation, which was
once delivered to the saints. Which some having heard oppose, and others turn the offers of it into
wantonness, and, through not heeding and not walking in the faith of this salvation, already wrought by
Christ for men, they deprive themselves of, and wind out themselves from, that salvation, which Christ by
his Spirit, in application of the former, has wrought in them, and so deprive themselves of the salvation to
come. A common salvation is a good shared among those who participate in it. The adjective declares
their communion without identifying everyone whatsoever as a participant. (Jude 1:3–5)
    [932] “And every [one] of these proofs be plain and according to Scripture, and each of force, how
much more altogether! — still justifying the sense that and imports, and the truth of the proposition in the
beginning.” Arguments accumulate strength by contributing warranted premises to a conclusion.
Repeated forms of the same unsupported inference contribute no new warrant and therefore leave the
original defect intact. (1 Timothy 2:6; Hebrews 2:9)

    [933] The only discernible ground here is that salvation purchased through Christ is called “common
salvation.” Were this sufficient to make salvation common to everyone, faith would also belong to
everyone because it is called “common faith”, despite its description as “faith of God’s elect.” Believers
constitute a community, and a good extended throughout God's whole church is common among them —
totus mundus ex toto mundo. Their common salvation is that through which they are all actually saved. It
gives no support to the strange common salvation which, in this disputant's account, saves no one. The
remaining words follow More's persuasion while repeatedly contradicting God's word and diminishing
Christ's merits, since they make his purchased salvation ineffective in itself and leave its efficacy to the
acceptance or rejection of sinful, corrupted, accursed men. The community possessing the good
determines its common possession. Sharing within that community does not transform participation into
an unrestricted predicate of all humanity. (Titus 1:1, 4)
    [934] More describes these proofs as “plain and according to Scripture,” and in them recapitulates
almost the whole book, omitting nothing of weight in its argument. Owen therefore selected this chapter
for a full and particular answer. The Christian reader who considers the proofs together with the replies
may judge whether Scripture plainly, clearly, and evidently confirms that Christ paid a ransom for every
individual, as More claims. If the collection has instead proved obscure, awkward, and often unintelligible
in expression, childish, weak, and ridiculous in inference, and perverse in its violent and mistaken reading
of Scripture, the defects have a common consequence. Ignorance, heedlessness, and corruption of
judgment have yielded claims in direct opposition to the mind and will of God there revealed. The replies
have held the same causal and logical distinctions in view throughout, and the account they establish
supplies the ground for answering the remaining objections about duty, grace, merit, and consolation.
     [935] The force of a demonstration increases with the premises which its several arguments establish.
Independent true premises may, when taken together, yield a consequence which neither affords
separately. But if the arguments all draw upon the same unproved proposition, their number adds nothing
to the evidence for it. The replies have accordingly examined what each citation supplies and what the
inference still requires.
             [936] Γ1⊢Conly ifP,       Γ2⊢Conly ifP;Γ1∪Γ2still requires grounds forP. (61)
     [937] Gamma one and Gamma two denote the premises advanced in the two arguments. P denotes a
proposition required by both, such as the extension of a scriptural expression to an unrestricted domain or
the identification of common illumination with saving renewal. The expression makes visible the
dependence which survives the multiplication of arguments.
     [938] The omission of a premise remains material however many times the reader is made to supply
it. The adversary must show that the proposition required by the several conclusions is true, that its
meaning has remained constant from one use to the next, and that the consequences of retaining the
proposed universal saving relation can be sustained. The labor of reading a collection is no part of its
demonstrative force.
    [939] The controversy closes with the objections which the Arminians have lately put into common
circulation. Others have treated these sophisms at length, and the scriptural account already established
removes the ground on which the rest depend. A brief answer is therefore sufficient, though the
confidence with which some of these arguments have been put forward makes it necessary to show where
their apparent force arises, lest the weakest reader take the assurance of the speaker for the evidence of his
case. The objections concern duty, assurance, grace, and merit. What is commanded, what is known, what
is bestowed, and what is earned each have their own grounds, and the conclusions change when those
grounds are confounded.
     [940] Objection I. Few recent arguments in this controversy have gained so much reputation on so
little evidence. The argument is as follows. “That which every one is bound to believe is true; but every
one is bound to believe that Jesus Christ died for him: therefore it is true, namely, that Jesus Christ died

for every one.” The inference depends on a universal obligation to believe the particular proposition
stated in the minor. The procurement the argument is offered to establish cannot itself establish that
obligation.
     [941] The Arminians and their friends commonly accompany this argument with encomiums and
affronts and threats against their opponents, betraying their sense of the weakness of their other
arguments, with the result that both parties have come to call it the Remonstrants’ Achilles. There is no
need to transcribe the many full answers by which our divines have repeatedly laid this Goliath on the
ground. Indeed, the very length and labor of their answers have given the argument a thousand times the
reputation which its own force or the ability of its advocates could have secured. Its apparent strength
disappears once the terms are made constant. First, the argument uses “believe,” and the term must bear
the same sense in each proposition or the syllogism fails in form. Second, the faith alleged to be
obligatory is a saving application to the soul of Christ as he is offered in the promise, for the duty alleged
is to believe that Christ died for me in particular. Third, the proposition to be believed must concern the
Father’s purpose and Christ’s own intention in dying. It is the universality of that purpose and intention
which is denied. Fourth, the argument uses “every one,” and the expression must extend to persons
considered under a common condition, since distinct conditions even in the same person give rise to
distinct obligations. The only condition common to every human being is the condition of wrath and
death. With those terms preserved in their respective relations, the minor says that “All men in the world,
as considered in a state of wrath and unregeneracy, are bound to believe, as before described, that it was
the intention of God that Christ should die for every one of them in particular.” To change the sense of
faith or the condition of the persons between the premises is to offer a different argument for the
conclusion. (Ephesians 2:3)
    [942] The major itself is false, for many have observed that what men are bound to believe in the
sense at issue is properly described as good, rather than true or false. The argument may nevertheless be
decided upon its minor. If “every one” means each individual in the world, no reason or Scripture
supports the act alleged to be obligatory in the minor. If the adversaries can establish their proposition on
that reading, their case will have won our assent. First, some would thereby be bound to believe falsely,
since it has already been proved at length that Christ did not die for every individual of humankind. Every
obligation of faith comes from the God of truth, who cannot impose an obligation to believe what is false.
Second, men would be immediately bound to believe a proposition which revelation has not given,
although revelation supplies the object of all divine faith. Scripture declares Christ’s death for sinners
indefinitely and often specifies them beforehand by the divine purpose and afterward by the obedience
purchased for them. It nowhere declares his death for this or that particular man merely as such. Third, the
intention of God presently under inquiry is not proposed as the immediate object of anyone’s faith. His
commands, promises, and threatenings are proposed, and the soul comes to assured recognition of his
intention through the experience of an infallible and welcome effect of it actually enjoyed in the heart.
Fourth, no command to believe takes its meaning from that hidden intention as if it said, “God intended
that Christ should die for you in particular;” nor does any promise contain that meaning. Fifth, the death
of Christ is not proposed to all as an object of faith at all. The act of believing presupposes hearing, and
no obligation to believe an unrevealed report can be inferred where even the first report has never been
heard. Millions of infants and of persons in nations without the gospel go to their “own place” without
hearing anything of Jesus Christ or of his sufferings for themselves or others, even in the gospel age. The
case applies with greater force before Christ’s coming in the flesh, when the means of grace were
confined to one small nation and a few proselytes. If these persons and their living counterparts were all
bound to believe that Christ died for them individually, the men who maintain that duty are surely bound
to tell the living among them what they must believe. Unbelief is the great damning sin where faith is
required, yet Paul expressly shows that many are condemned for sins committed against the light of
nature. The difference in the ground of condemnation demonstrates that the obligation alleged is not
universal. A revealed duty takes its object from what has been disclosed, and a judgment about God’s

hidden intention toward an individual needs grounds which a general command to trust Christ does not
supply. (John 3:36; Romans 2:12)
    [943] The adversaries may preserve some appearance of strength by limiting the duty to persons
called by the word. This restriction answers the difficulty concerning those who never hear. The
obligation of the hearer must still be established according to the act commanded and the object which the
command presents. They will accordingly have to put their inference in the following form.
     [944] “That which every one called by the word, to whom the gospel is preached, is bound to believe,
is true; but that Christ died for him in particular, every one so called is bound to believe: ergo,” etc. The
whole force of the amended argument lies in what can be shown to be the required belief of every hearer.
Its domain is now the hearers of the gospel.
    [945] Only the fifth objection is removed by this reformulation. Each of the preceding objections
retains the force necessary to overturn the inference. The reformulation also makes the argument useless
to the cause it was produced to defend, for the exclusion of one person would suffice to defeat a general
ransom, and the greater part of humankind has now been excluded. The innumerable multitude of all has
already become the many who are called, and it will presently give way to the few who are chosen. The
reply that the same thing would be true of all if it were proposed to all does not repair the inference. First,
an argument from scriptural obligation reaches those to whom the obligation actually extends. Second,
suppositions about what might or ought to obtain had God disposed things otherwise furnish no ground
for a conclusion about his appointment. The will displayed in the existing order supplies the ground of the
argument. Third, were the gospel preached to every person in the world, its general declaration would still
be “He that believes and is baptized shall be saved, but he that believes not shall be damned;” God has
joined faith and salvation by his appointment so that the enjoyment of the latter depends on the exercise
of the former. If the gospel were preached to the Turks and the Indians and they rejected it, they would
assuredly be condemned for refusing what they became bound to believe on hearing it. The object of that
obligation is disclosed in the proclamation that “There is none other name under heaven given among
men, whereby we must be saved,” and in the identification of Christ as the name made known in the
gospel. Their rejection would consist in refusing the wisdom and counsel of God for the salvation of
sinners by the blood of Jesus, and in refusing to believe both that a Redeemer is necessary and that Jesus
of Nazareth is that Redeemer. So Christ said to the Jews, “If you believe not that I am he, you shall die in
your sins;” their peculiar unbelief consisted in refusing to recognize as their Messiah the one whom they
saw declared to be the Son of God with power. This refusal to come upon the gospel call is the unbelief
that condemns the obstinate hearer. The proposed belief concerning Christ’s death for each individual
could never be tendered upon the rule of the gospel, and the hearers in question have never come far
enough to entertain it or set any value upon it. Limiting the audience cannot establish a proposition which
the message itself does not contain. (Acts 4:12)
   [946] The minor of the restricted syllogism therefore remains false. The preceding reasons suffice to
show it, and the nature and order of the faith actually required make the failure still clearer.
    [947] 1. Those who hear the gospel are bound to exercise the faith required for justification. A full
persuasion that God purposed and intended Christ’s death for this particular person discloses neither the
object upon which justifying faith rests nor the way by which a sinner is justified. The act of saving
reliance and the believer’s recognition that the saving intention includes him have an order of dependence
which the minor has yet to establish. (Owen, The Death of Death IV.7, editor’s note on the wording of
this sentence)
    [948] 2. The objects of belief have an order both in their nature and by the appointment of God. A
later act of apprehension comes to be required when the grounds on which it depends have been received,
just as the ascent of a ladder affords neither command nor reasonable expectation that a man should reach
the top by skipping the lower rungs. (1.) The hearer must repent and believe the gospel to be the word of
God, declaring his will and revealing Jesus Christ as the wisdom and power of God unto salvation. (2.)

He must believe that by God’s appointment faith and salvation are inseparably joined, so that the faith of
the gospel carries the sinner out of dependence on himself and his righteousness. (3.) The Spirit brings
home to him the need of a Redeemer for his own soul, so that he becomes weary, heavy laden, and
burdened. (4.) He then deliberately and fully rests the weight of his soul upon Christ as promised in the
gospel, persuaded of the sufficiency of a Savior who can deliver and save to the utmost those who come
to God by him. The preciousness of his blood and the sufficiency of his ransom establish his readiness,
ability, and willingness to save every soul that freely gives itself to him to be saved, and the hearer
resolves to be among them. No step of this order requires an inquiry into the particular persons for whom
God intended Christ’s death, since every hearer has sufficient assurance in the gospel that his death
benefits those who believe and obey him. The dependencies among the objects govern the order in which
they are apprehended.
     [949] The believer comes to the further duty of assurance after this order of faith has been realized. As
he finds the fruits of Christ’s death in himself and in its effects toward him, he has grounds for assuring
his soul of the goodwill and eternal love of God who sent his Son to die for him in particular. The
contrary course would have a man assure himself that God intended Christ’s death for him before he had
been persuaded (1.) of the truth of the gospel in general, (2.) that faith is the only way of salvation, (3.)
that he himself needs a Savior, or (4.) that Christ has sufficient power to save and recover him if he gives
himself up to him in the appointed way. Such a demand sets the conclusion before every ground on which
it rests and overturns the order of the gospel. Only those who have come to Christ in the manner described
are bound to believe the proposition at issue. The fruits enter their knowledge as evidence of the saving
relation which produced them, and the order in which they come to recognize that relation leaves its
causal priority intact.
     [950] The hearer first receives the Savior and the promise as proclaimed, and the actual exercise of
reliance supplies the personal premise from which the believer can know his own interest in Christ. Both
the saving object and the relevant condition must therefore be known before the inference can be made.
An obligation to reach the personal conclusion before the acts which warrant it would require knowledge
in the reverse of its appointed order.
     [951] Kx(Fx⇒Sx),      KxFx⇒KxSx        through the stated warrant of the Spirit and promise. (62)
    [952] The knowledge operator denotes recognition warranted under the theological conditions already
specified. The personal premise is actual faith, whose distinction from outward profession was established
above. An assertion of certainty does not supply that premise.
    [953] Assurance has grounds proper to the kind of knowledge it affords. The saving implication gives
the believer one premise, and his recognition of faith in himself gives him the other. From these he comes
to know the particular saving relation without making his knowledge the cause of the faith by which he
recognizes it.
   [954] The argument may now be put in a form that specifies the condition of those who are actually
bound to draw the particular conclusion. In that form the extent of the conclusion is fixed by the very
premises on which it depends.
    [955] “That which every one, convinced of the necessity of a Savior, and of the right way of salvation,
hungering, thirsting, and panting after Jesus Christ, as able alone to give him refreshment, is bound to
believe, is true; but every such a one is bound to believe that Christ died for him in particular: ergo, it is
true.” The whole inference may be granted without injury to the doctrine maintained here. (1.) The duty
of believing that Christ died for oneself belongs to those whose hunger and reliance are as described, and
the mere fact of being called by the word does not give every hearer that duty whatever his condition. (2.)
The precept to believe with fiduciary confidence that Christ died for oneself is neither proposed to every
hearer nor binding upon every hearer. Its nonperformance is sinful only insofar as it lies in the root and
habit of unbelief, the failure to turn to God in Christ for mercy. (3.) No reprobate for whom Christ did not

die is condemned for failing to believe falsely that Christ died for him in particular. His condemnation
arises from his refusal of the truths to which he was actually called, all of which are true with respect to
him. (4.) The command to believe in Christ, urged with particular confidence as a command to all, entails
the disputed obligation only when its requisite conditions are met. (5.) The command to “believe on the
name of Jesus Christ,” requires that we rest upon him for salvation. It does not make the particular
intention of God in Christ’s death the immediate object of faith. (6.) What God testifies, and what we are
bound to attest as true, is this. “He that has the Son has life, but he that has not the Son of God has not
life”. Reprobates who disbelieve that testimony do what lies in their power to make God a liar, and their
condemnation for it is just. The reader may pursue the argument further in Piscator, Perkins, Twisse, the
Synod of Dort, Du Moulin, Baronius, Rutherford, Spanheim, and Amesius, among others. (1 John 3:23;
5:12; Isaiah 50:11)
    [956] Obj. II. “That doctrine which fills the minds and souls of poor miserable sinners with doubts and
scruples whether they ought to believe or no, when God calls them thereunto, cannot be agreeable to the
gospel. But this does the doctrine of the particularity of redemption. It fills the minds of sinners with
scruples and fears whether they may believe or no, and that because they are uncertain whether it was the
intention of God that Christ should die for them in particular or no, seeing it is supposed that he died not
for all, but only for his elect; whereupon the soul, when it is called upon to believe, may justly fall a-
questioning whether it will be available or no for him so to do, and whether it be his duty or no, seeing he
knows not whether Christ died for him or no.” The question is whether the doctrine itself supplies
grounds for such hesitation. That can be determined only by examining what the gospel requires the
sinner to know before coming.
     [957] 1. Experience sufficiently shows that unbelief, while it remains unconquered, produces fears
and scruples which sometimes oppose the truth of the gospel directly and sometimes take occasion from
it. The objection has force only if particular effectual redemption in its own nature gives persons who
rightly perform their duty grounds for hesitation. It gives them none. Where corruption and unbelief set
themselves against the truth as it is in Jesus, God alone can remedy the disorder. Doubt may give occasion
for renewed examination, exhortation, and reliance without establishing an error in the doctrine by which
it is corrected.
    [958] 2. The alleged difficulty assumes that the sinner must already know and believe that Christ died
for him individually by the designation and appointment of God before he may believe in Christ. Indeed
the objection rests entirely on the claim that without this prior assurance he may reasonably doubt
whether faith is his duty. But what Scripture describes as the full assurance that Christ died for oneself is
the highest advancement of faith, involving the apprehension of the spiritual love of God shed abroad in
the heart. It is the summit of the apostle’s consolation and the ground of his joyful confidence. The
objection thus requires the sinner to have attained the very faith it asks whether he ought to exercise. It
would entangle a doubting conscience more deeply by making the effect of an act necessary to its
beginning. The order established in paragraphs 607–617 would have to be overturned before this
supposed prerequisite could give anyone a reason to hesitate. (Romans 8:34; Galatians 2:20)
    [959] 3. The sinner need possess no persuasion that God willed Christ’s death for him in particular in

order to be drawn to faith. What Scripture addresses to sinners as such is Christ’s call, the command of
God, the promise of life upon believing, and the threat which attends unbelief. It declares also the
complete sufficiency of Christ’s blood for the salvation of believers and the certainty of salvation for
every believer without exception. These are sufficient to remove the doubts in question, and they are the
grounds Scripture provides for that purpose. The sinner knows his duty through the Savior disclosed and
the promise made to those who come. His own saving interest comes to be known through the effects
which follow. The hearer is directed to Christ by the promise addressed to him. His place in the wheel
becomes known through the saving work in which he comes to partake, so the inquiry into that place
follows the act of believing to which he is called. (Matthew 11:28; Isaiah 55:1; 1 John 3:23; John 3:36;
Acts 20:21; Ephesians 5:2; Mark 16:16)

    [960] Ignatius commands prayer for others, and Polycarp expressly carries that obligation even to
enemies and persecutors. The command therefore authorizes the church’s address to persons whose final
condition it does not know. So too the sinner’s warrant to come is given by the Savior and promise
actually disclosed to him. To make either duty depend on inspection of the decree would require prior
knowledge of a hidden conclusion before the appointed means could even be used. (Ignatius, Epistle to
the Ephesians 10; Polycarp, Epistle to the Philippians 12)
    [961] 4. The doctrine of particular effectual redemption gives the burdened sinner every ground upon
which the gospel warrants faith. (1.) The death of Christ infallibly saves every believer. (2.) The
command of God and the call of Christ infallibly declare the duty of the person addressed, whose
performance of it will assuredly be acceptable to God. (3.) Purchased free grace is held out generally to
every distressed and burdened conscience. (4.) The fountain of Christ’s blood suffices to cleanse every sin
of every person in the world who approaches it by the appointed means. A doctrine which affirms these
things cannot in its own nature cause convinced sinners to doubt whether they ought to believe. The
universal conditional promise belongs to the gospel dispensation and gives warrant to come, while the
efficacy of its application follows the saving provision already established.
    [962] The advocates of universal redemption may answer one question before this objection is left. If
every man already knows that Christ died for him in particular, what remains for him to believe? On their
account he already has the persuasion of God’s love and Christ’s goodwill. If faith is coming to God by
Christ to receive the fruits of his death, they must explain what fruit is sought in coming, since they make
every immediate fruit of that death common to all. What is held in common with those who perish
accompanies damnation as readily as salvation, for more are damned than saved, and infidelity as readily
as faith, for most remain unbelievers. The universal availability of the benefit supplies neither the
actuality of the condition nor confidence in the individual’s final enjoyment of it. Upon their principles,
faith in Christ ends in Socinian obedience. (John 3:16; Romans 5:8)
   [963] Both sides claim the excellences still to be considered, though the grounds of their claims differ
enough to permit an easy judgment. The first concern is the exaltation of God’s free grace and Christ’s
merit, together with the consolation which the saving provision gives the soul. The issue in each case
concerns what that provision is and what it effects. Its character cannot be inferred from the number of
persons to whom a disputant says it extends. (Owen, The Death of Death IV.7, editor’s note on the
number of objections)
    [964] Obj. III. The advocates of universal redemption maintain that the account they give exceedingly
magnifies the love and free grace of God, to the point of identifying free grace itself with God’s love of
every person, the giving of Christ to die for all, and his readiness to save all if they come. “Herein,” say
they, “is free grace and love magnified indeed; this is the universality of free grace,” and they add with
like confidence, “whereas the contrary opinion chains up the love and grace of God to a few.” But the
freedom of grace belongs to the source from which it proceeds, to its independence from an antecedent
claim in the recipient, and to what it actually gives. Enlarging the number of supposed recipients
establishes none of these things.
    [965] The universality asserted here cannot be assessed until the grace itself is identified. It cannot be
election, since God has not chosen all to salvation, or effectual vocation, since “whom God calls he also
justifies,” and “glorifies.” Indeed, the outward call itself has never reached all. If it is cleansing and
sanctification, the question is whether all have been washed in the blood of Jesus or whether this
cleansing belongs to the church. Some remain defiled, and the heart is purified by faith, while “all men
have not faith.” Justification is the free love and mercy of God in pardoning and accepting sinners. Are all
pardoned and accepted? If the grace claimed is redemption in Christ’s blood, what operation has been
shown to be universal? The proposal leaves only a figment of the disputants’ devising or a new name for
the old idol of free will. It impairs grace in every branch and tends to overthrow the whole covenant of
distinguishing grace, because it denies that the promise of that covenant works its conditions in any of

those who receive it. Its two aims are to mock God and exalt men. It represents the Lord as making a
pretense of love, goodwill, free grace, and pardon to everyone, while never making it known to
incomparably the greater number, though he knows that apart from his own operation they can never
know it. Even to those outwardly called it makes him profess to love them all, to have sent his Son to die
for all, and to desire the salvation of all, upon a condition which they can no more fulfill without his
operation than they can climb a ladder to heaven, and which he declines to work in them. It makes God
say, “Such is this my love, my universal grace, that by it I will freely love them, I dare joyfully embrace
them, in all things but only that which will do them good?” A man would rightly be called a counterfeit
hypocrite if he said to a poor blind man, “Alas, poor man, I pity your case, I see your want, I love you
exceedingly; open your eyes, and I will give you a hundred pounds?” Yet this is the conduct assigned to
the most holy God of truth. What is common to all has never saved anyone. Scripture ascribes
discrimination and efficacy to the grace of God, and this universal provision has neither. Grant for
argument the whole extent alleged. If grace so understood ever saved a soul, why are all its recipients not
saved? “Why,” they will say, “because they do not believe.” The gift of faith therefore belongs to no part
of their free grace, and free will is either installed in grace’s place or reserved the chief part in salvation,
since its act of believing makes everything else profitable. Thus, to give reprobates a share in what they
call free grace, the disputants deny the elect the grace which saves. Their grace can belong to all only by
relinquishing the efficacy by which saving grace is known. (Romans 9:11–12; 8:28, 30; 11:25–26, 29;
10:14; 1:17; 3:22; 5:1; Ephesians 1:4; 5:25–27; Titus 1:15; Revelation 5:9)
   [966] Polycarp speaks of faith given, Barnabas of repentance given, and Melito of deliverance from
bondage. The gifts are distinguished by what is done in the recipient. A general assistance affords no
ground for claiming those effects until it is shown to produce them, and enlarging its circle of recipients
makes no progress toward showing that it does. The grace alleged must answer to the operation which its
name is invoked to explain. (Polycarp, Epistle to the Philippians 3; Epistle of Barnabas 16; Melito of
Sardis, On Pascha 68)
    [967] The effectual redemption of God’s elect in the blood of Jesus preserves the freedom, efficacy,
and extent of grace. Its freedom is entire, for a gift partly owed would cease in that respect to be grace. Its
efficacy extends to the whole of our recovery and return to God, which we ascribe to grace in “solidum,”
and by which we are saved. Its extent comprehends every person who is, has been, or ever shall be
delivered from the pit. What accompanies a man into hell has no title to the name of free grace in the
gospel sense, for wherever that grace chooses its object it brings God, Christ, and salvation with it for
eternity. The recipient’s dependence on the gift expresses the freedom with which it is bestowed, since he
contributes nothing which purchases its fruit.
    [968] “But you do not extend it unto all; you tie it up to a few.” De te largitor, puer. The dispensation
of divine love and favor lies in the power of God, who has mercy on whom he will have mercy and
hardens whom he will. We affirm that grace extends to the whole company of those who are saved. Are
we to throw the children’s bread to dogs? The grace of God in Christ works faith in every person to whom
it extends, and the conditions of the covenant ratified in his blood are effectually wrought in the heart of
every covenantee. There is no ineffectual love of God, the blood of Christ was not shed in vain, and we
who are dead in trespasses and sins can do only what his free grace works in us. On these grounds the
grace in question cannot extend to all. Those who affirm that millions taken into the new covenant perish
eternally, that belief is left to men in such a way that God’s will may fail and his love come to nothing,
and that we distinguish ourselves one from another, may extend their grace wherever they please. Its
supposed operation is indifferent to whether its objects go to heaven or hell. The revealed will of God
governs the extent of his purpose, and a preference for a greater number adds nothing to the premises.
   [969] It remains to ask whether this universal provision is God’s free grace or a fiction in which men
substitute their own wills for his love. Its operation shows it to be the latter. Our prayer is that God would
give its advocates infinitely more of his love than their much vaunted grace contains. Meanwhile, poor
souls must be kept from mistaking the painted cloth for the assistance which alone can deliver them from

their condition. The name leads them to expect the almighty and effectual grace of which they have heard
in Scripture, which certainly brings every recipient to God. The inventors of this empty show laugh in
secret at the simplicity of those whom they mislead, for what their provision really affords is leave to be
saved if one can. Its whole substance is “Go your ways; be saved if you can, in the way revealed; God
will not hinder you.” The thing given, and what it accomplishes in its recipient, determine what may
rightly bear the name of saving grace.
   [970] Obj. IV. The parties also contend for the merit of Christ, each finding its exaltation in something
which the other denies. The argument already made permits a brief comparison. The merit belongs to the
accepted act and is known through the benefits it procures, in the relations established in paragraphs 515–
523. Once the respects in which the parties ascribe merit to the death of Christ are brought into
comparison, the claim that each makes against the other can be decided directly.
    [971] The advocates of a general ransom claim superiority in a single respect. They extend the
benefits of Christ’s death and offering to every individual and call it a dishonor to the Lord Jesus Christ
that the opposing doctrine confines them to the elect, whom they describe as very few. First, the honor of
Jesus Christ is his to declare. We are dust, and what Christ receives as honor is what he gives and ascribes
to himself. He has no need of our falsehood to establish his glory, and an invention of our hearts would be
abominable to him even if we thought it glorified him. Second, the extension of the efficacy of his death
to every person, if there were such an extension, would proceed from the free pleasure and determination
of God rather than from the intrinsic sufficiency of the act. The preceding argument has already shown
how the supposed universality empties that efficacy of its force. Third, the value of a thing consists in its
own worth and sufficiency for the end to which it is applied. In this respect the doctrine of effectual
