redemption ascribes much more to the death of Christ than the adversaries ascribe to it. Extending the
domain while diminishing what the offering accomplishes changes the very proposition whose honor is at
issue.
   [972] The scriptural doctrine ascribes to Christ, to his death and passion, and to its fruits excellences
which the governing principles of universal redemption cannot sustain. A full enumeration would repeat
much already established. The following propositions give the substance of the difference by placing each
doctrine’s assertion beside the corresponding assertion of the other. The comparison must extend to the
whole set of benefits as well as to the persons who receive them, since only on those terms can the
consequences of either doctrine be assessed.
    [973] The Universalists make the scope universal. “Christ died for all and every one, elect and
reprobate.” Scripture identifies the persons for whom the death was undertaken. “Christ died for the elect
only.”
    [974] On the universalist account, “Most of them for whom Christ died are damned.” The saving
issue follows the undertaking on the scriptural account. “All those for whom Christ died are certainly
saved.”
    [975] Universal redemption excludes saving grace from the purchase. “Christ, by his death, purchased
not any saving grace for them for whom he died.” Scripture includes that grace in its entirety. “Christ by
his death purchased all saving grace for them for whom he died.”
    [976] The universalist doctrine separates the death from the provision of its outward means. “Christ
took no care for the greatest part of them for whom he died, that ever they should hear one word of his
death.” The scriptural doctrine joins the means to the saving act. “Christ sends the means and reveals the
way of life to all them for whom he died.”
    [977] Universal redemption gives the death a merely permissive relation to the covenant. “Christ, in
his death, did not ratify nor confirm a covenant of grace with any federates, but only procured by his
death that God might, if he would, enter into a new covenant with whom he would, and upon what

condition he pleased.” In the scriptural account the offering itself ratifies the covenant. “The new
covenant of grace was confirmed to all the elect in the blood of Jesus.”
    [978] Universal redemption allows the offering without a redeemed people. “Christ might have died,
and yet no one be saved.” Scripture makes the people the assured purchase of the offering. “Christ, by his
death, purchased, upon covenant and compact, an assured peculiar people, the pleasure of the Lord
prospering to the end in his hand.”
    [979] On the universalist account the intention makes no distinction between the church and the
serpent’s seed. “Christ had no intention to redeem his church, any more than the wicked seed of the
serpent.” The scriptural account gives his love for the church its own object and act. “Christ loved his
church, and gave himself for it.”
     [980] Universal redemption excludes unbelief from the sins for which Christ died. “Christ died not
for the infidelity of any.” Scripture gives the offering its effect in respect of this sin also. “Christ died for
the infidelity of the elect.”
    [981] These instances could be multiplied, but an experienced Christian needs no longer enumeration
to see how little the general ransom contributes to the glory of Jesus Christ or to the worth and dignity of
his death and passion. What determines the comparison is the efficacy established in the saving
undertaking itself. The juxtaposition displays the difference because the foregoing argument has proved
the relations on which it rests.
    [982] Obj. V. The final question concerns the consolation which God in Christ is abundantly willing
that his people should receive. The doctrine which gives the firmer ground of reliance is the one which
establishes the relation between the saving provision and the believer’s final good. The inquiry brings the
controversy to its end. May the God of truth and comfort bring all our labors for the truth, which are
themselves his workings in us, to peace and consolation.
   [983] Consolation has a proper office because its promise has determinate recipients, a determinate
benefit, and grounds by which participation in that benefit is known. The following distinctions arise from
those relations.
    [984] 1. True evangelical consolation belongs to believers, the people of God, while upon unbelievers
the “wrath of God abides.” The truth of the conditional invitation to every hearer stands together with the
particular saving relation on which this comfort depends. (Hebrews 6:17–18; Isaiah 40:1–2; John 3:36)
   [985] 2. A minister is bound to apply the promise in the conditions in which God reveals it. The
concealment of those conditions in giving comfort to persons to whom it is not due is as criminal as the
withholding of comfort from those in whom they are present. Both acts violate the same office. (Isaiah
5:20; Jeremiah 23:14; Ezekiel 13:10)
    [986] 3. Thomas More represents the death of Christ as warranting comfort for all, in the sense of
every individual in the world. The attempt is opposed to the gospel and has the presumption of making
straight what God has made crooked. A desire to reassure everyone supplies no ground for assigning to
everyone the saving benefit on which reassurance depends.
   [987] 4. A doctrine which gives unbelievers consolation from the death of Christ cries, “Peace,
peace,” when God says, “There is no peace.” The proclaimed truth includes the absence of peace under
unbelief, for the condition from which salvation delivers cannot be denied in the act of offering its
consolation.
    [988] Four consequences follow when the kinds of benefit and the grounds of its enjoyment are kept
distinct. (1.) The universality alleged for the object of Christ’s death gives no ground whatever for the
consolation God would have the gospel impart. (2.) A denial that his death effects salvation for those for
whom he died severs the very connections which make gospel consolation strong and which make it the
peculiar possession of believers. (3.) The doctrine that the elect alone are redeemed contains nothing

which would withhold comfort from any to whom it belongs. (4.) The effectual redemption of Christ’s
sheep by the blood of the covenant is the true and solid foundation of all lasting consolation. The first
concerns the alleged availability of a benefit, the second and fourth concern what the death actually
procures, and the third concerns the grounds on which enjoyment of it may be known.
     [989] 1. The extension of the object of Christ’s death to every person has no property of its own which
affords the least ground of consolation to those whom God would have comforted. Gospel consolation in
its proper sense follows actual reconciliation to God and therefore belongs peculiarly to believers, a point
already established and beyond dispute. No proposition peculiar to the doctrine of a general ransom adds
grounds of comfort to persons under that relation. The following reasons show that universality supplies
no premise connecting the believer with final deliverance.
     [990] (1.) Scripture nowhere proposes a general ransom as the ground, cause, or matter of believers’
consolation. What has no being has no capacity to affect anything or bring about any operation.
Moreover, every ground and material of consolation which Scripture supplies belongs particularly to
some, as the argument will show. Saving confidence takes its warrant from the revealed relation on which
it rests, and that relation cannot be enlarged without evidence.
    [991] (2.) What believers have equally in common with the following classes gives them no ground of
consolation. First are those whom God would not have comforted, second those who will assuredly perish
eternally, third those who openly rebel against Christ, and fourth those who never hear a word of the
gospel or its comfort. Yet the best of believers have no more title than any of these persons to the
consolation said to arise from a general ransom. What belongs equally to those who perish cannot
establish that another who shares it will be saved.
     [992] A premise adequate for the conclusion of final deliverance must distinguish the saved from the
lost. A property belonging equally to both cannot do so by itself. A common temporal benefit may have
its own goodness, but that is a different question from whether its possession warrants the particular
conclusion on which saving consolation depends.
                     [993] B(a)∧S(a),     B(b)∧¬S(b)      ⇒ ¬∀x [B(x)⇒S(x)]. (63)
    [994] B denotes the common benefit proposed, and a and b designate persons with opposed final
outcomes. Since b possesses B and is finally lost, B is insufficient for the implication asserted. The
premises leave any value the benefit may have in a different inquiry to be assessed on its own grounds.
    [995] Where final perdition remains compatible with receiving the universal ransom, knowledge that
one has received it cannot establish final salvation. The premise still required is one which secures the
decisive condition or the outcome itself. Effectual redemption finds that provision within the undertaking
through which the saving benefit was procured.
    [996] (3.) Let the argument be tried in desertion and temptation, when the soul seeks peace and has
more at stake than the success of a disputation. “Christ died for all men; I am a man: therefore, Christ died
for me.” A man’s heart will tell him that the wrath of God may still abide on him forever without
falsifying a word of the conclusion. He can see that the Lord shows so little love to millions upon millions
of the sons of men, of whom the proposition is equally true on this account, that he never makes himself
or his Son known to them. If knowledge that Christ died for me leaves me liable to perish forever, what
good is it to me in the distress which occasioned the inquiry? My desire is for assurance of the love of
God in Christ, and the comfort drawn from a common provision must specify what all enjoy that can
satisfy that desire. What was given, or might have been given, to Judas cannot by itself settle my soul.
Those who have no more than this to offer are Job’s visitors, physicians of no value whose skill adds
affliction to the afflicted. The syllogism proves only the death for the person in a sense consistent with his
perdition, and that sense supplies no adequate ground for the comfort sought.

    [997] “But be of good comfort,” the Arminians will say, “Christ is a propitiation for all sinners, and
now you know yourself so to be.” True, but does the propitiation extend to all the sins of those sinners? If
so, none of them can perish. If it does not, the sin by which I perish, perhaps unbelief itself, may be the
very one for which Christ made no propitiation. “But exclude not yourself; God excludes none; the love
which caused him to send his Son was general towards all.” I have already excluded myself. What I need
to know is whether God will bring me in by his power, and whether Christ procured the ability to enter
his Father’s arms together with admission to them. “Why, he has opened a door of salvation to all.” An
open grave supplies no power by which the dead man may come out, and a lighted candle enables no
blind man to see. To open a prison door for one who is blind, lame, bound, and dead is to deride his
misery when the liberty he needs consists in being made able to come out. The provision under which the
greater part of humankind perishes eternally cannot give strong consolation while it leaves the very sin or
condition by which I fear to perish without a secured remedy.
    [998] 2. A general ransom takes away the sources of strong consolation which believers have from
Christ’s death. It divides things which in their conjunction constitute a certain ground of confidence, and
it denies the efficacy of that death for those for whom he died. Both consequences belong to the doctrine
itself. Once its operations are detached from one another, none of them can be taken alone to establish the
effect which follows only from their conjunction.
    [999] First, the advocates of a general ransom so divide the procurement of redemption from its
application that the former may belong to millions who have no share in the latter. They take procurement
to be the sole proper and immediate fruit of Christ’s death, and on that account redemption might be
obtained for all without being applied to any unto salvation. This is a merely possible and ineffectual
redemption. The whole company for whom it is alleged to have been obtained might perish everlastingly
while it remained what it was, because the application by which alone it profits anyone has been set
outside its purchase. Were application a fruit of the death on their principles, it would be common to all
for whom he died. What comfort does such a purchase give the soul in trouble? “What shall I do?” asks
the sinner. “the iniquity of my heels compasses me about. I have no rest in my bones by reason of my sin:
and now, whither shall I cause my sorrow to go?” He is told to take heart because Christ died for sinners.
“Yea, but shall the fruits of his death be certainly applied unto all them for whom he died? If not, I may
perish for ever.” Whoever can answer this man upon universalist principles without sending him to his
own strength in believing, or to something which in the end resolves into that strength, “et erit mihi
magnus Apollo:” If they do send him there, they acknowledge that the consolation they boast of proceeds
properly from ourselves rather than from Christ’s death. Knowledge of a procurement without secured
application leaves final exclusion entirely possible.
    [1000] Second, the distinction which the general ransom makes between the offering and intercession
of Jesus Christ severs the connection on which the promised good depends, and thus overturns the
consolation proper to believers. The relation so divided is the one established in paragraphs 91–113.
    [1001] Two places of Scripture especially show how the Holy Ghost comforts believers against the
principal sources of their sorrows, affliction and sin. The first addresses afflictions and the second
addresses sins. In both, the apostles make the inseparable connection of the oblation and intercession of
Jesus Christ, and the identity of the persons for whom both are undertaken, the very ground of the
consolation offered. The reader will find in either passage that the argument for comfort derives its
strength from this connection, and that the communication of comfort is the express design. No more
direct treatment of that office can be produced. The advocates of universal redemption divide these acts,
admitting no connection or dependence except what the will of man effects. They extend the offering to
all and the intercession only to a few. Nowhere is the death of Christ apart from his resurrection and
intercession put forward as a ground of consolation, and Scripture positively declares its inadequacy for
that purpose. Those who propose it thus divided take away the ground to which an afflicted or sin-
burdened believer may return. The continued intercession makes his appeal an appeal to an accomplished
offering whose consequences remain present. (Romans 8:32–34; 1 John 2:1–2; 1 Corinthians 15:14)

    [1002] Third, the universalists deny that the death and shedding of Christ’s blood procured faith,
grace, holiness, and perseverance for any of those for whom he died, though these make up the whole
intended benefit of the new covenant. They have both acknowledged and undertaken to prove the want of
immediate dependence between these benefits and Christ’s death, as was already shown. There is no solid
comfort for the soul at breasts from which none of the things it needs can flow. If these gifts are sought,
where should the soul seek them but in Christ’s purchase, and from where should they flow but from his
side? Without them there can be no consolation, and the strongest plea for them at the throne of grace is
the Lord Jesus’s own procurement. Every promise of God is yea and amen in him, and we attain none of
these benefits by our own strength. Is it consolation to be sent from free grace to free will? The man who
finds himself without these things is told to go “To God, who gives all freely.” But God bestows every
spiritual blessing in Christ, and all that he bestows in him has been procured for us by him. All grace is
procured through the Mediator as well as dispensed in him. To tell the soul that Christ’s death procured
none of the things without which it cannot be comforted, and then to offer that death as its comfort, is an
extravagant claim. “Credat Apella.” Faith, holiness, and perseverance remain necessary goods, and if their
procurement is removed their future presence needs another sufficient ground.
    [1003] Ignatius speaks of the Smyrnaeans as established in faith through Christ, and Polycarp directs
persevering hope toward the Savior who bore sins. The believer’s relation to Christ gives consolation
through the benefits it communicates. If the undertaking is said to secure neither faith nor continuance,
their future presence must have some other sufficient ground. An enlargement of the persons to whom the
undertaking is said to extend affords no account of that ground. The troubled conscience needs to know
what saving good is promised, how it is procured, and how it continues. The believer's consolation carries
us along the spoke to the Hub in paragraph 0. Its strength lies in the promise whose fulfillment secures his
continuance, and the argument must follow that promise through the goods actually bestowed. (Ignatius,
Epistle to the Smyrnaeans 1; Polycarp, Epistle to the Philippians 8)
    [1004] The supposed general ransom is therefore destructive of the very ways by which the Lord has
declared his willingness to comfort us from the death of his Son. It dries up the breast from which the
comfort should come and poisons the streams through which it is conveyed to the soul. The firmness of
consolation depends on the adequacy of the provision upon which faith rests and consequently upon what
that provision actually secures for those to whom consolation belongs.
    [1005] 3. Effectual redemption of the elect by the blood of Jesus withholds no part of the consolation
God is willing that believers should enjoy. The outcry that this doctrine shuts innumerable souls out of the
blood of Christ concerns persons confessedly reprobate, unbelieving, and finally impenitent, and does not
touch the consolation promised to believers. The one objection with any appearance of reason is this.
“That there is nothing in the Scripture whereby any man can assure himself that Christ died for him in
particular, unless we grant that he died for all.” Particular assurance requires a true implication given in
revelation and warranted knowledge of its condition in the person who applies it. It will be shown that
both are present without the supposed universality.
    [1006] First, the experience of believers to whom God has given assurance of their interest in Christ
as offered in the promise is sufficient to expose the falsehood of the objection. They have obtained such
assurance without a thought of universal redemption. Second, the infallibility of assurance through a
practical syllogism has hitherto been acknowledged by all, where the truth of one premise is given in the
word and that of the other by the witness of the Spirit in the heart. In this way any believer may know that
Christ died with the intention and purpose of saving him. The first premise is “Christ died for all
believers,” that is, for all who choose him and rest on him as an all-sufficient Savior. All believers belong
to the people for whom he died, although he did not die for them in their capacity as believers. He died
for the elect as elect, and the benefit of his death makes them believers, who in that condition come to
recognize their interest in it. Christ died also for the elect who have yet to believe, but while they remain
unbelievers they can have no such assurance. The adversaries themselves would judge a contradiction in
the claim that a man has assurance of the death of Christ for him in particular while he continues in

unbelief. The first premise is established throughout the word, as was laid down at the beginning. The
heart of the believer then assumes in the witness of the Spirit, “But I believe in Christ;” the sense of
which is “I choose him for my Savior, cast and roll myself on him alone for salvation, and give up myself
unto him, to be disposed of unto mercy in his own way.” Many testimonies of the word establish the truth
and infallibility of this premise in the heart, as is well known. It follows that “Therefore the Lord Jesus
Christ died for me in particular, with an intention and purpose to save me.” The Spirit’s witness warrants
the personal premise supplied by actual faith, and the conclusion recognizes the relation already shown to
have produced it.
     [1007] In the practical syllogism the soul knows the saving relation through its experienced effect.
The inference depends on a general relation established as true and on warranted knowledge of the effect
in the person. The Spirit’s witness supplies part of that warrant in Owen’s account. An equation which
grants certainty to every apparent profession would remove the distinction already established between
the profession of faith and its actuality.
                                [1008] ∀x [Fx⇒Rx],        Fa    ⇒ Ra. (64)
    [1009] The universal premise places every genuine believer among those represented in the
undertaking. The personal premise affirms the believer’s actual faith under the warrant just described. The
conclusion states his own interest in the saving procurement. The inference can proceed in this direction
because the converse connection has been proved independently. An implication cannot yield its converse
merely by being read backward.
    [1010] The certainty of the practical inference rests upon the truth of the promise and the knowledge
given to the believer under the witness of the Spirit. The formal statement displays the dependence of the
conclusion on those premises, whose truth and warrant are supplied by the theological argument.
    [1011] This inference belongs to all believers and to them alone, and it is to them alone that the
treasure of consolation so obtained is to be imparted. Christ’s death is sufficient to save everyone without
exception who comes to him, and its sufficiency fills out every gospel invitation and entreaty by which
sinners are called to believe. When they close with the promise by the grace of Christ, the assurance
already described makes known to them his intention and purpose of redeeming them by his death. The
reader may judge whether this supplies a better ground of rest and peace than the common premise to
which the adversaries’ principles oblige them. “Christ died for all men; I am a man: therefore Christ died
for me,” is false in its first premise, and its conclusion, even if true, affords on their own account no more
ground for consolation than Adam’s fall. God alone creates consolation in the sovereignty of his free
grace. The comparison concerns the fitness of the doctrines to the means by which he works it in and
toward his elect. The inference becomes available through actual reliance on Christ, so the sinner need not
establish its conclusion before coming as he is invited. (Matthew 1:21)
    [1012] 4. Gospel consolation follows from the death of Christ in its efficacy for the elect for whom
alone he died. A practical treatment of that consolation would furnish an ample conclusion, but faithful
and eminent laborers in the Lord’s vineyard have already provided it in abundance, and its
communication to the people of God is the daily work of gospel ministers. Its fuller exposition belongs to
a practical treatment beyond the design of a polemical work, and learned readers expect it here no more
than hearers seeking edification in a sermon expect intricate scholastic objections. One passage suffices to
close the inquiry. A reader uninfected by the error opposed will find there that all comfort, assurance, rest,
peace, joy, refreshment, and exultation of spirit attainable here below come from the blood of Jesus shed
long ago and the intercession he continues now. Both acts are united and appropriated to the elect of God,
and their precious effects and fruits draw the elect to believe and preserve them in believing until they
obtain an immortal crown of glory which will never fade. The final issue includes the appointed means
and acts by which they are preserved in the course that brings them to it. (Romans 8:32–34)

     [1013] Preservation through an appointed history and eventual completion of it are distinct. The
relation may be preserved in one who undergoes real conflict and correction, repents, and grows.
Retention alone, however, does not establish that he reaches the final good. The promise secures both the
continuance of the saving relation through its appointed means and the eventual attainment of glory by its
recipient.
                   [1014] Xx(t)∈K⇒Tt(Xx(t),ut;d)∈K,           Rx⇒∃tg Xx(tg)∈G. (65)
    [1015] K denotes the class of creaturely conditions compatible with the preserved saving relation, G
denotes the final glorified condition, and T denotes the actual transition under the appointed means u and
government d. The first expression gives the preservation of the relation through those operations, and the
second gives eventual attainment. Both are grounded in the promises and intercession already defended.
    [1016] The relation can remain secure through changes of conduct and interruptions of conscious
assurance. Correction is among the temporal operations through which the believer is preserved and
brought forward. The course is ordered to the attainment of glory itself, whose actual completion is the
promised end of all those operations. (John 10:27–29; Romans 8:29–34; Hebrews 7:25; 12:5–11)
    [1017] The end includes the resurrection of the body, as Athenagoras shows, and the church of
Smyrna closes its narrative by commending believers to the power of God to bring them into his heavenly
kingdom. Preservation through conflict, repentance, and correction receives its saving significance from
the attained condition to which it is ordered, as established in paragraph 14. A course which remained
forever incomplete would leave the promised end unrealized. (Athenagoras, On the Resurrection of the
Dead 25; Martyrdom of Polycarp 20)
   [1018] Μόνῳ σοφῷ Θεῷ, διὰ Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ ἡ δόξα εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας. Ἀμήν.

Some few testimonies of the ancients.
   [1019] I. The holy church of Smyrna, not long after the commendation which the Holy Ghost gave it,
made the following confession upon the martyrdom of Polycarp. Ὅτι οὔτε τὸν Χριστόν ποτε καταλείπειν
δυνησόμεθα τὸν ὑπὲρ τῆς τοῦ κόσμου τῶν σωζωμένων σωτηρίας παθόντα, οὔτε ἕτερον τιμῇ σέβειν. —
“Neither can we ever forsake Christ, him who suffered for the salvation of the world of them that are
saved, nor worship any other.” (Eusebius, Ecclesiastical History IV.15; Revelation 2:9)
   [1020] The confession gives the saving work an identified people. It names the represented
community by the salvation which Christ’s suffering is confessed to have accomplished for it.
     [1021] [The passage comes from the letter in which the church of Smyrna gave the churches of Pontus
its account of Polycarp’s martyrdom.]
    [1022] II. Holy Ignatius bore this witness as he was taken from Antioch to Rome to be cast to beasts
for the testimony of Jesus [a.d. 107]. Οὗτός ἐστιν ἡ πρὸς τὸν Πατέρα ἄγουσα ὁδός, ἡ πέτρα, ὁ φραγμός, ἡ
κλείς, ὁ ποιμήν, τὸ ἱερεῖον, ἡ θύρα τῆς γνώσεως δι’ ἧς εἰσῆλθον Αβραὰμ καὶ Ἰσαὰκ καὶ Ἰακώβ, Μωσῆς,
καὶ ὁ σύμπας τῶν προφητῶν χορός, καὶ οἱ στύλοι τοῦ κόσμου οἱ απόστολοι καὶ ἡ νύμφη τοῦ Χριστοῦ,
ὑπὲρ ἧς, φερνῆς λόγῳ, ἐξέχεε τὸ οἰκεῖον αἷμα ἵνα αὐτὴν ἐξαγοράσῃ. — “This is the way leading to the
Father, this the rock, the fold, the key; he is the shepherd, the sacrifice; the door of knowledge, by which
entered Abraham, Isaac, Jacob, Moses, and the whole company of prophets, and the pillars of the world,
the apostles, and the spouse of Christ; for whom, instead of a dowry, he poured out his own blood, that he
might redeem her.” (Ignatius, Epistle to the Philadelphians 9)
    [1023] The persons to whom the benefit is communicated are the persons for whom the gift is made.
Their conjunction in Ignatius’s testimony bears on the relation of procurement and enjoyment developed
in paragraphs 252–273.
   [1024] The spouse for whom Jesus Christ gave his blood as a dowry is his own.

    [1025] The gift belongs to the recipient in the union which constitutes her Christ’s spouse. Its saving
content takes its determination from that appointed relation.
    [1026] III. Clement, “whose name is in the book of life”, joined the whole church at Rome in writing
to the church at Corinth. Διὰ τὴν ἀγάπην ἣν ἔσχεν πρὸς ἡμᾶς τὸ αἷμα αὐτοῦ ἔδωκεν ὑπὲρ ἡμῶν ἐν
θελήματι αὐτοῦ καὶ τὴν σάρκα ὑπὲρ τῆς σαρκὸς ἡμῶν καὶ τὴν ψυχὴν ὑπὲρ ψυχῶν ἡμῶν. — “For the love
which he had unto us, he gave his blood for us, according to his purpose, and his flesh for our flesh, and
his life for our lives.” (Clement of Rome, Epistle to the Corinthians; Philippians 4:3)
     [1027] The persons whom Christ loves are the persons for whom he gives himself, and what he gives
in their place gives his act the office of a representative exchange. The source, object, and operation
belong to the same assertion.
     [1028] The testimonies enter the proof at the points which their assertions establish. The world
confessed at Smyrna is the world of those who are saved, and so bears on the determination of the persons
intended. Clement’s account of Christ’s gift bears on what is done for those whom he represents. Both
statements must be read in the argument about scope, satisfaction, and application, for their force consists
in what they affirm and in what follows from those affirmations. A disputant who denies the resulting
conclusion must answer the relevant testimony where it enters that proof. (Martyrdom of Polycarp 17.2;
Clement of Rome, First Epistle to the Corinthians 49)
    [1029] Clement assigns first the cause of Christ’s death in his love to us, then its object in the
believers denoted by us, and finally the manner of our redemption in the exchange which he made for us.
    [1030] Each relation supplies its own part of the testimony. Their distinction makes it possible to state
precisely what this sentence establishes while other questions remain to be determined through their own
evidence.
   [1031] These three testimonies belong to the earliest and undisputed antiquity.
    [1032] What they affirm in their several scopes is evidence of the doctrine as received in those
churches. Their historical weight is borne by the particular assertions which they transmit.
   [1033] IV. Cyprian, the holy, learned, and celebrated martyr, wrote in A.D. 250, “Nos omnes portabat
Christus, qui et peccata nostra portabat.” — “He bore all us, who bore our sins;” by which he means that
Christ sustained on the cross the persons for whom he died. (Cyprian, Epistle 62 to Caecilius)
   [1034] Christ bears the sins of the persons he represents, and in bearing their sins he bears them. The
same persons therefore receive the saving consequence of the act Cyprian confesses.
   [1035] Cyprian also writes in his address to Demetrian, “Hanc gratiam Christus impertit, subigendo
mortem trophæo crucis, redimendo credentem pretio sanguinis sui.” — “This grace has Christ
communicated, subduing death in the trophy of his cross, redeeming believers with the price of his
blood.” (Cyprian, To Demetrian)
    [1036] Death’s subjection and the believer’s redemption are benefits of one saving act. Their
conjunction accords with the temporal application of the work established above.
    [1037] The work on the cardinal works of Christ is cited as the work of Cyprian or of another ancient
and pious author. (On the Cardinal Works of Christ, Sermon 7; Rivet, Critici Sacri II.15; Scultetus,
Medulla Patrum; Erasmus, preface to the work; Arnoldus Carnotensis, On the Cardinal Works of Christ,
as identified in the editor’s note to Owen)
    [1038] The doctrine stated in the work can be examined on the strength of its proposition, while the
authorship remains subject to the qualification under which the testimony was transmitted.
   [1039] This author expressly derives the sufficiency of the ransom from the dignity of the person who
paid it. “Tantæ dignitatis illa una Redemptoris nostri fuit oblatio, ut una ad tollenda mundi peccatum

sufficeret.” — “Of so great dignity was the oblation of our Redeemer, that it alone was sufficient to take
away the sins of the world.” (On the Cardinal Works of Christ, Sermon 7)
     [1040] The offering derives its worth from the dignity of the one who offers it. The persons to whom
its effects are intended to come are determined through the appointed saving relation, a distinct question
from the sufficiency of its worth.
    [1041] V. Cyril of Jerusalem [a.d. 350] connects the sufficiency of the death with the identity of the
one who suffered it. Καὶ μὴ θαυμάσῃς εἰ κόσμος ὅλος ἐλυτρώθη, οὐ γὰρ ἦν ἄνθρωπος ψιλὸς ἀλλὰ υἱὸς
Θεοῦ μονογενὴς ὁ ὑπεραποθνήσκων — καὶ εἰ τότε διὰ τὸ ξύλον τῆς βρώσεως ἐξεβλήθησαν ἐκ
παραδείσου, ἆρα διὰ τὸ ξύλον Ἰησοῦ νῦν εὐκοπώτερον οἱ πιστεύοντες εἰς παράδεισον οὐκ εἰσελεύσονται.
— “Wonder not if the whole world be redeemed; for he was not a mere man, but the only-begotten Son of
God that died. If, then, through the eating of the tree” (forbidden) “they were cast out of paradise,
certainly now by the tree” (or cross) “of Jesus shall not believers more easily enter into paradise?” (Cyril
of Jerusalem, Catechetical Lecture 13)
    [1042] The sufficiency Cyril confesses has its ground in the worth of the divine person. That ground
accounts for the worth of the act without itself enumerating the persons who enjoy what it purchases.
   [1043] The sense in which the ancient writers use all becomes plain in another of their testimonies.
    [1044] The community and the benefit of which the writer speaks determine the extension of all,
according to the distinctions established in paragraphs 573–593.
    [1045] VI. Athanasius [a.d. 350] joins the delivery of Christ’s body to the salvation for which the
price was given. Οὗτός ἐστιν ἡ πάντων ζωή, καὶ ὡς πρόβατον ὑπὲρ, τῆς πάντων σωτηρίας ἀντίψυχον τὸ
ἑαυτοῦ σῶμα εἰς θάνατον παραδούς. — “He is the life of all, and as a sheep he delivered his body a price
for the souls of all, that they might be saved.” (Athanasius, On the Incarnation of the Word)
     [1046] The life communicated determines who is meant by all. The expression has its scope within
the relation Athanasius asserts between the persons and the salvation for which Christ gives himself.
   [1047] In both occurrences the persons denoted by all can only be the elect, as the following witnesses
show.
     [1048] The restriction is established by the predicates which govern the expression in its context. It
follows from the relation asserted of those persons, and the word all by itself would furnish no such
premise.
    [1049] VII. The testimony attributed to Ambrose, or rather to Prosper, [a.d. 370] makes the relation
explicit. “Si non credis, non descendit tibi Christus, non tibi passus est.” — “If you believe not, Christ did
not descend for you, he did not suffer for you.” (On the Calling of the Gentiles I.3; Prosper, I.9, Olivarius
edition, as cited by Owen)
    [1050] Belief identifies the recipient’s actual relation to the benefit. The act by which he comes to
know and enjoy his interest in Christ does not produce the incarnation or passion to which that interest
refers.
    [1051] Ambrose gives the people of God a fullness of their own. “Habet populus Dei plenitudinem
suam. In electis enim et præscitis, atque ab omnium generalitate discretis, specialis quædam censetur
universitas, ut de toto mundo totus mundus liberatus, et de omnibus hominibus omnes homines videantur
assumpti.” — “The people of God has its own fullness. In the elect and foreknown, distinguished from
the generality of all, there is accounted a certain special universality; so that the whole world seems to be
delivered from the whole world, and all men to be taken out of all men.” (Ambrose, On the Faith to
Gratian)
   [1052] A community complete in its own order constitutes a qualified universality within the larger
population from which it is taken. Its fullness is the completion of the people under their saving relation.

    [1053] Ambrose goes on at length to explain why “all” and “the world” so often denote “some of all
sorts.”
    [1054] A people drawn from the whole world has worldwide extent through the places and kinds of
persons it includes. That breadth and the inclusion of every individual are different properties.
   [1055] The writers who follow wrote after the emergence of the Pelagian heresy, which occasioned
more diligent inquiry and greater care in expression than some had previously exercised.
     [1056] The controversy explains the occasion of the more exact distinctions. Their force in theology
follows from the particular propositions the writers establish.
    [1057] VIII. Augustine [a.d. 420] joins the blood by which Christ purchases the redeemed to the good
which God effects in them. “Per hunc Mediatorem Deus ostendit eos, quos ejus sanguine redemit, facere
se ex malis in æternum bonos.” — “By him the Mediator, the Lord declares himself to make those whom
he has redeemed with his blood, of evil, good to eternity.” He goes on to connect the price with the
possession. “Vult possidere Christus quod emit; tanti emit ut possideat.” — “Christ will possess what he
bought; he bought it with such a price that he might possess it.” (Augustine, On Rebuke and Grace 11)
   [1058] Redemption has an actual transformative effect in the people whom God makes enduringly
good. This is the relation between procurement and sanctification developed in paragraphs 371–405.
     [1059] The enduring goodness asserted by Augustine gives the historical witness a definite place in
the causal argument. What Christ procures is realized as sanctification in the redeemed, whose actual
transformation involves both the efficient operation of grace and their own renewed action, in the
distinction already established.
             [1060] Rx⇒◇Hx,          Hxincludes actual renewed disposition and conduct. (66)
    [1061] H denotes the holiness affirmed by Augustine and established in the preceding scriptural
argument. Its eventual presence is the actuality of a saving fruit in the person. The expression records the
theological proposition, for which Scripture supplies the principal proof and the testimony supplies
evidence of historical confession.
     [1062] The citation enters the developed argument through that particular proposition. The same
treatment gives each of the surrounding witnesses its proper force, because the statements they actually
make are retained and carried into the reasoning through the relations they establish.
    [1063] Augustine states the consequence of the purchase for those whom Christ has bought. “Qui nos
tanto pretio emit non vult perire quos emit.” — “He that bought us with such a price will have none perish
whom he has bought.” (Augustine, Sermon 44 on the Words of the Apostle)
   [1064] The will by which Christ preserves those he bought joins the purchase to final enjoyment.
What Augustine assigns to the purchase is the accomplished saving issue, and its recipients are those
whom Christ wills to preserve.
    [1065] Augustine distinguishes the two communities which Scripture calls the world by what it
predicates of each. “Ecclesiam plerumque etiam ipsam mundi nomine appellat; sicut est illud, ‘Deus erat
in Christo mundum reconcilians sibi;’ itemque illud, ‘Non venit Filius hominis ut judicet mundum, sed ut
salvetur mundus per ipsum;’ et in epistola sua Johannes ait, ‘Advocatum habemus ad Patrem, Jesum
Christum justum, et ipse propitiator est peecatorum nostrorum, non tantum nostrorum sed etiam totius
mundi.’ Totus ergo mundus est ecclesia, et totus mundus odit ecclesiam. Mundus igitur odit mundum;
inimicus reconciliatum, damnatus salvatum, inquinatus mundatum. Sed iste mundus quem Deus in
Christo reconciliat sibi, et qui per Christum salvatur, de mundo electus est inimico, damnato,
contaminato.” — “He often calls the church itself by the name of the world; as in that, ‘God was in Christ
reconciling the world unto himself;’ and that, ‘The Son of man came not to condemn the world, but that
the world through him might be saved.’ And John in his epistle says, ‘We have an Advocate, and he is the

propitiation for [our sins, and not for ours only, but also for] the sins of the whole world.’ The whole
world, therefore, is the church, and the world hates the church. The world, then, hates the world; that
which is at enmity, the reconciled; the condemned, the saved; the polluted, the cleansed world. And that
world which God in Christ reconciles to himself, and which is saved by Christ, is chosen out of the
opposite, condemned, defiled world.” (Augustine, Tractate 87 on John)
     [1066] The world at enmity and the world reconciled are the two objects of contrary predicates. The
recurrence of the word world preserves neither the identity of the communities nor the identity of their
relation to Christ.
    [1067] Other passages from Augustine could readily be adduced to the same effect, but his judgment
on these matters is well known.
    [1068] A further witness would add whatever its own assertion establishes. The scriptural premises
already proved continue to govern the causal account.
    [1069] IX. Prosper [a.d. 440] connects participation in the crucifixion with membership in Christ’s
body. “Non est crucifixus in Christo qui non est membrum corporis Christi. Cum itaque dicatur Salvator
pro totius mundi redemptione crucifixus, propter veram humanæ naturæ susceptionem, potest tamen dici
pro his tantum crucifixus quibus mors ipsius profuit. Diversa ab istis sors eorum est qui inter illos
censentur de quibus dicitur, ‘Mundus enim non cognovit.’ ”— “He is not crucified with Christ who is not
a member of the body of Christ. When, therefore, our Savior is said to be crucified for the redemption of
the whole world, because of his true assumption of the human nature, yet may he be said to be crucified
only for them unto whom his death was profitable. Diverse from these is their lot who are reckoned
amongst them of whom it is said, ‘The world knew him not.’ ” (Prosper, Responses to the Gallic Articles
9)
    [1070] The membership asserted by Prosper determines the person’s participation in the saving act.
His universal language must be read with the relation which distinguishes those to whom Christ’s death
profits from those who do not know him.
    [1071] Prosper identifies the people to whom redemption properly belongs through what Christ
effects in them. “Redemptionis proprietas, haud dubie penes illos est, de quibus princeps mundi missus
est foras. Mors Christi non ita impensa est humano generi, ut ad redemptionem ejus etiam qui regenerandi
non erant pertinerent.” — “Doubtless the propriety of redemption is theirs from whom the prince of this
world is cast out. The death of Christ is not to be so laid out for human-kind, that they also should belong
unto his redemption who were not to be regenerated.” (Prosper, Response to the Objections of Vincent,
response 1)
   [1072] Regeneration and deliverance give the restriction its determinate content. The persons for
whom redemption is asserted in its saving office are those in whom these operations are realized.
   [1073] Prosper carries the argument into verse.
   [1074] “Sed tamen hæc aliqua sivis ratione tueri
   [1075] Et credi tam stulta cupis. Jam pande quid hoc sit,
   [1076] Quod bonus omnipotensque Deus, non omnia subdit
   [1077] Corda sibi, pariterque omnes jubet esse fideles?
   [1078] Nam si nemo usquam est quem non velit esse redemptum,
   [1079] Haud dubie impletur quicquid vult summa potestas.
   [1080] Non omnes autem salvantur”

   [1081] “If there be none whom God would not have redeemed, why are not all saved?” (Prosper, On
Ingratitude 9)
     [1082] The question binds the scope of God's saving will to the result it produces. The will in dispute
is effectual, and the salvation of every person is denied. Its object must therefore be identified by
following the consequence of that very will, rather than assigning it a population whose members do not
all receive the good it effects.
    [1083] The tenth testimony, from the Council of Valence, assigns the price of Christ's death to the
people named by the Lord in his promise concerning the lifting up of the Son of man. “Pretium mortis
Christi datum est pro illis tantum quibus Dominus ipse dixit, ‘Sicut Moses exaltavit serpentem in deserto,
ita exaltari oportet Filius hominis, ut omnis qui credit in ipso non pereat, sed habeat vitam eternam.’ ” The
canon speaks with equal precision in translation. “The price of the death of Christ is given for them alone
of whom the Lord himself said, ‘As Moses lifted up the serpent in the wilderness, so must the Son of man
be lifted up, that whosoever believes on him should not perish.’ ” (Council of Valence, canon 4)
    [1084] Valence thus closes the historical testimony where the argument began, with the relation
between an appointed people and an accomplished end. The Lord identifies the recipients of life, and the
council identifies those same persons as the objects of the price through which that life is procured.

An appendix upon occasion of a late book published by Mr Joshua Sprigge, containing erroneous
doctrine.
   Reader,
    [1085] The dispute over satisfaction returns here because a further objection reached Owen after the
discourse and its preface had already been finished and printed. That circumstance accounts for the
position of the appendix. In paragraphs 429–514 the doctrine was defended chiefly to show why
satisfaction and a general ransom cannot be maintained together, and the Socinians were the principal
opponents because their denial of the person, grace, and merit of Christ put the substance of the
controversy in plain view. The account proceeded from Scripture, drawing on controversial writing for
the particular purpose of identifying the παρορᾶματα, the failures and mistakes in Grotius's statement of
satisfaction, some of which Owen suspected were deliberate. The test lies in the texts themselves.
Compare the expositions Grotius offered against Socinus with the expositions he afterward published in
his much admired Annotations. The inconsistency shows how little his reputation could secure the
principles of his first undertaking, and his descent into the error he had been credited with opposing gives
every reason to examine that undertaking closely. A second difficulty concerned readers who accepted
God's eternal love for his elect but supposed it inconsistent with Christ's satisfaction. They published that
supposition as soon as they had conceived it, leaving others to give form to an opinion they had not
worked through themselves. Christ, on their account, came to declare the Father's love so that its
discovery might draw men to him, and satisfaction and merit consequently became empty names which
obscured the very gospel they purported to explain. The following argument takes up that consequence
directly. (Grotius, On the Satisfaction of Christ; Vossius, Defence of Grotius)
    [1086] The question is what depends upon the offering. Once the eternal source of redemption and the
historical work appointed to procure its effects have been stated in their proper order, the alleged
inconsistency can be tested against each of them.
    [1087] 1. The supposed discovery of free grace revives an old Arminian objection drawn from the
objections and replies of the schoolmen. Its advocates could turn it against effectual redemption or against
eternal election as occasion required, contending in one direction that the persons could not have been so
redeemed if they were already chosen, and in the other that they could not have been so chosen if their
redemption had to be procured. The success of either attack satisfied the design. This “truncus ficulnus,”
an old and exhausted objection, is the stock from which the vaunted discovery grew.

    [1088] The appointment of an end supplies the ground for the means ordered to accomplish it. Eternal
purpose therefore gives the offering its place in the saving work and provides no reason to remove it.
    [1089] 2. The same doctrine descends into the Socinian gospel which has long vexed the churches and
earned their condemnation. Follow the discovery through to its substance. Vindicative justice disappears,
wrath against sin is emptied of its judicial consequence, and satisfaction becomes unnecessary. All that
remains for Christ to do is announce this arrangement and disclose a way of access to the Father. That is
the very doctrine for which the Socinians have contended with all their fraud and Sinonian arts, however
glorious the new name in which it is now presented.
     [1090] Satisfaction concerns what the offering does to the liability of the represented persons. Once
that operation is removed, the proclamation left in its place has a different object and cannot produce the
same judicial effect.
    [1091] 3. The dishonor falls upon Christ himself, whose offering is stripped of the office Scripture
gives it. The same denial deprives gospel faith and Christian consolation of their ground, and its defenders
are driven either to the Familists' contempt for the word or to the sophistry required to make the word
support their account.
    [1092] Faith rests upon the benefits Christ procures for his people. To deny that procurement changes
the ground of their confidence, for the saving good once ascribed to his offering must now come from
some other sufficient cause.
    [1093] These consequences explain why the earlier discussion took up both the assertion and the
inference by which eternal love was made to exclude satisfaction. Owen records that he had then
discussed the objection with only one person. The few works he had seen offered the familiar Familist
claims with such contempt for Scripture that they gave him little occasion for a separate answer. His reply
therefore went straight to the principal assertion in the treatment of satisfaction already cited.
     [1094] The Father's love gives rise to the sending of the Son, and the Son's offering procures the fruits
of that love. The objection can succeed only by assigning both effects to the same act and making the
historical procurement compete with the eternal purpose from which it proceeds.
    [1095] A few days before Owen wrote this appendix, Joshua Sprigge's book put the disputed
proposition before him in a developed form. Sprigge's preface and the argument throughout the treatise
make Christ the preacher of a peace he never purchased and the revealer of a love whose benefits he
never procured. On the strength of Romans 5:11 he assigns reconciliation wholly to our side of the
relation and denies that Christ procured reconciliation with God. He presents this discovery as the thing
for which believers had long prayed, dismisses the thought that God's heart was set upon having a little
blood for the sins of his people, and treats the scriptural account of satisfaction as a pleasing tale fit to
draw children. The resulting ministration of Christ is made to disclose a mystery for which the word
supplies no warrant. (Joshua Sprigge, A Testimony to Approaching Glory, preface; Joshua Sprigge, A
Testimony to Approaching Glory, 65, 101; Joshua Sprigge, A Testimony to Approaching Glory, 59;
Joshua Sprigge, A Testimony to Approaching Glory, 46; Romans 5:11; Ephesians 5:2)
     [1096] The declaration addresses the hearer with knowledge of peace. Procurement establishes the
relation of peace declared. Sprigge can reduce the latter to the former only by showing that the offering
has no such effect in the scriptural account.
   [1097] The dispute with Sprigge turns here on that single proposition. Other assertions in the book,
whether sound or specious, do not settle the question of what Christ procures, and the examination
proceeds from the claims that bear directly upon it.
    [1098] The test is the dependence of the saving effects upon the offering. Wherever Scripture assigns
a benefit to Christ's sacrifice as its procuring act, the account that leaves him only a preacher has removed
an operation which the passage expressly affirms.

     [1099] The book's attached “imprimatur” also gave Owen occasion to reply. The licenser's name
carried so much weight that Owen had himself left the book with a commendation in the hands where he
first saw it, before examining its doctrine further. He questioned whether so eminent and learned a man
had duly been associated with the work and left him the opportunity to vindicate himself. The doctrine
still falls to be tried by Scripture, and the preceding argument supplies the grounds on which the
following observations depend. A Christian's confidence in the word should survive a pretended
revelation whether or not it arrives under a distinguished name. (Owen, The Death of Death, appendix,
note on the licensing of Sprigge’s sermon on Song of Solomon 1:1)
    [1100] The licenser's authority explains the book's reception. The truth of its proposition depends
upon the saving offices Scripture assigns to Christ and the consequences which those offices actually
entail.
    [1101] God's love is an act of his will. The reasoning which makes the Son's sending an effect of love
and then concludes that he procured no life, peace, or salvation confuses that eternal act with the goods it
appoints. Love in spirits, and in men insofar as it belongs to the intellectual appetite, is in appetitu
intellectivo. Its definition is θέλειν τινὶ τὸ ἀγαθόν, “to will good to any one.” In God this love is a pure act
of will, the good pleasure from which he sends the Son, free of the affection or passion which some have
ascribed to him under the name of an unchangeable sentiment. The temporal death of Christ presupposes
this eternal willing as the source of his mission. What Christ purchases are its effects, which Scripture
itself calls love according to the familiar manner of naming divine affections by their fruits. God's eternal
will directs the sending to his own glory through two determinate acts. The first answers his vindicative
justice and removes wrath, death, curse, and guilt from the people for whom the Son was sent. The second
actually procures grace and glory for those same persons by merit and impetration. In these fruits the
recipients enjoy the love that subsists eternally in God's will, and the purchase of those fruits stands upon
the proof already given. To argue it through again here would be actum agere.
     [1102] The Father's eternal willing gives the Son's mission its source, and the offering gives the
saving benefits their appointed ground in history. Those benefits belong to the love whose purpose Christ
fulfills. His temporal work consequently secures the enjoyment of what God eternally wills for his people.
    [1103] The eternal act is immanent in God's will, while the change takes place in the creature when
the appointed operation takes effect. Scripture therefore speaks of elect persons under wrath before they
are drawn. The fruits of Christ's death are kept for them in God's justice and fidelity until he bestows
them. The text must govern our account of that order, even against the highest pretended authority.
“Though an angel,” and the rest of the apostolic warning hold the hearer to the gospel already delivered.
(Ephesians 2:3; John 3:36; Galatians 1:8)
    [1104] Athenagoras's confession that God needs nothing secures the starting point, and Diognetus
follows the prior divine counsel into the benefits afterward bestowed upon us. The creature receives the
good through the appointed administration of that counsel. Eternal purpose gives the work its order, the
offering procures the good in history, and personal application brings the recipient into its enjoyment.
Confound the first with the second and God is made to acquire a perfection. Confound the first with the
third and the creature is made to possess a benefit before the act that communicates it. (Athenagoras, A
Plea for the Christians 13; Epistle to Diognetus 8–9)
    [1105] The purpose fixes the appointment, and the appointed transition fixes the time at which the
recipient enters the new condition. The same purpose thus governs both states and the actual operation by
which the creature passes from the former into the latter.
    [1106] The saving history proceeds under a settled purpose through operations which really change
the creature. Knowledge of the purpose follows one order, the performance of its means another, and
possession of the procured benefits belongs to the person as those means take effect. The cross stands
within this order as the historical purchase of the fruits which eternal love appoints and its administration

bestows. Return at last to the Hub. From the Father's purpose proceeds the appointed offering, and
through that offering comes the good which the Spirit actually communicates to those for whom it was
procured.
          [1107] d⇒Appoint(o,Nx,Ax),            Xx(t0)≠Xx(t1),        Cause(o,d)is not asserted. (67)
    [1108] Terms. The divine purpose is denoted by d, the historical offering by o, efficient application
by N, and the benefits enjoyed by A. The difference of states records the real transition of the creature.
The final expression gives the direction of dependence its limit by excluding the temporal offering as a
cause of the eternal purpose.
    [1109] Consequence. Love appoints and gives the Son, the Mediator completes the offering, and the
Spirit applies its fruits so that the recipient believes, receives, and comes to recognize the good procured
for him. The formal expressions trace that connected work from its governing purpose to its effect in the
persons appointed to receive it.
    [1110] The foregoing account can now be tested against the several acts Scripture assigns to the
Mediator. A satisfactory answer to each question must follow the act through its proper object and the
effect for which it is performed.
    [1111] Each question takes up an operation which the proposed reduction has to account for. The
priestly direction of the act, the price of deliverance, and the reception of its fruits give the inquiry its
determinate subjects.
   [1112] First, Εἰς τὸ ἱλάσκεσθαι τὰς ἁμαρτίας τοῦ λαοῦ, “To make reconciliation for the sins of the
people,” names an act which Christ performs as priest toward God. By what account of that priestly office
does an act so directed become only a declaration of God's love to man? (Hebrews 2:17; 5:1)
     [1113] The priest deals with God on behalf of the people. That direction belongs to the operation
itself, and an account of a message heard by the people leaves the priestly act still to be explained.
    [1114] Second, the typical sacrifices make atonement with God for the persons on whose behalf they
are offered. The question is whether the stated office of those sacrifices concerns the averting of wrath or
can be exhausted by a change on the side of the men to be reconciled. (Exodus 29:33, 36; 30:10, 15–16;
Leviticus 6:7; Numbers 16:46)
    [1115] Atonement and acceptance specify the relation enacted through the types. Their fulfillment
consequently requires an offering which accomplishes the office the types assign to it.
   [1116] Third, Christ's death is a proper sacrifice and the antitype in which the sacrifices have their
accomplishment. The real effect must answer to what they represented in their carnal and typical forms.
The proposed account must therefore explain both what the sacrifice effects and why Christ offers it to
God. (Ephesians 5:2; Hebrews 9:11–14, 26, 28; 10:1–7; John 1:29)
     [1117] Melito follows the Passover into the act in which its saving relation is fulfilled. The sacrifice of
the lamb is joined to the preservation of the people, and the work anticipated in that preservation comes to
its accomplishment in Christ. As examined in paragraph 205, the effect belongs to the offering. A
doctrine of sacrifice must therefore give the offering an effect commensurate with the deliverance
attributed to it. (Melito of Sardis, On Pascha 1–10; 31)
     [1118] The sacrifice is offered and accepted in history, and the good which follows belongs to that
relation of offering and acceptance. The declaration of an inward change presupposes the proper ground
upon which the change is attributed to the sacrifice.
   [1119] Fourth, Christ deals with God on behalf of his people as their priest and with the people on
behalf of God as their prophet. The direction of his priestly acts is given by the office itself, for he acts

toward God to procure good things for the persons whose cause he undertakes. What remains of that
office under an account that permits only the prophetic declaration? (Hebrews 2:17; 5:1–2; 7:26–27)
    [1120] Both offices belong to the same Mediator, and each has its own immediate relation. Their
unity in the person who acts gives the work its coordination and leaves the direction of each act intact.
    [1121] Fifth, Christ appears before God for his people in intercession. The act must be explained
according to its stated direction and purpose. Does his appearance procure further fruits of love for those
he represents, or does the opposing account require him to declare to God the love which already
proceeds from God himself? (Romans 8:34; Hebrews 7:25; 9:24)
    [1122] Intercession carries the bestowal of the fruits forward on the ground of the offering, in the
conjunction established in paragraphs 91–113.
    [1123] Sixth, in offering himself through the eternal Spirit Christ pays into the Father's hand a ransom
for the sins of his people. A ransom is a price of deliverance and entails an exchange. The account which
makes the payment a declaration that no such price was needed must explain what Christ pays, to whom
he pays it, and how the declared dispensability of payment can be the deliverance for which the price is
given. Tertullian's old question reaches the same reduction. “Omnia in imagines vertunt, imaginarii ipsi
Christiani?” (Matthew 26:28; Mark 10:45; 1 Timothy 2:6; Ephesians 5:2; Job 33:24; Exodus 21:30;
30:12)
    [1124] The price is given to the authority whose claim is answered, on behalf of the persons
represented, for the deliverance which the payment procures. These relations give the ransom its actual
saving content.
     [1125] Seventh, Christ bears our sins in his death. To bear sin in the sense Scripture assigns to this act
is to bear its punishment, and the bearing of that punishment on our behalf answers the liability for which
it was due. The satisfaction follows from the representative office of the suffering itself. (John 1:29; 1
Peter 2:24; Isaiah 53:6, 11; 2 Corinthians 5:21; Leviticus 5:1)
    [1126] The sins are ours, the punishment is borne by the representative, and the effect follows in the
persons whose liability he undertakes. The judicial relation supplies the ground of the consequence.
    [1127] Eighth, Christ acts as our surety and undergoes all that Scripture threatens against sin and the
justice of God requires of it. The account of his suffering must extend to the full obligation he assumes in
that office. (Hebrews 7:22; 4:15; 5:7; Galatians 3:13; 2 Corinthians 5:21; Luke 22:44)
     [1128] The surety takes up the obligation of the persons represented. The consequence for their
liability follows from the acceptance of his fulfillment under the terms of that undertaking.
   [1129] Ninth, Scripture assigns to the death of Christ the purchase and procurement of determinate
goods. The account of his death must therefore explain the dependence of those goods upon the work to
which the passages ascribe them. (Isaiah 53:5; Hebrews 9:12; Acts 20:28; 1 Thessalonians 5:9; Luke
1:74; Romans 5:10; Ephesians 2:16)
    [1130] The benefits actually received attest the procurement accomplished by the offering. Their
enjoyment in time follows the relation between purchase and application established in paragraphs 252–
273.
   [1131] Tenth, the words “By whom we have now received the atonement,” are urged against the
purchase of peace and reconciliation with God. The inference turns upon what the apostle calls “the
atonement” and what he says the believer receives. If he means only our conversion or our act of
becoming reconciled to God, in what proper sense do we receive or accept the conversion which is itself
our act? (Romans 5:11)

    [1132] The recipient enjoys the reconciliation which is bestowed upon him. That reception proceeds
from the operation which procures the benefit and gives the apostle his ground for calling it a thing
received.
    [1133] Eleventh, to make everything done in and by Christ a sign of what is done spiritually in us is to
transfer the whole effect of his work into our inward experience. Follow the consequence back to the first
promise and through the gospel which fulfills it. The promised accomplishment is reduced to what the
objection calls a “childish thing?” (Genesis 3:15)
    [1134] The historical accomplishment gives inward application its procured good. The Spirit's real
operation in the person therefore belongs to the complete saving account in dependence upon the real
operation of Christ outside him.
    [1135] Twelfth, men who profess Christ are bound to the word of God in the trial of doctrine. Once a
disputed proposition withdraws itself from that rule, the refusal to submit it to the very authority by which
Christians judge the matter gives its own evidence of falsehood. What warrant can an alleged revelation
have to exempt itself from the test which the gospel imposes upon it? (Deuteronomy 4:2; 12:32; Joshua
1:7; Psalms 19:7; Proverbs 30:6; Isaiah 8:20; Luke 1:4; 16:29; John 5:39; 20:30–31; Galatians 1:8–9; 2
Thessalonians 2:2; 1 Timothy 6:20; 2 Timothy 3:16–17; 2 Peter 1:19)
    [1136] The authority of the word determines what may serve as a premise in this inquiry. An
assertion which claims exemption from that authority cannot establish a conclusion concerning what
Scripture teaches.
    [1137] The place of this examination follows from the late occasion of the dispute, and its grounds
have already been laid in the foregoing treatise. The controversy concerns the faith once delivered to the
saints, for it concerns the work by which Christ actually procures life and peace for his people. The
apostolic benediction gives the argument its proper close. “Peace be to the brethren, and love with faith,
from God the Father and the Lord Jesus Christ. Grace be with all them that love our Lord Jesus Christ in
sincerity. Amen.”
     [1138] The Father appoints the persons and the end, the Son procures the benefits by his offering and
intercedes for their bestowal, and the Spirit brings those persons into their possession through the
appointed operations. The saving work comes to its end in the people for whom it is undertaken, and their
faith and enjoyment follow from the same provision which ordained their salvation. Here the spokes of
the Wheel of Creation come again into view. Their relation to the Hub, proposed in paragraph 0, has been
followed through the persons given, the price paid for them, and the good into which God brings them.
